THE MAYOR OF NEW YORK

CHAPTER I

TOM RATLER


It was a picture of wonderful beauty, with elements strangely
transporting. The sky was clear, with vaporous vestiges of a thunder
storm, whose dark draperies of lightning stricken depths lay
effectively on the southern horizon; the moon was at its full, and
its unimpeded rays burnished the waters of the harbor with a silver
splendor. A group of men and women on the upper and forward deck
of an incoming steamer were the silent spectators of this scene as
the steamer lay anchored towards the New Jersey shore of the harbor
of New York in the coming year of grace 2000, and as the lingering
spring, with an already lost freshness, was passing into the warmer
flushes of the summer. The margins of that great bay which once at
such a time had been dark with the clustering volumes of leaves
upon the arms of encircling forests, was now girdled with a zone of
artificial light. It did not encroach upon the domain of the moon’s
regnancy, but it seemed to throb nebulous and soft upon all those
shores like a titanic wheel of fire. It represented the last triumph
of electrical science. It was an electrification of the air above
the cities, and that air was palpitating with shooting wreaths of
color that gave, for each changing instant, a new festal brilliancy.
The gazers were shocked into an admiring astonishment, which only
had an audible expression in murmurs of delight, or took the form of
appreciative exclamations.

The particular glory of this crown of illumination over the cities
about the harbor was its irregular surface or outline. Above the
island of New York it rose far up into the outer spaces, burning with
an exquisite rosiness; above Staten Island, where it was carried
still higher, the color was a strangely mutable blue, and over
the lower regions of Long Island and New Jersey it lay in auroral
sheets, that every now and then, with a billowy exhalation, shot up
phosphorescent projections that seemed actually to detach themselves,
and float away like luminous clouds. It gave a spectral splendor
to everything and on the deck of the steamer, and at the feet of
the mute on-lookers its reflections for an instant shimmered and
vanished. But the moon, unchecked by this terrestrial rival, shone
on, and its limpid refulgence on the faintly rippling waters lost
none of its charm, in contrast with the schemes of artifice around
it. In the penumbral glory dark lines appeared, which may have been
unilluminated and cavernous streets, while its changing lustres
revealed many a towering turret, especially over Staten Island and
New York island, and terraces of light marked the arched bridges and
their precipitous piers, over the East and North rivers.

The steamer, whose occupants thought they had arrived at some
celestial fairy land, was a coast wise “tramp,” and had steamed
from Portland, Maine, with a cargo of stone and lumber. Among
its passengers was a sturdy youth from the lumber lands of that
state—lumber lands which yet, with decreasing resources, strove to
supply the markets—who had stolen away from home, and determined
to try the tempestuous and stirring arena of the huge metropolis,
that now, at the dawn of the twenty-first century, expressing the
awfully expanded powers of man over material things, expressed too
his illimitable luxury, and held within its area an unfathomable
possibility of strife and devastating conflict; a city that had
become the centre of the world of thought, confining the seething
tides of a radicalism that pushed forward a militant atheism against
the still struggling standards of the Church; wherein too lay the
ripe and bursting seeds of an ambitious artistry that would scale
the heavens and reincarnate the flesh of man in an immortal youth,
while it lit all the torches of a brazen defiance of the moral law.
Tom Ratler had heard of this tremendous city, and his blood boiled
to meet its intoxications and his mind delighted in the dream that
he might scale its dazzling heights, and linger long beneath the
canopies of its indulgences. He possessed those things which go to
make the conquering man, pride, courage, an instinctive readiness
in danger, a supple mind, a compelling passion dressed in all the
allurements of a strong and noble form, his voice had chime and music
in it, and a certain spirited demeanor held attention and enforced
respect, and all this not unreasonably yoked with judgment and no
inconsiderable self-control.

Tom had been born in the Aroostook country of his native state, and
had lived the fresh healthful life of the fields. He had availed
himself of the advantages of a high school, had read, and, belonging
to a farmer family of excellent reputation and commercial prestige,
wore the garb of good manners, and listened to the incentives of good
taste. His mother was a woman of exceptional purity of heart, and
his father a rugged Ajax skilled in extemporaneous prayer, and not
unskillful in hammering an opponent to the ground. Tom was a young
giant perfectly moulded. His size did not surpass natural limits of
comeliness, but it filled with caution its unlucky antagonist. His
face was fair, and the rounded bullet-head not strikingly expressive
of scholarship—was sheathed in crisply curled hair like a Grecian
Apollo, his mouth broad and ingratiating held an armory of ivory
teeth—the charm of all mouths—and his eyes of chestnut, were sweet
and tempting, by the side of a straight nose and beneath a just
obviously receding forehead which however was a little narrow, while
at the union of the nose and forehead, a depression gathered a few
lines of tension around these formative kernels of flesh and bone,
which, to the phrenologist are the signals of observation.

None among those on the deck of the Barrymore had been more
astonished with the miracle of science and loveliness before
them than Tom, and he thirsted eagerly to reach this prospect of
delectation, and plunge into its weaving web of life confusion and
intrigue. As he leaned over the rail gloatingly feeding his eyes
on this vision of delight, and somewhat apart from the others, he
heard, and then he noticed a movement at the stern of the boat,
and on the side, which was followed by the swish of oars and the
emergent outlines of a row boat. He ran down the rail and whistled
softly. The next instant the row boat was alongside revealed from
stem to stern in the moonlight, with one figure in it, a Whitehall
fisherman or “dockrat,” who was looking for such chance opportunities
of profit as a newly arrived ship afforded in passengers, freight,
errands, or—less meritoriously—in “smuggle.” His keen ear had caught
the sibilant invitation of Tom, and he quickly brought his little
boat immediately beneath that interested person’s observation. Tom
whispered to him to “wait,” and then hastily disappeared, returning
in a few moments with his modest dress suit case, which vanished
as quickly as it had come over the side of the ship, and was
unceremoniously succeeded by its owner, the latter making use of a
loose rope fastened to the taffrail of the steamer. The row boat shot
out into clear water, and Tom then became the object of expostulation
and entreaty on the part of his former companions, who no less
eagerly than himself, wished to reach the planetary wonder before
them. Tom paid slight attention to these remonstrances, and urged his
guide to renewed efforts, while he seized an unused pair of oars, and
added an impetuous speed to the rocking and arrowy motion.

But Tom’s participation did not last longer than was necessary to
bring him closer to the docks of the city. Then he drew in his oars,
retired to the stern of the boat, and looked around him. And need
he had for he was within the scope of the opalescent marvel, and its
stupendous loveliness alternately crushed him with amazement, or
roused him to enthusiasm. His position may be understood from a few
contemporaneous land-marks.

The dancing row boat lay in that criss-crossed and vexed acre near
Governor’s Island, where the quarreling tides of the East and Hudson
rivers form a patch of troubled waters. On Tom’s right hand was the
island, now kept as a military reservation, but one hundred years
later forested and mantled with flowers, marked with paths, and
surmounted—in the old Fort Hancock—by a conservatory, while the
ancient keep of Castle William still remained, embraced in climbing
vines, with its roof converted into a garden of roses. On his left
the flaming pharos on Bedlow’s Island still burned, but in the hand
of the colossal figure there was no longer an electric star, but she
held a torch of flaming gas, whose long, wayward threads of fire the
winds now hurried upward, now stretched in horizontal films. Near
this dramatic station the immigration buildings on Ellis Island were
seen framed in light, and thence northward in all directions, along
the great rivers and in the median zone of Manhattan, tall structures
climbed upward, now dark by reason of the desertion of their human
households, but bathed over their summits with the descending rays
of that incredible aurora that filled the air above them. It was yet
early evening, and immediately in the foreground of this nocturnal
spectacle, was the old Battery park twinkling with lamps, in the
intervals of a renewed forestation which made it a dark spot on the
water’s edge, yet crisply dipt in a silver bath, that came from the
moon, mingled with a penumbral halo that came from the electric
arc far above it. Tom could now see the reason for the irregular
elevation of the luminous ceiling over the city. It was due to the
varying heights of the tall buildings that seemed to climb into the
skies.

The refulgence was also not everywhere; he could see that over broad
spaces of the peopled shores in Brooklyn and New York black lacunae
occurred where the shining curtain was absent. The boat had pushed
its way through the strife of waters and was nearing the little
Whitehall basin which then as now, afforded a retreat and harbor to
the battery boatman. Tom overwhelmed his pilot with a generous fare,
and jumped quickly to the landing, hurried up the stone stairs, and
was in New York, ready for adventure, not disinclined for mischief,
and fully committed to the wayward guidance of his fancies and his
fortune. His heart glowed with delight, and his mind leaped forward
in anticipations of forming a part in that shifting scene, which had
drawn to it the eyes of the world, for here again, as in ancient Rome
a tumult of social collisions had arisen, mingling with miscellaneous
and picturesque confusion with the last expressions of mechanical and
architectural daring, with the fevered promotions of every luxurious
taste, with erratic dissipations, and with a mental ferment of
scientific and literary and philosophic speculation.

Strangely there reverted to Tom’s mind from out of all his desultory
but avid reading the picture of Apollonius of Tyana arriving in Rome
in the cruel days of Nero. The book of Mr. Tredwell’s he had picked
up among a nondescript collection of odds and ends at a country-side
library to which gathered the tailings of private scholarship,
the chance offerings of missionary enterprises, an occasional
presentation copy from an enterprising publisher, and the discarded
extras of a newspaper office. Tom in a restless and skipping
manner had scoured these books for diversion, for instruction and
as youth will, for intimations, palatable but unhealthy, of the
currents of life in the channels where life moves most boisterously
and least restrained. Apollonius of Tyana quite caught his fancy.
That wandering figure, well furnished with the goods of the world,
appropriately screened from danger by a half supernatural grace,
wearing the garb of a preacher, and well drilled in local customs,
gentle with the bravado of spiritual superiority, and not averse
to finding out what the world contained, suited his own dreams of
action, though the companionable guardians of Tom’s fate in their
seclusion among the stars, might have smiled and shed tears over
the hazardous attempt to put Tom Ratler and Apollonius of Tyana in
the same category. Tom was not Apollonius, nor was New York in the
year 2000, the Rome of the year 50, though filled to the brim with
seditious, dissolute, serious and aggressively progressive elements.
Tom however—in his own thought—was seeing the world, or about to see
it. As he gazed upward that on-coming evening at the dark bulwarks of
the shrouded city, intermittently bathed on their summits and then
down their cliff-like sides by the aerial and diaphanous glows in the
skies, Tom felt the thrill of the unknown advancing towards him, and
all his eager senses reached out to receive its message and its gifts.

Nor was Tom a Nigel Olifaunt, the young Lord of Glenvarloch whose
adventures in London town at the beginning of the seventeenth
century Scott so humorously sets before his readers. He had come
upon no errand of restitution; and while his handy hand and emulous
spirits might engage him in some unlucky business, and his spirited
resentment bring him within the limits of a metropolitan Alsatia
he was not likely to become the victim of so unscrupulous a knave
as Lord Delgarno, nor run any risk of being tried for treason to
the state. If any comparison will help the reader to put Tom Ratler
in his proper phase of action let him recall Kenelm Chillingly’s
justification of his abrupt departure from his father’s home; “I go
in quest of adventure like Amadis of Gaul, like Don Quixote, like Gil
Blas, like Roderick Random; like, in short, the only people seeking
real life, the people who never existed except in books. I go on
foot; I go alone. I have provided myself with a larger amount of
money than I ought to spend, because every man must buy experience,
and the first fees are heavy.” Tom certainly bore little resemblance
to that accomplished cynic, and would have heartily derided—if he
understood them—most of his paradoxical conclusions, but like Kenelm
he had left home to see the world, and see it, where at the beginning
of the twentieth century it presented the most contrasted garniture
of forms and sentiments.

Tom noticed that while many persons were walking along the sea wall
of the Battery Park, and the settees were occupied by many couples
deeply interested in their mutual felicities, and a few families were
engaged in animated discussion of their household affairs, prospects,
and designs, the larger numbers were gathered around a central point,
whence the strains of very enlivening music proceeded. By reason of
its elevation, the band—evidently of large proportions—succeeded in
conveying these strains to the outskirts of its acoustic domain,
where Tom found himself. This concentration permitted him to walk
unimpeded around the wall, against the beautiful and shining bay,
looking towards monstrous beacons of light on the hills of Staten
Island, the shimmering walls of the Narrows and the nimbus of rays
that played over the populous shores of Bay Ridge, no longer retained
as a semi rural contour on the edge of the Harbor.

Scarcely conscious of any definite intentions, where all around him
invited him to continue a delighted and aimless ramble, Tom reached
the walls of the Aquarium, now occupying the old foundation of
Castle Garden, and, at the distant period to which we transport the
reader, yet retained, but immensely heightened and most inordinately
ornamented.

Tom could not clearly see the chase of zoological detail over the
glistening terra-cotta sides, though salient representations of
whales and porpoises, dolphins, tunas, tarpons, sword fish, and
sharks were descried by our traveller, whose astonished eyes however
were not always well enough informed to report their correct
identity. His eyes, bright and eager as they were, quite failed
to discern the wealth of marine naturalism, in sea urchins, coral
groups, medusas, hydroids, sea-cucumbers, molluscs, which encumbered
the ornamentation, mingled with the larger motifs, and luxuriantly
clustered at every interval between the swelling and pompous
immensity of the cetaceans, and the giant fish.

It was at this spot that Tom had reason to recall with gratitude the
agricultural training of his muscles, and to be roughly reminded that
the splendid city to which he had come was not unqualified in its
apparent possession of peace and order. In going round the circular
outskirt of this immense pavilion Tom reached its northern walls
approaching the confines of a great basin for the accommodation
of motor boats and naphtha launches, small pleasure yachts, and a
miscellaneous assortment of less definite water vehicles. Here the
shadow of the building was less successfully dispelled by the park
lamps, and as Tom turned to leave the angle, prompted to do so by
the deficiency of light, three figures seemed to spring from the
pavements, and with a consecutive movement too rapid to be easily
discerned, to fly at his back and front aiming fist blows at his head
and neck, and vicious kicks at his shins.

The most listless admirer of self defense, or the least intrepid of
its critics, would have rejoiced to have witnessed the lightning-like
metamorphosis in our whilome inert wanderer. His valise dropped from
his hand, and the motionless arms—so long and powerfully braced
with muscle, multiplied themselves with incredible rapidity in a
Briarean thicket of appendages before whose centrifugal violence his
assailants quickly vanished. The scuffle attracted some notice not
divorced from a desire, on the part of many, to see the stranger
worsted, and this sentiment was reinforced by the sudden approach
of a policeman entrusted with the wardenship of this quarter. A
sullen group of men and boys, vaguely referable to the Syrian section
of Washington Street, followed Tom, and their inquisition of his
appearance grew less and leas favorable, while a few epithets,
accompanied by a chance and unsavory missile in the shape of rotten
fruit acquainted him with his unpopularity.

Tom experienced a faint sense of disappointment, but his own warlike
instincts, readily aroused, permitted no movement of retreat, and
his disdain of his cowardly critics finally prompted him to turn and
walk with threatening alacrity towards them. This movement served
to disperse them, and as Tom remarked their skulking shapes, he was
joined by a much older man who had watched with interest and sympathy
the shifting relations of Tom and his tormentors. The stranger spoke,
and his measured and pleasant tones fell refreshingly on Tom’s ears,
to whom, overwhelmed by the immensity of his new surroundings, the
incident had seemed painful and unlucky.

“I see you are quite able to take care of yourself. I was ready to
take a hand myself against this bunch of loafers. I guess that you
are a new-comer, but don’t misjudge us from this little adventure.
The city is fairly orderly, as much so as most large towns, and you
can safely ramble over it at any hour of the day or night, without
much danger of molestation, though”—here the speaker drew a cigar
case from his pocket and handed its open end to Tom who with a slight
hesitation engendered by unfamiliarity with smoking, took one of the
proffered Wizards, as his new friend continued;—“though in some ways
we are dangerously near a domestic conflict much more serious than
street brawls or the incivility of hoodlums and nocturnal poachers.
Come over to the sea wall and sit down. I don’t wish to presume upon
the acquaintance my admiration of your strength has given me, but as
an older man and one not indifferently acquainted with the conditions
and the opportunities of this city, you may welcome some friendly
directions and advice.”

“Indeed, sir” answered Tom now thoroughly interested, if not quite
assured as to the honorable intention of his interlocutor, “I should
feel grateful for that. I am not a baby, and I believe that I can
pull through easily enough, but a few pointers at the outset may save
one from dangerous or expensive blunders. What is learned without
cost can be used without loss, is an old saying with us in Maine.”

As the two men moved towards the edge of the reservation it was
easily seen that Tom surpassed his companion in height, and if
this were not a sufficient assurance, any incipient suspicion of
danger would have been dispelled by the striking visage of Tom’s
acquaintance. He was a man of middle life hardly showing any of the
usual symptoms of increasing years. His hair, the weakest defence
against time’s encroachments, was yet untouched with gray, his face
was unmarred by a single wrinkle, his eyes of a peculiar brilliancy,
betokened intelligence and frankness, and the broad mouth, firm and
rather narrow lips, the chin perhaps a little too massive, confirmed
the unmistakable impression that our young adventurer had fallen
in with an unusual man—a man of observation and experience and
convictions.

Tom had himself quickly noticed these details and his confidence
increased, and even a sudden impulse of affection sprang up in his
heart as the momentary sense of his extreme isolation deepened into a
half reminiscent sadness. They had come up to one of the many seats
facing the bay, and the older man motioning to a bench, cut up into
separate seats by iron straps, sat down, while the end of his cigar
shone vividly as he puffed away, apparently abstracted, and making no
further overtures at conversation. Tom himself had become thoughtful
and silent, though he fully expected that the dialogue would be
resumed without his encouragement, and his mind dreamingly fell into
that mixed revery and consciousness, which overcomes us at such
restful moments. The waters before them, dark and viscid in their
unctuous flow, with scolloped concavities glazed with the moonlight,
lapped softly against the stones of the wall, and the band of music,
recoiling from some tempestuous climax, was breathing over them an
enticing melody.

It was Tom who first broke the silence between them.

“I suppose I can expect good employment in New York, sir,” he said,
yet unwilling to expressly declare his purpose. There was no answer;
and Tom looked more inquisitively at his neighbor, fearing that his
favorable deductions might have been premature. Again he spoke; “I
had a fancy that in this city of rich people I might get a good job
as motorist; I understand machinery, have used a steamer myself at
home, (as indeed he had), and reading of fair pay given capable men
in New York came on for employment.”

His companion woke up, or at least alighted from his soaring
meditations, turned a little towards Tom, and without the slightest
reference to Tom’s inquiry and declaration, began a deliberate sketch
of the social conditions of New York, its political dilemmas, and
its industrial power, almost as if, in response to some unheard
interrogation, he took advantage of the occasion to deliver himself
of a carefully prepared disquisition, though he conferred upon Tom
the honor of being the subject of that inaudible question.

“Yes, my friend,” he began, “New York has become the microcosm of the
whole world. Here are gathered together the nuclei of every school
of thought, every phase of social state is here; the abject poor,
the fabulously rich, and the industriously competent. Our population
is over sixteen millions. We are spreading to the furthest limits of
our territorial domain. Every art is practised here, and here each
art secures its highest rewards. Architecture has here risen to its
most stupendous and impossible tasks. Music finds here its greatest
audiences, and its exhibitions take place in the grandest temples.
Painting has here displaced the former superiority of France;
factories making every article that human ingenuity or human appetite
can invent for its satisfaction or its profit, are here in operation.

“We have become the cynosure of the world, and one million of
visitors a year has reversed the tide of curiosity from the old
world to the new; because our enormous wealth permits us to build
marvels and realize dreams. We have become a state, and this city
now possesses its own legislature, and frames laws for its own
government. Enormous universities furnish us with learning, and
under the impetus of an outrageous intellectualism large classes of
our population discard old habits of life, old creeds, old morals,
struggling with an impossible ardor to compass the meaning of life
in the surfeit of enjoyment and the rhapsodies of speculation.
The Catholic Church almost alone upholds the dogmas of the past;
Protestantism has deteriorated in Agnosticism, and Agnosticism
falls a prey to a devouring melancholy, which councils a happy
death, whenever life forgets its pleasures, or Age robs the senses
of their uses. Euthanasia, although a forbidden crime as the enemy
of the state, is secretly practised and from day to day sumptuous
Suicidariums are discovered and destroyed by the police.

“Science makes contributions, to our convenience, but is still dumb
on the questions of our purpose and origin. The transmutation of
metals is said to be almost discovered, and when gold can be made
from lead the tocsin of industrial activity will be sounded, and the
social machine, no longer able to maintain financial differences,
will run down, and, clotted with a metallic lubricant—no longer
serving its just ends,—will stop working, and our social state
resolve itself into a primordial protoplasm, whence the savage tribe
with chief and slaves, will slowly emerge, and the earth begin over
again its tiresome political evolution.

“But just now in this city the questions of policy grow daily more
complicated and dangerous. We have a gloriously rich class enjoying
the highest culture and withal benevolent, but living in undreamed
of splendor, and we have an educated class of operatives and middle
men with a proletariat of the very poor, who, often by their own
misdirection or proclivities, live stunted and desperate lives. These
classes form a vast body proclaiming a radical socialism and their
organization grows stronger and stronger with increasing population,
for family life, except within stringent limits, is discouraged by
the rich, and is despised by the _emancipated_.

“The balance of power will soon fall to the socialist, who lives
for breeding, and breeds by living. You have just come to our city
(and here the speaker for the first time turned directly to Tom, and
involved him directly in his allocution), at the close of a general
election for Mayor—the Mayor being the governor of our city-state.
It was a hot rancorous contest, and the successful candidate was
a compromise, a fusion brought about by the rich classes, the
moderately well off, and the Catholic labor vote. The new Mayor’s
position is a perilous one. He has hardly a bare majority in the
city Legislature. He himself is a dreamer, and over balanced with
metaphysical guesses, well trained in psychology, not inexperienced
in business, but at best a compromise, chosen to be the tool of
the rich, yet constitutionally a believer in some creed about the
Brotherhood of Man, and so without absolute direction, and without
force; and so also a farce, and a failure. Well—” (here the singular
monologue ended, and the speaker, who more and more astounded his
absorbed listener, stood up) “Yes—there are good chances for a
chauffeur of the right stamp in this city, where over ten per cent.
of the accidental deaths occur through motor accidents. Have you any
money?”

Tom no longer doubtful as to the integrity of his friend—his
rhetorical tirade convinced him that he was some one with
reservations of conscience—answered, as he also rose to his feet:

“I have three hundred dollars. Enough?”

The stranger had resumed his musings, looking away in the direction
of the Liberty Light whose flaring chords of flame now rose straight
into the sky like a contorted and tapering pinnacle of fire;—but he
heard; and turning again to Tom he said; “Very good. Go to the Hotel
Kohinoor near the Times Square. Get well lodged; Look well; Dress
well, and put an advertisement in the New York Times for a position
as a chauffeur. Give the hotel address. You will have answers to
choose from; mark me! Take my card! I will see you at any time.”

He handed Tom a card, and walked away after cordially shaking his
hand, though as he did it, he looked less at Tom, than at the
dispersing crowds who, no longer kept together by the band, were
dissolving in streams through the Battery grounds.

Tom moved to a lamp and read:

 BENTON BRIGGS
 (Secretary to the Mayor)
 CITY HALL

Tom of course was duly impressed, and thanked his stars that the
assault on him had been made. He placed the card carefully in his
pocket book, and proceeded forthwith to find out the location of the
Hotel Kohinoor. He found the policeman who had aided in interrupting
the machinations of his enemies, and from him was directed to the
hotel which his informant assured him; “was the all-firedest most
expensive house in town.” Tom perhaps quailed a moment, but he
partially divined the philosophy of Mr. Briggs’ advice; he wanted a
rich employer, his own qualifications were, he knew, unimpeachable,
a little spurious distinction given to him by his handsome quarters
would not form a prejudice against him; on the other hand it was
intended by his adviser to form a direct appeal to the fastidious
personal tastes of the rich employer he was after. He intended to be
careful about his advertisement, and he had quite enough reliance
upon himself and his good looks to improve a favorable impression.

Ruminating over these things Tom found his way to the Subway which in
the year 2000 maintained its supremacy as an artery of travel, and
was flashed through its blazing conduit, to Forty-Second Street,
where he alighted. The hotel Kohinoor occupied the present combined
sites of the Metropole and the Saranac and represented the most
munificent expenditure of skill and fancy known to the publican world.

It was the marvel of two continents. The most daring mechanical
device had been united with the strangest and most defiant artistic
conceptions, and a result roundly denounced by the better taste of
the day, was also blazoned and paraded as the consummate expression
of the New School, a hackneyed phrase devoted at all periods of art,
to give distinction to oddity, if not to the vulgarity of empty
display.

It was a monstrous pagoda of stone and stucco, ornamented with
extraordinary grotesques of great size, and involved a curious use
of colored decoration with surprisingly copious development of
electrical illumination. At night the tapering structure, prolonged
upward to almost two hundred feet became a shimmering and splendid
spire of light by reason of chains of lamps swung from tip to tip of
its projecting antennae, each chain from top to bottom being swung,
as it were, by a mounting line of grinning goblins and laughing
nymphs, rendered in terra-cotta and affixed to the ends of the
antennae, which, on each story numbered twelve, making a sort of
angulation of the whole building, and these polygonal faces were
constructed to form as many façades, and a different treatment was
allotted to each in ornamentation and color.

This facetted and rather brilliant composition had suggested the
name Kohinoor and, as a term descriptive of scintillation and
gem-like glory, it applied well enough to the chromatic and photal
encrustation of this singular architecture, whose effrontery of
design drew to its doors and rooms the richest and the most eccentric
patrons.

The interior arrangement was not less striking nor less effective,
limiting that term to the range of ideas expressed in surprise and
disordered novelty. It consisted, in outline, of a central air
space, or well, reaching to the apical skylight—itself a fantastic
medley of colored glasses—conical in form. From the contracting areas
of the successive floors, encircled at each floor or story by a
gallery, whose edge upon the conical well was protected by a _rococo_
railing of brass, hung aprons of English ivy. The ascending floors
were designed as radial spaces with diverging corridors producing
rhomboidal rooms, and the corridors opened at their outside limit
upon a broad balcony, cut up into stalls, for the occupants of the
rooms.

The ground floor, entered from the street by ascending steps, was a
labyrinth of columns, arranged in stars, and holding between them
groined ceilings luxuriously painted with multitudes of enlacing
figures, borrowed from all the stages of life, the personages
of history, contemporaneous character, the idols of the theatre
the legends of poetry, figures of mythology; an intemperate
extravaganza of portraiture, in whose chaotic investiture and train
the impudence of the artist had placed politicians in compromising
union with goddesses, and mingled the decorum of the pulpit with the
bacchanalian antics of the chorus hall. Momentarily it was denounced
as a scandal and later it was welcomed as a joke, and at all times it
crowded the place with sightseers and gathered to it a spendthrift
retinue.

Lounging chairs and sofas of a deep cherry red, imitating coral stems
and growths, upholstered in a sea green tapestry, filled the space at
convenient intervals, and every hour was signalled by soft utterances
of music from a mammoth orchestrion. It was all in bad taste
relieved by extreme skill, but from top to bottom of the building,
and throughout its details the loosened revelry of libidinous fancy
was given full play. For want of a method it chased over the known
world for the exotic, the bizarre, the beautiful, and brought them
together in all phases of contact, producing a colossal maze of
features that could only, with trained artists, react in a monstrous
and terrifying weariness. It was principally a marvel of decorative
ingenuity, picking from the resources of industry every thing rare
and costly. What might have been styled a system of house gardening
prevailed within, and patches of flowers were installed in ample
vases and bowls, themselves richly conceived and executed, kept alive
by artifice, and stimulated by electric shades.

Into this unusual hostelry Tom Ratler came with no inconsiderable
trepidation, provoked, and naturally enough, by some apprehensions as
to the sufficiency of his pocket book to meet financial requirements
which might conform to so marvellous a place.

He found that however, in spite of its splendor, it could afford him
in its skyward limits a small room at, if not exactly a small price,
no ruinous toll. So he was whisked upward in the elevator tube that,
as a steel tube in open work, represented a towering and stupendous
palm, extended through the central air-space, with bridges at each
floor for the escape of its travellers.

The room was tiny but deliciously neat and, to Tom’s eyes,
ravishingly beautiful. It was in enamelled white, with a wall
paper that began at the bottom with a flush of white blossoms
on a dainty pink ground, and ended at the top in pendent boughs
bearing yellow—golden yellow—oranges. The furniture was made from
a delicately stained green wood, designed in patterns of rustic
simplicity; the windows were shaded by crisp chintz-like curtains,
whose figures represented various idylls of the country, from
chestnutting to May-day frolics. A soft downy red mat or rug covered
the floor, and water colors of charming freshness, delineating the
undressed pleasure of the seashore, decorated the walls. A little
bath room opened off this fascinating boudoir, and Tom standing
in the centre of the bewildering beauty and comfort, dropped his
hand valise, whistled softly, and for an instant thought of his own
plain bed-room at home, in which, were the reader able to follow
our friend’s dream, a well stored book-case, rude but serviceable,
occupying an entire wall, brimmingly filled with books of all sizes
and on all subjects, would have been revealed.

Tom at once attacked the task of preparing his advertisement. The
writing escritoire was supplied with pen, paper, and ink, and a few
moments only were necessary for the rough outlining of his “ad” which
changed a few times finally took this form;

WANTED—A position as a chauffeur by an experienced driver of
automobile cars. References furnished. Address Tom Ratler, Hotel
Kohinoor.

Tom again descended the elevator shaft; was directed to the
Times newspaper office opposite to the hotel itself, entered his
application, and, unwilling to yet trust himself to the throbbing
and blazing streets, retired to his exquisite aerie to think of his
new surroundings, and to gaze over that carpet of light punctuated
with brilliant orbs or crossed by sombre shafts of stone, that like
an undulous and phosphorescent scarf floated over and through the
Capital of the World.

And to Tom’s musing eyes there seemed to be at times speeding meteors
in trained or panoramic succession passing in the sky. And there was.
For to all other marvels of the growing years Science had added the
partial conquest of the air.

CHAPTER II

HELEN LORIMER


On that long and high ridge known to-day as Inwood that extends
from about 155th Street and Lafayette Boulevard to the northern
edge of Manhattan Island where the rocks with moulded and spherical
outline dip into the peaceful currents of the Spuyten Duyvil Creek,
in the year 2000, wealth and art had created an encrustation of the
most wonderfully beautiful architecture, harmonious in expression
and imposingly ornate. It was called—the region—Carrara, a term
signalizing the almost unbroken use of marble or limestone in the
construction of the palaces that dominated this rocky and precipitous
comb of vertical schists, tumbling picturesquely into the waters of
the Hudson.

Looked at from the eastward elevations at Fort George, itself at the
beginning of the twentieth century a public park, extricated from
the present serpentine horrors of scenic railways this Inwood slope
presented a splendid façade. It borrowed features wrought in dreams
and pictures, it contained delicate and noble effects incorporated
in stone, blended together with unerring mastery, and linked with
each other throughout its entire length, as if in a tracery of
architectural wonders. Imagination, controlled by scholarship, played
here its part with subtlety and composure. It dealt with creations,
and it dealt with them as the Creator has dealt with the world,
through the imposition of Law. A little of that bizarrerie and
witchcraft, rampart elsewhere in the stupendous City, had faintly,
yet noticeably touched the august and serene construction, but had
touched it as the coruscations of golden edges touch the towering
clouds at sunset.

It became almost roseate under the spell of some transfiguring
sunsets, dark and effective beneath scowling skies, and again
shone like alabaster in the zenith sweeping sun. Conceive a long
heaped hill of chateaux, built entirely of marble, except at a
few telling points relieved with colored stones, their principal
pediments crowning the summit of the hill, and their approaches—that
on the Kingsbridge road—an ascending terrace of steps, with broad
platforms hedged with green thickets, plentifully sprinkled with
wide and capacious vases, opulent in flowers and trailing vines—the
trumpet creepers sometimes lit up the snowy walls with orange
carmine coronas—here and there a noble tree, usually a pine or
fir, fitted with artistic precision, cut out, cameo-like, against
balustraded loggias. Conceive the crest of this hill, a sky-line of
towers, minarets, angulated gables, flat roofs—on which again by a
somewhat indecorous fancy flowers were strewn—and clotted ambuscades
of spires. Pass beyond the eastern exposures to the westward,
and imagine a contrasted aspect over the steep escarpment of the
Lafayette Boulevard; see how, limited by the precipitous declivities
there, the multiform expansions on the east have changed generally
into flat walls, graced indeed by niches and balconies, overhanging
extensions, but rising with perpendicular austerity to considerable
heights. It was as though a growing and lovely crystal had flattened
against an impregnable partition, and, revealing its color, concealed
its form. The topography of the region compelled this curtailment,
since the villas could not well be built over the ragged and vertical
edges of the cliffs, though by an extreme audacity, at points, with
great expense, the ornate or diversified architecture had been pushed
out over the beetling rocks, by means of iron piers estopped in the
gneiss beds, whereon, upon a platform of iron also, the structures
had enlarged symmetrically to their formal type.

This partial abbreviation was not necessary in all cases, as where
the platform at the summit of the ridge was wide enough to allow
the development of the building. The hill side on the east, thus
constructed in related and harmonious architectural contours,
summoned an enthusiastic admiration. It represented the aristocratic
isolation of extreme wealth, in a class born to indulgences, fostered
by education, and not devoid of intellectual distinction.

And Carrara represented a wholesome unity of purpose; in an artistic
sense its design was consolidated into an expression of the
renaissance, and except at a few spots, where the prevalent taste for
irregularities and the conceited thirst for strangeness—a strangeness
based on kaleidoscopic conglomerations—had impertinently entered its
purer and poetic thought, like a hectic weed among chaste flowers,
the aggregation was a lovely structural symposium.

Northward, where now the protecting spire of the House of Mercy
pierces the ambuscade of trees, towers small and round, large and
square, many affixed in corbelled tiers to angled mansions, and some,
campanile-like, aspiring upward with tenuous and diminishing lines,
filled the sky. Further south on the declining slopes that bend to
the Dyckman road an interfering group of flat topped Norman keeps,
their walls broached by wide embrasures, with brick chimneys on
their sides, picturesquely soaring above the interlaced and somewhat
compactly combined trees, brought the eyes of the reviewer to the
more stately Italian villas, that decorously encumbered the southern
section of this long Inwood elevation.

The harder rugged contours northward, with their architectural
austerity, here met an expanded and luxurious invention. Extreme
taste and the insinuation of transatlantic memories were apparent.
Park-like expanses along the Kings Bridge road, traversed by paths,
ascended to the marble stairways whose stately and interrupted ranges
led to the marvellous villa palaces irregularly delineated on the
crown of the ridge.

Among these was one which by some idiosyncrasy of ornament,
some bewitching adjustment of parts, and predominantly by a rare
invocation of the graces of plant decoration drew the attention
most quickly, and held it by a richness of detail, controlled by
refinement and discrimination. It was throughout coherent in style,
with such exterior embellishment as to attract notice, without going
so far as to disfigure its technical virtue. Its architect would
have suited Mrs. Rennselaer’s requirements of being “content with
conforming his structure to its function, and modelling the masses
thus arrived at, so as to heighten their inherent expression.” Yet he
had taken his renaissance quite sincerely, and absorbed with devotion
the mainsprings of artistic design it furnished. The mansion was
Italian, and by reason of its scholastic fidelity freed itself from
the aberration of the treatments which had become rampant in the City
elsewhere, and which, to revert to the same authoress’s denunciation
of an earlier forerunner of this boisterous school assumed “rather
the character of an explosion, than of an evolution.”

The villa was not however a stone fortress, such as guarded the
treasures of the Florentine nobles, nor precisely those less simple
constructions of Rome. Deprived of the foil and the accompaniment
of the water, it yet simulated the style of the Venetian palace,
and rose, a tetragonal block of incised and decorated masonry, its
stories separated by ribbons of ornamentation in balustrades, their
windows arched and spandrelled, their pilasters grouped and salient,
with the corniced roof dropping in pendant spindles of cut stones,
while still higher rose a low parapet pierced by circular apertures.
Sculpture and chiselled delicacy of the brocaded stone surrounded
some of the basement windows. These latter continued to the floor,
became doors, and these opened upon the highest terrace, itself
fenced with columns separated from each other by a low intricately
cut wall, while each carried on the flat top of its capital an
electric lamp within an opalescent shade.

The obvious incongruity of this pile on the table top of the ridge
was successfully masked by the addition of a grove of trees on either
side, and by the spreading terraces, that with a medial convexity and
deployed edges, melted into lateral coppices of bushes. The terrace
formed by this peristyle was the entrance courtyard of the mansion,
and the distant observer gazed upon the facade of the house, through
a wide interval between the columns themselves, which could be closed
when desired, by two richly designed iron gates.

This composition possessed the rational elements of beauty. An
intellectual grasp gathered its not over-charged detail into a unit
of purposeful expression. We are to enter that beautiful home and
within it again the evidences of culture, and the outlays of fortune,
would fascinate us, but they will pay tribute, in our minds, to that
woman, who, like Hypatia in Alexandria sways the heart no less by
her loveliness, than by the strength and depth of her nature, Helen
Lorimer. This challenging spirit, clad in feminine allurements,
had been called in the socialist paper of the day—The Beehive—“the
demoniacal Syren of Injustice.” This abode of cunningly devised
beauty is hers.

The few words dropped by the Secretary of the Mayor to Tom Rattler
in his fortuitous encounter with that young man, and which may have
sounded enigmatical, and certainly ominous to the reader, may now,
at this stage of our history, be usefully enlarged upon, as it will
bring this unusual woman into intelligible relations with the coming
events to be here described.

Movements throughout the twentieth century had reflected that
changing molecularity in the social stage, which presaged new and
perhaps perilous rearrangements of its political constitution. Their
potency was more conspicuous in America, than in Europe, for in
America the mobility of the social fluid was more established, and
the solvent of opinion worked more rapidly and with less opposition;
the organs of publication and propagandism plied their trade utterly
unfettered, and not without admirable aplomb and sincerity. Similar
movements of course had made themselves felt even more obviously
in Europe, and had provoked repression, that was violent, but
successful, for in Europe so deeply rooted and inexpugnable are the
tradition and the fact of class, that power had only to appeal to the
military support to still maintain ancient forms. In fact it had long
been felt that the armies of Europe were less necessary to maintain
existing states than to curb and extirpate the brooding forces of
insurrection and discontent, less necessary to defend frontiers than
to establish garrisons as a network of protection against internal
foes. Throughout large portions of Europe a certain sloth of mind,
the inertia of custom, the niggardliness of choice, and a certain
artistry in the common people with an undeveloped culture, attached
them to the livery of caste, and created a residue of resistance to
radical change.

Still in Europe there had been trouble, and the arguments of
socialists and other more unrestrained and dangerous, illicit and
quarrelling cults, had kept the social cauldron boiling, and the
ci-devant crust, formed over hitherto quiescent and hidden contents,
in many places was broken into fragments, rotating and pitching
in a turbulent commotion. But that, at the exact moment of this
story, had subsided. In America prosperity and education, which had
reached deeply down to the lower areas of society, had resulted
in contrasted conditions, but in a no less active and indeed more
energized demand for some sort of renovated and metamorphosed state.
And for this reason. The Trust system had enveloped everything;
and quite acceptably to the majority of the people. Established in
the nineteenth century, its first status had considerably changed,
since, by reason of the regulative legislation inaugurated by
Theodore Roosevelt, it had instituted enormous popularizing movements
throughout the nation, whereby its stocks of all sorts became
distributed, so that separated and numerically large communities
were its beneficiaries. The dangers predicted one hundred years
before, of its subversive power over justice, and its high handed and
conscienceless demolition of competition, seemed to have disappeared,
as in the first place the trusts were forcibly regulated from
Washington, and in the second their power over trade had practically
exterminated the individual merchant, or left him a tributary to the
Trust’s needs.

Their avenues of emolument were unimpeded and they were numerous.
But however much they had absorbed the savings of the laborers and
middle men of all sorts, of course, immense fortunes, rolling up into
colossal sums, had accumulated in the hands of a rather vast group of
rich men and women, and the tireless development of all the utilities
of the state, and the resources of the soil, with an enormous average
prosperity, had augmented swollen thrift, and created fortunes which
were the marvel and the gossip of two continents.

By the mere fact of their almost superhuman possibilities of
indulgences, these rich classes, at several points in the nation’s
domain had gathered into brilliant and luxurious colonies, and in
New York they had concentrated into a nucleus of really dazzling
splendor. Now contrasts work the ruin of states, if interested
agitators and the exorbitant envy of malicious men, or even the
plausible volubility of self-deceived reformers attack them.
Physical conditions in New York were not bad at the beginning of the
twenty-first century. Science and a most munificent and extravagant
government had given to the populace almost everything they might
reasonably expect, but there were poor and poverty, a great deal of
it, indeed, and amongst the dreams of the socialistic philosopher was
the abolition of both.

New York city had become a state with its governor (the mayor) and a
legislature, and it had incorporated the elements of socialism quite
momentously, in so far as the street cars and all public utilities
were controlled and operated by the city government, the ferries,
telephone, telegraph systems within the city’s limits, and it was, at
the era we are now studying, contemplating exercising some sort of
discretionary power over the disposition of wealth beyond a certain
amount in the interest of public improvements, public hospitals,
and public entertainments. It had advanced the ideas of public and
free education beyond all previous conceptions of convenience or
propriety, by maintaining a free theatre, and a free opera, whose
primary institution had in view the presentation of classic art in
the drama and in music, but which, under a lax administration and the
encroaching influences of a deleterious gayety, had declined somewhat
from these ideals.

For it must be remembered that socialism had at this time assumed the
features of a most frank and to our mind, most repellent naturalism.
Without suffering from need and lacking the incentives of acute
deprivation, the poorer classes had attached themselves to leaders,
some of whom as to-day were rich men whose political ideas involved
the utopia of universal competence, individual equality, radically
extended to every function and faculty of man, and they mingled
with this irrational aim a cloud of violent vagaries, in which
age disappeared, sickness, the family, traditional religion; and
humanity, by the preposterous hocus-pocus of their blandishments of
words, became something different, ineluctable, and divine.

As a matter of fact, in spite of all this vaporing the visitor to New
York in the year 2000, from this present year of grace, would have
found a prevalent social resemblance to the civism of to-day. The
theory of the suffrage remained the same, also its use, courts of law
tried cases, the police repressed crime, the streams of people in
the streets presented sharp and informing contrasts, papers reported
the news and, with art and circumspection, discussed their meanings,
occasional correspondents ventilated their nostrums or their wit,
the book stores and the publishers still fed the eye and mind with
elegant books and sumptuous illustrations; in Wall Street the same
fevered haste and nervous throw of guesses; elsewhere the marts
of trade plied unremittingly their business of getting money, and
dissipation and amusement claimed their votaries, and claimed them
with a freshened splendor of apparatus and intoxicants.

He would indeed see churches, cathedrals, though his distinguishing
observation would have noted the vast predominance of the catholic
houses of worship. One astonishment might be expected for him; it
was the larger size of the men and the robust beauty of the women,
together with the appearance of a type of face especially pleasing,
in which an artistic or poetic fire dwelt, a suggestion that would
have been provoked in his mind by a certain depth of coloring, by a
suavity of demeanor, a graciousness of address with a predisposition
to expressive smiles, a luxuriance of hair, and essentially by dark
and luminous eyes.

This type had been projected, through intermarriage and
domiciliation, from the Italians, and somewhat, by a remarkable
refinement of face, produced in Hebrews who had married Italians.
The Italians had developed in America an extraordinary and consuming
patriotism, with an incessant and almost belligerent skill in making
themselves felt in its art, and its politics. They had eventuated
into an important fibre in the national issue, and markedly so in New
York. As a rule this type were smaller than the average in height and
size, and again this larger type belonged to the Germans, Americans,
_in stricto sensu_, and the English, and this macropodia, as the
faculty designated it, had resulted from health and exercise.

But our anticipatory visitor would have quickly noted the physical
magnificence of the great city, its inordinate ambitions in building,
in design, in contrasts, together with the evidence of a positive
mania to erect statues, itself a most illuminative proof that the age
had undergone some mental or emotional changes, since that period,
one hundred years before, when the claims of art in the streets had
either been neglected or awkwardly remembered by limping executions.
This change again had been effected by the Italians, in conjunction
with the strong American school of art. And among all the marvels of
the metropolis, Carrara, in its marble glory would have most acutely
raised his marvelling admiration.

This unique precinct, with its amazing glory of architecture blending
into an expression of consummate taste and boundless wealth, the
reader has already been asked to notice. A more difficult task for
the writer is encountered in imposing upon his imagination the
character and the temperament and mind of Helen Lorimer whose force
and fascination swayed so effectively the people who built and
inhabited this region of delight. Here again a retrospective study
is necessary, and the reader must inspect the phases of this woman’s
ancestry. Helen Lorimer had not been the originator, though she
was, at the moment, the most influential and eloquent exponent of a
remarkable movement which had elevated the rich into an intellectual
distinction, which had made of them a class by themselves, not from
the possession of wealth, but from an indisputable eminence of
aspiration and achievement. All the rich were not certainly in this
class, but the processes of selection and sympathy had separated into
a phenomenal isolation and a real prominence those, who, conjoined
by a religion of practical ideals, met the requirements of a very
exigent and noble standard, wherein also grew a lofty and not
altogether prepossessing pride.

As early as the end of the nineteenth century a few rich men had
begun a process of self-appraisement and conciliation. Rockefeller,
Carnegie, Stanford, Widener, Mills, Morgan, Jesup, and others had
signalized their oneness with the great majority, and their personal
sense of responsibility, by large gifts to the common weal, both
in benevolent and in educational foundations. They established a
certain nobility of conduct, as essential to wealth. Such an impulse,
elaborated and added to, in the prerogatives of superiority, had
developed, in the closing years of the twentieth era, notably by the
powers and talent of a group of the rich, who had achieved eminence.
_Noblesse oblige_ in them had assumed an expression of high motives,
painstaking industry, and deliberate culture. They established a most
curious system of self inspection. They insisted upon an aristocracy
of talent, and scholarship, and prowess as well. A social congress
among them settled the possible career of the younger members of
their class, whether in art or literature, or public service, and
this settlement was quite rigorously recognized and obeyed. They
aimed at a splendid publicity before the people and they won it. At
every point—such was their programme—they intended to make their
class felt as powerful and as illustrious.

And it was the doctrine and the policy of self protection. The more
astute had felt the dangerous—in their eyes—movements of the day and
they knew that a high bred and insertively practical and ornamental
prestige before the world, would protect and establish their union
with the state. The indication of talent among the young of their
class was noted with rapture, and the young possessors were pushed
along prescribed paths of prescribed duties; so that this wealthy
aristocracy were influential at all points in the civic circle, and
shone with an enviable brightness before the country. Relentlessly
pursuing this plan they even went outside of their class to ally to
themselves new blood to engraft upon their own stem the strength
of its promise and gifts, wherever it was discovered; and these
new recruits, chosen—but with circumspection—anywhere, were caught
up into the heavens of culture, and their talent augmented by
education and opportunity, while they themselves, body and soul, were
incorporated in the caste that became their sponsor and host. It
was a really singular phenomenon, the inward growth of a realizing
conviction that a rich society should knit its life with the nation’s
in a meritorious service, where, ever and anon, the fire of genius
lit a resplendent flame.

This movement, at the beginning of the twenty-first century, was
at its zenith, and among the most noble, and intellectual of its
exponents and actors, was the beautiful woman, Helen Lorimer. It
was said of her that she combined the brain of a Harriet Martineau,
the soul of a Florence Nightingale, and the bodily glory of the
Fornarina. In her conjunction of circumstances and the generosity of
nature had united to form a very perfect being. The blood of Italy
was in her veins bearing its passion, and the blood of the New World
bearing its sense. She was a dreamer but she lived in the field of
action. She painted her horizons with pictures, but she fashioned the
instruments of her designs with judgment, and made those pictures
part of the solid earth. Phraseology with her became a portent of
new plans, and the plans vividly recalled the language that depicted
them, without losing much of that fidelity, when they became facts.

Miss Helen Lorimer presented to the world the fusion of traits and
powers, which, like some admirably contrived fruit in nature, was
the result of lineage. Not that her descent involved any reference
to distinguished forebears, but that a union of characters had
developed in her which heredity had secured from a cross in stocks,
whereby, to use a Mendelian phrase, the dominant traits were all good
and remained, and the recessive were all bad and had disappeared.
Her grandparents had been Italians, who in a love match had come to
America, and had quickly won success, so that their children, one of
whom was the mother of Miss Lorimer, secured all the advantages of
education, with those of travel.

Her grandfather belonged to a good family of Florence and her
grandmother was a fashionable belle in the city of Genoa. Their
elopement provided romantic incidents, in so far as the parents on
either side, united in a frantic effort to separate or intercept the
runaways, as they had completely disarranged some prepared alliance,
commercially more valuable. The chase ended with the sea-side, and
Antonio Ferrara and Francesca Espozito enjoyed their married bliss
uninterrupted, and surrounded themselves, in their new home, with a
family of five children, the third of whom was a girl of physical
perfection, possessing musical talent and a voice of melting
contralto richness. This popular addition to her native charm, and
the excellent social and business position of her father made the
young Julieta a widely popular _debutante_, and, at some public Ball,
she had ensnared the affections of Robert Lorimer a scion of one of
the richest of the New York circle of millionaires and a devotee of
art, naturally not inattentive to the enchanting sweetness of the
young Italo-American girl.

Young Lorimer with the studied gracelessness of artistic and
emotional youth failed to inform his family of his desires and
designs, and, realizing at rather a late moment, the value of the
family recognition of his matrimonial choice, contrived to introduce
his fiancée to his family’s notice under the following promising
circumstances. He inaugurated a series of impromptu concerts which
were given at houses on the Carrara. In one of these Robert arranged
a picturesque situation depicting himself as a wandering artist,
ravished with the beauty of an Andalusian orange grove, and less
impersonally seduced by the maiden, who, lingering behind the other
gatherers, at the close of the day, turns on him her inexorable eyes
and just as a sentimental and well regulated moon sheds a mellow
radiance upon an abundance of Florida Grape fruit heaped up in a New
York market basket.

The lingering orange picker of course was the irresistible Julieta of
whom the wandering and transfixed artist asks a number of irrelevant
questions, and on expressing a surmise that she probably could sing
is answered by the sudden outpouring of a melodious Spanish love
song, in which Julieta, herself hopelessly entangled in the same
silken chords with the handsome Robert Lorimer, exerts her skill to
immoderately confess her own predicament. The trick was a complete
success, and the young woman rapidly won a technical position,
whence her introduction as the daughter-in-law of Algernon Lorimer,
the head of the Lorimer establishment, though somewhat disparaged
by the timidity and decorous hesitation of the mistress of the same
institution, was definitely accomplished.

Robert Lorimer inherited his father’s brain and much of his
mother’s consistent delicacy of feeling, and developed what was
quite undiscoverable in either, a deep artistic creativeness, less
signalized in broad conceptions than in subtlety of composition, and
the mysticism of shadows, half lights, and spectral shapes. Julieta
magnificently completed her conquest of music. Her vogue was immense,
and she and her husband engaged with enthusiasm in that regulation
and elevation of the rich classes which we have pointed out which
became a consecration and mission in their daughter Helen.

Helen was their only child, and as upon her would develop the trying
responsibilities of the Lorimer millions, and as also she revealed
precocious gifts of mind, with a startling beauty, Robert and Julieta
lavished upon her what might be called a bountiful care. In other
words, they took every imaginable means to enrich her mind, to mature
her nature, to refine her feelings, without losing sight of the
immense opportunism nature had granted them, because of her beauty,
to make her glad and buoyant. And the subject of all this care
responded with precision.

She grew daily in graciousness not unmixed with roguery; she engaged
her time with scrupulous regard to every demand, and fulfilled the
promises of her parents in music and art. Her taste became more
massive than her father’s and her music more German than was the
flavor of her mother’s luscious Italian melodies. She had advanced
upon her parent’s standpoints, and they admiringly noted the firmness
of her grasp in scholarship, the speculative earnestness of her
reflections, and the unexpected depth of her religious feeling.
In a sense and way they felt a curious awe of her, as if her
thoughtfulness betokened some dreadful reaction of weariness and
collapse, and they watched her at times with sincere anxiety, and
with whispered comments of mutual confession.

But Helen ripened with absolute normality. She loved friends, she
entered the customary diversions of society with a vigorous thirst
for all its reasonable pleasures, and she brought to her feet the
young men who found her delightful in discussing a regatta, or a
lawn tennis tournament, or in the tripping coquetry of winning
raillery, and who left her enthralled, without dreaming (except as
common talk gave her reputation) that she could narrate the history
of the popes, or give them a review of the early systems of American
philosophy, or even with less effort, set before them the triumphs
of modern music. In one aspect of human learning Helen Lorimer was
apathetic. She spurned science; not indeed with self-satisfaction
and disdain, but by a quiet repulsion of silence when its vaunted
triumphs were rehearsed, and its importance was mentioned. Something
in the hard and fast outlines which science gives its topics,
something especially in the sodden matter-of-fact dreariness of its
professors, and yet more relevantly, in her case, something repellent
in its unmistakable rejection of the unproven and the imaginative.
Its war with religion jarred her, and she withdrew from it as from a
monstrous and fatal thing which would wreck the beauty of the world
of poetry and art. At the age of twenty Robert and Julieta went with
her to Europe, to its capitals, its galleries, its historic centres,
completing an exhilarating survey with a prolonged visit to the city
of Rome. The impressions left by Rome upon Helen were critical. They
influenced her whole life, and made of her instantly the instrument
of accomplishing the most momentous religious change that the modern
world has seen. It was at Rome under the seraphic touch of inspired
painting, under the thrill and the mystical appeal of antiquity,
under the occult fascinations of symbolism in its ritual, that Helen
imbibed faith in the Catholic church, and threw herself at once into
its arms, without misgivings and with a trembling joy.

This was not wholly unexpected by Robert, who in many conversations
with his daughter had been struck with her reactionary tendencies in
thought, and her many museful inquiries into the power of the Roman
church, while in a natural way the habit of Julieta, brought up a
Catholic, reasserted itself with the ardor of resurrection in the
younger woman. The matter at first gave her parents some concern, but
in so cosmopolitan a temperament and so loving and soothing a nature
as her father’s, there could not be long a permanent objection, and
in the case of her mother there was just a secret satisfaction.

And so Helen Lorimer became a Catholic, and her profoundest
affections turned—at first lightly—to a strange project, one barely
to be mentioned aloud, one only to be dreamed over in the darkest
hours of the night, or brooded over in the uttermost seclusion of
revery and anticipation; _Helen Lorimer yearned to bring the Pope to
America_.

It was a mad thought perhaps, some Catholics might have deemed it
iniquitous, and most would have found in such a plan the dissolution
of the church from all its temporal and historic verities. But Helen
was not thinking of Rome and its associations, perhaps not clearly
concerned was she at first with the Catholic church itself.

She was intensely patriotic, she felt a divination of the vast
political tendencies of the age, and she saw around her in the
states a disintegrating Protestantism, or thought she saw it; with
too, a very militant Catholicism, she believed the restitution of
religion to the country she so ardently loved could only come through
a momentous application of sacramental grace, and that, she also
believed, would be brought by the Pope’s removal to America. She
had indeed become entirely familiar with the savage dissatisfaction
of a large portion of the Italian capital’s population with the
Pope, and she knew that the body of Leo XIII yet remained unburied
in its selected sepulchre at the St. John Lateran, the beautiful
basilica outside the immediate limits of the City, because of this
dissatisfaction. She indulged too, in other fancies, as that the
Pope, taken away from Rome, and placed in America, would mean a
renovation of the church itself, an infusion of the fresh blood of
her country, with its unselfish attachment to ideals, in the parched
and worn channels of circulation in the church, so that its altars
might more worthily carry the weight of that supreme sacrifice which
daily fed its votaries. The emotional tides of her being, stirring
now with restrained and yet compelling force, swept her on into
pageants of speculation, where her susceptibilities received the
stimulus of dazzling and painted pictures, pictures of the church’s
triumph.

When Robert and Julieta returned to America with their wonderful
daughter in whom they began to see the promises of a powerful and
creative personality, they encountered the secularistic movement
among the rich which we have partially hinted at. Nothing could
have better suited the lofty temperament of Helen Lorimer, with its
aristocratic isolation as also its sane industrial zeal to add to the
beauty and usefulness of things, than just this attempt to give a new
and special significance to wealth, building up a group, a class, a
caste, eminent by talent, by accomplishments, by public devotion, and
so enshrining their heritages of mere money within the protective
virtues of character and mentality.

Helen turned to this new thought with all the energy of her
temperament, and wrote, talked, missioned, gathered around her
coteries both of men and women, who received power at her hands to
accomplish the desirable result, and her home—fully surrendered to
her for her own best purposes by her parents—became the headquarters
of the secularists. Older heads than Helen’s, also saw the
usefulness of this. They knew the widening strides of, to them, an
unaccountable socialism; they heard its threats; and while nowhere
else in the world was there such remarkably distributed competence
and household happiness as in these United States of America, at
this one point of New York socialism raised an ominous head, showed
a spiteful tongue, and at the last election—that referred to by the
Mayor’s secretary with Tom Ratler—manifested a most unwelcome and
alarming numerical power.

It was well and necessary that socialism should be robbed of its
influence, cut loose from the moorings of discontent by the utility,
the generous attitude, the fine quality in its acts, the nobility
of its sentiments, the individual strength of its members, of the
rich. Helen had advanced all previous schedules of action in this
particular. She had given public lectures, had entered the enemy’s
country, had translated the lessons of history to her popular
audiences, and had immensely swayed her listeners by her eloquence,
and no less by her conquering beauty, a beauty which came to them
dressed in the habiliments of an opulent taste, for Helen Lorimer
instinctively rejected that shallow condescension which would
palliate differences by false pretences, and the masquerade of
imitation.

Her strength had indeed disconcerted the editorial corps of The
Beehive, whose younger members became as much reconciled to her views
by her face and form, as they might have been by her syllogisms had
they taken the time to carefully rehearse them. Helen reiterated
the philosophy of regime, and dwelt on the analogies of nature. She
contended frankly for the gradations of society, and urged it as a
biological law or as a principle of physics. She popularized Hobbs
and she disseminated Montesquieu. She christianized Hume and lent a
new flavor to the amenities of Talleyrand. She was a success, and The
Beehive had retorted by epithet and innuendo.

In time the windows of life darkened to Robert, her father, and he
died almost in her arms, a deeply loved parent. He had watched his
daughter with a gracious pride, and an almost pathetic obedience to
her slightest wish introduced him to her intimacies of thought, and
made him the confidant—more than her mother—of her unusual hopes. On
her father’s demise the Lorimer family consisted of Robert’s older
brother Theodore, her mother Julieta, and Helen herself. Theodore
was a childless widower, and although somewhat more severe and
practical in type—being a good business man—than his brother, fell
too under the fascination of the gifted woman, who now represented
the residuary heir to the immense wealth of Algernon Lorimer, her
grandfather, a Napoleon of finance with more enduring qualities than
this misused appellation usually connoted.

In many ways perhaps Helen Lorimer recalled the image one might
form of Margaret Fuller, but it could not be said of her, as Miss
Martineau had said of her distinguished contemporary, that she lived
and moved in an ideal world, “talking in private and discoursing
in public about the most fanciful and shallow conceits which the
transcendentalists of Boston took for philosophy.” Helen Lorimer
doubtless moved and lived in an ideal world for herself, but she was
practical, and this aspect of her nature gave her that humanness that
made her delightful to those immensely removed from her intellectual
eminence.

And she had the saving grace of humour, wholesome, natural,
indisputable fun. She denounced indeed the widespread flippancy of
the day which represented the levity of a world which had seriously
lost its faith, and restlessly sought a sensuous satisfaction,
drinking, to the death of all ennobling feeling, the ready made
potations cleverly concocted of mere pleasure; but she knew, none
better, that the world was wonderfully ingenious, wonderfully smart,
apt, and in the works of construction and convenience wonderfully
helpful. Only she felt that the world revelled in too much delight,
she felt that it would forget any outward and ulterior destiny
in its passionate absorption in its own triumphs, its incessant
excitements. Death and old age and infirmity had had sobering and
realizing shocks to apply to carelessness in former times, but at
the beginning of the twenty-first century, science had marvellously
dissipated even these, in conjunction, let it be admitted, with a
kind of psychic medicament whereby thought helped to banish worry and
ailments.

Miss Lorimer recognized that the world moved, but she would have
substituted other motives. She often said that the world must have a
motor power and it would always be either pleasure or religion, as
the thirst for knowledge actuated too small a numerical number to
become a virtual force in its economies or plans, and of these motors
she preferred religion. To the poor it had always been the great
consolation.

And rich as she was, Helen Lorimer felt that the arbitrament of
life’s worth rested at last with those who most sorely tasted its
disappointments and yet, with so developed a sympathy with suffering,
she clung tenaciously to the hierarchical conception of society’s
classification, reverting with a kind of mediaeval intensity to past
social states.

About this woman Romance spun its silken and iridescent webs.
Youth bowed to her smiles and lost its heart in her favoring
glances. Perhaps her cloud of chevaliers was made up of a tested
selection of admirers, or those confident and brave enough to
match their acquirements with one so loftily endowed, but if so,
it was not because of any pedantry in her. A delightful geniality
made her always companionable, always winning, and put at ease any
_soi-disant_ swain, who indeed might have been thereby captivated
with his own eligibility. The blood of Julieta Espozito was in her
veins, and nature disdained to release her from the impulses of all
her sons and daughters. If this academic review has aroused the
reader’s interest, let him now step into the presence of the woman
herself, see her charms, hear her voice, and begin with us the
history of a short episode of her life.

CHAPTER III

A DINNER IN CARRARA


The _entresol_ of the Lorimer mansion was occupied by the dining
hall, a room of magnificent proportions and superbly conceived in
decoration, the elegance of its detail being dominated by the size
and breadth of a few features.

One wall was breached by a wide entrance, from which descended a
series of steps to a lower hall; opposite this entrance the high wall
contained an admirable reproduction of the painting of Alma Tadema of
the Hunt of Diana, between two high windows, issuing upon balconies
overlooking the Hudson; either end of the room held two enormous
mirrors framed in Japanese bronze designed in flowers, fruits, and
trailing creatures of the field; under these astonishing mirrors were
two fire places equipped in monumental brass; on the sides of the
entrance the walls were partially concealed by two large Madonnas,
one by Perugino, the other by Tintoretto, and under these pictures
two sideboards in carved teak wood held a rich assortment of blue
India ware grouped around a few high silver vases, whose surfaces in
repoussé reviewed the spoils of the hunter; the corners of the room
received four large groups of statuary, which separately symbolized
the seasons; the ceiling was a massy plane of gold unrelieved by
any designs, and from it depended three chandeliers of cut glass in
blue aqua-marine like Brazilian stones; the walls were treated with
a pale veronica blue, the chairs were of twisted Honduras bay wood,
and yellow silken curtains masked the light that poured in at the
casements, whose outlook faced the Palisades, and caught the purple
banks of the New Jersey hills.

The floor of this apartment was in Pompeian mosaic, and at its centre
rose the singular dining table, which was literally nothing else
than a huge block of stucco of a pale terra-cotta ivory color, cut
out underneath into arches, with a frieze at the edges of the table
top representing oak leaves and acorns. This rigid and somewhat
unaccommodating erection had a very beautiful effect indeed, and
added an entablature to the room that made it more stately and
impressive. Heavy rugs invested the floor, and banished its stony
coldness, which effort on their part was successfully reinforced
