by the flickering and homelike light of the candles in the quaint
majolica candelabra over the mantels at both ends of the room. This
at night, but otherwise and when not animated by the presence of its
natural human occupants the great chamber uttered a note shading into
an unpleasant solemnity, as of a public hall or a museum foyer.

It was that liquid season of the year as the spring retires from
the presence of summer, leaving at the latter’s feet the garlands
of her flowers, sweeping away the last fragrant curtains of April’s
showers, and inviting the auroras of the sun to rise higher in the
skies, while, as she fades away on the white blossoms of May, she
already touches the petals of a thousand waking roses, and summons
the tides of life, upon whose floods June approaches, to renew the
world. It was indeed a little before the opening of June 2000,
that, as the shades of night fell, or rather as its shadows grew
accentuated beneath the orbed splendor of the moon, a group of six
persons were seated around the dining table of the Lorimers, whose
physiognomies and demeanors were sufficiently contrasted, though
harmonized by a common culture and deportment. The table—the stucco
block we have mentioned—was itself a picture of bounty and refined
effects. A large golden _epergne_ shaped as a cornucopia borne in the
arms of tritons on the back of a silver wave, stood at its centre,
holding a tumultuous wealth of roses, the table itself was covered
with a delicate sheeny diaper of silver threads, a _chef d’oeuvre_
of eastern workmanship, found by Robert Lorimer in a bazaar on the
east side of New York a tantalizing and wonderful mixture of linen
and metallic filaments, teazed into a low relief, on which figured
peacocks and weeping willows by sweeping streams; and the service
was—at the moment when we are called upon to observe it—made up of
faintly golden chased Bavarian glass, with old Sevres china.

At the head of the table sat Julieta Lorimer, the mother of Helen,
and the singing Andalusian orange gatherer of Robert’s youthful days;
on either side of this still beautiful woman were two contrasted
figures, one a Catholic clergyman wearing a clerical garb in which
the distinction of the wearer among the princes of the church was
simply denoted by the red buttons on his vest. Facing this dignitary
was a tall and foreign looking man with a high forehead that gained
an unnatural prolongation by reason of its retreating lines of hair
with rather somnolent and unresponsive eyes, and the relics of a
constantly vanishing smile. By his side sat his wife, a flourishing
and elderly lady somewhat over dressed and wearing a complexion of
surprising brightness and vivacity. Facing this personage and by the
side of the cardinal was a handsome man, younger than any of his sex
in the room, abundantly supplied with soft flowing chestnut hair,
having pleasant and scrutinizing eyes, a mouth of much mobility, a
matured moustache, and hands peculiarly well shaped, which, at the
moment, were drawn together before him, resting on the table while
his half turned head looked, as did all the rest of the company,
towards the sixth member of the party who had left her chair at the
opposite end of the board, from her mother, and now stood at the
foot of the Perugino Madonna, from whose sheenless surface her face
was just withdrawn, as she returned the attention of the company and
spoke. It was Helen Lorimer herself.

A very inattentive observer of the human face and form would have
been arrested by the figure before us, and his interest would have
noted the enhancement given to both by dress and action. A gown of
blue silk bound with a belt of meshed gold, held by a Chinese buckle
framing a carved, apple-green jadeite, the waist fitting loosely
and falling away from an uncovered neck encircled by a thrice wound
necklace of pearls, with sleeves slightly _epanché_, trimmed with a
gold thread, and covering tightly closed undersleeves of Irish lace,
that were kept to the wrists by two hyacinth bracelets, arrayed
this unusual person; and, as the observer’s eyes traveled upward to
the face his admiration might have passed through various phases
of approval, wonder, or delight, according to his years, and the
temperamental warmth of his appreciation of a woman’s beauty. The
face was moulded in ample lines, it carried the impress of generous
impulses, it was full and strong, but in no measure lost thereby
the imprint of a delicate and lovely femininity. Soft folds of dark
brown hair, gathered at the back of the head in a chignon, and
plaited over the forehead in rather high folds, were decorated by
two side combs of filagree gold enriched with garnets, between which
and over the chignon itself rose a twig of silver, jewelled with
red tourmalines. The hair so sumptuously arranged became the lovely
visage it framed. The noticeable impression of its shortness was
caused by the full cheeks deeply colored and wearing the complexion
of the south, but, that observation passed, the mind fastened upon
the glowing and tender eyes, that, by some habit of their owner, at
moments half shut, as if in reverie, and then opened slowly with
ravishing smiles that played around them, and lingered long enough
to permit the parted mouth to close, and reveal its rosy cushions,
which did not become voluptuously weak, but formed themselves into an
organ fitted for speech and for caresses. The nose was straight and
firm, not pronounced, not lending to the face the repugnant sense of
intellectualism; the ears were small and shell-like, barely detected
beneath the encompassing hair; her hands, raised in description,
were perhaps characterized by strength, perhaps a little broad, but
finely veined, long fingered, facile, and gracious, and wearing, as
she stands under the madonna, two rings, on the right hand, a spinel
solitaire and a diamond marquise. And her voice?—that most excellent
thing—it completes our capitulation! It is the mingled tones of a
rich contralto and the lower registers of the soprano, perhaps it
was too sweet and only seemed to gain the requisite steadiness of
authority, when raised in pitch, and urged by some dominant emotion.
The whole composition was one of character and physical charm,
with the accompanying color values of artistic dress and a noble
environment.

“Yes,” said the voice in which was vibrant a reminiscent joy, “it
was at Florence that father saw the advertisement of the sale of
this picture. I remember the evening that, as the sun embrowned the
Palazzo Vecchio, in a gorgeous sunset over the distant Piedmont
hills, we drove to the auction room in the Via Brunelleschi, and
found the room crowded with a discordant mixture of English, French,
Italians, and a few rather timid Americans upon whom however, the
discrete auctioneer placed his hopes of establishing a profitable
standard of value for his pictures. It was amusing enough. Father
was determined to have the picture because I told him I liked it,”
and here the eyes drooped, and then the play of smiles, “but he ran
against an equally determined Englishman. I think he must have had
a daughter who liked it too. Well the auctioneer was in a frenzy of
happiness trailing his bait before two such promising prizes—when
suddenly there were no more bids,” the laugh again, “and then I
saw that the Englishman and father had recognized each other; they
made some signal and declined of be used against each other for the
auctioneer’s advantage. And the last bid was not from either father
or the Englishman. The owner of the picture had made it himself. The
picture was not sold that evening; in reality, the owner was its
purchaser, but the next day the auctioneer came to father and said
something about the ‘regrettable incident’ and offered it to father
at his own terms. And so it came to America.”

Miss Lorimer had returned to her seat and the company again
readjusted themselves to their former positions. We may pause a
moment to make the individuals of this company better known. The
cardinal was the recently elected prelate Monsignor Fenton of Newark,
N. J., the tall man with his wife, Count and Countess Schinazi of
Hungary, and the handsome young American, Professor Dale of the
New York City college, a philosopher in his own behalf, and the
instructor of metaphysical youth.

“An entertaining and serviceable story indeed,” said the cardinal as
he touched his lips with the Chartreuse in a sparkling thimble of
red and gold, “but I expected you would let us know what reference
that had to the subject nearest to my own heart—the subjection of the
Church in Italy, which my daughter,” and the speaker half oblivious
of the rest turned to Helen Lorimer with a paternal gesture of
affection, “is a subject not lightly considered by yourself.”

Miss Lorimer leaned back in her chair, a tide of color deepened in
her face, an interior light added a stranger power of beauty to her
face, seeming to issue from her eyes, and she assumed such an ardor
of expectancy that the young professor gazed at her with a deliberate
and not altogether courteous absorption. Her seriousness quickly
disappeared, she moved forward, and raising the cordial to her lips
invited the group in a tone of raillery to drink in a bond of secrecy.

“Yes, I have a secret and you shall know it, but what the Cardinal, I
think, wishes you all to hear is something that happened after father
bought the picture.”

The cardinal demurred, just a trifle, saying that “he had not had the
story so much in mind, as some discussion of the savage intolerance
of the enemies of the Church, a topic of some interest to Miss
Lorimer and himself, and not distantly connected with the secret Miss
Lorimer referred to,” and the prelate again turned to the Chartreuse
appreciatively, and smiled at the matron at his side.

Julieta interrupted, “Tell the story, Helen; I remember it faintly,
but I do recall the excitement it threw your father in, and how
quickly it ended our visit in Florence. That was ten years ago; how
time flies!” and a shadow of pain, that all noticed, crossed the
speaker’s face, vanishing almost before seen, as she with a gesture
of encouragement looked up again at her daughter.

The Countess Schinazi raised her glasses inquiringly, and then
touched the arm of her husband who had been subjected to the same
entrancement which kept the young American speechless, and wondering;
“Alfred, ten years ago we were at the baths at Carlsbad, and don’t
you remember,” she dropped the glasses and tapped her dreaming
spouse a little more sharply on the shoulder, who turned to her with
the vanishing smile undergoing a peculiar expansion that threw the
lower portion of his physiognomy into an ocean of creases; “we were
at dinner with the young Misses Tarbox, when their brother came in
and said there was a report that Robert Lorimer had been killed in
Florence? Can’t you recall that?” and the tap became so sharp a
punctuation in his side, that Count Schinazi exploded volubly; “Veree
vell, my dear, veree vell. It was an occasion of some interest to me.
I svallowed a peach pit.” The laugh was general, the count’s chronic
risibility developed to his eyebrows, and the countess oscillated
with pleasure.

“It is evident that the story must be disposed of,” continued Miss
Lorimer, and her eyes moved pleasantly from the professor to the
cardinal, and back again lingering, it might have been observed, a
little longer on the former, “You see father, after he had finished
with the auctioneer, went back to the sales-room to give some
directions as to the packing and address, and to be quite sure that
the picture he had really bought, would be the one which would be
shipped. Pardon,” turning with a deprecatory smile to her Hungarian
guests, “the intimation, but these mistakes have occurred. Next to
the sales-room there had been a meeting of anarchists the night
previous, and somehow one of the speakers had alluded to the fact
that a rich American, an oppressor of the poor in his own country,
had bought at the auction then going on, an expensive painting of the
Pope, which in his opinion should be destroyed, and not be permitted
to go to a new land, to spread the seeds of superstition. Those were
his very words, as reported in the Florentine papers;” the cardinal
flushed, and he reviewed the company with a hasty and indignant
glance; “Father identified the painting, and later in the day watched
its removal. A little remnant of the anarchistic meeting was present
as the picture came out from the house, and as it was put on the
wagon to be taken away these men, as if by prearrangement rushed
on the men carrying it, and driving them off, began to hammer and
hack at the box, shouting, ‘_death to the enemy of our country_,’”
the cardinal’s eyes were now riveted on the fair woman, who was
herself conscious of his inspection, “Father saw the melee, and ran
among them, denouncing the outrage. Incensed at the interruption the
marauders attacked him, and he was knocked down and kicked, and then
the _gendarmes_ flew to the spot, and the demonstration was over,
with the culprits in the hands of the police. Father appeared against
them and they were fined. That is the story.”

“Ignorance inflamed by malice, flattering itself with the empty
nostrums of a shallow and conceited pedantry, torments—and will
torment—the church,” was the sententious reflection of the cardinal.

“And yet,” broke in the Professor, whose voice while very clear,
with the best enunciation of an American scholar, had a most musical
quality, a peculiar fluty cadence that caught the ear quickly; it was
not lost on the cardinal, and it brought a stare on the countess’s
face that meant worlds; “and yet, is not the church much to blame for
the enmity it provokes; has it wisely construed its responsibility
to the advance of human knowledge? Was it sagacious, or, less
obsequiously, was it just, to excommunicate Father Alfred Loisy at
the instigation of the superior of the Jesuits, at the beginning of
the last century, when the conflict raged over the attitude of the
church towards what was contemptuously termed modernism? The vulgar
tirades of street agitators, and the taunts of mere worldlings are
indeed despicable, but must the church remain impregnable to thought?”

“The church,” answered the cardinal, who was now thoroughly aroused,
and who, as he spoke, kept his eyes fastened on Helen Lorimer, “is
impregnable to guesses which pass for thought. It never forgets its
mission or its commission, one is to save the world—as far as it
can—the other is a divine consecration which keeps it imperturbably
within its office amid the turmoil and the tempests of contemporary
arguments. What is modernism? It has been a texture of speculations,
and practically, as far as the church antagonizes it, it has meant
the dethronement of God, so far as his creative functions are
involved, and its exculpation is what it is pleased to call the
testimony of the rocks. Nothing in physics or chemistry is alien to
religious feeling, but the process of detraction and subtraction
which reads Evolution for God is intolerable. Against a determinate
evolution the church may have little objection; it is itself an
example of determinate evolution. Newman showed that.”

“Pardon me, but I tink,” interjected the Count, while the countess
viewed him with an irrepressible pride, “I tink that the church is
as you say here in America all right. But I tink it is veery old and
just a leetle sick. It might, I tink, as you say in America ‘get
a fresh hold’ if the Pope came right here, here in these states,
vere he might be, as you say in America, a leetle more chesty, more
independent, more free, more, what you say, ‘on the job.’”

Julieta smiled, the countess frankly laughed, Helen looked musingly
at her plate, the professor was mystified, the cardinal glanced an
instant at the speaker with a fleeting expression of scorn, and the
count himself, plainly a comedian, kept the vestiges of his smile
forcibly confined to the corners of his mouth.

Again the cardinal spoke and again he directed his remarks to the
professor, while his eyes sought those of Helen Lorimer.

“As a metaphysician are you quite sure that the reports of your
senses are real, that the so-called phenomena of the world are
indubitably, profoundly, essentially true. Is a cartload of bones in
the Cretaceous as really as much of a fact as the thirst and asking
of your own soul, as the hunger after righteousness, for which there
can be no permanent assuagement outside of the postulate of God, and
some sort of authorized convention between him and man?”

The professor turned to his critic, and said with a smile that was
urbanity itself, “My dear cardinal, as a metaphysician I am not
averse to regarding the world and the reports of our senses from
several points of view, but as a natural human being I unquestionably
bow to the requirements of my environment and have no more doubt
as to the reality of all that geology tells us than I have of the
desirable existence of our hostess,” bowing to Julieta, “or the
charm of her daughter, or the necessary functions of your own
zealous service to those of your faith, and I think that the church
misjudges her prerogatives if she regards the cartload of bones from
the Cretaceous with anything else than the gravest interest, and is
prepared to accept their witness to something, as sacredly as she now
ascribes meaning to the words of revelation.”

The cardinal fidgetted a little, fingering his chartreuse, and
audibly murmured, “Philosophy inside a classroom and common sense
outside of it, are, it seems, to be irreconcilable aspects of life to
the end of the chapter.”

It was then that the voice of Miss Lorimer rich and full, captured
the ears of the disputants as she leaned over towards the professor
with a smile of reconciliation; “My friends, while the fruit is being
served to you, let me monopolize your attention to the confession
of a secret of mine own, which the cardinal knows about, and which
curiously the count has most recklessly invaded, though I bear him no
ill will on that account,” and she extended her hand deprecatingly
towards that pleased personage.

“I am sure ze company vill be much in my debt Mees Lorimer, if I
haf ze honor to have forced you, as you say in the politic, to, to
disgorge,” and the count with an ineffable grin of amusement and
satisfaction over his verbal conquest leaned back in his chair,
while the countess in a tone of petulant cajolery, waved at him with
her glasses, saying, “Really, Schinazi, you are too ridiculous,” a
reproach which developed the count’s smile as far as the ends of his
twisted moustache.

The chairs were drawn somewhat closer to the table and Miss Lorimer
continued, “Doesn’t it seem true to you all that occasions are
created to be used, and that those who use them make history. An
opportunity missed means a disfigured, or abortive, at least, record
perhaps for an individual, perhaps for a nation. Now, perhaps I will
offend you, Count, it seems to me that Europe is growing very old,
that its interest is in its memories; its energy is traditional
only. A breath would destroy it. The chastening power of revolution
has been felt there, but since 1790 no strong and lasting currents
of feeling have done much to change its political surface. Russia
is hopelessly inert, stiffened into inaction after its anxieties at
the beginning of the last century. Life there is a routine, death a
release. Faith has vanished because faith in living, in thinking, in
being, in doing has vanished too. It is a tomb of ruins, peopled by
sacristans, threaded by tourists: is it not a rubbish heap, in which
by dint of digging one can find mementoes of greatness, but where any
splendors of the spirit no longer can be described, much less felt?
Is it not wrong to leave the fountain head of our religion—yours and
mine, Cardinal—in a choked up and littered field, where its waters
of healing and life run the serious risk of being hidden in the dust
of neglect and the refuse of abuse? My secret, friends, is to bring
the Pope to this land of ours where life, movement, action, progress,
ideals, thought, play over every thing and where even the things we
hate or oppose have color and look alive?”

The silence that followed this announcement was a tribute to its
novelty, and the listeners appeared to be taking note of their
sensations, as if wondering to what extent they either cared for it,
or approved it. A slightly whimsical smile of interrogation rested on
Miss Lorimer’s face, as she looked at the Count, and perhaps it was
in response to that which made that person first break the silence,
just a little disconcerting to the last speaker.

“I tink, Mees Lorimer, that Europe iz not a charnel house as you
suggest nor edzactly a grave. It iz old, it iz a leetle, as you say,
played out. It iz tired. A good many peoples haf lived and died
there, and it iz monotinous to go on doing ze same thing so long. I
tink the trouble just now in Europe iz that she go to sleep and haf
nightmares.” The Countess glowing abundantly as she watched with
transparent happiness her husband move along the slippery path of his
elocution, led the laugh, “But it vill vake up; it iz imposs—ible dot
someting not happen—and soon veery quvick. Dere vill be what you call
a ‘meex-up,’ and den de Europe spring up, and shout, and jump up, and
cry _Allons_; I tink so.”

Professor Dale who had eagerly watched the count with not a trace of
amusement, answered across the table with his eyes still seeking the
count’s,

“Few things could be stranger than the present stupor of Europe. It
is asleep; it has the sleeping sickness. Fifty years ago in 1950 it
looked as if its toy game of kings and queens, and the rattle of its
tin soldiers was coming near an end and the people were responsively
rising to new opportunities. But it passed as a shower passes in
the night, and then deeper, deeper, the spell of a hypnotic apathy
falls, and we hear only its snores, unless as the Count Schinazi
intimates it occasionally screams at a bad dream.”

“Theodore, just the other day,” it was the pleasantly modulated
voice of Julieta, “read me a strange impeachment of Europe. It said
that Europe had become paralyzed; music and painting and literature
had come to America, and it pictured an ancient hearthstone in an
artist’s studio with chairs and instruments and easels exquisitely
made, and with a fire burning with a comforting invitation, but with
no artists, no hands to guide the strings or the brushes, and only an
old janitress looking curiously in at the half open door, while her
cat strayed forward and slept at the fire’s edge.”

“I don’t pretend to be able to talk about such things,” alertly
interjected the Countess, “but really, you know, I feel that Europe
is a play ground, a big show, an expensive continental museum with
regimentals and dressed things walking up and down and rattling
their spurs and saluting, amid splendid old palaces, and lakes with
charmed boats and rivers, fixed up with castles, and all kept going
you see, as long as America pays for the machinery and the orchestra;
Bless me! I shouldn’t say such things in the Count’s hearing, except
that as a breeder of Game Cocks in America he still honors a line
of warriors;” the malignant sally, that practically put the entire
company in a quandary, was not resented by the Count, whose smile
escaping its seclusion behind his curled moustache, as if with the
levity of an unusual freedom, spread to the roots of his hair and
the back of his ears. This benignity kept every thing in place, and
it was the intellectual accent of the Cardinal that next solicited
attention.

“Europe to-day at the beginning of the twenty-first century has
the _rigor mortis_. Its genius and talent follows the incentive
of gain. They come _here_ and they thrive. One hundred years have
administered to us an enormously enlarged culture and it insatiably
pulls from Europe every promising blade of creative power. The
stimulus of applause, of an augmented audience, and in no vulgar
sense is here also. The battle ground of opinion is here. Our hard
and boisterous crudities are passed, we are no longer menaced with
alien segregations, the ethnic problem has vanished, not altogether
by solution, but in nature, like some mythic tree, we bear many kinds
and many colors of flowers, as it were; a modified Italian—but still
Italian—rose here, a changed French—but still French—lily there, a
new Hebraic artichoke—but still Jewish—on this bough, and a less
redolent German hibiscus—but still German—on that, and all fed,
and rejoicingly fed, from the same national root. We have become
cosmic, and in our land the final clash of the conflict of opinions
approaches, unless indeed this fagged out earth is doomed to an
eternal struggle with the battle grounds changed from continent to
continent. Science and Religion offer their clues to the central
mysteries. The measure of their values is determined _here_. I think
Miss Lorimer’s plea should be respected. Let the Pope—the Head of the
Church—come to close quarters with his antagonist, the Apollyon of
material Lust, and of intellectual denial.”

“Thank you, Cardinal,” it was again the voice of the first speaker,
“I almost thought that my audacious fancy, bold as it was, had been
covered up, and thereby just a little abashed, by reflections that
were intended to keep us from considering it at all. Every one was
jumping the traces, and running a field. Yes! shall we bring the Pope
to America?”

“Quevstion! quevstion!” murmured the Count, like the impatient
voters at a primary, while the cardinal with an exorbitant curiosity
painting itself upon his expressive countenance, looked around the
table, the goal of his scrutiny evidently being the Professor, and
the unrebuked silence continued, until, as if answering the voiceless
implication that it was he who should break it, the Professor said;
“Yes! the challenge has been often given; Let it be accepted, where
the swords of both duellists are near enough to each other to not
only strike fire, but to give the _coup de grace_.”

This truculence was perhaps a little harsh for the occasion,
and Professor Dale felt its awkwardness; he turned blushing to
his neighbor; “Pardon, Miss Lorimer, but the Cardinal is an
uncompromising foe, and I suppose,” leaning to the Cardinal, “the
Church would prefer death to sufferance?”

“Hardly,” answered the prelate, “the Church has often prospered upon
little more than toleration, but on the other hand if it came to
Science, asking for sufferance, it would be told to close its doors.”

“But vy! vy!” and the Count’s voice rose with a compromising
seriousness, “dere is room for every ting, dis and dis, and dat and
dat, and so ve grow a beeg, beeg, rug, vith a tousand colors and
tousand threads, and on it the centuries come and go, and eet nefer,
nefer wear out,” the ardent Hungarian had an inspired look, and his
wife who apparently compensated him for her snubs, by an unimpaired
adulation whenever he opened his mouth, became visibly grave, and
dispensed a glance around the table, as if in admonition of his
captivating simile.

“Well! Speaking of rugs,” said the Cardinal dryly “they are
associated in my mind with Turks, and Turks are not the most
desirable citizens; they certainly are not members of the Church,
and a national rug of a thousand threads and colors is only another
metaphor for chaos.”

“You mean Cardinal,” said the Professor, “that the Count’s
promiscuous carpet, would never harmonize in color or design with the
tapestries of the Church?”

“Perhaps,” gaily rejoined Miss Lorimer, “I can twist the Count’s rug
into an image that suits the Cardinal and myself better. It shall
have a thousand colors and a thousand threads—ten thousand for that
matter—but they will not make a miscellaneous and inconsequent woof;
they will form a beautiful and gracious figure,” and the lady smiled
as if there then floated before her mind’s eye something that fled
from the profanation of a name.

The company rose with probably a different conclusion as to the value
and meaning of Miss Lorimer’s disclosure, resting in the mind of
each. But it dwelt with a singular poignancy of interest with two,
and one of these, at least, lavished upon it a wealth of improving
comment.

Scarcely had the group with gliding step reached the entrance of the
grand chamber, and the attendant had drawn aside the curtains which
revealed as they parted the noble stairway descending to a lower
plane, constituted by a wide hall now enveloped in a golden glow,
that descended from an electric corona, almost two stories above it;
from which again suites of rooms extended to the right and to the
left,—scarcely, had this moment arrived, when a resounding rap smote
the front door of the hall, and, as a servant darted towards this
entrance, the door itself was pushed from the outside, and the tall
figure of a handsome elderly man entered. The face of the stranger
was disturbed, and he flung down his walking stick with a gesture of
despair. The next moment he raised his eyes to the arrested guests,
and repressing his impatience moved to the foot of the stairway and
spoke;

“Ah! my friends you must pardon my absence. It was not intentional. I
see too well what pleasures I have missed. Of course Helen explained
to you that I was called away down town to attend a meeting of
directors of the bank, who are negotiating with the city for the
latter’s accommodation of a loan. The meeting was interrupted by an
alarm, that proved only too true, that the socialists had started
a tremendous riot on the East side, and that fire, incendiary in
origin, and attributed to them, had broken out in several quarters.
Your lecture hall, Helen, where you have attempted your propaganda of
the Divine Mission of Wealth, is burned down, and those scoundrels
of The Beehive have doubtless had a hand in it. It was a compliment
gratuitously offered to yourself, but I think it has in it the darker
meaning of a menace. Come to the porch and see the signs of the
Future.”

It was Theodore Lorimer, Helen’s uncle, who spoke, and the company,
quickly assenting to this invitation hastened down the steps, and
moved out upon the front terrace, lit by the electric globes upon
their pillars. From this point an actual fire could not be descried,
but the skies south and east beyond the intervening ridges were
plainly illuminated, the light rising and falling, and at its highest
intensity, as it scored the low lying banks of clouds with reflected
flashes, it revealed the summits of high buildings that momentarily
spanned the firelight beyond them with black columns.

Theodore Lorimer led the company to the railing of the enclosure, and
it so happened that the Professor found himself standing beside Miss
Lorimer, between two of the marble pillars. Her face was discernible
and it bore the marks of a kind of subdued anguish. She looked at him
and pointed to the horizon growing more and more lurid; “Are these
lights indeed signs of the Future as my uncle has said?”

Something intensely tender burned in the Professor’s eyes as he
answered softly; “Miss Lorimer I do not know—but when the Pope comes
we shall see whether they go out or whether they burn higher.”

“Ah!” answered Miss Lorimer, “You mock us,” and she came closer to
him with her hands clasped.

“Mockery!” he rejoined, “Mockery! Surely mockery ill suits one who
may become a supplicant, perhaps a penitant,” and the professor’s
voice had a rueful accent, and his face looked at hers with ever
so slight a glance of passion, from which she turned away with the
faintest possible sigh of disappointment.

CHAPTER IV

THE SUICIDARIUM


Tom Ratler had been almost a week at the Kohinoor, and as yet no
intimation had come to him that he was wanted in the vast gregarious
city, that grew to him more and more monstrous, and rapidly put
on the form and functions of a maelstrom; a maelstrom where life,
property, reputation, were engulfed, again momentarily to reappear,
as if the regurgitation of the whirlpool shot its human victims back
to the surface, to enjoy a new glimpse of respectability before
they finally were sucked under, forever carried away in underground
sluices to the merciful and impersonal ocean of oblivion.

Or else at other instants the city seemed to him a kaleidoscope with
its human occupants, its myriad fancies of design and architecture,
its dissipations, its gluttonies, even its piety and all the agencies
of its philanthropic life thrown round in a spinning circle,
confusedly glimmering and changing color, from which too arose an
accompanying roar of voices, not unfrequently punctured with cries of
pain, not always without passages of music.

Tom had made friends cautiously, from a sense of danger, and with
some agitated memories of what he had read in books. At first
the number of social advances to him were suspiciously numerous,
and he somewhat indiscriminately classed them as a concerted
conspiracy upon his pocket book. That indispensable support had
already considerably shrunken, vanquished into a sort of querulous
obedience to the gestures of necessity in the glittering Kohinoor
and to the no less unavoidable gestures of its dazzling occupants.
He later recognized that his own personality, with a good face and
the evidences of a trained and equipped body, had much to do with
these social advances. Among the friends or acquaintances whom
he, by a companionable sense of mere liking, had chosen as more
exactly intimates was a young man, who confided to him at a moment
of especial expansion, that he was connected, in a business way,
with the most extraordinary of the many features of the multivarious
city. It was nothing less than a _suicidarium_ of which Tom had heard
something from the Mayor’s secretary, and which he knew was one
of the forbidden extravagances in New York. The information given
to him by his young friend, was to the effect, that enough people
who wished to die for diverse reasons, connected with all sorts of
experiences could be found in the City to assure the maintenance
of this grewsome institution. The young man was himself engaged in
the work of making a peculiar heavy and lethal gas or vapor, which
was used in accomplishing the appalling end. Tom naturally recoiled
from the horrible idea of ending life, and could hardly believe the
story of his friend that these outfits variously designed, and of
varying degrees of elegance and size, had proven to be profitable
enterprises, because of the exorbitant prices paid for so abhorrent
and hideous a purpose. It required considerable persuasion on the
man’s part to bring Tom to an acceptance of an invitation to visit
the scene of this unnatural conduct.

He went with some misgivings, but he went at last actually fascinated
by the incredible weirdness of thought, so unspeakable and strange.
His friend—Edward Links, or Ned—took him by the subway far up to the
end of the Island of Manhattan, at the old Kingsbridge, where the
place, disguised as a shabby tenement on the outside, was situated.
And as Tom approached it, somewhat separated from the other houses
around it, and on the edge of the Spuyten Duyvil creek—offering,
as Ned explained, a convenient way for the conveyance of the dead
to their sepulture, or to the cremator—on the opposite bank—it
unmistakably presented the familiar aspects of a dilapidated and
almost abandoned tenement. It was a little too long, and some of its
dilapidation looked made up for the occasion; displaced clapboards,
perforations and disfiguring splashes of mud applied to its sides,
the forcibly wrenched doors, the broken windows, broken beyond any
possible reconcilement with their human uses, all gave it a rather
contrived appearance. This might not have been obtruded upon the
casual passer-by, though men and women were moving in and out of it,
but it was detected by Tom quite quickly, and he asked his friend
if people couldn’t see it was a “blind,” especially the trained and
unsympathetic optics of the police.

“Well yes and no! You see it’s this way. There is a good deal of
swagger in the business, and joining the majority this way means
a clean spill of a thousand, or anyhow five hundred spielers. Now
you wouldn’t believe it, but the applications are thicker than they
can accommodate, sometimes. It all depends. Epidemics, failures,
panics, love-matters, all work in spells, and when they happen to
come together why they just run over each other to get the quietus
they talk about in the playhouse. Not here. Oh no. The game is on the
Q. T., and the proposals and assignments come in at the office down
town. Well as I was saying it pays, and it pays the police to have a
kind of blind eye for the hock shop; that’s the name for the outfit
here, though it goes among the squad as The Oblivion, a pretty name,
but nothing to the point.

“I don’t so much care for the job, but it pays good, and I don’t have
to see things, but just switch on gas and wait. It’s risky. Every
mother’s son of us would be pinched and run to jail, if they nabbed
us. What helps the whole affair is a kind of talk that goes on now
in certain papers and journals about the emptiness of life, and a
funny fancy that they all come back and get better places. It isn’t
a bit bad how they do it. You’ll see, and I guess that some kinds
of men and women, who have just about squeezed all they know how to
get out of life, get a sort of faint heartedness and sickness, and
kinder fetch up here with a jerk, bunch it together, and let ’er go.
Horrible ain’t it? But it’s slick, slicker than a wet rat. You just
see. It’s a kind of a dream.”

They came to the piazza that extended along the front of the
slovenly domicile, its floor rent and gaping, and some of the
pillars supporting its roof weakened by decay, while the windows and
blinds broken, torn, and dismantled completed the rather overwrought
imitation of poverty and disorder. As Ned Links approached the place,
his companion noticed his nervous inspection of the neighborhood, and
when with a whistle he turned to the tenement, and stepped within one
of its open hallways, Tom saw to his astonishment a slight unevenness
in its floor move upward, exposing a trap door, and beneath it
descending steps. Tom felt indeed he had embarked upon an adventure
appropriately theatrical and strange, enough so to satisfy his
romantic fancies, and even the mingled odors reaching his nostrils
from the aperture, in which a kind of fragrance was not unnoticed,
added a peculiar zest of danger.

Ned caught the door of the opening and held it up as he motioned
to Tom to descend. Tom, with no misgivings of danger, but with a
slightly sickened heart obeyed, and observed a female figure rather
carelessly dressed, or less equivocally, rather immodestly undressed,
going before him. It was her hands that had lifted the door for their
entrance. The female did not turn her head but she held above it a
small glass lamp, which vouchsafed sufficient light to enable her
visitors to follow, and it was not too dim for Tom’s observation,
that the woman before him was comely and unabashed. Her occasional
backward glances were lighted by very becoming smiles, and Tom,
under their ravishment, failed to notice that they were treading
a very narrow and damp passage, and that it was leading downward
upon a rather steep grade. The opportunity for such observations
was suddenly checked by their reaching the end of the defile, and
stopping abruptly at a door. The door opened, and Tom, with Ned
upon his heels, and with some unmannerly pressure upon the giggling
maiden, whose dishevelment became more and more pronounced, shot into
a room or an enclosure before whose strange features at first dimly
descried, but in an instant later revealed in a dazzlement of light,
as the bacchante touched a button which sent an electric thrill into
thousands of little lamps, Tom fairly gasped in astonishment.

He seemed to have walked into a bower of shrubs, flowers, and
low trees, under whose protecting shade couches were carelessly
distributed, while everywhere a rosy light cast its softening
illumination, enchanting the eye with tinted fantasies as where he
saw a stream of water lisping among rocks, just perceptibly stained
with the pink sheen, or where a marble grotto, opening at the end of
a gravel walk, bordered with tulips, caught on its flaring white lips
the same delicate blush. Ned and the girl withdrew a little, while
they watched the bewildered stranger, turning with new wonder to the
points of this sunken garden, and Ned was not loth to intimate his
irrepressible satisfaction by facetious antics behind Tom’s back,
some of which could only be described as evidences of his personal
infatuation for his companion.

But Tom was not long intimidated by his strange surroundings, and
had already begun to walk forward over a yellow gravelled path that
wound through a sward magically gleaming with a light that appeared
to radiate from its innumerable blades of grass. He had hardly gone
four steps forward, when a blast of air struck him, and sent his hat
whirling off among the branches of a flowering almond tree, while his
discomfiture was hailed with hearty approval by the shouting Ned and
the indulgent and favoring maiden. Tom received another surprise. He
sprang to the flowering almond shrub that looked gracefully real,
and on whose crowded flowers the rosy light fell, imparting a fairy
glamour to its petals. His hat was caught in the slender limbs,
and Tom raised his hand to recover it, clasping the tree trunk for
that purpose. The tree trunk under the pressure of his body swayed
forward and broke, while Tom rolled over in the confusion of boughs,
and the tumbling counterfeit entrapped him in wire, strips of painted
linen, paper corollas, wax leaves, in short the paraphernalia of
a clever imitation, put up consummately, and only one part of the
wondrous mimicry about him. This last incident was greeted with
more boisterous shouts by Ned and the young woman, who not loth to
increase the stranger’s dismay pelted him with theatrical pebbles
picked up from the banks of the theatrical rivulet where indeed the
sibilant water belonged to nature, but where everything else was an
artful disguise of paper, plaster, and pigment.

Tom extricated himself and in great good humour rejoined his friends.
He realized now that he had entered a subterranean theatre which
as he gazed around him assumed the proportions of a beautiful
picture. Its deceptive nature disappeared in the sickly radiance,
while the artifice of invention had been exhausted in completing a
fascinating illusion. And the picture had been skillfully composed.
It represented a blending of features whose mixture, unified under
the strange purpose of arresting life, lulled the spirit, through the
senses, into a dreamy languor. By means of the hushed and crowded
visions thus summoned or inwardly created, the surrender of the will
was gently accomplished. The opiate of a luxurious feeling, swayed
ever so discretely into an anticipation of delights just dawning in
the Life to Come, dwelt with increasing pressure on the swooning
and dying clients of this strange hostelry. For the means and the
methods were ruthlessly explained to Tom by his new friend, and Tom
responding intuitively to the more poetic suggestions around him,
almost resented Ned’s commonplace coarseness, and rude matter-of-fact
indifference. To Ned the Suicidarium was a business episode, and the
awful ghastliness of its work, a detail of commercial manipulation,
so hardened had he become to its tragic consequences. To Tom’s
less sodden nature, while he shrank with healthy repulsion from its
meaning, yet as the scene grew into a congruous whole—artificial as
it was—the mystery of its work somehow reflected a wonderfulness upon
it all, as the exhalation of a perfume sometimes makes a poisoned air
salubrious.

The deep pit lay in the dolomitic limestone of the north end of
Manhattan Island, at a point not far removed from the splendid
encrustation of Carrara upon the Inwood Ridge. It had been deeply
dug, and its lateral extensions stretched far beyond the limits of
the sordid tenement that, like a rag upon a jewelled shawl, covered
its entrance from the upper streets. It combined the features of a
Japanese garden and a Grecian theatre, and it was enclosed by the
rising walls of stone, up which its numerous lamps sent streaks
and fluttering bands of light, lost at last somewhere above in a
canopying sky, wherein the implanted and clustering spots of light
simulated a waiting Heaven. The Japanese garden, with bridges,
dwarfed cedars, clambering wistarias, painted hedges, yellow walks,
and white buddhas perched upon iron trellises, was carefully
developed. Stone seats under overhanging peach and plum trees were
draped with a green gauze that invited the rueful guests to await
there their sepulchral summons, closing their eyes delightedly upon a
picture of shimmering loveliness. Further towards one side, the floor
of the apartment abruptly fell a few feet, and here was a marble
amphitheatre almost bare of vegetation, lustrous in its polished
whiteness, holding banks of gray stone seats, and descending to an
arena of colored pebbles, beyond which again a stage inclosed in
glass was seen, now empty and dark.

Tom and Ned, the latter encircling the lascive lass who leaned upon
him with an embracing arm about his neck, stood at the back of the
highest tier of seats, and here Ned poured out the torrent of his
explanations.

“You see Tom these quitters come here at night, and they have a
banquet just below there on those colored sands and they eat and
drink, about as they like, and a good many take the booze pretty
straight, and aren’t half more than all there when the gas hits ’em.
Others just sit around and listen to the music, and then the fans are
slowly turned on—that was a fan that blew your roof off—and the gas
flows in, and they get softened like, while the girls do the angel
act on that stage, there behind the winders,” and Ned took a more
convincing hold of the assenting lady, who presumably had something
to do with the “angel act,” though there was little in her generous
outlines that comported seriously with the conventional frail figures
of the disembodied; “the girls come in there, and swim around with
gauzy flippers on their shoulders, and there is a lot of singing,
and then colored lights, and the gas keeps on, and the people grow
delirious, just as though they had eaten hasheesh, and sing and talk
and dance, and then they fall down any where, and they ‘pass over’
just like that,” and Ned snapped his fingers, kissed the girl, and
jerked Tom to one side, and hurried him down a central corridor which
led through the stone steps of the amphitheatre, and all, just as a
heavy and sweetened bell slowly tolled the hour of six.

The girl ran before them and turned into the stage at a side door,
which she pushed open, while Ned with gestures of despair hurried Tom
away in another direction up a good copy of a ragged defile, leading
to the diminutive cliff that sheltered the ineffective grotto, from
which issued the rippling waters of the more effective brook. At the
top of this alpine ascent, Ned shoved Tom into a cavity which proved
to be a kind of trap door leading into a laboratory which was the
work shop of the chemist and where the lethal gas was made, whose
poisonous vapors inundated the spaces they had both passed through,
carrying its occupants away on the waves of a vision-making, sense
deadening ecstacy. For Ned explained as he moved between the big
bell-jars, and the stills, and the huge beakers, heaped to their
brims with green and yellow chemicals, that an obscure man had
dreamed long on the problem of manufacturing a gas that would be both
fatal to life when inhaled, and intoxicating to the senses. This
gloomy plodding man buried in his experiments and living upon almost
nothing, often an object of pity to his neighbors, who would force
him from his crucibles and furnaces to take nourishment, had at last
achieved the grand result. He had formed a new organic vapor which
had all these properties, and yielded a pleasant fragrance as well.
It soothed as a narcotic, it stimulated as an intoxicant, it killed
with unswerving certainty, and as its victims inhaled it it roused a
fierce hunger for its subtle influence. “And” concluded Ned, “this
hock-shop is not the only one in the city that uses it. It just cost
a million of dollars to get it from the old man. Funny. He dropped
his chemistry and lives like a lord on Fifth Avenue to-day. He ain’t
much to see, but he certainly has the boodle,” and the reflective
youth sighed with unconcealed envy.

He was loading the green and yellow chemicals from the beakers into
the stills and lighting the bunsen burners beneath them, which slowly
heating the mixture, evolved the colorless gas or vapor which was
then conducted into large chambers, where at first mixed with air
it entered the suicidarium, and was gradually increased in strength
by cutting off the air supply, until the unadulterated flood of its
flow filled the chamber, and the unfortunates who had become tired of
their lives, passed away, drowned in its invisible bath.

Tom asked Ned if the suicidarium was about to be used and Ned,
absorbed in his task, nodded his head affirmatively, and pointing
to a little recess at the end of the narrow and lengthened room, at
length spoke; “Yes it’s off to-night. You can see the whole shindig.
You won’t forget it in a hurry either. Come in back here, have a
snack, and then you just squeeze up in that corner, and flatten your
nose on the window pane and the whole show is yours.”

Tom shuddered at the idea of this abhorrent spectacle, but he felt
too the fascination of the unique experience, and at the moment
nothing would have induced him to relinquish the opportunity.

The snack proved to be a very substantial meal of steak, potatoes,
beer and pie, and when Tom and Ned had done full justice to it under
the repeated admonitions of the latter, that they would be there some
time, and Tom might need a little extra strength for the ordeal, they
both returned to the laboratory, where now one additional helper had
taken his place, and Tom was put at the small aperture covered with
glass, from which at a most advantageous height he commanded the
whole Garden of Death.

It was now about eight o’clock, just the time in the upper sun-lit
world, when, as Tom thought, the twilight was settling down over the
city, and the blaze of the electric arcs were spanning the streets,
and the crowds were pressing in eager haste to enjoy the dissipations
of the night, while his hotel, the magnificent Kohinoor, rose
towering on the sky like a colossal coruscation. As these reminders
chased through his mind he noticed the door at which a few hours ago
Ned and himself had entered the place, open, and a group of men and
women—the latter young and pleasing—came in. The men set about the
work of examining the garden, adjusting here and there disturbed
spots, repairing breaks, smoothing the scarfs over the benches,
testing the lights, opening and shutting the fan doors, until Tom
saw them come to the broken almond tree of his amusing surprise.
Here there was an obvious astonishment and disgust, and then their
movements quickened, and bringing in new material and working with
zeal, the reorganized almond tree again swayed life-like before the
breeze of the fan, and its pink petals again fell softly on the green
turf, or floated upon the ludicrous little brook. Somehow now too a
change in relation to the whole trick came over Tom’s mind, and the
prettiness of the scene changed into something hollow and horrible,
so that he almost was inclined to leave his place and escape if
he could. The confined and excavated garden which at first, under
the shock of discovery, had filled him with a childish delight,
and had actually, so adroitly were its characteristic proportions
arranged, given him an impression of size, now became shrunken,
almost contemptible in the light of its unnatural uses. It was a
scintillating painted harlequinade of deceits, a little theatre
of diseased emotions, in which the morbid emaciated victims of
self-inflicted despair sought a foolish and wicked relief.

He felt like rushing away, and moved back to ask Ned how he might get
out of the horrible hole. But Ned was there at his side. He opened a
window and told Tom to look out:

“The gas doesn’t go in yet. First they have a banquet. You can see
and hear them from here. Then when they have got through, the dances
of the angels begin on that stage, behind the glasses, and then the
gas creeps in; they get intoxicated, and slowly as it grows denser
and thicker they faint and die. They’re coming in now.”

Tom leaned out of the little casement, and as the light glowed again
in full power over the garden, from the pink shades, while all the
glinting lights within the garden shone, he noticed about ten people
passing down the gravel walk among the counterfeit trees and plants
towards the petty amphitheatre. They went down the steps of its
central corridor, and sat on the benches waiting for the banquet. A
table had been placed upon the colored pebbles, in the space before
the stage, and it was rapidly being covered with cold meats, fruits,
and wines. The decorations and service partook of the theatrical
bravado of the rest of the place. There were silver cornucopias,
spilling grapes, oranges, figs, and nuts; silver urns filled with
wine, silver goblets, platters carrying cold meats, savory pickles,
olives, cheeses, jellies and pastes and small tureens of cordials.
White bread was heaped upon silver bowls and flowers were thrown over
everything, covering even the floor upon which the table stood. The
chairs were placed, and the ten strange creatures, who thus prepared
their own doom, took them in silence. Tom could have almost screamed
in his desperation of horror, but he simply clutched Ned, and the two
young men, to whom life seemed so good and desirable, watched the
little group in a stupor of attention. There were indeed just ten.
Tom noted them very distinctly. First at one end of the table sat
a withered sickly old man who was helped to this place by a young
woman, on whose face Tom thought he detected a choreal twitching,
and a moody stare that betokened madness; next to this young woman
sat two masculine wrecks—both men of middle age, their anaemic faces
pallid beneath the lights, and their heads turning bald, and their
listless hands moving fitfully from their faces to their legs, as
they slapped and rubbed the latter, and grimacing between themselves
provoked each other to a dreary sort of smiling, by jokes that failed
to reach the two young men.

Beyond this precious pair who seemed hardly to belong to the grim
company, was a heavy browed, thick haired young man, who kept his
eyes down, his head resting on his hands, and who seemed buried
in his own somber thoughts. He showed a handsome face to his two
attentive watchers, at such times as he lifted his eyes from the
table and looked backward over the amphitheatre and the artificial
garden. Then he seemed to smile, and he turned to his neighbor at
the head of the table, opposite the sickly old man, and spoke, which
caused his acquaintance to rise, and scan the spot with a very
evident interest. This last guest at the feast was a shorter man with
a round head, almost deprived of hair except such persistent wisps
as remained over his ears, or formed a fringe above the nape of his
neck. He had a curiously soured and grim face, and he squinted and
seemed to feel sudden pains in his chest, if the pressure of that
part of his body with both hands might be interpreted as a confession
of suffering. On the opposite side of the table four more figures
completed the assemblage. Of these one was a woman who seemed
ludicrously misplaced in such a group. She was a fat, not uncomely
person, that under less sinister auspices would have been set down by
most people for a prosperous and happy mother of a family, typically
bourgeoise in looks, typically commonplace, contented and impersonal.
Yet there she was, and Tom shuddered to think of it. Next to her
was a rather stout hard faced man who noticed no one but the little
glum man at the head of the table and with whom he was evidently
exchanging remarks. The glum man was short, the hard faced man tall.
The two remaining members of this suicidal gathering were a couple
of young people apparently in love with each other, a young man and
a young woman, and whose desires to leave a world already beginning
to grant them felicity struck Tom as almost more intolerably shocking
than anything else in the dreadful picture.

Some of the young women who later would present their celestial
evolutions on the stage to these tired souls, simulating, Tom knew
not what, of the future states of spirits, were now engaged—arrayed
as nymphs and who did not hesitate to accept the kisses of the men
they plied with wines—in serving the table. The drinking was more
conspicuous than the eating, and some—the young woman and the heavy
browed young man—abstained from both. But the rest were insatiable,
and even as he felt the chill of repulsion over the approaching
tragedy Tom could not repress a shifting smile as he saw the fat
comely woman, the glum philosopher and the hard faced hypochondriac
eat and drink with a gluttonous avidity, that suggested the grotesque
fancy in their minds of feeding well for a long fast. At length
the spirits of the company brightened, and songs and loud talking
betokened their animated condition. Even the sickly old man waved his
arms and his falsetto ululations sounded a forlorn mockery of gayety.
The lovers embraced each other with rapturous excitement, the glum
man stood up and orated, and his broken adjurations were interrupted
by the hard faced man, who smote the table with his fists, and pushed
away the decanters, the goblets, and the vessels near him, in a vast
effort to expand some notion of science or philosophy. The fat woman
sighed, and continued to drink and the two anaemics alternately fell
upon each other’s necks, or struggled to catch the flitting coryphaei
as they passed about the table, adding new delicacies, or filling the
empty wine jars. Only the twitching young woman, and the deep browed
youth maintained any decorum, and it was the latter who at last rose,
and in a deep musical voice commanded silence. Whether he was known,
or whether something stirred the bacchanalian rout to think a moment,
before they plunged into the dark uncertainly of Death, or whether
it was the relapse of satiety and sleepiness, that caused it, the
company became still, and only the voice of the speaker, sounding
with a fullness, that gave to every word that escaped his lips, a
peculiar resonance, was heard.

[Illustration: “PAIN, PAIN, PAIN; NOTHING BUT PAIN.”

                                                            _Page 79._]

“My friends,” he began “do you realize that to-night we shall be
together for the last time in this world, and do you care to go out
of it without one word to your companions, on the same journey,
telling them why you leave the world at all in this suicidal way?
With your permission let me at least tell you why I have come here,
what motive stronger than life, places me here with you at this
wretched mockery of a banquet, waiting for the death gas which we
have heard kills while it intoxicates;” the voice became compellingly
sweet and urgent, and the crew before him had turned their eyes up to
his face, apparently really listening with interest.

The speaker continued: “To me at one time nothing seemed so shocking
as death, nothing so unnatural, so hideous. To me this minute nothing
seems more welcome. I hoped in success, in love, to win the bright
things of the earth, as the bright things of the earth go, and I
missed all. Then I turned to friendship, and my friends wounded and
deserted me. I fled to religion, no voice answered my prayers, the
eternal blankness of silence, of disappointment; and nightly around
my couch rose only the spectres of want and desire, unsatisfied
and unappeasable. The sky was as hard as brass, the earth an iron
floor, the air around me without sound, I myself a puzzle or better,
I think, a bubble. I could not endure the stupid drag of monotony,
the abject meanness of life, its tread-mill duties, its sickening
flatness, its vulgarity. I fainted at the thought of a life led in
these tiresome and dusty thoroughfares of commonness, with food
wrenched from the toil of the repeating days; a chain of identical
dumbness from the beginning of the year to the end of it, and then on
and on without alleviation, without interest, without meaning. I end
it, and I end it here to-night.”

He sat down. His example moved the rest, and one by one as far as
they were able or understood their purpose they told their separate
stories. The sickly old man at one end of the table staggered to his
feet, and by dint of support from the waiters, and incessant catching
at the cloth of the table, managed to remain upright while he half
screamed what he had to say: “Yes I want to go too. It’s all pain,
all pain to me; aches all over me; red hot irons go through me; my
feet are blazing with sores; my sides are racked with misery. I can’t
walk; I can’t breathe; I can’t think. Pain, pain, pain; nothing but
pain.” And he sank down doubled up, and almost dropped to the ground
with a shriek.

The glum short man jumped to his feet, and stared around him with
ominous scowls, that set the two anaemics off, roaring with drunken
laughter. But he was not disconcerted; no one so tipsy could have
been. He waved his hands, and then folded them, and stiffly leaning
over the table, began: “I am a philosopher;” this, brought out with
an unmistakable hiccough, gave immense amusement to the two wrecks.
He continued: “I was educated to be a philosopher,” more laughter
“and I know all there is to know about philosophy; but it’s a farce,
it’s a damn lie, the whole of it,” a whack on the table, and a shout
from the wrecks, “philosophy is a fraud, it tells nothing, it’s a
patch work of guesses. I want to know, and I _am_ going to know,
and the only way to know is to go and see. See! See! See!” and the
ruffled philosopher fell over on the loving couple beside him, and,
hotly rejected by the boy, gathered himself together, and stumbled
outward to the gray benches of the amphitheatre.

The turn came next to the hard faced man, who was less enfeebled
by his potations, being in that respect a man of some endurance;
he proclaimed that he was a scientific man, an explorer, an
experimentalist, that he was there in that capacity then, and that
he had a commission to find out the fact of another life; “For,” he
went on, “I am convinced that there is a conservation of forces in
the living matter of the world, just as there is in its energy, and
that if the energy of life is not wearing down, we must come back to
it to keep up the store of life force. That’s clear, and the chances
are that when a man or a woman find out they’re not well placed, the
only way to possibly improve, is to go back to the primal energy, and
be projected again in some other form or place, on this earth, or
anywhere else, where conditions are more acceptable. I have seen that
for a long time, and I am permitted now to give it a demonstration.
When I come back I announce it, and I shall be the one man—the one
man” he was shouting vociferously, “the one man who has carried an
experiment outside of this earth, and made a research in the world
beyond the grave.”

His excitement communicated itself to the group, and even the heavy
browed youth and the twitching woman accorded him a serious attention.

“That’s so, that’s so;” reiterated the anaemics, who roused
themselves from a sort of connubial collapse, and standing, sang in
quavering trebles a remnant of some song belonging to their most
convivial days on the earth.

The effort to sing taxed the nerves and the strength of the two
gilded ones, and their raucous wavering tones threw a vulgar
expression into the affair which had won a weird or mournful or
grotesque interest from the other participants. This contribution
made it all wearisomely coarse. Dreadful as its intention was,
somehow it took on to Tom a theatrical imbecility as if he were
looking at the acting of puppets, who were going through some
grewsome mimicry of a bad play. He had hardly thought so, when the
woman with the twitching face arose and spoke in a soft voice just
audible to Tom, for she turned round facing the cage where Tom and
Ned were concealed, and looked at the painted picture behind her.

“You have all known trouble or sadness, but have any of you been told
to die? I have. My father, dead these seven years, came to me last
week, and told me he wanted me. He came with the big eyes I knew
he had, and he came in my sleep, in a dream, and woke me, and as I
woke I saw him standing at the foot of my bed, and he said, ‘Come!
Come! You will be happier. The Other Side is a blissful sleep a long
never-ending joy. Leave the world _any way_, but COME!’” She stepped
forward and moved up the corridor of the amphitheatre into the garden
and sat wearily down, on one of the benches not far from the very
spot where Tom had lost his hat up the illusive almond bush.

Then there was silence, when the sickly old man poked the comely
fat woman and she struggled to her feet, and said: “Vell, I go to
my childer. Dot’s all.” The young couple that Tom watched with a
frightful feeling that here was some mistake, and that he ought to
stop it, were expected to finish this extemporaneous confession and
the others stared at them questioningly.

The man stood up and became instantly dramatic. Tom riveted his
eyes on him, and could scarcely believe his ears. He waved his
arms in gestures, turned around to the company with ease and
self-satisfaction, declaimed, postured, and ran through a cheap
rhodomontade about the emptiness of life, the folly of prolonging
the fight, the ease of dying under the new helps of science, and
the necessity of bringing the world to a standstill by leaving it.
His wife nodded her head with concurrence to all this astonishing
rhapsody. And then while the attendants waited impatiently to begin
the final act of this fantastic drama the egotist recited the Plaint
of the Captive; it was his own title.

The insipid and hollow verses—and Ned whispered to Tom that they
were taken out of a circular printed by the management of the
Suicidarium—were no sooner over, than the attendants rushed in,
seized the table, and removed it, preparatory to the final stage
of the sinister business. The occupants of the table walked away,
most of them remaining together in the amphitheatre, the heavy
browed youth and the spirit-visited woman entering the garden above
it alone. Then the lamps paled, music and singing apparently from
the side, from a concealed organ began to softly sweep through the
dimming spot, and as Ned nudged Tom in their eerie he muttered, “Pull
that window, seal it up, the gas comes now,” and disappeared.

Despite the horror of his feelings Tom moved even closer to the
little pane peering through to see more plainly. Suddenly the glass
fronted stage glowed, and then grew startlingly bright with a purple
radiance, and Tom discerned a shifting slowly emerging swarm of
ballet girls, with gauzy wings oscillating and hovering from top
to bottom of the scene. Among them, he felt quite certain, were a
number of dolls or puppets worked in some way into a semblance of
soaring angels. The picture made, with the lights thrown upon it, a
curious illusion. The motion soft, sinuous, and constant, blended
with the music as, in a way, its material or physical embodiment.
The swaying bodies, the winding lines, the ordered movements forward
and back, the clustering wings, brought into juxtaposition in
shimmering canopies, the dissolving figures, sudden crushes to the
front of the stage, and then precipitate flights in retreat, single
graceful forms gliding in and out, all through the set phrases of a
formal evolution yielded an ocular impression, that harmonized with
the swelling and pulsating melodies and harmonies. This, _as the
exhilarating gas began to fill the garden_, acted as a delicate and
irresistible narcotic, bringing on the drowsy sleep, the haunted
sense of coming bliss, the wild delirious, forecasts of eventful
happenings, a delectation and revelry of dreams.

Tom watched intently. He could see the people moving, jumping to
their feet, extending their arms, then relaxing and apparently
falling down; he thought he heard cries and songs, certainly he saw
the heavy browed young man, skipping absurdly along one of the gravel
paths, while the woman near him flung her arms about in a strange
excitement, and then rolled off, as if lifeless, in the little brook,
whose petty current was dammed thereby, and the water, rising, flowed
over the pasteboard scene, and trickled away to the amphitheatre.
Tom’s eyes were not the only ones that observed this accident, for
the water soon ceased to run, its very prosaic source in a croton
pipe being at some unseen point intercepted.

The others on the amphitheatre were not all so easily observed,
but it developed that the scientist—he who went to prove his
commission—and the philosopher—he who went to “_See_”—had embraced
each other and fallen, while the sickly old man sat in the comely
German woman’s lap, and the young couple had remained seated and in
each other’s arms. The lights faded, the gas rushed in with such
overpowering force that it penetrated the secret cell where Tom
stood, and the last flickering pulses of life in the suicides were
brought to a standstill, and the awful act was closed.

Ned told Tom that the gas would remain in the garden for an hour or
so more. Then the fans were turned on for the rest of the night, so
as to thoroughly ventilate the place. Towards morning the bodies were
taken away, across the creek, and there delivered to friends—if such
had been designated—or cremated or buried according to the direction
received beforehand by the suicidarium.

Tom and Ned got back to the upper earth by means of a ladder
stretched upward through a narrow shaft. They emerged not far from
the Kingsbridge road, and the first flutter of the balmy June air
upon their cheeks, the fresh floating odors of the flowers wafted
from the gardens of Carrara, the paling stars seen vaguely through
that polarized and mystic light that shone from the city, the glad
couples laughing with the gayety of the life that surged through all
things, the glowing lamps on the shaded slopes of Fort George, the
occasional sliding noiseless splendor of an illuminated automobile—at
that remote period the automobile becomes a spectacle of garlanded
electric lights—with the terraced mansions beyond the river on the
east, the smell of the earth, the swishing rattle of the subway
trains over their viaduct, all the many moods and signs of glorious
vitality, of aspiring and incessant joy, made Tom’s heart leap with
thankfulness.

The remembrance of the hidden hollow pageant he had just witnessed,
the distorted mental emotionalism and effrontery of its victims,
their gluttony, their last convulsions, and the sombre misery of the
picture of the dead bodies, distributed over the pink garden, and
along the gray benches of the white amphitheatre, in that recess of
abominations, nauseated him. He felt a secret distrust and repulsion
for his companion; he was silent, and while Ned, with eager garrulity
attempted to allay and avert what he felt was a rising antipathy, Tom
seemed sullen and brooding, hurrying on with ravenous strides, as if
running from a pestilence.

He did indeed hear Ned’s communications, and they were startling
enough to his corporeal vision yet hunting for the blossoms of
existence, and fully satisfied with the radiant panorama around
him. Ned told him of the growing apathy of men to the duties and
burdens of life, the rapid spread of a relentless scorn for
the promises of religion, and the propaganda of a remorseless
flouting cynicism for the realities of society. He made it very
clear that the impulses to suicide were increasing, and that over
the city the concealed establishments such as Tom had seen were
spreading; that it was commonly believed that the escape from
pain, sickness, poverty—although in such cases as they had just
witnessed the expenses were heavy—was legitimately sought in a
violent death; that the marvellous properties of the gas used
in these suicidariums—conveying to those inhaling it a sensuous
delirium, accompanied, it was said, by an exaltation strange and
transporting—had made suicide even a desirable indulgence. Things had
gone so far as that the difficulties of the labor markets had been
viewed as offering to labor the advantageous opportunity of settling
them by wholesale withdrawals, or at least a selected and progressive
system of immolation, by which the supply of workers might be kept
exactly regulated to their demand, so that good or extreme wages
might always be paid.

Tom listened to these revelations with an appalled attention,
though he still kept his mouth shut, and still covered the ground
with violent and angry steps. They had turned from the Kingsbridge
road eastward over Dyckman Street, and had ascended the Fort George
Hill, whence the whole expanse of Carrara was seen palpitating in
the velvet night with a dreamy phosphorescence, that hung over it
like an arching tapestry pendent from the electrified air above it.
Tom turned back and gazed at it with renewed wonder. It seemed so
incredible. He turned to Ned and spoke for the first time. “Where
does the electricity come from that fills that air?”

Ned scarcely moved his head to notice the marvellous glory of it, and
answered, “That comes from the pits in the earth.”

“Pits?” ejaculated Tom. “Yes, pits,” retorted his pleased companion,
“pits bored down in the earth for thousands of feet They draw on the
storage batteries of the earth for that, as I understand it. But I
can’t explain it to you.”

Tom rumimated for a time. He ran back over those memories of his
which were associated with the reading of the wonders of the wizards
of the past—Edison, Marconi, Tesla, Ramsay, Lord Kelvin, Perkins—and
later names, whose achievements had so often led him on to the
small hours of the night, reading in his little room at home, the
books, magazines, and papers, that came to his hand, and which had
contributed to make him so zestful to reach the outer world of
designs and marvels. There was silence between the two men while
their footsteps rang out on the pavements, and they approached the
dazzling arch of the Washington Bridge. Instinctively they turned
by one impulse, to the left and walked out upon the steel span from
which the city beyond and below them, and the defile of the Harlem
river, formed together a magical and almost bewildering picture.

They leaned over the railing at the side, and then Tom spoke:
“Perhaps it’s this way, I mean those pits. There is electricity
in the earth, great currents of it, and lesser streams, and it
disappears by becoming neutralized with itself, as it were. Now if
insulated wires or rods could be sunk for great depths, and these
rods and wires spread out in the earth in finer threads, these fine
threads would pick up a good many small unneutralized currents of
electricity, and they would run together like little streams of
water, and they would rise on the insulated rods just as accumulating
water rises in a hole or well, when it runs in it. That was done with
the electricity of the air. The currents run up to the receivers, and
when these are over-charged, they play out in the air. It seems queer
that nobody is hurt, or that there are not lightning flashes instead
of the quiet glow!”

Ravishingly strange was the scene before them from the bridge. The
walls of the Harlem River cañon were surmounted with lofty houses,
the edge of the stream was defined with an unbroken path of light,
the houses shone with interior lamps, the streets around them were
ablaze, and the nebulous crown of light added its ghostly glare. The
shadows were intense, and the picture was not unlike some deeply
bitten etching, with monstrous opacities in it, skillfully curtained
in high lights, that lent it an unearthly exaggeration.

The two young men stopped a long time, buried each in his own
thoughts, which with Ned had probably slight reference to lofty
feelings, but which with Tom, by the indefinable but very real
repulsion he experienced for the part he had played at the
suicidarium, moved pleasantly forward into deeper hopes for his
coming life and sterner resolves to make it worth living.

Finally Ned spoke: “Well, it’s getting late and perhaps we’d both be
the better for a good sleep.”

“True enough,” answered Tom, “I’m ready,” and they crossed to the
subway on Amsterdam Avenue as to-day, and quickly found themselves
at the resplendent porticoes of the Pillar of Light. Tom hastened to
his delightful little eerie, and found a letter pushed under his door
sill. He tore it open and a card dropped out. It read:

 BENTON BRIGGS

 Secretary to the Mayor, City Hall.

(and scribbled along the bottom) “Come to the Mayor’s Office
to-morrow at 11 A.M.” Tom felt in his bones that it meant occupation
for him, and his heart leaped with thankfulness. He would deserve it,
indeed he would, and so he fell asleep.

CHAPTER V

THE MAYOR


The Mayor’s office had been temporarily removed to the Governor’s
room in the City Hall. The lately elected incumbent of the mayorality
was seated there, looking out of the window which commanded the view
of a restored park area. From this window a fountain spouting its
silver drops high in the air could be seen, reviving, with a leap
backward of over a century and a half, memories of Old New York, as
it was known to pedestrians passing the City Hall Park in 1850. The
colossal ugliness of the present post office had been removed, and
the curving tip of the park, green and shaded and wet with drifting
spray from the resurrected fountain pushed its verdant tongue into
the human currents, moving in black throngs along Broadway and Park
Place.

And from these currents occasional individuals dropped out, halted,
entered the park, and caught the fresh sky over this tiny interval
in the clustering cliffs of titanic towers around it. The sky often
seemed like a vivid sapphire jewel set between alabaster walls.
Everywhere else the soaring babels rose, largely built of marble,
shadowing the crevices below them where the compressed tides of
business swept and surged almost noiselessly, but intense and
continuous.

The Mayor turned from the pleasant picture which lay so completely
framed within the limits of the window as to form a unique episode of
green trees, flowering beds, the lounging inhabitants of the park,
the bristling fountain, bathed in sunshine, and beyond the still and
solemn and peaceful walls of Old St. Paul’s Church. He turned to
his secretary, standing at the side of the table, with some loose
sheets of paper thickly covered with writing, and who seemed to be
studying his superior with an interest that could only be detected in
his eyes, and in the posture of his head, for his body was slightly
turned aside, almost as if he had been stopped in the act of walking
away.

“Yes,” the voice had a singular clearness, the articulation of
the syllables seemed almost strained, “it is a revolt; it is
