insurrection. The law is paramount; Sulla repressed the democratic
revolution in Rome. And it was necessary. A state must survive.
And yet the state is nothing but its members. A state has no
generalized expression. It means nothing but a group of people trying
to harmonize their ideas into a government, with some hereditary
conventions of plans and laws handed down to them as valuable
safeguards of society. But experience counts, and experience proves
one thing; clearly that chaos, even a chaos that breaks chains and
turns slaves free, is practically a worse condition than Order, even
if order means tyranny.”

“But, Sir,” it was the voice of the man who met Tom Ratler in Battery
Park, “these unbridled excesses cannot be permitted to go unpunished.
There is no tyranny in repressing murder, in putting behind lock
and key unruly and desperate men, who set no bounds to their savage
and incendiary lusts, who would overturn our order and make living
impossible. You cannot have sympathy with the growling curs of the
street, even if they are hungry when you know their fangs may bring
disease and death to the best man that happens to walk past them.”

“All right, Briggs,” it was the first voice, and it was the Mayor’s,
accompanied with laughter, “have no fear, that I intend to play
Cataline or degrade our city by any subserviency to riot and rioters.
No! I will meet your expectations on that score fast enough. I will
remit nothing. The law takes its course. And yet—” he looked up with
a surprisingly youthful countenance into the staring and disturbed
face of his secretary; “these poor fellows are misled. They are a sad
hearted lot, and life is a stern and empty ordeal, bringing them
nothing that you and I call life. And I can see how they get enraged
and reckless. What do you make out of a society which lives in the
light, the gayest brightest sunlight, at the top, and is held up to
that sunlight, by those who never see it; who stand on a pedestal
so high that all the world seems theirs, or at least is seen by
them, and that pedestal is the thronged column of human crowds who
bend their backs, one over the other, immovable in the rigidity of
patience, resignation, deprivation, so that the little groups at the
top may feel no motion beneath their feet, and may keep their eyes
fixed upon the stars, or for that matter,” the voice sank a note or
two, “upon themselves, unmolested by any terror beneath them, not
even concerned as to why and how they keep their place at all?”

“But, Sir,” the secretary laid the papers he carried down on the
Mayor’s desk, he leaned with both hands upon it, and his voice
betrayed his impatience; “pardon me, but I take exception to your
image. We often deceive ourselves by a false metaphor, and act
wrongly, at least in thought, by illusions rising like mirages
from a foolish prepossession. Your image is not exact, certainly
not in this country. Society is not a pillar with a few disengaged
hands at the top, and all the way down to the ground underneath
them a hunger-driven, duty-compressed mass. Society is a variously
arranged surface, hill and vale, wilderness and cultivated land.
People are where they find themselves in one or the other for many
reasons—inheritance, accident, self-will, stubbornness, imprudence,
stupidity—but they can move sideways, or in any way. They can fill
up the valley, drain the swamp, cut down the wilderness. If they
start fires and burn out the civilization, the culture, the comfort
they envy, will they not only make a devastation more terrible than
their previous destitution? Do you recommend plunder, as a remedy for
poverty? Do you believe rapine, arson, murder, are the educational
means for the elevation of the poor or the ignorant or the vicious? I
know well enough Sir, you do not, and you should not raise a finger
to help these culprits, convicted by every honest man’s conscience.”

The Mayor’s expression was a study. There could be no doubt that he
admired his secretary, but a reserve crept into his eyes, that seemed
to say—“very well, very well, my fine Sir, but perhaps if you were
looking at the matter from the other end of the social field glass
some infirmities of temper that now loom, under the circumstances, to
you, as very large, might appear very small;” still he made no motion
of vexation. He listened, and when Mr. Briggs stopped, he simply
said, leaning back in his chair, and folding his arms; “What were all
the circumstances exactly?”

The secretary moved to a map of the city that was laid upon a table,
and which he raised in his hands pointing to the spots, which
illustrated his narrative, as he referred to them.

“It began in Grand Street, in that den and sink of infamy, the office
of The Beehive. The whole plot was hatched there. The paper should be
suppressed. Longtinsky has made himself an enemy of decency. He is
a Jacobite; an anarchist, a ruthless demon, who recognizes no rule,
but the same bloody and murderous acts that he thinks can be used to
stifle industry.

“The first known about the fire was the flames shooting from the
stairway of Helen Lorimer’s Settlement Hall on Pitt Street. A dozen
men started out from the basement, and six of them were nabbed; among
them Polinck, Longtinsky’s right hand man. He had paper and matches
in his pocket and a smell of oil and smoke was in his clothes.
The wind was blowing and before the firemen reached the place the
building was seething with flames. Then came an explosion. It was
that which killed the people in the streets. There was a stampede,
and again another explosion sent burning timbers over the streets and
started two other fires. Almost a block was destroyed and there were
ten deaths. Let these men alone, let them stay here, and they will
send their accomplices over the city: they will make outbreaks at a
dozen points; and they will make us a laughing stock to the whole
world. Immunity will encourage them to believe that they are feared.
They will breed a pestilence of disorder that nothing can quell. The
electric chair for each of them is mercy.”

Mr. Briggs had, long before this climax, dropped the map, and again
stood at the side of the Mayor’s desk. The latter arose, and as
he stood there he measured fully the height of his secretary; but
he was thin, with a remarkably well shaped and evenly balanced
body, and his face, without a distinct claim to beauty, possessed
a singular interest, from its expression, its paleness and a
winning look, of grace and sympathy in the full blue eyes. His
brow was rather too high for the finest facial effectiveness, but
this was in a measure corrected by the chestnut hair which richly
covered his head and floated downward over the brow; his hands were
noticeably long fingered, his mouth wide and too narrow, the ears
bent backward to the skull and the nose straight, handsomely formed,
but not intellectual. His eyes caught the eye of an observer and
held them. They were unaffectedly delightful to see. Many things
looked out from them, and not the least inviting was candor and
affection whilst in other moods they expressed a keen enjoyment of
fun and no inconsiderable delight in living. And then again they
became introspective and sad and perhaps their beauty was thus most
conspicuous. The face certainly did not produce an impression of
force or great mental acumen, but it was a face that men would trust
and women might love.

If the attraction it exerted might be safely referred to any
one physical feature, it could be ascribed to the delicately
pencilled eyebrows, which possessed a coloring slightly more
accentuated—deeper—than his hair, and which gave his face an artistic
form, the suggestion of aesthetics, or literary or even poetic
aptitude.

The Mayor had placed his hand on the shoulder of his secretary; “My
friend you are too severe, but you are right as to the theory at
least of my conduct in this case. There will be no suspense of the
judgment, and the men must die. Ah! how hard it is to say that word,”
and the man, who had evidently gone through a storm of conflicting
feelings sighed, and again turned with grave eyes to the picture seen
through the window at his side. At that moment the shivering arrest
of an automobile drew the attention of both men to the outside,
and it was Briggs who announced, with a breath of vexation in his
voice; “Your Honor will now have the supreme pleasure of meeting the
superb Helen Lorimer. She approaches with all her charms. If I am
not greatly mistaken she comes to plead the cause of these condemned
criminals. She has suffered herself in property and in personal
abuse, but she belongs to the ‘progressives,’ she belongs to those
who think that ‘the quality of mercy is not strained.’ She has an
incurable fund of optimism, and some of her aphorisms sound better
than they work. May I earnestly,” he had stepped close to the Mayor,
and his demeanor showed his extreme anxiety, “beg your Honor not to
be too susceptible to her eloquence.”

“You stay here,” replied the Mayor, smiling, “with such a Cerberus
of denial and refutation though she brought Apollo’s lute I should
be safe. Besides I feel some timidity about entertaining so great a
woman. From all accounts she is the Hypatia of our Alexandrian New
York.”

The page at the door approached, and handed the Mayor a card. It bore
the name Helen Lorimer. The Mayor moved to the door and disappeared.
In the next instant he returned, preceded by the woman we have
already seen, and who met the apprehensive greeting of Mr. Briggs
with a cordial and reassuring friendliness.

The Mayor occupied temporarily the great room, formerly called the
Governor’s Room, during the period when the city of New York was part
of the State of that name. The sacred relic of the Revolutionary
days—Washington’s Desk—was opposite his own at the west end of the
apartment, and he sat facing the heroic portrait of Washington above
the mantel on the west wall; behind him still remained the portrait
of George Clinton, to his right hand those of Governors Tompkins
and Lewis, and on his left through the unchanged green lambrequins
he saw the extended park. The fine distinction of the room, with
its east and west chambers as well, the delicate repression and
formal simplicity in its ornament, delighted him. Often in his few
unoccupied moments he would review the storied walls, standing long
before the painting of Hamilton and Jay, lost in reveries over the
splendid past, recalling the daring skill, the patriotic fervor,
the almost prophetic insight of the great Jamaican. He felt the
spur of the renowned example, and in his heart—hardly known to
himself—stirred the promptings of a magnanimous envy.

Helen Lorimer entered the room, and her brilliancy gave to the sedate
place a moderate radiance, which somehow startled both the Mayor and
his secretary. Her beauty was more revealed than usual, from the
freshness of color in her cheeks, and the animation and interest
in her eyes, while a considerable grace in her approach united
with simplicity of dress, made her charming as a mere object of
inspection. The knowledge both these men possessed of her qualities
of mind and heart evoked in their faces—and for different reasons—a
visible response of excitement.

The Mayor received his visitor with admirable ease, though a faint
tide of color flushing his pale countenance betrayed an interior
emotion which did not escape the observation of Mr. Briggs, who
subjected the new comer to a scrutiny which suggested his personal
reluctance to leave his superior officer alone with her. Mr. Briggs
felt too surely the vacillating attitude of his chief, in the affairs
of state then pressing for solution to look with complacency upon any
influence less uncompromising and severe than his own.

Helen Lorimer extended her hand, which was grasped by the younger
man. She looked at the mayor for a moment quite steadily and to his
secretary with a noticeable repression of the first smile of friendly
salutation. The Mayor motioned her to a chair conveniently placed at
the front of his desk, and, as she sat down, was the first to speak,

“I appreciate the honor of your visit, Miss Lorimer, and I beg to
make you acquainted with my secretary Mr. Briggs.”

Mr. Briggs bowed a little stiffly, and evidently convinced that his
presence was essential, or at least, up to this point, not explicitly
discouraged, took a chair somewhat near the side-table carrying the
maps he had been examining. The Mayor returned behind his desk, but
remained still standing, his gaze fixed with expectancy upon Miss
Lorimer, who for a moment turned her eyes to the floor, and then,
raising them towards the Mayor, with a resumption of her graciousness
and her inviting confidence disclosed her mission.

“I presume I should avoid all mere feminine circumlocution and be
very prompt and business-like, Mr. Mayor, in telling you the purpose
of my visit. It is about the men who have been so quickly convicted
of manslaughter, in the affair of the fire and explosion in Pitt
Street which destroyed my Settlement Hall. This condemnation to death
seems to me harsh, unnecessary, perhaps I should say cruel, and I
believe that vindictiveness in this case will breed still greater
mischief.”

Mr. Briggs was now decidedly aroused. He turned his eyes upon the
lady with the faintest intimation of scorn, and seemed almost
unable to repress his desire to speak. But it was the Mayor who
answered——and the peculiar clearness of enunciation and the substance
of his words as well, evidently impressed his hearer. She leaned
towards his desk and looking upward watched him with an eagerness
which might have been readily mistaken for personal admiration of
the speaker. With alert minds such as hers sympathetic response
involves interior subjective harmonies that may be felt, but are not
at first understood. Something very personal shone in her eyes, and
whether it was art or reason or taste, or goodness in what he said or
how he said it, Helen Lorimer perceived, or better, felt, the element
of nobility in the young governor of the City of New York before her.
Nor was Mr. Briggs less impressed. He congratulated himself upon
the result of his assiduous tuition; at least he put it that way to
himself.

“No! Miss Lorimer there can be no review of that decision. You are
moved by an admirable compassion. But you have for once at least
forgotten to yield to your better judgment. These men were atrocious
criminals. The fact that they have been condemned to death makes
them no less so. Punishment is the defense of order. There is no
other. Moral suasion has its precautionary value in their case, death
or imprisonment removes the agent and the disease. It is a kind of
segregation that stops infection. I do not wish to appear cynical,
but government on this earth will never escape the requirements of
the penal code. The hearts of governors may bleed—mine does—but
rulers must act outside of the indulgent motives of sentiment, and
have a fixed and even stern eye for the interests of order. The
constitution of states may be irreparable blunders: those chosen to
execute their commands cannot permit themselves to criticise their
operation. This is not tyranny or a stupid conformity, or simply an
irrational servility. It is duty.

“Are you invoking a higher ministration? Do you believe that your
pity or the State’s commiseration and forgiveness will change these
men? Convince yourself that they desire an overthrow of our present
social relations that that desire is, with them, conviction, and
if you believe in the existing order, what possible alternative of
action can you recommend, referable to that order’s continuance, but
their suppression?”

“Ah! Yes! your Honor I know all this is appealingly clear—in a
way—but is the evidence so clear against the men themselves? And
should the State be vengeful? Does not hatred breed hatred and are
not the victories of Mercy more secure, more enduring?”

Helen Lorimer leaned forward on the Mayor’s desk and her face
startled the young executive by its beauty, its beauty of form and of
expression, and he sank into his chair, impatiently warring within
himself with a strange willingness to surrender to any petition which
came in so lovely a guise. Had he been repeating stock phrases?
Could anything so tenderly so ravishingly beautiful as this woman
be resisted? Was she not more? Her voice was a penetrating music
that delighted him. And of praise of her mind was not the City full?
He felt a hopeless inability to repel an approach so enthralling.
His words had been stately enough, his voice in its best form,—but
somehow he felt it was a mask, and his heart, his live heart—not
the executive organ encrusted with traditions—rejected his own
words, and craved the very thing his fair visitor intimated. Still
there was a regnant sense left in him of position, of authority, of
responsibility. He remembered Briggs. He cast a fugitive glance at
his secretary, who had drawn closer to him, indeed stood directly at
his side, as if he understood his ordeal, and would invigorate his
strength, with his own uncompromising hostility to any temporising
with the ends of justice.

Again the young Mayor spoke, and no trace of indecision, not even the
respectful condescension of courtesy sounded in his voice which would
have carried, to the critical ear, a note a little too severe, as if
uttered under the constraint of a misgiving which, for appearance’s
sake, gave it harshness.

“Pity is a tender virtue in woman, Miss Lorimer. It has its
beneficent uses but it can enter too carelessly into the
considerations of policy which determine the existence of society.
The State, in which are gathered for safety thousands—in our
community millions—of souls, becomes inviolate, as an entity, the
moment they engage to live together for mutual security and progress.

“These men would take your pity at a value which degrades it. They
would consider it fear. And they would only rage more ferociously
against the social state. They are frankly anarchists, they assert
the solidarity of chaos, the unity of discord, the prosperity of
idleness, the stability of disorder. Their philosophy is a jumble
of contradictions. But worse, they are self-elected criminals, or
else they are hopelessly mad. They are your personal enemies, Miss
Lorimer. Their detestable organ incites the vicious to mischief
against yourself.

“I should be recreant to my own oath of office, and I should insult
the public intelligence if I changed the sentence of these men. Have
you any proof that the sentiment of this city asks for commutation.
I have heard something of a petition in their favor, but I have not
yet seen it. Miss Lorimer I cannot consider for one instant your
intercession. The quickness and rigor of the sentence upon these men
is no slight testimony to both the enormity of their crime, and the
sureness of their guilt.”

“Mr. Mayor,” again the appealing voice, the eloquent motion of
importunity as Helen Lorimer moved her hand almost supplicatingly
towards the speaker, and above all the flushed beautiful face,
“You must know I have no reason to find fault with your position.
You are held rather tightly I know within the limitations of your
oath of office. But is it quite certain that these men—I mean the
very men condemned—were guilty? Are you quite sure they are such
red-throated curse-breathing, death-dealing fiends as the papers
paint them and the preachers say? Are the mass meetings of the smug
and self satisfied and well-to-do oracles of wisdom or sense or
even moderation? Let us be very candid indeed. But let us also be
careful about our self-respect. Are you willing to send these men to
death, convicted as they have been in a two days’ trial not on the
evidence, which the State admitted was open to doubt, but convicted
by the feverish hostility of a populace, frightened out of its wits?

“Can you forget the devastation that is wrought in the moral sense of
a community when it gratifies the gluttony of its rage over those who
have half scared it to death? These men were not murderers at heart.
They were victims of their own credulity, tools, swinish tools let us
say, of cleverer and deeper brains. Kill them, Mr. Mayor, and those
cleverer and deeper brains will have new formulas to repeat and in
the repetition they will win a perilous plausibility that will give
socialism a martyr’s crown, not the ignominy of a felon’s grave.”

“But,” the young Mayor had also stretched out his hand and held it
for an instant rested upon the back of the gloved fingers of Helen
Lorimer, “do you forget the innocent who have suffered, those who
have actually been killed, those who have been wounded? Are we coolly
to recommend patience to them, while they sit unsatisfied and bitter
about the graves of their dead? Are you quite sure that neglect here
might not start a reactionary outcry, against the heartlessness of
Society, against its laxity, its inefficiency? Are you willing to
place us between two fires, the torch of an incendiary anarchy, and
the bullets of a Vigilance Committee?”

“I forget nothing, your Honor:” Miss Lorimer arose, and, as if to a
gesture of command, the Mayor also arose, “I have seen the families
of all the wounded, of all the dead. They need nothing, and their
mouths are not filled with threats of vengeance; they speak of the
timeliness of clemency. They actually fear the anger of society, as
that it may blindly outrage the groveling thousands who will see
in it a Juggernaut of selfish fear, not the sword of Justice. They
live among these classes and they know. Neither are they convinced
that this hunger for punishment is in their behalf. It is the self
preservative spasm of respectability.”

Again the Mayor glanced at Mr. Briggs as if he faltered in his
thoughts, but a sudden surge of blood mantled in his cheeks. Even to
Helen Lorimer he became handsome, and it was this strange new beauty,
in a man, that brought for an instant, the color in her own face, and
challenged her heart with a question of incredulity.

“Odd, Miss Lorimer,” he said, “that I should place myself in
opposition to yourself as the guardian of your own class. I can
understand your feelings, their magnanimity; indeed I see more than
that. Your purpose might be termed an esoteric patriotism.

“Apparently condoning a wrong, apparently forgiving an outrage
you intend to turn into legitimate channels the angry tides of
unrest which you think mount higher and more dangerously as they
were resisted or overborne. You mean to win back to allegiance by
kindnesses the unruly spirits that may grow, as you think, more
turbulent by repression. I understand, of course, but is it wise?”

The Mayor’s question was addressed to two listeners, for, as he asked
it, he turned half away from the absorbed, lovely figure before
him—more beautiful than ever—to his inward sense for the nobility of
the soul that dwelt in it—to his rigid and observant secretary.

The invitation to speak was accepted, almost precipitately by that
individual, who directed every word to the person of Miss Lorimer.
He seemed to have forgotten his superior, although his first word
answered the latter’s query.

“It is not wise; it is not sane; it is frivolous and it is cruel.
Cruel because it will lead to greater disasters, to more loss of
life and property. I know these people too, the men who read, who
believe the creed of The Beehive. They are maniacs of unrest. They
are demented with illusions; the distortion of their minds distorts
their feelings, and they are social outlaws. They have become
ravenous with impious hopes. In them the jackal and the hyena are
united; the lust of their hearts is murder and rapine, though they
do not always care to put their own hands to the job. They have
ambition. It is formless, inconsiderate, of colossal selfishness and
preposterous egotism. But they are without true courage. The leaders
at heart are a pack of besotted braggadocios, empty of all daring,
furiously vindictive, meanly cunning, and skulkers behind the bodies
of braver, more robust men. They can be terrified into inaction. They
can be prostrated by one overwhelming act of condign punishment.
They will shrink into nothingness before the retribution of summary
annihilation. The paralysis of terror will kill their will and their
energies and they will vanish.

“Parley with them and they will devour you, for they are as ruthless
as wolves, and, judging others by their own degraded instincts, the
first intimation from your lips of clemency will form a new incentive
for their insulting malice to propose more preposterous concessions.
They are a class engendered by vicious exaggerations of the ills
of society. They were originally formed in the prison houses, the
miasmatic swamps, the pest houses of European tyrannies. They exult
in the supremacy of their shameless dreams. If there are exaltations
of speech or even of ideas among them, they are baseless lunacies
built up on tricky words and on ignorance of their supine victims.
No, Sir,” turning to the Mayor, “Society as we understand it is
incompatible with these people, except as under the coercion of fear
they remain inert and helpless.”

The Mayor had not taken his eyes from the face of Helen Lorimer,
and the gleam of a flitting smile crossed his lips as he noted the
concentrated attention she gave his secretary. But her attention
changed visibly to anger. The liquid depth of her eyes grew firm
with a light of repudiation and denial; her lips straightened just a
little with disdain and impatience her posture was more erect as if
courting the blow that seemed aimed at her mistaken benevolence, and
then all this changed again, into a sad look which gave her—in the
Mayor’s mind—an even loftier loveliness as of a seraph mourning over
some carnal contumacy that he could not help or bless.

That quickly passed, and Miss Lorimer gave at last a wondering glance
at the Mayor, as Mr. Briggs concluded his fiery exhortation, and then
withdrew a little, almost as if meditating an abrupt exit. But she,
in this way, put herself just perceptibly in opposition, she secured
a physical separation which adroitly emphasized her complete dissent.
Both the anger and the momentary graveness had left her face. She was
smiling. It was a smile of deprecation, an unspoken reproach in which
pity dwelt. She spoke with a hurried earnestness that betrayed her
suspense.

“You wrong these people, Sir, indeed you do. I have not been slow to
learn a great deal about them from their own lips. I have not busied
myself solely in a lecturing propaganda. I have indeed striven to
make less hostile their resentment against the rich, to overcome
their ingrained sense of separation and repudiation, but I have also
been a listener and a pupil. I know some of the very men condemned.
It is not possible that they are murderers at heart. Their families
have fallen at my feet in weeping agonies of protest that they were
misled, whipped into a blind rage by artifice, by drink, by bribes,
by threats. If you thrust these men into Eternity you are doing a
bitterly cruel thing and making a grave mistake. I ask you, Sir,”
she came forward and addressed the Mayor, “only the slight favor of
a delay—let it be that—let me present new evidence to the courts
to show that these men—some of them—are the victims of perjury,
that they were absolutely free of guilt—that the bombs were put in
the building by other hands—miscreants who fled and left them—and
then forced or persuaded these unfortunates to enter the building.
I have a curious suspicion that my own class are not innocent in a
big plot—favored, for money by the creatures of The Beehive—to bring
about an ignominious triumph over the enemies of their order. There
is something to be learned on that score.

“You speak of Society and its indignation. I too run risks and
encounter forfeits. But an injustice will more grievously wreck
your career than the transient rage of ignorance. Prove rather the
greatness of your office by defending these speechless victims of a
popular fury. More, Sir, I will accept the challenge of the people, I
will establish their innocence by proof—the innocence of some. It can
be done. My lawyers have been successful in gathering fresh evidence
at many points. I can prove perjury. There have been paid witnesses
commissioned to destroy these lives. If the State trembles at the
thought of an assault upon its life, how much more deeply it will
grieve, if, in its own defense, it has struck down innocent men. What
will it say to the assassins of its honor? Be at least rational. It
is common talk; the precipitate action of the State; the apparently
prejudiced and frightened decision of the judge; the hasty verdict.
Shall the blot of an equivocal judgment send six men to death, five
of whom I believe can be proven innocent, and can either a State or
an individual survive such a miscarriage of justice? Listen to me,
Sir:” the Mayor was indeed listening, he hung upon her words, he had
pushed past his secretary, he bent himself forward, keenly searching
the beautiful face before him, which itself by some prompting had
come closer and closer to his own, and which, in the hurrying current
of words made its very breath fan the parted lips of the young
governor.

The Mayor spoke. Mr. Briggs would have stopped him, he even touched
his arm in an admonition more strenuous than respectful—but the Mayor
paid no attention; he shook his arm free of the detention, his voice
rose somewhat, and he held out his hand to Miss Lorimer who took
it, and even pressed her disengaged hand upon it, and thus in the
chancery of the touch of a woman, who made his heart beat strangely,
the Mayor forget the rhetoric of his office and spake frankly.

“You _shall_ be heard, Miss Lorimer, you _shall_ have the reprieve.
Let me see your evidence, let the courts see it. Time and place
shall be allotted to you to put this all in order; but why have you
withheld it, why urge abstraction when you had the concrete facts to
support your claim. God forbid that this State or I, shall commit
judicial murder. An order goes from this office now, and by the hands
of my secretary,” he turned with a slight almost indifferent gesture
towards Mr. Briggs, who stood flushed and angry behind him, “to delay
the execution, and, on the showing of new evidence and the suspected
treachery of witnesses, to recommend the re-trial of the whole
matter.”

Miss Lorimer withdrew her hands and said; “It may seem to you
peculiar that I did not come to the point of the new proof I have,
to establish the innocence of some of these men, at the outset. But,
Sir, I did indeed wish to withhold it at this moment, unless less
convincing arguments failed. And they did. You may have been right in
forcing my hand; I do not regret it, except that, so much known it
may too quickly reach the ears of the prosecutors, and help them to
overcome or avoid it. I hope, Sir, that this confession, may, for the
present, be considered sacred. From the bottom of my heart I thank
you. You will have no regrets.”

She bowed and, extending her hand again, shook heartily the Mayor’s;
she stepped past him, and advanced to the recalcitrant Briggs. That
non-plussed and utterly disgusted person was not to be outdone,
and he received the lady’s proffered advance with a discretion and
civility that was entirely courteous but also significantly formal.

The Mayor passed out of the room with Miss Lorimer, and, as they
moved down the serpentine stairway of the rotunda, he told her that
the reprieve would be officially published, the lawyers she retained
should then apply for a new trial to the courts, and that she would
personally grant him a favor, essential for his own defence, if she
would permit him to know more definitely the nature and the details
of her action and case.

Miss Lorimer assented to everything gladly, was profuse in her
thanks, and, as they descended the outside steps of the City Hall,
intimated the honor and the enjoyment it would give herself, her
mother, and her uncle to have the distinction of a visit from his
Honor. Almost exultantly the Mayor accepted this invitation—he felt
within himself nothing had ever seemed so desirable, so inviting, so
valued—and only a little hesitatingly stipulated that she would allow
him to make the appointment. And that favor was politely granted.
Miss Lorimer entered her motor car and the Mayor watched her depart,
and was himself invested with other watchers, attracted to the spot
by so unusual occurrence; but among the many eyes rivetted upon the
distinguished couple, two only, from the room they had both but an
instant vacated, bestowed upon them an inspection, a hard reflective
scrutiny, that meant neither satisfaction nor approval.

As the Mayor returned up the steps of the Hall he passed a young man
whose fine proportions and handsome face, at the very moment when
another face filled his thoughts with a singular tenacity, caught his
eye and extorted from him an involuntary notice of admiration. It was
our young friend Tom Ratler. Mr. Briggs had also seen him from the
Governor’s room, and was moved to an ejaculation thereby, that opened
his lips, but was not embodied in any articulate sounds.

Tom felt that he was following the Mayor, and he turned backwards
a few steps to delay his entrance in the Hall from a modest and
becoming sense of the propriety of respect. He looked at the
disappearing automobile with its precious freight of beauty, and a
pleasant smile—that smile that always accompanies the subjective
sense in any one of having discovered a love affair—lit up his face,
and it was with its lingering grace still in his young eyes, that he
entered the Mayor’s office.

Mr. Briggs had expected him, and left the room as the Mayor returned
by another door; he met Tom and ushered him in. He was introduced
to the Mayor as the “young man Mr. Briggs had suggested for his
chauffeur, and whom his Honor may have remembered he (Mr. Briggs) had
told him of, as having defended himself with so much spirit at the
Battery Park.”

The Mayor was delighted. He very soon satisfied himself that Tom
understood the nature of the avocation he intended him for, engaged
him, and sent him to his home, in one of the most charming sections
of the city, the revived, renovated, restored area of the Ninth Ward,
in the old precincts of Greenwich Village. Tom was provided with a
letter of endorsement and identification and with an elation that
invigorated him, started for his new place. He left the Mayor and
his secretary standing in the embrasure of a window, and heard their
voices distinctly rising in a discussion that showed—if he was not
greatly mistaken—that both were angry.

The changes of the centuries have their compensations. They seem
to old fashioned folk the more memorable and pleasing when they
accomplish graceful restorations of the past. Such a wonderful thing
had happened in the old section of the city which one hundred or
more years before this happy revival, had formed the Ninth Ward of
New York. It had boasted of the sweetness of its past, but at that
time (1900, or thereabouts) found itself slowly slipping into the
squalor of a miscellaneous and mongrel existence with increasing
tenements, “flats,” and slowly multiplying miseries of dirt and
dilapidation, where once had been (away back in 1850) lovely homes,
thrifty, American, and enticing, shaded with beauty of trees, and
linked with a still more remote and romantic period, when the village
of Greenwich caught the reflected rays of the setting suns from the
broad waters of the Hudson, and its lanes ran down to the water’s
edge.

A marvellous revival had again covered this district, (anywhere from
Canal Street northward to Fourteenth Street and between Broadway and
the Hudson) inserting a finer phrase of expression and feeling than
it had ever known, actually transfigured into a joyful ostentation of
gardened homes and spacious houses, enclosing streets of cleanliness
and all knit together into a texture of deliberate and calculated
comfort. Here was the Mayor’s home.

The movement which brought so pleasantly and improvingly this ancient
reserve of the city into prominence was the creation of a Governor’s
House within the city limits, and the selection of this region, by
the fortunate suggestion of an adroitly reminiscent artist. The
city had taken six houses along the present St. Luke’s Place, just
north of the little renaissance park, and made there—moving some
back from the sidewalk, and taking others away, and adjusting garden
spaces around them—a central house, in white and red, with creamy
white shutters, an iron fence about it all, and a few white mulberry
trees in front. It formed such a pretty picture, and revived so
keenly an old time veneration for the historic site of the ancient
village, that others copied the city’s example, and from point to
point, houses were bought, torn down, or moved, and changed; and
slowly, but with a well sanctioned enthusiasm, the blocks, now so
dull and unresponsive, blossomed and grew warm, with sunny and
shadowed homes. The extent and outlines of this urban restoration,
just mellow enough, just delicate and grave enough, embraced the
spaces from St. Luke’s Place to Christopher Street, with meandering
threads of extension up Greenwich Avenue to Seventh Avenue through
Hudson Street, across to Washington Square, in Waverley Place and Gay
Street, even at last undauntedly attacking the mercenary interests of
Bleecker and Bedford Streets, until a polite and literary or artistic
fever thrilled the city, and gathered together groups of such
models, filling place after place, till the oasis became charming
and entertaining and restful. Like a green and spreading plant that
pushes its way over ash heaps, and up dry and ugly walls, so did the
stupidity of vulgarized and threadbare neighborhoods become decent,
and pure, and lovely, before the tasteful invasion.

This alteration meant a goodly expense. In some instances it meant
the appropriation of whole blocks which became under the treatment,
delicious gardens, blending delightful affectations of antiquity—with
box rows and oleander bushes—with smart contrivances of the day in
horticulture. These inter-urban ruralities sent meandering fingers
into Seventh Avenue and stretched along Hudson Street as far as the
present St. Luke’s while most curiously an outlyer was created in
Varick Street near the rescued St. John’s Church where its altheas
bloomed against the background of the railroad freight depot. The
extension of the movement was then—in the year 2000—well afoot and
pressing for more extension.

Tom reached the Mayor’s house in St. Luke’s Place, and presented the
Mayor’s card. He was received by a quiet faced old lady who sweetly
explained to him she was the mother of the Mayor. He was led through
the broad corridors of the house to a back yard, where he found a
sizeable garage, and a well equipped vehicle. He was told his own
room would be above the ground floor. He felt some curiosity about
_that_, and went up the stairs for its inspection. Perhaps it was not
so bristlingly pretty as the room in the Kohinoor, but it suited him
much better. It was large, and a book-case on one side filled with
the overflows from the house gave it a home like air. The little yard
lay under his windows and he just heard the occasional murmur of the
city life.

Tom thanked his stars and set about an overhauling of the Mayor’s
handsome motor car, in which, that evening, he was to bring the Mayor
home from the City Hall.

The day had waned, but for an hour Helen Lorimer had sat dreamily
resting her head in her hands, as her arms leaned up on the sill of
the broad window, looking straight to the still talus-encumbered
slope of the Palisades. It was a strange idleness. She had loosened
her hair until it flowed down tumultuously over her shoulders, and
she had gazed at her own reflection and even approached the mirror
with a smile of satisfaction. As she mused, or planned, or slept,—it
was a trifle hard to say what she was doing—at the window,—the door
opened softly, and her mother, the dark Julieta entered, and came
almost unheard to her side. There was a start, and as the uplifted
face met her Mother’s, Helen rose, and said; “the Mayor is coming
to see us. I like him immensely.” She kissed Julieta and the two
women—both so beautiful and so contrasted—stood for an instant,
silent, together. What impulse suddenly raised Helen’s arms and made
her fling them about her mother’s neck, and kiss her again with an
ineffable tenderness?

Tom had brought the ruminating Mayor to his home, and that alert
official had bounded up the front steps pushed open the door, sprang
in the drawing room, where his mother sat knitting, and had also put
his arms around her with sudden eagerness, and kissed her. It was
apparently an unprecedented performance, at least in that way and at
that time, for the old lady pushed him back and asked with candid
wonder, “Why Marston! what’s the matter?”

Now it is an inexplicable coincidence, which may or may not interest
the reader, that both these exquisite coquetries happened at very
near the same hour and minute of the day.

CHAPTER VI

THE BEEHIVE


In the year Two Thousand the tides of human travel through Grand
Street had grown beyond all measure. They surged from morn to night
in swaying multitudes in which the admixture of all the races of
the great city were seen and heard. The babel of sound recalled
the thoroughfares of a dozen cities of the world. The East Side,
problem and despair of the twentieth century, remained the enigma of
the twenty-first. Its congestion, at least, had met an approximate
solution. Enormous high tenements had been built there, whose
clustering stories became microcosms of national amalgamation,
wherein were born diverse and blended groups who were endowed with
remarkable pugnacity and a sort of fierce ability to engage in
political argument and metaphysical civics.

They were not devoid of business sense and an inordinate thirst for
money. On the whole they had made a most reputable element in the
public life of the city, but they were prone to excitement, and they
were not American. They responded with Celtic avidity to rhetoric
and poetry, but they were not Irish. They were composed of a mixture
that had outrun the mere boundaries of religious prescription, and
Catholics and Jews in the close companionship of the Russians and the
Italians, Hungarians and Turks had intermarried. These unions had
grown more common, and there was a corresponding drift away from the
orthodox formulas of each church. Socialism as a theory had taken a
remarkable hold upon their imaginations but, though they were poor,
there was no immediate justification for discontent in their lives.
Pauperism was uncommon.

To the resident populations of the East Side, at this point (Grand
Street), and north and south, there were commingled the steady
streams of commerce and industry that crossed the Williamsburgh
bridge at Delancey Street. These converging currents had produced
a vortex of business along Grand Street, and so huge department
stores, a few big and second class theatres, and tailor or clothing
shops with the adventitious association of gregarious restaurants
had grown into a solid wall of many hued occupations. At night the
streets, especially in holiday time, and on the hot nights of summer
swarmed and seethed with the jocund, good natured, and somewhat
highly dressed throngs. The little parkway of the bridge’s approach
on Delancey Street was set with tables and here gathered companies
of friends and fun makers, who were supplied with wine and lager
from the restaurants, while at the entrance of the smaller shops or
stores similar tables had been instituted. The street then assumed
the complexion and the purpose of a great and lengthened caravansary,
or, on such nights when the stores were open, and the side walks
accommodated the warring peddlers, an open air bazaar, or a Kalmuk
fair grounds. The street then blazed with electric lights, and in
front of the theatres and the department stores, the restaurants,
and indeed at every hand, coronas, arches, and surcingles of
lamps, contributed a chaotic confusion of haloes, and reflections
and illuminations, that dispersed the darkness beyond the highest
buildings, and eclipsed the discomforted and neglected moon.

It was in the midst of this multivarious human maelstrom and complex,
that Longtinsky, the Russian, had made his abode, and established his
paper, The Beehive, with which he furthered his insidious agitations
and furnished a not unskillful compound of threats and grievances
with the truth half dressed in fiction, and fiction wearing, a
little stupidly, the mask of truth. The man had ability, and being
unscrupulous, with a tenacious memory and an almost morbid alacrity
in hunting out the peccadilloes of the rich (none of them worse than
his own and his companions) he had gained a clientele, his paper
a sort of subterranean circulation, and himself a modest fortune,
though quite incommensurate with his ambition. Some fancied insults
to him by the organ of the better classes had rankled in his mind,
and he had exceeded propriety and prudence by recent utterances which
were seditious and revolutionary; much more so than usual.

He had been goaded into a blind rage also by the unexpected and most
unwelcome invasion of the district that he thought was peculiarly
fitted for the ripening of his theories of social reform by the
successful propaganda of Helen Lorimer. This had been phenomenally
effective. Its courage fascinated a spirited people, and Miss
Lorimer’s address and personal power, with a well established and
practical commission of mercy was making havoc with Longtinsky’s
reputation and prestige. It drove him to madness, and the fact that
some of his young men-reporters, hired to dog and spy upon the less
wary and more undeserving of the class he hated and plotted against,
had, in their nefarious work encountered the beneficent agencies set
in operation by Helen Lorimer, and having also met that engaging lady
at her own settlement, had been flattered or actually been enslaved
into a sort of devotion, which made of them furious mutineers against
their soi-disant master loosened the last restraint of decency and
caution.

Longtinsky understood well enough his danger. He did not propose
to hang himself for any crime, but he had no hesitation in getting
others into a noose that would have better become his own neck. He
had become depraved to the point of murder, and his selfish and
inflated egotism, provoked beyond endurance into resentment, slowly
degenerated and assumed the worse complexion of brooding, sullen,
hatred, the precursor of the worser incentives to atrocity and
revenge.

The office of The Beehive was behind a clothier’s store not far from
the corner of Pitt Street and Grand. It was entered by a narrow alley
which debouched into Grand Street by a covered shed, and might have
been easily overlooked by any one not accustomed to use it. The alley
threaded, a door, entrenched in the brick wall of a back extension
opened upon a small irregular room which served as the editorial
sanctum, and the council chamber of Longtinsky, and his sympathizers.
Behind this again a small projection shielded the archives and the
printing press of the incendiary sheet.

It was the day after Miss Lorimer had entered her plea for the
reconsideration of the sentence of the convicted Socialists, and
which the Mayor had deferred to with such unction, that a rough and
shaggy looking man, with a slouched hat, half covering features
that might have reasonably craved some such concealment, walked
into the open alley that led to the penetralia of the Longtinsky
establishment. The man showed no hesitation in making his way, and
a shrewd suspicion, that he had been there often before, would
have occurred to any observer who noticed his nonchalant ease, the
half dissimulated scowl with which he turned towards the street,
and scrutinized the passing and oblivious crowds, his slow ingress
through the crooked corridor, and the questioning and companionable
rap that he gave to the inner door which the next instant he pushed
in, and within which he disappeared.

In the furtherance of the ends of this tale we must follow this
figure. The room that the man thus invaded was dark, the few windows
that were intended to help out its interior illumination being
extremely dirty, and in a measure curtained by ragged curtains,
that rivalled in their disarray the neglected panes they partially
covered. A few gas burners had been frequently used to dissipate the
gloom, and the effects of their imperfect combustion were discernible
above them, in sooty patches on the low ceiling. At this particular
moment, although it was almost mid summer, the jets were alight, and
by their helpful aid the visitor, if the matter interested him, could
recognize the two pictures which decorated—or were expected to serve
such a purpose—the walls. One stared him in the face, as he entered
the room. It was a colored chromo of the great Russian novelist
and humanitarian—Tolstoy—depicted in his peasant costume, amid his
ancestral acres, his hat in his hand, and his crumpled and benign
face looking out upon the world he repudiated and failed to save. The
other possessed a less perceptible congruity with the room and the
purposes of its usual occupants. It represented a most commonplace
scene of rurality and domestic peace, and was called Rest at Home.
This pleasing pictorial note in an atmosphere that quite generally
was filled with threats against all such chimerical fancies,
portrayed a farmer’s family, gathered around a hospitably covered
board, in which the artist by a series of conventional imitations
showed the products of the farm, with the ostensible father of the
prosperous and numerous group, standing at one end of the table, his
face upraised, and his hand, in the posture of a blessing, stretched
out over the abundant food; while his sons and daughters in attitudes
of respect or reverence, formed before him a most irreprehensible
assemblage of duty and devotion. The alert mind might have thought
that this unexpected illustration was intended, as an eloquent
reminder, to those assembled below it, of the very thing and things
they were commissioned to upset and prevent.

The remainder of the room’s furnishings requires but a few words.
There was a long narrow table—so narrow as to comfortably allow those
who sat at it to attack their opponents or critics across it—in the
centre of the room, a few chairs, an additional oil lamp on the
table, and in one corner a wooden box of the sturdiest construction,
armed at its corners and along its sides with iron. The door leading
to the editorial precincts beyond was open, and the few operatives
there, could be seen leaning over their tasks or engaged in
conversation which seemed curiously low and timid.

The man whom we thus persistently have followed, overturned a
chair at his entrance, and then striking the table with his fist,
proceeded to reveal his lineaments more completely, by removing
his hat which he threw on the table. He was a short thin man of a
Sicilian type, with black hair which would have abundantly screened
his small head, if it had not been so suspiciously shaven into a crop
of bristling points. His deep set eyes, rather high cheek bones,
prominent nose, regular mouth, shaded by a restrained moustache, a
handsomely chiselled chin, united into a facial whole of properly
noticeable contour and expression. Craft was marked in his face, and
a physiognomist might have been readily led to prognosticate a sum
of evil possibilities in the Italian visage, which again in less
questionable environment might have been mistaken for that of a star
of the opera. So easily do the reflexions of our surroundings lend a
deleterious, or responsible air to our physical personalities!

He wore the clothes of a workingman, somewhat refined by a little
more careful preservation, and by more expensive texture. One
surprising and noticeable feature of his dress was the small shoes
which clothed his feet, made from patent leather, and a scarf and
pin, the former of a brilliant scarlet, and the latter of emerald,
expensive items which shone all the more conspicuously in their plain
and discordant associations. As he rapped the table he flung himself
into a chair, and waited for the response which apparently he fully
understood would quickly follow.

He was not disappointed. A heavy movement of chairs in the
neighboring apartment announced the approach of his host, and the
next instant the broad squat figure of a man filled the doorway,
between the rooms, and the visitor looked lazily up and accosted his
friend.

“Well, Tinsky, have you heard the latest? The Old Man has given a
reprieve and the thing’s to be done over again, and I don’t feel
sure that the nippers won’t get a twist on your wrists this time.
There are some squealers about, and perhaps they are as little
anxious to get the ‘tickler’ as you.”

A curious smile covered the speaker’s face, and at length he raised
his eyes, previously dropped to the floor, and gazed at the editor
of The Beehive with a queer suppressed grin of malice, that was
distinctly irritating.

The man thus accosted was a teuton in type, blond haired, thickly
whiskered, with blue eyes, inconspicuous eyebrows, a short and
heavy frame and a disproportionately developed mouth, and—his most
striking feature—immense ears that protruded from the side of his
head with an unquestionably porcine saliency. For an instant this
aberrant arrangement caught the eye of his observers before they
fully scanned his face. The face itself had a mean look, and this
arose in part from the lowness of the brow and the smallness of the
eyes, but particularly from a misshapen nose, full and drooping
cheeks, and from the formless gap across his face, which served as a
mouth. Had any one unexpectedly encountered him under circumstances
of possible danger they would have instinctively shunned his company
and suspected his intentions. His eyes squinted slightly, and this
added to the sinister and depraved expression of his face. In some
lights, and under the influences of conviviality the editor of The
Beehive might have appeared more reassuring. At this moment pricked
into a momentary aggravation by his remorseless friends, his looks
were disappointing. His eyebrows drew together, his little eyes shone
vindictively, his great mouth twitched with anger, and his malformed
nose, set between them like a red proboscis, displayed its cavernous
nostrils. Longtinsky was distinctively unpleasant to see at that
moment.

“Oh, the jig is not up yet, Pony. I am not afraid of the tyrants. My
arm can wither at my side before I’ll stop the crusade of justice and
liberation I have begun. What about squealers? Do you think I have
anything to fear? Can you or anybody else put any crime at my door.
I don’t want to hear any nonsense and I won’t listen to any threats.
Am I the man to be chased to death by a mad rotten pack of smooth
faced, slick tongued, wine-fed vampires of wealth, men who do the
bidding of that besotted purse-proud boasting wench, Helen Lorimer?
I’ll pull destruction down about their ears. I’ll make this city
Hell-hot for them. If they dare to touch me I’ll burn their roofs
over their heads. Let us see how far the arrogant slaves of money and
gluttonous indulgence dare lift their hands against the sons of toil
and suffering!—”

The speaker had risen in his imprecatory rage, and his body, not
visibly afflicted with any signs of want, pressed against the table,
while the voluble spirit that possessed it glared through the
shrunken blue eyes at the stranger. That person quite imperturbably
waited for the exhaustion of the Socialist leader, and even ventured
to raise an impatient hand as if to check his speech, while at the
few moments when he condescended to look at him at all, something
like a masked grin covered his features. This was not unnoticed by
Longtinsky, upon whom it had an unexpected influence, for, as it
finally forced a loud laugh from his auditor, he stopped and sank
back in his chair with a cowed motion, as if of fear.

“Say!” it was Pony who was talking, “what do you think I am. Perhaps
you had better keep your mouth shut and the tongue in your head
tucked a little closer to your lamb-chops. I don’t want any oratory.
Get that off on your scrubs. I know you, Tinsky, and you know me, or
if you don’t by God, perhaps the time has come for me to show you who
I am. I’ll do it damn quick.

“Now get down to business. Where’s the swag I’m to have for playing
your spy. Don’t you get behind my back and rip me. I know you for a
mean spirited swaggering coward, and I know, Tinsky, as well as you
do, that you did the business for the Settlement House. YOU,” the man
stood up, “put the bombs there YOURSELF; YOU hired the poor wretches
to go in there after you and your fellow—and, damn you, you haven’t
had soul enough to try and save him—Polinck, had started the fuses.
What did you care if they got blown to Hell, as long as you kept your
mouse haired body safe? Well, there’s been a leak somewhere, and that
woman Lorimer has got on to it. She’s wise. Now Tinsky, keep your
soft soap jabber for those who relish it, I want none of it. I’m here
to do you a service. Get out of this. Scuttle the shop and move. The
Mayor and that woman have get their heads together and they’re after
you. The case comes up again next fall and you’re in for it. I want
my money, I want it now, and I want to see the little girl. If it
wasn’t for her I might not have come back. See! Let me see her now,
and let’s start. Get your word twisters off their perches, pull down
your winders, pick up your snacks, tuck some boodle in your breeches,
and get out of this. Canada for us.”

The effect of those adjurations and reproaches upon Tinsky was
remarkable. It only offered another tribute to the force of truthful
words, forcibly uttered. The editor had lost some of his color, some
of which was temper and much of which was beer. His hands nervously
plucked his beard, and he looked at his stormy interlocutor with real
dismay. His bravado had suddenly vanished, and in its place a sullen
discomforting sense of danger overwhelmed him, but it was resisted
by the man’s refractory egotism, and his stolid apathy to advice.
Flight meant surrender, impotence, loss of prestige, it might mean
detection, conviction, and punishment. He could not reconcile himself
to an abject retreat. The danger pictured by his picturesque and
implacable friend was a real one, and yet he shrank from an absolute
disclosure of ineffectiveness and alarm. Under the smarting sense of
guilt his mind, not unfamiliar with labyrinthine devices and coarse
subterfuge, worked fast.

There emerged before him a desperate project, which if successful
would establish his fame, might help him to rid himself of his
embarrassing assistants—such for instance as the man before him—and,
blackened as it was with the aims of murder, might finally light
those fires of insurrection which he fondly thought were latently
glowing in the hearts of the people, would clear the way for his
assumption of power, and—so vividly did the picture work itself
out—lift him into a position of eminence. Once there he might hold
the balance of power and dictate the terms of his own acceptance
and the shape of a future socialist plebiscite. The outlines of
this plan—if plan it could be called which involved many elements
of crime—had been vaguely worked out before in his audacious and
credulous mind, but now at the storming point, when the enemy was
forcing him to bay, when the incredible play of chance seemed to
stretch out to his waiting hand the implement to strike the blow
which would bring him immunity and place, they grew more distinct.

The cerebral convolutions behind that low forehead were intricate;
thought of some inventive force hurried through them with an easy
flow, and designs, pictures, dreams, rose before him with imaginative
poignancy. His fear of danger—a danger which seemed literally
heralded by its messenger before him—his hatred of Helen Lorimer, the
stupid vindictiveness of his nature, which urged him on upon a course
of rapine and disorder, his thirst for wealth, the sudden inner
consciousness that he had been playing false to his own people—though
bribes which he had taken from the “Aristocracy of Wealth” had only
been accepted with hypocrisy and rage—his powerlessness hitherto
before the inertia of the masses, who had failed to raise any
standard of revolt, already broken hearted as they were over the
conviction and condemnation of their companions, his perception that
he had played into the hands of his enemies in doing as he had done,
the residual force of a conviction that he was really working out,
besides his own fortune, a greater destiny for society; all these
considerations drove him to an ultimate step which he often vaguely
realized might be useful.

It now became to his mind a necessity. It was desperate, and like
some tempting demon, it rose before his eyes with beckoning gesture,
though in his hands it carried the weapons of destruction, and its
face was darkened with the shadow of assassination. Longtinsky was
not averse to involving in his plan some steps looking towards the
forcible removal of the unwelcomely candid man who had just entered
his retreat.

Longtinsky had been thinking fast. As his visitor paused Longtinsky
got up, shut the door leading to the inner room more carefully,
and locked it. He moved to the windows and drew down their shades,
then he passed around the head of the table, walked past Pony who
had become a little restive, though he eyed these preparations with
no apparent disturbance, unless a fumbling motion towards his hip
meant anything. Longtinsky closed the outer door—that leading to the
alley—and also locked it. Then he returned to his first seat, leaned
over the table and with a warning finger resting on the corner of his
slovenly lips, looked over curiously to Pony and motioned for him to
come and sit beside him.

[Illustration: “NO FUNNY BUSINESS, OR UP YOU GO IN SMOKE.”

                                                           _Page 120._]

That character, hitherto quite indifferent and evidently influenced
by very contemptuous feelings for his friend, whose nature he knew,
as well as some of his subterranean contrivance and plots, had become
by this time aroused into curiosity. He looked at Longtinsky a moment
with a very disagreeable leer, and then he laughed, picked up his
chair and lurched around the table, and put himself at Longtinsky’s
side;

“See here, Whiskers,” Pony’s voice sank to a guttural croak,
“what’s your game now. No funny business, or up you go in smoke,”
and a relevant movement of his right hand brought a six-shooter in
proximity with Longtinsky’s ribs, with such painful effectiveness
that that person’s color vanished, and his deprecatory retreat
backward all but failed to overturn his chair, and deliver him on his
back on the floor of the room. Something humorous shone in Pony’s
eyes, and the next instant after a circular flourish of his weapon at
a variety of objects around the room, he laid it on the table before
him.

“Come Pony,” said Longtinsky, with jocular familiarity, “put it away.
There’s no harm here for you. You’ve known me a long day and there
never was a time when I haven’t been a good friend to you. Put it
away. Jessie might come in here too. It would do you no good with her
to have that iron out.”

The effect of this apostrophe was remarkable. The man grabbed his
revolver, shoved it back in the holster at his hip beneath his coat,
a smile entered his cynical face, as a beam of light might illumine
into seemly grace a patch of darkness; he looked at Longtinsky with
almost a beseeching motion, as he laid his hand caressingly on his
arm:

“Tinsky, is Jessie here? Ah! Tinsky, you’re the father of that girl,
and I _do_ love her! Blast the folly of it, but I’m a child in her
fingers; I’m putty with her. I guess it’s the only thing that makes
me run your errands, and hang at your elbow, like a dangling bell to
a hound’s neck. Come Old Man, what’s your game? Jessie is the prize.
I’m oyster for anything.”

This sudden mollification and surrender was more than gratifying to
the plotting Tinsky. He showed no signs of immediate reassurance,
but he felt the unmistakable power he possessed through the charm of
his daughter for this blackguard’s eyes. Longtinsky laid his hand
on Pony’s shoulders, shrugged his own lightly, and half closing his
little eyes, watching shrewdly his confidant, spoke slowly, in a low
voice, revealing bluntly his nefarious scheme.

“Pony, I’m not going to admit or deny what you’ve said. The high ends
of popular liberation are served by all means the agencies of freedom
find convenient to their hands”—a hostile imprecation, or something
like it intimated to the apostle of the people that that line of
adjustment would hardly be accepted. He continued:

“But we’ll drop that. There is danger for us all in this new move.
That arch contriver Helen Lorimer intends to undermine us by the
hypocrisy of clemency. She is the spring of action behind the Mayor.
She expects to make us suspected by our own people. She is a whited
sepulchre. For her own ends, for her class, for the greed of the
debauched tribe of money-changers she is playing a double part to
catch us in the net of her selfish ends, to thrust down the Eagle of
Liberty—”

“Cut it out! Cut it out! Don’t for God’s sake, don’t cram any more of
that lingo down my throat. What’s your game? Ecco! Out with it. Give
it to me straight. Murder, arson, riot, poison, anything but your
damn rot of fine words and humbug, Tinsky. You’re in this for keeps,
and I’m with you—at least,” and the speaker shoved himself away from
the anxious editor,—“as long as you play fair and game.”

Longtinsky smiled faintly: he knew he must go on. The risk was
considerable, but concealment meant delay—delay, danger, and if this
tool before him turned on him—now that his most faithful henchman
Polinck was languishing in a public jail—there was a slim likelihood
of his accomplishing anything. He was silent for one moment, and
then trying to draw nearer to Pony whose robust criminality always
antagonized his tortuous and deceitful projects, he whispered: “Pony,
_Helen Lorimer must die_.”

This announcement veritably startled his hearer. Pony jumped to
his feet, seized Longtinsky by the shoulders with both hands, and
looked down on him with a ravenous surprise in his eyes, that made
Longtinsky half regret his audacity. But the surprise passed. Pony
had killed his men before. He fell back in his chair, and a kind of
sanguinary unction sounded to Tinsky in his hoarse whisper;

“Good Tinsky—you’re a handsome guy to be plotting murder to save
your own skin, and get a poor devil’s neck, like mine, kinked in
a noose. Work a little quicker, old cut-throat. You know I like
corpses, but just now I’d hate to get into a cold grave myself.”

Encouraged, Longtinsky continued. He dropped the gilded sophistry
of his professional cant, and assumed the prerogatives of an
initiated criminal. His own hatred for the class which constantly
embodied to his eyes his personal enemies, his particular animosity
for Helen Lorimer, whose virtues, goodness, and lofty altruism
were incomprehensible to his sodden nature, except as the mask of
an ulterior ambition, the wretched mess of deception into which
circumstances—largely precipitated by his own waning prestige—had
forced him, his consequent urgent danger, the sudden glimpse afforded
him of a forcible egress into a field of new opportunities, and the
pushing constraint of a kind of feverish despair made him suddenly
truculent, unabashed and reckless.

“Pony,” he began again, “I’ll make a clean breast of it. I blew up
the Settlement House. Polinck and I put the bombs there; Dalzell,
Vinta, Ishpy, Charles Bingo, Fabrizio, Spesca, Rinker, and the rest
were hired to go in, one hour after we lit the fuses. The janitor was
bribed—and—” the speaker grinned diabolically— “it was money from the
Silk Stockings that did it. They sent an agent to him. It was a plot;
that witch Helen Lorimer wanted her House blown up. She meant it to
bring ruin on us. I was fooled. The janitor came to me and promised
to open the place if I wanted him to. He left, the house open, and
went away—and I dropped into the trap. I meant no harm to the men.
Some one had to do it—for the effect—don’t you see—_the effect_. I
thought the East Side would rise,—but the police got wind of it too
soon. The men might have got away, and none the wiser but some one
peached, and that very night when the house burned, and I had started
the revolution—” Longtinsky for an instant looked self-deceived,
as if his aspirations for a New Dispensation, with himself as its
protagonist was a sacred hope, “—that night every reserve in the
city had been doubled, and—you know the rest—six of my men were
nabbed with Polinck; the uprising nipped; locust clubs killed the new
republic.”

By this time Pony was fully interested; he no longer shrank from the
portentous confession. His indolent attention had been replaced by a
very alert ear and eye, and not a word was missed, not a reflexion
of the utterance, in the face of the narrator, escaped him. He had
suspected much of this, some of it he almost could have sworn to.
He felt repugnance for the perfidy of the man who could thus coolly
rehearse all of this with a callous and untouched heart. But it
stimulated his own thoughts, to run rebelliously along a chain of
images, in which he saw Longtinsky dead, and himself with Jessie in
his arms fleeing the country, the demolition of The Beehive, and his
own comfortable existence elsewhere, with a sack of plunder torn from
its vindictive and over credulous editor. The dream pleased him,
and Longtinsky for once shone to him with an interior and native
light of reasonableness, that made him—Pony—urbane and receptive, a
transformation not concealed from Longtinsky, whose perception of it
only made him more communicative and less cautions.

“You see,” continued Longtinsky, “the reprieve will act as a stay to
the execution, of course, and the new trial will not come off until
the fall. That is sure. There is time for me to undo the mischief we
have suffered. I’ll fill The Beehive with storming articles. Careful
you know Pony. I’ll rouse the men we need and I will be bold too;
I’ll let out at that woman again, and she can come back at me. I can
tell her plans to a dot. She will be down on the East Side again; she
will put up some shack and start in with her talks. I’ll bait her
all right, and get her in some sort of an argument and then—with our
men ready—armed, Pony—we do it. _The bomb, Pony, that blows her to
perdition will win you Jessie._”

The speaker paused, watching with a flicker of madness in his
eyes, as he fed by anticipation upon the ruthless sweetness of
his vengeance, and the intoxicating prizes of success, the ardent
countenance of Pony, to whom the offer glittered with all the
radiance of happiness, complete and inexhaustible.

There was a moment’s silence between the men; Longtinsky never lifted
his eyes from the face before him, which responded to an inner
elation, and grew good and wholesome under the gentle influence of
the exultant and comforting thoughts that Jessie’s name aroused.
Longtinsky himself felt the benign touch somehow of his daughter’s
name, and there poured into his lowering glance the restful smile
of a father’s love. Surely Love—thinking of the abused word in
the loftiest sense, as the guerdon of unselfish sacrifice, and
devotion—can work, and it alone, the miracle of Redemption!

Pony extended his hand, and Longtinsky was not slow to grasp it.
“Done. I’m game for the throw, hit or miss. But a bargain’s a
bargain, and _you_ Longtinsky either stick to it, or QUIT BREATHING.”
Again the baleful lights in the eyes of both, and the evanescent ray
of Heaven’s sunshine on their faces faded away with the harvest of
suspicions springing anew in their plotting brains.

There was a light tap on the inner door, which led to the printing
and composing room of the paper, and Longtinsky recognized it; “Here
is Jessie now,” he said, and before he could extricate himself from
the table and chair in whose legs his own had become wound, Pony
leaped to his feet, and opened the door.

How strange are the contrasts of Nature’s works; with what necromantic
art she weaves the dreadful and the harmless, the lovely, together!
How perfectly she contrives—though there is no contrivance, only the
mastery of metempsychosis—to mix her materials, the same material
it would appear, and form from them now a demon, and now an angel.
Or is it in this wise; that a little, Oh! so very little of some
transforming juice of virtue can sway the ferment of a mass of
rougher ruder stuff, making its effect at last beautiful, choice,
and even more exquisite, because forsooth it has risen from its very
opposites. Chemistry in our human arts does such things. Heredity is
a fact, and yet Jessie was Longtinsky’s daughter. There she stood
like some wild and winsome flower, flourishing in the midst of
harshness and commonplaceness and reptilian motives. And so fresh
and lovely! The arch play of her smile like a golden beam falling on
ragged walls and blanched careworn faces, fell on the two men and
their lifted heads bathed in it as if in a benediction.

Jessie Longtinsky—her mother was dead—was Longtinsky’s only child,
and he lavished on her an unselfish care. She had learnt the most
excellent rudiments of an English, German and French education; she
spoke all these tongues; she had musical talent, and she developed
taste and talent. But best of all the graces of an unsullied nature,
a nature so vital and womanly, so confiding, earnest, and hopeful,
so searchingly sincere, so buoyant, and yet very brave, dressed, God
knows how, in the nobility of goodness.

Such graces were hers, and Jessie became them. They became her; it
was an ideality of character and beauty, more piquant and entrancing
because unexpected at first sight. It might have seemed almost
impossible.

Jessie Longtinsky was fair, her hair literally golden, her features
regular and perfectly harmonized, each formed so that it, by the
most delightful collusion with the rest, added to the total witchery
and charm. If hunting for some particular culprit among them all,
that made her win from every one the meed of praise, it might best
be attributed to her eyes, as indeed why not, seeing how joyous and
soulful eyes can be. Jessie’s eyes were indeed most pretty, and they
were not blue, as became convention, in the matter of eyes, with a
fair skin, and the story teller’s golden hair. They were the faintest
green, though that color craved no prominence in them, and often
surrendered its claim, to be considered there at all, to a soft
pearly grayness. And they were such honest eyes! Soft and reminiscent
sometimes they were, and filling unbiddenly with tears, and then the
home of mirth, but perhaps more usually they were tender and grave.

Longtinsky had had great luck in keeping his workmen, because they
were young, and because they could frequently feast their amazed
eyes on Jessie. Some had been ambitious in her regard but none had
been successful, though Jessie managed to make the wound endurable,
being calm and sensible and considerate. Among the habituées of
the Longtinsky establishment—and they were many—Pony, or exactly
Poniatowsky, had proved to be the most formidable suitor. Perhaps
Jessie did not care much for him, but she humored him, she may have
been a little afraid of him, and then her father favored him, _or
seemed to_, and Jessie was a dutiful daughter. Longtinsky, as men
will, even of the roughest, or the worst types, had guarded Jessie
from contact with his own world of men; he did not converse about
the social aims he had, he even kept his own paper out of his house,
not because it was dirty or vile—which it was not—but because it
was violent, sanguinary, maledictory and abusive. Longtinsky knew
that, but these ways were his weapons. Mrs. Longtinsky probably was
an amiable sweet woman, Jessie may have been like her, and so her
father wanted to keep her, feeling too some masculine aversion to a
femininity becoming raucous with debate and policies and doctrines.

As the door opened, Jessie glided past Pony who failed to catch
her, and stood apart from the men turning from one to the other as
if questioning their purpose to be locked up there. Then she looked
around the room, which she seldom entered, in which no trace of that
clean housewifery she practised in the story above the press room,
and where Longtinsky dwelt, was visible. Her glance was furtive and
shrinking, the place was distasteful; its staleness and meanness
oppressed her, there lurked in it an unavoidable meaning of crime.
She somehow felt that.

“Father, do let me change all this. I can. You won’t know it. With
clean windows and curtains, fresh paper, lamps, a new ceiling,
scrubbed floor, and that pretty old furniture in the attic; why it
will be so different, so pleasant!”

“Now Jessie that wouldn’t do at all, not down here. If you made
everything spick and span, we couldn’t think as well. It is hard
enough sometimes to say what we want to say in the style we want, but
because we don’t have to think where we are, and what’s about us, we
do better. Come my girl, leave us. Pony and I have some things to
talk about, which you aren’t interested in. Go up stairs.”

The voice of the speaker had lost its querulous murmur, and was
soothing and even affectionate, with a plausible earnestness that
discovered to the listener a father’s heart. And indeed there
flickered up in Longtinsky, when Jessie was near him, a tenderness
that would have saved him from many unruly and wicked impulses. It
was the psychic radiance of her own excellence that brightened the
dusky souls that came near her, and warmed their sensibilities, such
as they had, into a responsive vigor. Jessie sat down, and a cloud
of some sort of sadness passed over her face. Pony had been intently
watching her, with now and then a perturbed glance at his rumpled
clothes, which were not the habiliments he usually wore when anxious
to move Jessie’s reluctant heart.

Pony considered himself the recognized swain of Jessie, though it
would have been regarded as a mere piece of self flattery, a quite
unsupported assumption, by any one permitted to study Jessie’s
attitude towards him. She frankly did not like him, though Pony was
at his best with her, and really loved her, and because he did,
and was quite willing to go any lengths in supporting his claims,
against all rivals, he, by a rustic and aboriginal force of argument,
concluded she belonged to him, a conclusion not always agreeable to
Longtinsky, and certainly not approved by Jessie herself.

Pony started towards her, with a smile of pleasure in his face,
that intimated the probable intention of an osculatory salute, when
Jessie moved quickly away to the further end of the table. A frown
troubled Pony’s features, and threatened to break out in a stormy
expostulation, when Jessie unexpectedly fixed the attention of the
two men by a raised warning finger.

“Father, there are men outside in the yard watching the house. They
came in through the alley a minute ago. I think they may be there
yet. They may be your enemies!” Jessie of course knew her father was
