public, and what were termed “aeronauticals,” were popular excursions
through the atmospheric regions over the city, and the surrounding
country. The balloons could be hired, carrying parties of not more
than six, and they were taken at stations called “levitories.” These
were large—usually square—enclosures, in which the balloons were
stalled. Here they were inflated, repaired, and prepared for use.
They were known also as Zeppelins, from the count of that name who
had in Germany, at the beginning of the twentieth century, made
his excursion in the air in this manner. An attached car suspended
behind the operator of the balloon (himself in the elevated “pilot”)
accommodated the travellers, an apparatus of revolving fans propelled
it, and a steering gear of simple efficacy turned and wheeled it
through the invisible thoroughfares of the air.

The happy chance came when Ned was able to invite his friend Tom to
take one of these aerial trips and to both the young men the thought
came instantly that the trip would be of incomparable charm if the
ladies of their hearts accompanied them. Thus alone, in the circling
heights of the star covered sky, their loves would take new delights.
It certainly was a pretty thought, and Tom pronounced it “stunning,”
which gave Ned much pleasure as he had an immeasurable estimate of
Tom’s opinion, and he knew that Tom had repulsed him after that
dreadful night at the Suicidarium. So it was done. Ned got the
permission to use one of the balloons at the “Zeppelin” to which he
was attached, and the time was settled, and Tom, after much timorous
reproaches from Jessie, and a sinking in her heart that it would
somehow turn out badly made the appointment. Miss Delia Mutchler
was a bit frightened at the thought, but quite incompetent to think
of anything else for weeks, and uttered such ecstatic tributes to
Ned’s generosity and goodness that that overjoyed gentleman found
it necessary to relieve his feelings by pointing out the fact that
his wages would permit them to be married sooner than had seemed
reasonable. And then he surrendered himself to such a masterful
exhibition of osculation as to extort from the usually insatiable
Delia the remark;

“Neddy you’re just a kissing machine, and it’s not me that’s going to
have my mouth blistered with your smacks;” and then, as the wrathful
Neddy started to his feet: “Well, a dozen more Neddy for good
measure, and I’ll not miss them, dearest.”

The party made their _rendez-vous_ at a Zeppelin in the Bronx, not
far from the edge of the Zoological Park. The little group stared
a few moments at the brick walls of the balloon-house. Gas jets,
stuck out of its walls, vaguely illuminated its leviathan size, but
some three stories above they descried the huge iron and wooden
doors which sloped together like a roof, when closed, opening by
counter weights, running on steel columns outside, a curious grim
construction which made Jessie shudder a little, as she drew Tom’s
arm closer to her side, and gave the nonchalant Delia a squeamish
feeling of a difficult adventure. As they came round to the front
entrance of the “barn,” a swirling humming noise, a throbbing
methodical rhythm, as of beating fans, struck their ears, and the
light from within flamed through the crevices of the doorway, and
through the two windows in front, and the peering visitors saw the
inner rooms filled with moving figures, while a voice giving orders,
elicited various responses among them as they here and there adjusted
ropes, wires, and rods.

The light within the enclosure came from hissing flaring gas flames,
and this light, almost all placed below, threw upward a pale
penumbral illumination over the enormous shape above, swaying heavily
with an imminent expression of suddenly losing patience, and freeing
itself with a monstrous spasm to leap to its wide home in the skies.
It was an enormous bulk; depending cables of rope and rods of steel
held two platforms, one arranged like a cushioned box, the other
holding the chair of the operator. Its monstrous size resembled some
colossal cigar, and around it, clasping it like giant bracelets
were five rings of electric lamps. Above and below the slopes which
defined its forward and backwards ends, were also placed four strong
lamps with reflectors. All of these lamps were fed from an electric
battery on the operator’s platform, and could be turned off and
on, with the lever of a switch. Thus designed the aerial boat swam
in the atmosphere like a shooting column of light, some translated
phosphorescent ascidian from the deep, and, when the lights on
some fabrics were variegated in colors, the strange shimmering and
scudding object, in the night, became a fantastic whisking prism.

None of these lamps were illuminated as the friends entered the
doorway, and the whole mass over their heads, inertly slumberous and
yet stirring with wheezing oscillations, looked dark and dangerous.
Ned had made arrangements for the service of an operator so that he
and Tom and their companions might enjoy uninterruptedly the pleasant
frolic. Everything was in readiness, the balloon filled, the fans
in working order, while the dead still night auspiciously augured a
successful trip. The balloon was released, slightly slipping upward
along steel guides with a rasping groan, and the passenger car was
attached, the operator took his place, and the party of merry-makers,
with little shrieks from Delia, and subdued exclamations of wonder
from Jessie gathered quickly in the comfortable roofless car with
its windowed sides, while almost at the same moment with a few gaspy
creaks the whole monster rose softly, and like a cloud was lifted
above the barn, through the wide open roof, and floated like a
gigantic thistle down into the amazing beauty of the night.

How wonderful it was; Suddenly the lamps upon the great drum over
their heads flashed upon the eyes of the voyagers, a shrill piping
whistle announced the flight, and upward with broad curves, swinging
right and left, the machine accomplished its magic circuit through
the sky. Higher and higher, the delicious warm air fanning their
cheeks, the infinite delicate splendor of the city beneath them,
and far and far away the veiled country-sides sprinkled and pricked
with lights. It was like passing into a world of unearthly things,
and the charmed Jessie and stunned Delia with Tom—the affair was
not so uncommon to Ned—were silenced into admiration of the strange
experience.

And, as they turned from side to side, watching the shifting features
below them, darts of moving lights swept from the same earth,
announcing the arrival into the fields of the air of new phantoms
like themselves. These increased in numbers. The time was propitious,
and ascending from the nether shades, like immense glowing insects
the luminous bodies stole in from all quarters crossing and
recrossing, dipping and bending, swooping here with awful speed, and
there as if becalmed, swept by the mild currents of the atmosphere,
alone, when, as it often happened, songs and music issued from the
balancing cars until—it seemed so to Jessie and Tom—they had become a
part of some heavenly realm.

For some time they kept in with the sporting crowd of vessels,
sailing up and down over the spread chromosphere of the great city so
far below. They slid upward upon invisible runners far aloft, then
turned and launched backward, and down, with such impetuosity as to
draw from the girls faint cries, and a whistling wonder from the lips
of Tom.

It had grown to be July since Tom had reached New York, and the
nights were warm, though in the serene upper airs with salty
emanations from the sea sweeping over them the temperature was soft
and cooling, and—here was the wonder of it—it was the glorious
Fourth. It was amazing to note the city like a curdled pond of light
with disks, streams, undulations, spots, and dark clots in it. If
they approached it nearer a faint hum came to their ears; when they
rose high the antennae of lights stretched indefinitely off in skeins
of lines and braids. Now and then they caught the roar of an earth
train of cars, sometimes the chirp of a bird passed them in the sky,
and sometimes they ran through a cloud of insects. The wonderful
instrument obeyed every whim of its pilot, and followed the aerial
heights and depths as a ship rises and falls on the liquid hills of
the ocean.

The new moon blinked at them when they first started out, and it
somehow seemed a companion in the clouds, its golden crescent leaving
them at last over the vaporous edge of the swinging earth. They
mounted higher, and they shivered with a strange cosmic cold, the
tiny pavement of cirrus dipped cloudlets seemed just above them, and
the weirdness kept them still with amazement.

They all laughed when Jessie whispered; “Don’t let us leave the earth
yet,” and Tom swept her a little closer to himself and told her, “It
wouldn’t matter where they went as long as SHE was with them, and he
guessed it might be a little safer for both of them, if he and she
started out for another star,” which allusion to Poniatowsky brought
Jessie’s cheeks nearer to Tom, and then their lips touched, in which
mutual consolation they both attained a philosophic indifference to
Fate.

“Lor! But this is dandy,” exclaimed the suppressed Delia at length,
“Those scoops at Coney, and the Ferris Wheel aren’t in it. It sorter
makes my head go round and it’s just shivering cold,” to which
complaint Ned resorted to such readjustments of his arms as might
correct this inconvenience.

Again they fled from these heights to the warmer zones, and again
skimmed over the broad city, that bubbled below, and now, in the
darkening night began its wondrous celebration of the nation’s birth
day. Along the lines of increasing splendor, in all its functions,
the great city in the year 2000, had developed a regard for the
spectacular possibilities of the Fourth that made that day the climax
of the year’s shows. A southern temperament had stolen into the
city’s blood. It was hard to say exactly how it happened, unless
the settlement of French, and the expanding influence of the Italian
fusions, had effected it, but in all things artistic and ceremonial,
this American city attained vaster designs of beauty. Even the _Mardi
Gras_ had started its grotesque and glittering processions within New
York. Some affirmed that the milder climate had aided the aesthetic
regeneration, and others ruefully regretted a change, which began to
put on the city the semblance of an extravagant dissipation.

The exaltation of the Fourth began about the middle of the century,
about 1950, when it was proposed to give it a new official dignity
in the calendar of the city’s holidays. It was felt by some agitated
patriots that the great anniversary grew clouded in its meaning, that
the sense of it could be supremely accentuated by taking advantage of
the growing instincts for display, and making of the day a memorable
splash of splendor in the year. Subscriptions were opened, throughout
the year, and the money thus given with a liberal largess from the
city’s treasury, was devoted to the placement of marvellous firework
exhibitions. The occasion was really remarkable. Night and day were
given over to its celebration. A gorgeous procession filled up the
forenoon of the day; at noon the bands of the city played at all
available points for two hours; then succeeded a free performance at
the theatres of a play, usually patriotic, or quite as frequently
classic and elevating, some Shakespearean drama being performed
at the enormous auditorium built by the city for free educational
drama. And at night, after a furious cannonading, at nine o’clock the
pyrotechnic ebullition began.

The growing habit of “ballooning” made the precautions necessary, to
regulate, on the night of the Fourth of July, these aerial revellers,
and so a certain time was instituted for the beginning of the show.
All private pyrotechnics were discouraged or forbidden, and the
city turned out at night, the crowds threading their ways to the
point of display. The regulations for the balloons were for them to
remain at the elevation of one thousand feet, or to congregate over
the centre of the city, for the firework stands were placed around
the city, further towards its confines, and here they were set off
from tall towers. This permitted the air voyagers the advantage of
centering their barks over the centre of the city from which view
point the splendor of the spectacle grew enormously. The picture
above, below, was one of festal power. The city was illuminated,
the bells of the churches were rung in chimes at seven, the shining
balloons congregated over the central parts of the city, or far
above the range of rockets and starry exploding bombs, and at nine
of the evening, began the stupendous assault upon the skies, of
the ascending battalions of terrestrial meteors. The affair became
noteworthy throughout the country, and although imitated in many of
the larger cities, the New York outburst easily excelled the rest.
Strangers poured into the city, and the bonifaces of the metropolis
found it profitable for them to swell with liberal donations the
public funds for this dazzling function.

Ned Link’s party, in the midst of congregated equipages, was
floating, immobile, over the great middle districts of the city,
waiting for the imminent splendors that would soon terminate the
inordinate and ambitious day. A shout from the aerial sightseers
announced the first glimmerings of blue stars, rising into the
skies about the edges of the huge town. They sprouted up around the
cliffy congeries of buildings. For once the electric fervor in the
air died out, and the propitious suppression allowed the power of
the fireworks full sway. The blue stars crept higher, higher; they
fenced the horizon with spouting, azure, soaring, and eclipsing
flashes. It seemed as if the voyagers were enclosed in some enchanted
and deepening circle of light. Then suddenly above these first
reconnaissances of fire, rose swiftly, and with firm gushes of
spreading flakes of color, the sheaves of sky-rockets. They made a
higher fence than the first blue stars, and set in the dark sky a
tiara of thickly sprinkled jewels, borne like a crown by the imposing
and noble city. These flaming messengers dashed upon the blackness
of the night, the faint hiss of their ascent just heard, while, with
wayward and ever graceful vagaries of motion the speeding sparks shot
outward, mingling in chains of dropping, glowing, ashes.

The rocking flotilla in the summer air was encircled by the spouting
fires. Ever and anon some audacious blade of light seemed to hover
over them, and the descending sparks of the wooden bolt which had
risen with the rocket fell among them. Then there was a little
fright, and scared cries came from here and there to be succeeded the
next moments by shouts and exclamations of wonder, as fresh climbing
ladders of flame rose in the air. The third and most marvellous
stage of the show was reached when the enchanted margins of the wide
circle below them sent up the whizzing, whistling, bursting bombs.
The zenith seemed to rain stars, the pale orbs of the heavens were
extinguished, and a carpet of mixed lights was spread through the
sky, into which with sudden coruscations, at regular intervals,
sprang a sun-like meteor which scattered far and wide golden jets,
shafts of fiery fury that sped hither and thither eclipsing all their
colored companions, and looking like improvised suns, or perhaps
monstrous unfolding chrysanthemums of light, their sinuous petals
ejected from their centres, in writhing lines, each ended with a ball
of intenser gold.

Cheers broke from the hitherto stilled audience in the air, and it
was reechoed from the earth, where thousands stood in the blackness
far underneath; it seemed as if some elemental stage of creation
was going on around them, or as if the ruptured earth was vomiting
its interior fires upward from its burning depths, and they stayed
within the centre of its belching energy. It was indeed altogether
wonderful, and Tom and Jessie felt its scenic majesty, while the
less responsible Delia and the idle-hearted Ned, spent themselves
in gurgles and exclamations. Suddenly again it was inky black about
them; not a spark lingered in the tired air. It was the theatrical
prelude to the last astonishment. The dead silence accorded with the
emptiness of sky, and then without announcement, without sound, there
stole upward from the towers, the shrubby Bengal lights, blue, green,
and red, like funeral pyres on the horizon. They sprouted and fumed,
and cast a strange reverberant patchwork of colors on the black
background, and then behind them, so it seemed, a higher erection of
pale purple lights strove outward like quickening ghosts, speeding
high, quivering and falling down, towering at this point, and anon
declining into fringes of light simply. Then came a wizard mixture
of bombs and rockets, and weird shrieking sticks of flame, the whole
medley in flowery confusion embroidering the sky with arabesques and
patterns, and then the hastened climax ended all. The bridges of
the city crossing the enclosing channels of the Hudson and the East
river flashed into sight as pendulous girdles of flickering, dancing,
mobile gold. It was most superb. The transporting beauty of it, the
sudden elation of it, the compelling splendor and the fairy opulence!
Beneath its radiance the city shone, its multiplied structure rose
startlingly, its serrations along the edges looked like huge saw
teeth, and the reflecting roofs glistened liquidly, while, as the
illumination died away, with hiss and smoke and the wafted smells of
powder filled the air, the electric lights slowly raised their heads,
as it were, again, and the penumbral glows succeeded, and the great
display was a memory.

The aerial equipages moved away this way and that, their resuscitated
lamps gleaming as they scattered off like gigantic fire-flies, most
of them descending to the earth, and some bent on a night’s sport,
soaring up, while numbers from surrounding towns shot hither and
thither, back to their resting places in New Jersey, Connecticut, and
the north. A few distant lightning flashes shown on the gathered
clouds, and faint low rumbles told of thickening thunder storms.

“What do you say to a run to the end of the island—I mean Long
Island—then a dip out to sea, and back again by the Narrows. It’s a
good night and our lunch baskets are full?” It was Ned’s voice that
made the proposal and there was an instant’s silence. It appeared too
risky. The man at the motor yelled something back to them that he
would take them home if they wanted to return, or would he turn the
ship and give them a longer sail?

“Come,” reiterated Ned, “it’s a splendid night and so still and
then—the girls;” but it was just the girls who were driven into sharp
spasms over the novelty of doing it. Jessie was really anxious about
going back, and Delia was frankly frightened. Ned had telephoned back
to the man, and the balloon was headed for the Sound. It sailed low
and the steamers could be made out as streaks of light on the sooty
waters. The motion was delightful, the spread world, over which their
winnowing way like a bird’s flight led, was specked with villages
and towns and—well the expostulations were overcome; Tom promised it
would be “all right,” and Ned soothed Delia into a charmed consent.

Away they went, the churning fans shot them on, and the sea air soon
blew in their faces. The outlying edges of Brooklyn were being left,
and the spangled ground, diapered with lamps, caught up into clots
and then spilled over the flat plane in twinkling lines, came into
view indistinctly, for they were flying low. A moan and the machine
with a half sickening dip swept upward, and the air grew chillier,
the stars came nearer, and far away on the dark rim of the world
sheet lightnings played again, and a rattle in the rigging of the
boat, was premonitory of a coming squall, perhaps, though Ned said;
“Humbug! no storm to-night. Anyhow we can beat round the island, and
win out at the start, before the wind stirs a ripple on the ocean.”

He called again to the sky skipper, and the scudding fans took on
a swifter motion, as a slight droning hum broke out from them, and
with a start the ship scudded like some fleeing bird over the land,
past Hempstead plains, a little web of light, past the wrinkled
shores of Northport, on—, on—, over the spark dotted outlines of Lake
Ronkonkoma, and swerving again to the hoarse shores over Patchogue,
to domiciled Moriches, over Eastport and West Hampton, with midnight
ringing out from its tall steeples; closer to the ground by the
Shinnecock Hills, where the roosters sent up to them their morning
cheer; again across the island to Orient, sleeping in the dark with a
returning dash on the wings of the wind to Riverhead, where Chalmers
lived and died; again with a turn that made their heads spin across
Peconic Bay, over Shelter Island and Sag Harbor to East Hampton, and
Amagansett, yet dreaming on its watery hills, the rest and haven of
artists, and then, with a tearing gait that made the machinery rip
and whistle, and shook them as they dashed against the inrushing
winds, to Montauk Point, where wharves, lined in lights, told the
sharp story of change, for now the steamers from Europe made this
their “touchdown,” and the emigrant and tourist was whisked across
Long Island to New York.

It was time to turn back, it was no longer certain that the night
would remain clear; the rumblings had increased, and from the caverns
of the east and south sprang forks of lightning. The girls were now a
little appalled, and Tom felt that they must make a run for it. Ned
told the driver to keep out to sea, and along the shore, and with the
wind behind them they went forward furiously.

But, quicker than they, came the hurrying storm, and the piled
clouds seemed to stand in their way like black buttresses, spitting
fire. The gloom encircled them; they suddenly tossed and spun like
a top in a whirlpool of currents. There was consternation, and, for
a moment only, indecision, then Ned roared through the “phone” to
lighten up and ascend, and the next instant with wheeze and crack,
the gray hulk pushed upward through what seemed a sort of atmospheric
funnel, while buffeting winds slapped the voyagers in the face, and
the rocking balloon, pitched hither and thither, sometimes actually
whirled around in circles, that threatened to rupture and destroy
her. But with a sort of obstinate energy it pushed its way through
seething clouds, amid a crashing report of thunder and the tracery
gleamings of lightning.

The rain splashed upon them in bucketfulls, and plugs on the floor
of the basket were removed to let the deluge escape; but the ascent
continued, while the little party crouched and clung together in a
very real terror. At a moment when deliverance seemed impossible, and
the darkness closed in upon them closer, while the billows of air
spun their craft around and jerked it here and there, and the ominous
chafing of the cables promised some sudden separation of the wheeling
and shivering carriage in which they rode, and a stern chilling frost
made itself felt in the rattling hail—at that very moment, when Tom’s
stout heart quailed, and the girls had fainted, and Ned clung to the
reeling seats, the nose of the ship issued into ether above all the
disturbance, and the staunch vehicle floated again on even keel in a
splendid radiance of stars. It was a miracle of change. They looked
down upon a surface like the surges of an angry ocean, a vortex of
colliding vapors, rent with seams of light, and belching thunders.

The stunned travellers aroused themselves, and Tom and Ned set about
resuscitating their frightened friends, when suddenly a heavy dark
object shot to their feet, and on the floor of the cage they saw
with horror, the crimped and curled form of the stricken pilot of
the balloon. They had almost forgotten him in their own excitement,
although Ned had noticed that he was swaying at his post. He might
indeed have missed falling on the lower compartment, and dropped
instead plump to the earth, had not a tilted position of the balloon
actually brought him above the under car. There he lay unconscious.
It was a desperate position, the soaring machine unguided, forcing
its way on undirected, and the man at their feet who had controlled
its wheel, tossed from his seat, overcome and possibly dying. A cry
broke from the terror stricken group.

Ned cast a sharp glance overhead, and saw that the platform of the
pilot was unharmed. _But it was empty._ Could he reach it? Tom bent
over the unconscious motorist and found him injured, but alive. He
moaned in pain and cried out as his limp form was moved. It was too
evident that some bones were broken, and Tom seized some of the
blankets and rugs, made an improvised bed, and then gave the sinking
man some of the wine they had fortunately provided themselves with,
as Ned knew the cold they might encounter.

All were now startled into attention by their singular predicament
and all looked to Ned for the next move. They were not long left
in doubt as to his intentions. He told them he must ascend to the
deserted platform. It was a hard, it seemed an impossible attempt,
but there was nothing else for it. As it was, they were drifting on a
pathless ocean, and actually headed away from the mainland. Tom and
Ned both knew the situation meant their eventual destruction, and
both grew pale with dread, and the poor women strained every nerve to
keep their consciousness.

Could he climb to the deserted seat by the cables and attachments of
the car? The conditions were favorable. The air was quiet, and the
unjostled framework permitted perhaps the dangerous enterprise. And
yet—there appeared to be one chance. The travelling car was swung
on chains from the central body of the balloon, and quite detached
from the platform of the pilot, that occupied a forward place, and
was itself fastened by iron rods to the gas-tank. There was but one
connection from the car to the steering platform, and that hardly
promised assistance. It was the chain that carried the telephone
wire from one to the other. The chain was moderately stout. It looked
serviceable; but was it? It could be tested. Then one other thought
suggested itself to the anxious scrutiny of Ned, standing stiffly
there in the strange exigency of a peril that was to him much more
like death than the painted shows which he had watched, wherein
indeed so many foolish hearts had quit the world.

This other way of possible rescue was to creep up along the heavier
cables holding the car, to the body of the equipage, insert himself
among the binding cordage around the cylinder of the balloon, twist
somehow through them to the part of the body immediately over the
platform, and drop there to the pilot. There was his own strength and
skill to be considered, and the measure of both required by the two
methods. This gave him pause, and Tom quickly realized the dilemma,
and estimated the balancing possibilities.

Both men strained their eyes to follow the chain up to its fastenings
on the platform, but into the darkness the links of steel slipped
away, and they could not see their continuity more than a few feet.

“Let’s pull it, and find out how it holds,” whispered Tom, and the
women stood up behind them listening too, and watching with blanched
faces. For them the ordeal was breaking, but while Delia moaned,
Jessie was silent. Tom and Ned pulled, at first easily, and then more
strongly, until they pressed almost their full strength on the chain.
It held. Ned was the first to speak;

“Yes. This is the way. I’ll try it Tom. It’s this or nothing. All the
control is up on that platform, and we can do nothing here. Give me a
drink and up I go and—Goodbye.”

The whisky flask was produced, and Ned took a fillip, but he had
scarcely handed the glass back to Tom, when the motionless figure of
the man at their feet stirred, and a voice spoke;

“Play out the chain its whole length, and slip off the edge of the
car; let one man hold the slack, and then you’ll get a straight pull
and go up hand over hand to the pilot. The chain will stand it. I
know,” and with a groan the voice ceased, and Tom and Ned without
looking at the sufferer, prepared to obey his instructions. First
Tom rubbed Ned’s hands until they burned; Ned in spite of the cold,
stripped off his coat, but underneath was a cardigan, which might
suffice to keep out the creeping chill. Tom told the girls to take
their seats, and then he opened the door of one side of the enclosing
framework of the car, and Ned seized the coiled chain at a sufficient
distance to allow him to drop about vertically underneath the pilot
while Tom caught the links beyond him, and they approached to the
dreadful edge of the softly swaying car. No word was spoken, and Ned
hung his legs off into space, slowly moving outward while Tom firmly
held the chain, and so lowered him. It was well done, and Ned hanging
in mid-air seemed like a long plumb-bob, just faintly visible. Then
the voice spoke again, the voice of the maimed man on the floor;

“You can touch up those lights from the car. There’s a wire at
this end of the car, that is tied to the lever of the switch. That
will give you more light. I turned them off when the squall struck
us.” But Tom was afraid to move to do this. The sudden afflux of
light where all had been previously half dark might so startle the
suspended man, that he would lose his steadfastness and fall. And
yet Tom felt the value of the suggestion, as the light flowing down
from the lamps above, would give Ned courage, and make the task less
awful, besides the material benefit of showing him better what he
was doing. Wavering for an instant as to what he should do, his ears
heard Ned’s voice calling him to him; “Pull me back! back! quick!”

Tom caught the chain, and gently, without any shock, hauled Ned back
to the car, on which the brave boy scrambled.

“What was it?” asked the wondering Tom.

“It’s too cold for my fingers. They’ll stiffen and drop me. The chain
is like ice. You’ve got some gloves here, cut off the fingers, and
put them on mine. All I want is the tips. My hand is good for the
job.”

Tom had a pair of old kid gloves in his pocket, and these were
quickly cut and fixed upon the ends of Ned’s fingers, and once again
the two approached to the edge of the car, and Ned prepared to
launch himself into the darkling air. But before this Tom had told
him of the communication of the wire to the lever of the lamps, and
Ned assented to have it pulled. The test was made, and indeed the
circle of lights above, on the lower sides of the great body of the
balloon, flashed into action, and the descending light softly bathed
the anxious group, and glinted on the hanging chain, and lost itself
in the dark abysses below, where yet the reverberations of the storm
lingered, and the shattered clouds pulsated with occasional strokes
of lightning.

Ned slid off, slowly allowed by Tom to reach a straight position
under the pilot and when this was done, the pull up began, and hand
over hand he ascended. Tom watched him with his heart in his mouth,
but with such a glow of delight in his splendid courage! Up—up—he
went. He was nearing the pilot and Tom could scarcely keep his voice,
it seemed so certain he would reach it, and succeed. But, no! he
stopped, and Tom could see that Ned had raised his head, Oh! so very
slowly—and peeked over to the platform, almost on the level of his
face; Tom thought he heard a cry, and then—my God!—the chain fell,
past him, its end dangling in the fathomless atmosphere, while its
weight dragging on the unspent portion in the car rattled the links
about Tom’s feet.

Tom’s eyes had followed the chain in its fall, but though he peered
over the sickening side below him, in the cones of light thrown by
the electric lamps, he caught no sight of the hurled body of his
companion. Had Ned been able to have held on to the chain, the
flight downward and the sharp arrest at its end would have wrenched
him away and shot him pitilessly to the earth. Tom dreaded to raise
his head, for one moment he pressed his hands over his face, and a
queer tearing shiver passed through his body, and then—

In that moment the girls had gathered about Tom. They had understood
the attempt Ned was to make, and instead now of crouching against
each other in a sort of swooning, convulsed embrace, they had watched
with eager strained eyes the whole effort. They had seen the loosened
chain, the cautiously disappearing body of their friend over the
boarded margin of the car, they had even held their gaze fixed upon
Ned there in the cold boundless air, and had followed him inch by
inch as he rose above them towards that haven of safely—the pilot.

Nor were they the only watchers. The poor fellow who had felt the
slip, as of a push to eternity, from his high perch, he too had
lifted his eyes, and forgot his broken leg as he noted each instant
of the perilous, blood curdling creep, up the glistening links of
the chain. He had even drawn himself up to Tom, and so the little
population of that speck of earth, winging its way through the
infinite void, caught there with one shuddering hope of returning to
the dear sunlight and the fair plains of the world, fixed upon Ned
their hopes and prayers, and Ned—!

Tom pulled himself together, the horrible fancy of Ned falling,
falling, falling, through that hideous depth, and plunging a
dashed mass of bones on the hard earth, had almost turned him into
a faint—slowly he too raised his eyes again upward, to where a
second—it seemed to him an age—before, he had caught the scared stare
of dear Ned. He looked and there—was Ned scrambling on the pilot;
without a word Tom hauled in the hanging chain, and when its end
appeared, he saw a BROKEN LINK. Ned had seen it too—seen it before
its treacherous sundering had come, and it was in that awful glance
that Tom caught his stare, and the sudden stoppage of his ascent.
But quicker than Tom could watch, quicker than the fickle iron could
part, Ned had snatched with one hand a stanchion on the pilot and, as
the chain left him, twitching and rustling against his body, he hung
to the support, and for one minute swayed in the vast gulf, unable to
rise.

The next, with all the terrified impulse of his good strength, he
hauled himself up, and with the disengaged hand seized another rod
on the pilot and thus anchored, managed to pull his body up on the
floor. He was pretty well done up. The strain had been awful. He
felt the cold too, though the exertion had done something to rouse
the currents of his blood and, luckily enough, the party had been
well provided with food. Once on the pilot Ned felt the danger of
succumbing to the reaction. His senses were dizzy, and it was hard
for him to think. A mental effort as powerful in its way as the
muscular one which had brought him there was necessary, and Ned made
it. And then things came easier. He knew the work, and seized the
levers.

But where were they? Was New York to the north, south, east or west.
In their wild ascent from the storm clouds and the drifting of the
ship, direction had been lost, and at the height it was impossible
to make out the configuration of the surfaces beneath them. Here was
a new perplexity. The propellers kept up their interminable beating,
and the aerial boat still fanned its way amid the stars. Ned stopped
the machinery, and made some preparations for his own comfort. His
friends who had glued their ravished eyes upon him were hailing him
faintly, and he could hear Tom’s voice above them;

“Ned throw us a line, and we’ll pack a few things together and your
coat, and—Simpson says, turn the ship around—and beat north.”

Ned had already consulted the Barometer and found out their
elevation—2500 feet. They must drop. He looked around him in the
pilot and found an assortment of lines, tools, and fortunately the
compass stand, which had been knocked down, with its dial unbroken.
The telephone connexion with the car had been ruptured by the fall of
the carrying chain, and Ned wondered if he could get a line to the
car. It was less easy to call to his companions than it was for them
to speak to him. He concluded to spare his breath. Rummaging about
he found an air-line-thrower, a small affair modeled after the life
saving machines. It did not take long for him to rig this in position
and fire a line over the car; Tom caught it, tied it fast, and he and
the girls set about making up a parcel of comforting things, among
which wine and whisky were not forgotten. It was soon got ready, and
waving to Ned—the parties on either side of the air gulf could see
each other by reason of the electric lamps—the burden of delights
was launched into the space, and Ned pulled it in. Ned got his coat,
and after a replenishment that gave him strength and courage, again
turned to the serious matter of getting back to New York.

The balloon had fallen some thousand feet, but beneath them nothing
but darkness reigned, even the glasses showed nothing, and Ned peered
through them a long time. It was warmer and altogether the marooned
party felt greatly encouraged. Tom turned his attention to Simpson
again, and succeeded in making the poor fellow more comfortable
while he got from him his idea as to where they might then be, and
how he came to fall out of the pilot. Simpson said that when the
squall struck them, he had been blinded by the lightning, and in the
flight up through the fitful howling tempest, he had been seized
with a chill; after that he knew nothing, till he found himself
stunned on the floor of the car; as to where they were he was certain
that the balloon had been sheered to sea, and that every minute was
taking them over the ocean and away from the city. Ned had turned
the vehicle, and was running slowly, watching intently, for the
manifestations of life below them, the compass pointed north, and in
that direction they were now sailing.

“Oh, I see lights way down;” it was the soft voice of Jessie, and the
eyes of her companions followed hers, and there indeed a long string
of pin point lights emerged in the night, and they grew stronger,
larger, steadier. It was one of the enormous steamers that brought
Europe to New York in three days. Ned had already detected the
leviathan, and he found out the balloon was sinking. Was it falling
in the track of the steamer? Was it falling on it? A second later and
the hoarse whistle of the huge barge below them sounded excitedly,
then they all could hear voices, orders, cries, a confused tumult
of sound, while the hoarse whistle swelled into a fierce revolting
protest; its harsh, straining, shivering huskiness seemed to hit the
very side of their car, and then more voices rose, volleys of calls;
the voyagers could hear the sough of the engines, they caught the
smells of smoke and habitation, and then the whole colossus sprang
into sight, a wavy broken terrace and pyramid of lights.

It was evident that the apparition of the descending aeronauts had
started all the activities of the great steamer, while it also became
clear that the cabins and berths were being emptied, and that the
rumor of the falling balloon had penetrated throughout the vessel,
for the storm of exclamations and outcries, not free from shouts
of alarm, showed that the floating population below them was all
aroused. Then through the commotion and buzz came the heavy calls of
the megaphone;

“Hold hard there. Keep on the centre. Fall abaft the stacks. Mind
all,” and then succeeded a sound of terrific scrambling, then crash
upon crash as the great mass of the balloon bodily drifted and fell
on the deck of the steamer. The occupants of the disabled “Chariot
of Flight” had not been without danger. As the balloon came down to
the deck of the steamer, which had ceased its motion, the car struck
the upper works of the vessel first, and, with a slow settling
oscillation, rubbed along the decks and finally hung fast. Before
this Tom had extricated his charges easily, leaping out of the car
first himself, and helping them. Then he succeeded in dragging
Simpson over the edge, and with help from the crowding crew of the
steamer got him away. Ned had soon jumped to the deck, and none too
soon, as the overhanging bulk then descended, thudding the deck,
splintering rods, pins, rails, seats, nettings, and cracking with a
few subsiding thumps the deck itself. When Ned saw the inevitable
collision he had opened the valves, and thus hastened the descent.
It also robbed the balloon of all possibility of motion, and so,
at last, the long cylinder lay on the upper decks of the steamer,
its axis parallel with that of the big liner. Taken altogether the
outcome of the affair was a matter of congratulation.

The captain took Ned and Tom to his cabin, and heard their story. He
was very grave at first, and muttered some remarks about “a piece
of damned hoodlumism,” but he softened at length, after learning no
extensive damage had been done, and seeing the forlorn state of mind
of the poor fellows, who now looked forward to some rasping rebukes,
and no little loss of prestige. Tom was a little moody over it, but
Ned more philosophically resigned himself, and began calculating the
damages. They turned out to be considerable, and this was depressing,
and then to Tom the outcome was in a measure disgraceful, and the
whole aspect of the diversion might seem reprehensible. They should
have returned after the fireworks, and not attempted the long
unnecessary trip. It was altogether disagreeable and discomforting.
Jessie and Delia felt shamefaced about it, and their position on the
steamer was unpleasant. It turned out that the balloonists had gotten
far out to seaward, in the line of the incoming steamers, and that
they were, when the balloon gave out, heading for Sandy Hook. That
morning the steamer entered the harbor, and the strange outlines of
the recumbent balloon on its decks was hailed with wonder by the
ships, tugs and ferry boats of the bay.

But the early morning breakfasters in the restaurants, and later the
sober faced citizens, at their morning meal, found the entertaining
tale in the papers, all served up hot and racy, sent before the
strangely loaded steamer, by “_wireless_” to the eager journals. None
perused the strange story more wonderingly than the Mayor and Mr.
Briggs, and none with the rage and threatening menaces of Poniatowsky
and Longtinsky. Some settlements and readjustments were inevitable.

“Damnation! I knew it,” howled Poniatowsky into the dismayed face of
Longtinsky, “that poodled-faced Ratler has been playing his tricks
here. That’s plain. And you’ve been fooled, led by the ear like a
stuck pig. Damn it, I’ll kill both of them. Why—”

“Pony you’re crazy. I tell you that the girl has been led into this
naturally enough. They went up on the Fourth to see the show, and
then they went off for a lark. Not her fault, I know Jessie has been
overpersuaded. Don’t lose your wits. Kill Tom Ratler;—yes to—with
him—kill the Mayor, kill Helen Lorimer,” and the disfigured face
darkened with hate, “they’re all your enemies—mine too—but Jessie
is yours, all yours then. Can’t you put two and two together. We’re
in this for keeps. The trial comes off in a little more than three
months. Don’t spill the mill now. Things are working our way. The
Beehive is at work. If we can make our strike before Helen Lorimer
gets the turn on us, and stands for more to our people than we do—all
a damned bluff, a game of hoodwink—why then—then—we’ll get out of
this.”

“Mean it?” and the furious deceived lover seized Longtinsky at his
burly neck and gave that discomforted gentleman another sharp fright,
after his frequent fashion.

“Mean it; of course I do. You know that. We’re swimming together,
Pony, for the same shore, we neither of us can go on alone”—and yet
while he said it, the soothing speaker felt that the time must
soon come when he could disentangle himself from this tiresome and
dangerous alliance; an alliance good enough for his purposes now, but
later, FORCIBLY to be dissolved.

Longtinsky persuaded—with difficulty—Pony to let him talk alone with
Jessie, promising to put a better guard on her goings and comings
and this time he was hard and bitter with his daughter. Jessie
was genuinely contrite, and her marketings on the west side were
summarily stopped, but the love she felt for Tom was as bright as
ever, as strong, as sure, and so, with Juliet’s, “as boundless as the
sea.”

Tom was himself in some dispraise, and worried with misgivings. Of
course there were explanations, and they were accepted, though Tom
felt himself resting in a shadow, and it tormented him. He looked
eagerly for the time to come when he might redeem himself.

Ned’s employers found a most unexpected source of profit in the
misadventure, more than enough to compensate for the damages
incurred. This came about through the merited publicity given to
Ned’s heroism. The exploit was well told in a dozen different ways,
and it so enhanced Ned’s reputation that he became a lion, and
the crowds that now resorted to the Zeppelin, whence the unhappy
experience began, were almost emulous to take the excursions with
so famous and brave an aeronaut. The receipts climbed up, and the
happy owners of the balloon and of Ned, forgave him all, made him a
feature, and pocketed their swelling revenues, without disparagement,
even with many secret self felicitations that it happened as it did.

Delia’s admiration for her beau—the comely Ned—became almost
delirious, and as she basked in his reflected glory, she assumed new
airs, and, as they too became her, Ned was himself more inextricably
entangled in his love, his reputation, and the assured bliss of a
hastened marriage.

From it all Tom and Jessie had the most to regret. Jessie’s
reprimands had been harsh, and she was now effectively guarded, for
the infuriated Poniatowsky took upon himself a careful watch, his
moody and sullen spirit hovering with a murderous menace over the
least effort of Jessie’s to see her lover. Not indeed that they did
not hear from each other, nor that they did not see each other. Their
loves became more indomitable and absorbing, it became that “madness
most discreet,” which found ways and means that even a Poniatowsky
and a Longtinsky could not circumvent or challenge. And as for the
man who suffered the only physical injury, poor Simpson mended and
got well.

CHAPTER IX

THE CARDINAL


It was a delightful room. One of those happy and artificial
conjunctions of architecture and decoration sufficiently chaste
to invite repose, sufficiently curious to awaken inquiry, which a
scholar enjoys, and a man of taste envies. It was an elliptical
apartment, illuminated from a shaded skylight, with low book-cases
on two curving sides, their tops or mantels holding marble busts,
and above them on the walls, faintly creamed with rose, oddly framed
wood engravings of Albert Durer’s. At one extremity of the room an
imposing full length portrait of Pope Sextus X, then reigning, with
a _priedieu_ before it, and an enormous candlestick holding a giant
taper of red wax; at the other end a remarkable gothic tapestry of
the 15th century, enclosed in a gothic frame. It represented “The
Visit of the Magi,” its background was a deep peacock blue, sown
with tiny crescents. The offering to the Holy Child was made by
a venerable man with a long beard, wearing a sleeveless tunic of
magnificent old-rose velvet, finished at the arms, neck, and around
the edge with pearls and garnets, on white silk. The other attires in
the woven picture were rich and the Virgin’s Mother’s of wonderful
beauty.

There were six chairs in the room, all different in design, archaic
and ponderous, cushioned with embossed leather and bearing the
singular decoration of a spread eagle.

A square heavily carved center table, on four stout mahogany legs
formed of clambering boys on slips of trees whose branches supported
the table top, and ended in twigs and leaves, which ran profusely
around the sides of the table in a kind of frieze below its scarcely
jutting edges, filled the centre of the room. It was in scrupulous
order—the files of books upon it being carefully arranged in book
racks, and so placed with reference to a leathern framed blotter, at
one side of the table, as to suggest their convenient use by any one
sitting there.

This place was indeed occupied by Cardinal Fenton—whom we have seen
before—and, in his red cassock, with a pectoral cross, a beretta
margining his black hair and that observant gaze, leaning back in
a chair that surrounded him with griffins and perching imps, and
with the brass emblem over him, half suggesting a bird’s flapping
consternation, he gave to the room the desirable finish of personal
dignity. And before him at the opposite side of the table stood a
young man of remarkable beauty.

He was indeed rather beautiful than handsome. His oval face, not too
weakly drawn, was crowned with curling jetty hair, his lips closed
in a perfect bow, his eyes liquidly glowing, indicated tenderness,
and the straight Grecian nose, with the white temples, the delicate
modelling of the ears, and the suavity of posture in the lithe, and,
a little undersized, figure imparted a half boyish, half feminine
impression. Still there were reasons for thinking he was not under
puberty and not possessed of power. A moustache covered his upper
lip, and as the Cardinal spoke, or when he himself spoke, at moments,
the color rose steadily in his cheeks, and his eyes flashed—and
then the voice itself was also fully conditioned in age; it was a
barytone, eloquent and sustained, with a tendency to deeper notes,
not often used but obviously there, if a stronger emphasis was needed.

His scrupulous dress was excellent in its perfect fit, and it
was noticeably quiet in color. Indeed in color there was in this
interesting figure just one note, a scarf of red silk and a garnet
pin.

“Yes,” it was the Cardinal’s voice, “we have reached a period
of forcible religious contraction. The Church seems strong, is
strong, but only because she is the surviving refuge. The world has
decorously repudiated faith.”

“Decorously?” interrogated his listener.

“Yes, I think that suits,” answered the Cardinal gravely, “There
is not hatred, though that exists; there is an amused toleration,
more pernicious in its effects, more detestable, but absolutely
indifferent. De Maistre, who believed in an _auto-da-fe_ and defended
the Inquisition, would be curiously confounded to-day. Rather than
be burned at the stake, the present up-to-date philosopher would
assent, and, under compulsion do all the things the Church asked him
to do, and—without a semblance of immodesty—continue to do so with
gayety in his heart and a lie on his lips. Not, believe me—” this in
remonstrance to an attempted interruption—“that the same philosopher
would feel as comfortable doing it as not doing it—not that he
would not anathematize the authority, that commanded it—but, doing
it to save his skin, feel it was about as important as wiping his
feet or lifting his hat, equally a deference to custom. The modern
philosopher is pusillanimous under durance; he has not the iron
courage of the heretic of six centuries ago.”

“But why bring the Pope to the United States, Father?”

“Why? Look about you. These are the latter days of scripture. I
believe the chosen number approaches completion, and religion will
soon become abridged to a vanishing quantity and—left with its
Science, its Philosophy, and with its Ribaldries of Sense—this old
world will be let to go on its way, whither it will, unchecked and
uncared for. Religion—I mean the Church—to-day has disappeared in
Europe—let it entrench itself, for the short time remaining to it, in
this virile country, yet responsive to the ordinances and formulas.
Let the Head of the Church be with his church, and not continue
the idle function of repeating a stale falsehood in a traditional
position, no longer honored, but openly scorned. Reclamation is
impossible—The Church can see but one finality, its own speedy
departure, lifted up, as it were, as its Sacrament is daily
lifted up, into the clouds, into the invisible realm of ideality,
to be suddenly returned again with a tyrannous power—with burning
fulminations—with sectionizing and filtering tests—with its Christ,
to judge and to ordain.”

The Cardinal sat erect, and the pale face flushed and the rebuking
eyes—those singular and noticeably inquisitive features in the
countenances of confessors—flamed, as if with inspiration. His
auditor was not however averse to argument, and after a short silence
which seemed a respectful acknowledgment of admiration—he spoke in an
accent which showed his Italian extraction, but it was pleasing to
hear, and detracted in no wise from his polished poise of language
and utterance.

“The world is not yet burnt out. Neither as a physical fact nor as
a spiritual being. It remains both ways. Is it not a better place
to live in? Do we not live better each year? If wickedness thrives
so does goodness thrive. I am a Catholic churchman. Yes. But I have
common sense, appreciation, sympathy. Because men are not catholic
churchmen—let us admit, because they are less and less catholic
churchmen, must the world be considered a futility, must it end? If
the Church is catholic indeed, I think, it must provide ways and
means for those who are not catholic. Will you, Father and Prince of
the Church, annihilate the blameless because they do not believe?”

The Cardinal leaned forward with both hands clenched on the blotter
in front of him; he seemed to stretch out his neck and the words
came, as if deliberately weighed, before they were allowed to pass
his lips, they came so slowly.

“Yes; that is the condition; it is hard—but not merciless.
Annihilation cannot be regretted, it might be feared. But this
civilization does not fear it, therefore to it, annihilation is no
penalty. Perhaps catholic theology has passed the point where every
human soul must be accounted for. The Church is larval in this
world, imaginal elsewhere—and as it grows together the constituents
of another transfigured existence in the hurly-burly of a world
disowning it, catching, as it can, the materials for its ultimate
perfectibility, it must obey at all times its LAW of FORM, the index
of its crystallographic symmetry. It knows no compromise, it does not
even know what compromise means. IT REMAINS ITSELF.

“Let the world be wise; let Science and Philosophy keep their
votaries busy, let it grow more comfortable and more self indulgent,
let it banish pain and prolong life—what of it? It means nothing; for
such progress is a superficial gloss, the impermanent fashioning of
perishable things. Utility is one thing; Immortality is another. The
world improves one, the Church absolutely safeguards the other.

“The Church has no quarrel with human improvement, ease of living,
bettered political conditions. It knows to-day it has itself
been stultified by its own human ignorance—but its fraility, or
its meanness, or its cruelty, inhered in its human elements. Its
stains cannot obscure its prerogative. Let these ameliorations
grow, multiply, and glory and gratitude be to those who bring them,
but after all, are they more than the shuffling materialities of
the crust? The Church moulds into immaterial permanence the human
essence. It has its PROCESS. If the great workman Feil intended to
make a lens so gigantic that its light gathering power would bring
the Planet Mars to our feet, that lens could be made in but one way.
The patented right to make an everlasting soul is the Church’s—let
that be the challenge.”

There was again a pause—the young man sank into a chair, his eyes
half strangely—almost with astonishment—perusing the Cardinal, who
had lost the tense attitude, and looked sorrowfully, and yet smiled
at him.

“Father I am not enforced to subscribe to all this—or am I? I cannot.
It would be treason to reason to put into nothingness the greatness,
sweetness, patience, virtue, that has died outside of the Church
doors. That is the madness of conceit. This doctrine Sir,

 “_Conjures the wandering stars and makes them stand
 Like wonder-wounded hearers._”

Cardinal Fenton stood and his voice losing its defiance became sweet,
and sad. He stood—looking at the pectoral cross which he had raised
above him; it might almost have seemed that he spoke to himself, and
IT, and, as the listener himself arose, he leaned over the table
between them as if catching a whispered confidence.

“The hardship of thy service is in being misunderstood! To proclaim
salvation and charity, but to proclaim them under limitations, that
is the test of conviction, and it is often bitter toil—yet, _in
hoc signo_—” he kissed the symbol, and sat down. He turned to the
youth again, and his voice resumed its former authority but more
quietly “I too have wrestled with this myself, but the stoic stage
approaches when I feel no more compunction; indeed I now stamp
assertion with the harshest lineaments of what appears to be an
illiberal exclusiveness. Why not! This emblem appertains to a body
of irreversible dogma. Why adulterate or dilute their meaning by
subterfuge or concession? And what if there has been goodness, and
patience, and virtue, and devotion, outside of the Church?—they
have been useful, they have made dark places bright and illuminated
misery, but they are _corpora divagua et principia ambigua et
speciosa_, because unattached to a primordial propaganda.

“Protestantism is a correction. It protects the Church from the
_poison_ of Success, and the abuse of power—and because these United
States have practically been a protestant country, here the Church
has borne a regal front, has watched and worked; yes, has most nearly
touched her best ideals.

“But, my son pardon me—I mean now to engage in no futile arguments
with you. You are at liberty, within the purview of the Church’s
correction to see these things differently. Let me return to our
beginning—the translation of the Papal See, to New York—”

“Yes, yes,” was the ardent answer, “that is what I visited you at your
own invitation to hear about. But—”

He was interrupted, a servant entered, bearing on a silver
card-receiver the card of a visitor. The Cardinal looked up with
pleasure; “How opportune. It is Miss Lorimer. She is an accomplice
with me in bringing this project to pass, if indeed it is ever
realized. She is beautiful and good, a wonderful woman, as effective
in doing as in thinking. Her resolves have a masculine energy, and
she strains to her ends with an opulent hand.”

At that instant Miss Lorimer entered the room. She was indeed
radiantly beautiful. Her grace, the dignity that she easily gave her
bearing, was not more marked than the heartiness of address, and the
cordiality which it accompanied;

“Ah Father, I surprise you perhaps. Is this an intrusion?”

“A most fortunate encounter. This,” turning to the young man, “is
a kinsman of mine, a true son of the Church—Daughter, and, though
born here in our own country, having a lineage like your own that
spreads its many branches in the tree of an intricate Italian
genealogy—Rudolph Amato, Miss Lorimer.”

The young man had been busy with his eyes. How could he help
it—anything so gloriously vivid and capturing he thought he had
never seen; her voice, her eyes, the witchery of her manner, and
the easily detected ardor of her heart, seized him as with the
force of a physical impact. His own tell-tale eyes betrayed this
sudden impression, and Helen Lorimer, in whose heart Love had taken
up its home—at whose feet too so many eloquent offers had been
supplicatingly placed—was able to read those same tell-tale eyes.
Perhaps she could not altogether restrain the happy feeling of power
they revealed to herself—a power every beautiful woman cherishes for
its own sake.

Nor was the handsome masculine creature before her unnoticed in this
light challenge of observation—an American in whose veins, as in her
own, coursed the liquid fires of Italy—and a Catholic; his admiration
gave his own beauty a heightened color, and by the instinctive
training of nature, whose art, in her best made creature, rests in
an equalization of parts, his voice, his manner, his words met the
requirements of her attention. She felt instantly friendly to him.
Helen Lorimer placed herself opposite to the Cardinal, with the young
Amato—indeed their chairs almost touched. It seemed so familiar and
pleasant to the Cardinal, that he framed them together in his speech,

“My children, I have made you my confidants in this singular design
because, from you at least, Miss Lorimer, the idea itself first came.
Since you spoke of it I have been most earnestly canvassing its
possibilities, and yesterday received from Rome a batch of letters,
which will certainly interest you, though but a few can now be
alluded to. Amato has just returned from Rome and Europe, and all he
tells me shows that our plan coincides well with conditions that have
grown insupportable. The Church like some sea bathed island is now
inundated with the rising tides of infidelity, and the swift changes
of thought threaten to exterminate her shrinking borders. What can
be done?—Well, in answer to my carefully guarded question as to the
concentration of the Church forces here in America, raising here
some stronger bulwark against the seditions of the age—which I did
not conceal flourished everywhere among us—this is the reply of the
Archbishop of Ferrate;

  Dearly Beloved Brother,

  Your inquiry came not amiss. We are to-day like the prisoners of
  Ice in Dante’s canto. We are encased in repudiation and enmity,
  and cannot move. On all sides the pressing repression seals and
  disables us. It is beating the air to preach or exort or prophesy.
  I look over a deserted realm, and as it were see the meadows and
  gardens of religion empty, perhaps a solitary man, or some ancient
  lady, here and there still plucking their flowers, resting in their
  leafy refuges, or walking by their affluent streams, and beyond
  these delicious walls of safety I see too the vast populations of
  the nations, tumultuous, unrestrained, forgetful, chasing whims and
  fantoms, gaudy butterflies of ornament, the delusions of sense, the
  pragmatisms of science, lulled to sleep by the worn out iterations
  of philosophy, or simply stretched over the sun-baked flats of
  Common Sense, without motion, helpless, destitute, naked, forlorn,
  hopeless.

  Perhaps there is a tide in our affairs which leads on to fortune,
  and it is this call of yours to come to America, to lift there
  again the Holy State. I know not; who can? I have importuned
  several, and they all think seriously of it. But it is a great
  matter. The Consistory will discuss it and it will be laid before
  the Holy Father. But will it be better in your new world? Let us
  not uproot an ancient heritage only to plant upon some shifting
  sands:

 _Nil agit exemplum litem quod lite resolvit._
  Write your explicit purpose, and all things will be
  considered.

 Your Brother and servant in Christ,
 Alonzo of Ferrate.

“My purpose Children would be this; you Amato return to Italy and
explain the essential unity and solidity of the Church in these
United States, its wealth, the formal splendor of its possessions,
and ceremonies, its liberal spirit, yet conserving too the faith once
delivered to the Saints, its honored estate in the eyes of men, its
numerical majority. Accentuate the sharp contrast with Europe. Let
the Church publish its repudiation of Europe, and with august dignity
leave it, surrendering it to the perversions of its own reason, while
its trailing banners cross the ocean, and in the New World its
recreated cradle be found, amid a vast service of song and prayer,
and, thus fixed again upon the earth, in this wondrous nation lift
its standards under fairer skies, waiting for its redemption from the
bondage of a longer stay upon the earth.

“And you dear Daughter, I hoped to join with me in preparing for
this sublime event; in making ready in this very city the future
seat of the Vicar of Christ. Your own council in this has been my
guide and hope. No one can whisper against me that I have used a
prelatical influence with you,—the common housewife’s abuse of
priestly craft——No! this was your own thought. Hardly the dream of
it had entered my mind, when you spoke to me of this strange and
glorious——for such I now regard it——project. It will crown our days.
It will be the signet of a new era; like some titanic convulsion

 _When all the sway of earth
 Shakes like a thing unfirm_,

but it may also be the harbinger of fairer skies.”

Helen Lorimer looked a moment at Amato, who remained silent, and a
blush rose to her cheeks, as she found his eyes fixed on her. It
almost seemed to her that perhaps he had not heard the words of the
Cardinal. The embarrassment was unnoticed, and Miss Lorimer spoke.
It was then curious to observe how the young man listened, and had
the Cardinal watched him he might have been impelled to rebuke his
too evident pleasure. He even turned his chair slightly that he
might more easily see the speaker and, or was it especially for the
pleasure of hearing her? Doubtless he heard, but no one would have
guessed he cared much for the things she said; any one might have
told he was falling in love with the speaker.

“Father” said Miss Lorimer “it has been _my_ thought and I am proud
of it. There is a spot that can be made into a beautiful home for
the Pope. It is the summit of Staten Island. Here is a glorious
outspread view of the ocean, and of the city. The place has a scenic
perfection, and it would also seem to have a kind of symbolic beauty
in it too. The incoming ships and steamers are seen from the high
top; yet

 _The murmuring surge
 That on the unnumbered idle pebbles chafes,
 Cannot be heard so high_—

“Yes it can be made supremely fit. The world’s clamor cannot come
there and yet the world’s voice can come. It can all be secured,
for there are few houses on it as yet, it being an old estate or
reservation. It has been called _Todt_ Hill meaning the Dead Hill,
from some far off legend of their having been a massacre there of the
early Dutch, by the first Indians. We shall make it the Hill of Life.
And my idea would be to keep it very simple and unostentatious, full
of trees with the wide glimpses below, and a simple serviceable home,
some jewel of a church on the highest point, and the home of the
Pope, also very simple. I would drop all the abundant pomp of Rome.
That would be pernicious here. The utmost severity of living, a kind
of homely husbandry. Nothing to tempt the criticism of our democracy.
Just the furnishment of the Pope’s court and that bare as it can be
made. What else is necessary? Nothing I think. Let the sublimity
be moral rather than physical; a few well selected treasures, the
impress of culture but all the larger corporeal grandeur cut off.”

The Cardinal smiled approvingly, and then Amato spoke. “Why Father
should you not go. I would be a poor embassador to Europe. I have no
authority. It is true I brought these letters, and I have seen the
fathers of the Church, and Father, perhaps, in that greater matter
that concerned me—well—I think that I must pause a while.”

The Cardinal started, and a shadow of wonder passed over his face,
the young man reddened to his fine white temples, and Helen Lorimer
felt the slight shock of some misunderstanding between the two men;
somehow she felt that she was its cause.

“I am sorry my son,” said the Cardinal, “we can talk of that another
time. I do not think that I could do as well as you in showing just
what is expected here, and what are the chances here, and for my own
part—well—there are several reasons that would make the mission, in
my hands, impertinent.” He looked earnestly at Amato, and his serious
countenance took on the expression of pained abstraction, almost as
if he had forgotten his visitors and his own intentions, while he
rested those scrutinizing eyes of his upon the young man with an
increasing severity.

Miss Lorimer felt the unseen obstruction, and rose.

“One moment,” began the Cardinal who also stood up, “one moment Miss
Lorimer. Just a few words will show you how far we have got along in
this difficult scheme. Nothing very definite has as yet been effected
abroad, but I am sending out communications to all the bishops,
over the names of a council of American bishops who you know have
discussed this subject. This council has told you of its gratitude
for your proffered help. It has been fairly unanimous in considering
the many aspects of the plan—and I had hoped that Amato would assist
us in reaching, in a personal way, certain influential members of the
papal household, before we came more out in the open——as it were—in
placing the whole subject upon a public basis of discussion. But—”
here a glance of mute reproach at the beautiful youth, who with
crimsoned cheeks, and an evidently rising temper, stood near Miss
Lorimer, “perhaps something more will develop shortly, and it will
be my pleasure to let you know everything. I agree with your views
exactly as to the delicacy of the innovation. Nothing must jar or
irritate the spirit of our countrymen. It involves for the Church
a new procedure, a new vista, a new apprehension of its tasks. It
is pregnant with the strangest possibilities of good. It is likely
to land us too in new vicissitudes. It is a challenge to the age
in one sense, and again IT IS RETREAT. Perhaps a clerical habit of
thought makes me dull, but I foresee a new warfare, and the Church
Militant deserving its name in a fiercer struggle. For the Pope to
leave Rome and confound the traditions of over two thousand years, to
reestablish his theological supremacy in this newer world, means a
violation of prestige whose consequences we can scarcely measure.”

Miss Lorimer turned to the young and yet silent man at her side, and
invited him to visit her, and handed him her card, and then to the
Cardinal, yet watching with an alert suspicion, she gave her hand,
bade him farewell, and protesting that they had not seen him lately
left the room, and, escorted by an attendant, the residence.

The two men were facing each other in the room that, to the younger,
had suddenly grown distasteful, after the glorious woman that had
been near him there, had left it.

“Rudolph,” it was the Cardinal speaking, “you have been entranced,
perverted by beauty. You renounce a mission you were anxious to
undertake. But more grievously you will deny the Church your
consecrated service. Is it so?”

“Father” answered the Amato, but without a tremor of hesitation, “I
do. I never will be a priest. You say I am perverted by beauty. It
is no perversion. I love that woman who left us. I care nothing for
promise, advowson, nor expectation. I have not been sure that I was
right to engage myself so closely in your plans. _I do love life._ My
visit in Europe stirred the fires of my emotion. It suddenly painted
the skies and put flowers on the earth. The hidden, separated,
cloistered, existence grew hideous to my eyes—impossible. I am not
traitorous to my ideals, to my service to the Church. No, no, they
remain, but all the beckoning truthful loveliness of life overwhelms
me now. It seems embodied in that superb woman, who has just left us.
Call it what you will, revile me, despise me, but I break with the
past. It has now no meaning; it was a mistake, a mad design made
thoughtlessly, in obedience to one strong feeling, which now turns to
weakness before a stronger—Oh—an irresistible compelling impulse.”

There was good reason for the Cardinal to trust his ears, though
he was angry enough to be compelled to; to his eyes the Passion in
Rudolph Amato’s face was palpable enough. The Mayor would have felt
some distrust of his fancied security in Helen Lorimer’s opening
affections—and perhaps Helen Lorimer herself—susceptible to beauty,
carrying in her breast the yet but half awakened power of love,
would have bowed before a storm that came so suddenly, a storm like
some onrushing freshet of the spring, bearing all before it in its
tumultuous rending strength.

The Cardinal gazed at the young man with interest. He was breathing
heavily, his arched lips parted, his eyes became intensely dark and
brilliant, and, through some impatient movement of his head, his
raven black hair, hanging almost artfully, shading the pale brow and
brushing the burning cheeks; and the Cardinal’s interest turned to
admiration.

Cardinal Fenton was a connoisseur, and he responded intuitively to
every glimpse and form of beauty; he was indeed a _bon vivant_, and
a man of subtlety, faculties conjoined in him with intellectualism,
administrative resources, and an almost inerrant judgment, within
which too ran the current of an optimistic policy. But he knew
Cleopatra’s maxim, “wishers were ever fools,” and, one expedient
dashed, it was not lamented, but itself trodden upon by a quick
successor.

He understood Rudolph Amato, and he knew that instant that he was
lost as a priest to the Holy Roman Catholic Church, but his flashing
instinct told him he was yet useful, a tool rejected in one service
can be brought to an infinite test of usefulness in another.

And so this bundle of unfixed desires must not be too loosely
pardoned, too carelessly freed from his own sense of obligation. He
stood yet in the place of service, of friendship, of fealty—much
value remained. The quickly coining Cardinal knew that, and just now
when his own ambition—for Cardinal Fenton was ambitious—had launched
a tremendous undertaking, an instrument so skillful—he knew Amato’s
talent—must be kept husbanded, not rejected, because wandering from
its first design.

The Cardinal kept his appreciation of Amato’s beauty to himself, and
when he answered, “wait,” he motioned the young man to his seat,
sat down himself, rang a bell, and told the servant, who attended,
to bring them wine and cordials and cigars—the latter for Amato—the
Cardinal never smoked, and, though he relished the qualities of
good wines, he was abstemious: he yielded to a perverse appetite
for cordials. When these were brought, and the two had regained a
composure mutually lost for an instant, the Cardinal without at once
raising his eyes from the liqueur glass before him—a delicate affair
of cut glass and gold—spoke;

“Rudolph Amato you must remember some things. Your dying Mother
entrusted you to me when her husband deserted her—and, when your
Father was brought home dead, killed in some brawl in Constantinople,
I secured for you his wealth, guarded it, increased it. You must
realize that your education—certainly better than is usually allotted
to young plutocrats—since you were protected, by me against the
indulgences your fortune made possible—was under my charge;” there
was a motion of expostulation from Rudolph—“Yes; I remember too some
things—I recall them with sorrow now. I did indeed hope you would
enter holy orders; I recognized your poetic power of language, your
unadulterated idealistic fancies, your spiritual grace; I knew the
needs of the Church; I hoped—alas; how vainly—that you would become
a priest, and I was not placing too great a sanction in my faith in
your powers and virtue to promise these eyes the pleasure of seeing
you exalted to the bishopric, and hearing your voice raised in aid,
in the desperate approaching crises of the Church. Nothing else I
can recall, ever disappointed these expectations until this moment.
You were obedient, grateful, indefatigable and devout, apparently
contented with this prescription, even accepting it with enthusiasm.
Are you quite sure you do not owe me a greater effort at self-control
than you have shown; is it fair to me; is it fair to the Church?”

Again there was a short silence. Amato—so quickly swayed by any
sympathetic appeal—had walked around the table and was kneeling
before the Cardinal; his upturned face told his contrition—if it
were contrition—or at the worst his honest regret for the Cardinal’s
disappointment; but it told nothing of a changed mind. The Cardinal
placed his hand on the handsome youth—he really loved him as a
son—and the severity melted from his face, though he yet withheld
his pardoning smile, reserving a little the new purposes he intended
Amato to undertake.

Amato regained his feet apparently satisfied with the relenting
gesture of the Cardinal. He spoke less impetuously, but the Cardinal
noted—and he was a keen listener—only a deeper settled determination
to banish the past, uproot its incentives, and its aims, to—forget
it, to

 _Wipe away all trivial fond records,
 All saws of books, all forms, all pressures past,
 That youth and observation copied there._

The Cardinal saw the logic in nature of this sudden change; a growth
leaping to the light, and brought into instant blossom by the beauty
of Helen Lorimer. At least, he should guide it in its further
advances there,—and keep it too. Here were two great talents, two
great fortunes; they should be forged into a great implement of
protection against the adversities of the Church.

“There could be no greater injustice to you Sir, nor to the Church,
if I covered myself with the confusion of a bitter renunciation,
which I rebelled against. I should have made myself into a
distracted fool, perhaps worse—a fool and an apostate;

 _Velut silvis, ubi passim
 Palantes error certo de tramite pellit,
 Ille sinistrorsum, his dextrorusm abit; unus utrique
 Error, sed variis illudit partibus_.

“What lay undiscovered has been, as with a lightning’s flash,
revealed. This moment has been providential. If, as you once taught
me, God stirs the wind, that stirs the leaf, that drops its tiny
pellet of dew upon the thirsting flower, at the foot of the tree,
that becomes the adornment of the altar whereon the Sacrament shall
lie, then I feel that I have been saved by the same interposition
from a blunder, and you from a disgrace.

“If service is wanted I am still yours to use, but a fire now bums so
brightly in my soul, that all the waters of abnegation could not put
it out.”

The Cardinal was now looking very earnestly at the speaker, who
returned his gaze unabashed, and strangely enough there dawned on
both faces a smile. On the elder man it appeared as an imperceptible
admission of defeat, on the younger as the harbinger of happiness.
Perhaps in neither case was it justified, for the issues for either
of them were yet undecided.

“Well,” said the Cardinal, “I must still remain your confessor. I
confess if I must withdraw those hopes, that you, to this day, have
consistently strengthened, the unions of your name with that of Helen
Lorimer would also give me pleasure, and—let us believe it is God’s
better way;” he paused, turned again in his hand the fragile glass,
and then continued, “Miss Lorimer is a beautiful woman indeed; she is
a remarkable one too, and much admired, and her hand has been sought
by distinguished suitors—some of them those hungry bankrupts from
Europe. I know she has been dismayed and angry with them; she is a
year or two older than you my son—but—Ah, well, she is indeed very
beautiful, and—perhaps—you may succeed—but, if you fail; how then,
how then; have you thought of that ambitious boy?”

“I shall not fail,” was the unexpected answer, an answer that made
the Cardinal turn quickly. His glance assured him that the ardent
youth felt no misgivings, and as he saw him there, knowing too the
