that proposals for her hand should follow a less common, a less
precipitate course. You forget Sir that in such a case some etiquette
is imperative. Neither Mrs. Lorimer nor myself have been acquainted
with your intentions, and perhaps neither of us would altogether
approve of them. Of course—”

The Mayor’s voice broke in, he would brook no more, he was not
there to be rapped like a school boy, and that native repudiation
of ceremonial stupidity and artificial systems, the fires of his
strong radicalism, which often made him blind to the importance of
conventions, awoke in him, and put him in a rage. His voice, always
persuasive, sounded with an unusual vehemence:

“There appears to me no longer any cause for the continuance of this
conversation, as I find no excuse either in manners or reasons for
your observations. I love your niece; any and all things that a man
may or can do for a great love I am prepared to do, but Sir I am not
prepared to endure the intimations of a callous and a superfluous
Pride. Direct your servant to escort me to your entrance. I am not
likely to disturb you again. You may explain your position to Miss
Lorimer and—for my—own part—” the words fell slowly and with a deeper
hushed voice, “I will not invade her liberty, or stand in the way of
her social promotion.”

The sarcasm was not unfelt by Theodore Lorimer, and for an instant
he was irresolute. The next he recovered his calmness, and easy
politeness, and moved to the door of the room. He opened it, and
the Mayor passed through. Mr. Lorimer followed him, and the two men
without a word, threaded the stairways and corridors, from which now
the guests had departed, made their way to the entrance, and with a
still unbroken mask of toleration bowed to each other and separated.
The Mayor passed down the broad terraces of steps, and took his seat
in the automobile guided by Tom Ratler, and sped away. He never
turned his head to the stately palace from which he felt himself
driven, leaving behind him an incalculable happiness; but at length
he leaned forward on the back before him, and buried his head in his
hands, and when he reached his home he hurried to his chamber and
sank heavily on the bed. Something in him yielded and he had fainted.

The next morning Miss Lorimer received—handed into her at her
bed-room door—a letter which she quickly tore open and read; she had
begun to read standing near her mirror, and she had not read it far,
when she sank upon a couch, and even while she continued to read, the
color faded from her cheeks, and then returned with a mantling power
that made her look more beautiful. The words she read were these:

  Dear Miss Lorimer:

 I fear that I have made a serious mistake. I have
  mistaken my own feelings. Forget everything, and let us be as
  strangers. Our mutual interest in the trial will be now fully cared
  for by counsel, and our further interviews become unnecessary.

 Yours faithfully,
 ——Mayor of New York.

Miss Lorimer crushed the little paper in her clenched hands: her eyes
strayed forlornly about the large bedchamber filled with artistic
treasures and touched everywhere with feminine taste, and then she
arose. An embroidered night tunic covered her, and her unconfined
hair poured its floods over her shoulders, and it rose and fell upon
her breast with sudden quickness. Again she unfolded the disfigured
note, and reread it, and again she sat upon the couch, and her hands
dropped between her knees that pressed themselves together over them,
and the disconsolate stunned look came again, and again the color
came and went. Curiously her face turned to an etching hanging on the
wall near her, which showed the Duke Orsino of Twelfth Night, lying
on a couch, listening to a trio of musicians playing from a balcony
above him, and her lips seemed to repeat the inscription, under it:

 _O spirit of love: how quick and fresh art thou,
 That notwithstanding thy capacity
 Receiveth as the sea, nought enters there,
 Of what validity and pitch soe’er
 But falls into abatement and low price,
 Even in a minute! so full of shapes is fancy,
 That it alone is high fantastical_

A hollow smile passed over her face, and the bewildered glance at
the torn sheet told of the conflicting thoughts racing in her brain.
And then with a sigh that made her for a moment steady herself at
the side of the cheval glass, where her form and _deshabille_ made
a picture which would have charmed the brush of a Bouguereau, she
resumed her toilet, and that finished, she went to the breakfast
table. She found her mother and uncle awaiting her. As she greeted
them she suddenly recalled that the Mayor had not taken leave of
her the night before; she had seen him leave the roof and had for
the instant attributed it to some necessary despatch of business
or the confusion into which they had both fallen over their mutual
confessions. But she made no inquiry.

Mr. Lorimer also had received a letter from the Mayor. It was the
soul of brevity; it ran:

  Dear Sir,

 Miss Lorimer understands my disinclination to
  continue my suit. There need be no reference to our late interview.

 Your etc. etc.
 ——Mayor of New York.

Mr. Lorimer had retired after his talk with the Mayor in a very
ruffled frame of mind, and, as it made him nervous and sleepless, he
charged his disorder and discomfort upon that unfortunate official.
The waking hours of the night only fortified in him a resolve to
prevent the union of his niece and the Mayor, and he determined to
hasten—were he successful in diverting his niece from this unwelcome
infatuation—Helen’s nuptials with some one to his mind more suitable,
and more agreeable to himself. The gentleman however understood the
strength of will in his niece, though with that was conjoined, as he
also knew, a tenderness of love and obedience, that he had often put
to the test.

But he felt uneasy over any discussion of the subject with Helen, and
he too keenly realized that his own discretion in the matter needed
her mother’s assistance, and this he prepared himself to obtain.
Her mother seldom, he knew, disturbed Helen’s “visionary schemes,”
but in some ways her influence upon her daughter was decisive,
and if—working upon these ideas of caste benefit, and the public
protection of an aristocratic superiority, which she preached—her
mother could make her see the importance of her reserving the union
of her family within a class restriction, his point might be gained.

The Mayor was an excellent man (even then Mr. Lorimer made
reservations)—Yes—an able man—Yes—a man with a future—Yes—but he
was a self-made man, a man of the people, and he was _bourgeoise_,
and that to the pinched apprehension of Mr. Theodore Lorimer was an
incredible predicament. As Mr. Lorimer had known very little about
any sort of Love, that ingredient in the problem gave him no anxiety,
though as a cultivated man he recognized certain poetic obstinacies
connected with a lover’s prepossessions.

When he opened the Mayor’s letter he could not repress a motion of
gratitude towards the writer; “Amazingly sensible,” he repeated;
“I will assure him of our political support,” and with a renewed
cheerfulness in his admiration of his own astuteness, the stilted
gentleman stepped into the dining-room, and after saluting his
sister-in-law watched for Helen’s appearance with undisguised
impatience. And, when she came, she seemed the same divine and
happy being he had always known; perhaps he noticed that she ate
but little, and seemed preoccupied, but that was mere physical
weariness. Surely this calm and serene being suffered from no
insurmountable disappointment. He had noticed that a letter directed
to his niece, had come in the morning’s mail, and doubtless that was
the communication alluded to by the Mayor, and the matter was now
settled, and after a little unavoidable regret—he concurred in the
popular superstition that there might be a _little_—everything would
be as before, and lest more dangers should arise, he would actively
prosecute his matrimonial intrigues.

As the three arose from the table—Mrs. Lorimer thought it had been an
unusually quiet breakfast, and detected the unaccustomed reticence
of her daughter—a servant entered, and handed Mr. Lorimer a letter
which he took with surprise observing, “Something from the Cardinal.”
The word caught the instant attention of Miss Lorimer and her mother,
and the two women paused as they rose from the table expecting the
announcements of its contents. It came with a delightful alacrity
from Mr. Lorimer’s lips:

“Cardinal Fenton sends me a good word for a young friend of his,
whom he wishes us to know. His name is Rudolph Amato. It seems he
had hoped to make him a priest, but the young protegée changed
his mind. He comes of a good Italian stock, originally, has great
wealth, and the Cardinal has expended much attention upon his
education. He solicits our consideration for the young man.” Stopping
to read further, Mr. Lorimer looked at his niece, and said, “The
Cardinal says you know the gentleman, Helen, that you met him at
his residence, and that Mr. Amato’s enthusiasm for the Church’s
prosperity is as great as yours, and that you should both make a
splendid combination for the sublime project—now resting in the minds
of progressive Catholics—of IMPORTING—those are his very words—_the
Pope_.”

To the questioning glance of both her mother and uncle, and with a
little renewed animation Helen Lorimer said:

“Yes. I met Mr. Amato at the Cardinal’s—a very handsome young man; he
has recently come from Europe. He was there in the interests of the
IMPORTATION question, and—Ah—” she ended, and clasping her hands over
her face she sank into a chair and broke into stifling sobs.

Mr. Lorimer left the room—this nervous agitation, he said to himself,
will end it,—while Julieta with tearful sympathy embraced her child,
and kneeled at her side pressing her own loving warm cheeks against
the face of the stricken woman, and yet, with a wise sympathy, asking
no questions.

CHAPTER XI

AMATO’S COURTSHIP


The summer was passing along and the hot days began their painful
visitations to the self-indulgent residents of Carrara. They fled
from the heat with varying speed, and, strangely, few fled to Europe.
The fashion of emptying New York, for the benefit of Europe, had
changed into a domiciliary fever for summer homes in the remote
and enticing counties of New England. The young, to see the world,
as the saying is, made their pilgrimage abroad, but even with them
the delights of the noble homes perched upon the Yankee hills, or
embosomed in their green vales, proved effectively more attractive,
and kept them in America. Many of the descendants of those who in
previous centuries had exiled themselves to European cities, had
deserted their foreign houses, and encamped again upon the domestic
soil, bringing with them accumulations of art and a somewhat
unamericanized habit of thought, expression and manners.

Living in the United States had indeed, in the year of grace, 2000,
reached among the rich much of that exquisite comfort, which had
distinguished it in Europe, enhanced by the many luxurious pleasures,
and expensive delicacies created by American ingenuity, wealth, and
fastidiousness.

The Lorimers were almost the last to leave the city. They had a fine
estate on the hills overlooking the sweet valley of Stowe, at the
foot of Mount Mansfield in Vermont, a place occupied a hundred years
before by the Tabers of New York. The wandering and comfortable house
hung on the edge of Cobb’s Hill, a broad large spacious villa on the
brow, looking directly against Mansfield, and over the wonderfully
varied and beautiful intervale of farms and woodland, hardly in
any way changed from its present aspect. The atmospheric charm was
scarcely less delightful than the rich abundant picture of physical
features, with the Hogback ranges to the east, and the sculptured
colossus of Mansfield in front, to the west.

Helen had expected some further word from the Mayor, and his utter
silence wounded, and abused her. She was left with no explanation of
his sudden change of will, no word of that reversal which she, in
her heart, believed was accompanied in him with pain and suffering.
It seemed to her so strange and indefinable, that she was willing
to attribute it to some psychological eccentricity, to a kind of
mental diversion, that she perhaps could not begin to understand, but
which she, by guessing a little wildly, saw, with certain nervous
and imaginative temperaments—and she knew the Mayor belonged to
these—might be an extravagant accompaniment (and outside of their
control) of their emotional make-up.

And yet——those words of love which sounded so sweet to her, which
seemed so sincere and fetched from an inward need, past doubt or
misgivings, how was it possible that they could be gainsaid? To so
quick an intelligence as Helen Lorimer’s the thought had not failed
to come, that her uncle might have interfered; that he might have
wounded her lover by some repulse,—and yet, though she saw this
possibility, by an interior conflicting pride, and desert too, she
would not mention it to Theodore Lorimer. Nor did that plotting
guardian say anything to her. He noticed the absence of the Mayor,
the abstraction and nervous reticence of his niece, and he safely
concluded that the best way out of this dilemma was for him, until
directly appealed to, to let things smooth their way as they would,
and the end would justify the means. For it was now clear to him
that the young protegée of the Cardinal more nearly met his wishes
and expectations for his niece, and he felt also, in his floundering
and stubborn way that Amato appeared at exactly the right moment to
administer consolation.

He felt that there was a necessity for that, and, without much
intuition in the matter—rejecting indeed any appeals to think about
it,—Amato’s various gifts, his cultured sympathy, his adroit and
incessant courtesies, his even gravity, not unmixed also with mirth,
and above all his splendid physical perfection, would, he felt,
increasingly banish the Mayor from Helen’s mind, all the more as that
obliging individual kept out of sight.

And though his dull perceptions cruelly deceived him at many points,
yet as nature is so made in us all, there was a practical and real
efficacy in the companionship of the young man for Miss Lorimer; and,
for the aspirations of the young man himself, her inward craving
for help—which at such crises grows very strong with vivacious and
intellectual natures——were favorable. And so it came about that while
the Mayor remained hid and silent, suffering his great hurt and
sickened in heart, the judicious spirits ruling over human affairs
advanced Amato’s suit.

And in the Mayor’s behalf there had been adjustments. A curious
succession of turbulence had startled the state. There had been
strikes and mass meetings of denunciation, on the part of tax-payers,
and the risings of a new party and an unexpected recrudescence of
Socialism involving a great number of discontented or unemployed
people, while beneath this again was the slowly seething leaven of
the anarchistic revolt, nurtured and spread by The Beehive.

The Mayor’s precocious mind grappled with these problems, his spirits
were brightened by just such conflicts, and he felt the renovating
ardor of combat. He saw—as Mr. Briggs already had seen, but with
a different spirit—the insurmountable tendencies of the times
approaching a broad cleavage in society between the well-to-do, the
generally contented, and a mass of workers, to whom indeed the world
did not deny the usual concomitants of satisfied existence, but
who, brought in sight of an overwhelming luxury and undreamed of
self-indulgence, experienced the pangs of envy, had begun to question
the reasonableness of the present social _status_. And joined with
these were a large body of men, holding the franchise, who were
inoperative and unproductive, largely weak and sensual, whose natures
craved immense satisfactions, to whom wealth had but one purpose,
just such satisfaction and who would welcome any change that might
bring within their uses the boundless aptitudes of money.

The aristocratic leaders saw the same thing, and some among them,
as Helen Lorimer, attempted to stem its progress by assimilating
the vast army of workmen, artisans, small shop keepers, merchants,
peddlers and street sellers into a group of admiring and enlightened
dependents upon the richer classes. It was meant that they should
realize the practical importance of the rich element in society and
becoming attached to it by ties of gratitude surround it with a
measurable force of protection, in case of its political attack.

With Miss Lorimer this movement was divested of selfish motives
entirely, and she worked under the inspiration of a true idealistic
altruism, conjoined with certain theoretical conceptions of a graded
and classified social state. Other aristocratic leaders actively
entered the service of the state, and proposed to use to its
uttermost the advantages of the franchise, assuming the adherence of
the conservative middle classes. This latter constituted the third
class—a class to which the Mayor belonged or at least from which he
had sprung—and which, by its instincts and the promptings of its
cool common sense and unimaginative acceptance of the substantial
limitations of life resented interference with its satisfied pursuit
of money, and its pleasant enjoyment of customary and legitimate
pleasures.

The Mayor had not lost his poise of judgment on account of his
disappointment, and the gnawing sense of humiliation, at the hands
of Mr. Lorimer, but, while he estimated the real value of a rich
society justly, and felt that the aesthetic and cultured aspects
of the city won an immense support from the rich classes, conscious
of their educational relations to the rest of society—as indeed the
aristocratic nuclei of the twenty-first century were themselves—yet
he relied upon the stubborn fixity of the _bourgeoise_ for the
stability of the state. To this latter element he addressed his
attentions, and he employed all the strategy and skill of a practised
politician, as well as an astute lawyer, and an eloquent advocate, to
strengthen his administration among them. He very narrowly watched
the proceedings of the legislature, and analyzed pitilessly all their
measures, exposing those which had the socialistic taint, and often
went to the extreme precaution of justifying his action in public
addresses. This was unusual but it proved immensely popular.

In the meanwhile all eyes were fixed upon the coming trial—set for
the beginning of November—of the incendiaries. It was generally
understood that there had been new evidence discovered, that would
liberate the accused and convicted men; intimations prevailed that
the Mayor himself had assisted in procuring this evidence, and that
he had been convinced of its existence by the famous philanthropist,
humanitarian, and scholar, Miss Helen Lorimer, herself a
representative aristocrat. Of course, this action displeased many of
the richer class, especially those who were themselves implicated in
the treacherous plot to destroy Longtinsky, by creating for him an
opportunity for mischief.

The conservatives among the middle class were about equally divided
as to its merits, and sagacity, but the socialists were immensely
pleased, and the more candid did not conceal their approval of
the Mayor. For the instant the Mayor attracted the attention of
the whole city in a very supreme way, and to a heart and mind
responding eagerly to the incentives of ambition, this popularity and
intellectual domination gave him satisfaction. For the moment, and
happily, these distractions served the purpose of repressing the
poetic, emotional, and dreaming proclivities of the Mayor, though
again too often he felt their intolerable cravings, and his smarting
heart kept him sleepless and despairing.

The repulse of the Mayor by Mr. Lorimer had defeated another scheme,
the murderous intrigue of Longtinsky and Poniatowsky. Longtinsky had
calculated that Miss Lorimer at the closing in of the foundations
of the new Settlement House, the plans of which had been published,
enclosing the novel idea of the Museum, would attempt some public
ceremony, and distinguish the occasion with an address. It was then
that he hoped to precipitate a riot, and in the melee actually
attempt her life, while at the same time a concerted rising might add
to the confusion, and—it was his deliberate conviction—lead to an
overthrow of the government.

This Clodian madness had been temporarily delayed by the
indisposition of Helen Lorimer herself, who ordered the continuance
of the building, but desisted from the especial corner-stone services
which had been at first contemplated. She longed to escape the city,
and the recurrent pictures of the gentle valley of Stowe, the morning
and evening face of Mansfield with its strong lineaments like a
sermon in philosophy and—unlike most sermons—made poetic by cloud
and sunshine, mist, rain and starlight, these, rising in her memory,
filled her with an insatiable longing; everything was hastened, and
at the beginning of August the Lorimers had set out for Stowe.

Young Amato had been well received at the Lorimers. It could not
have been otherwise; his introduction was influential, his own
talent prepossessing, his manner charming, and the essential
earnestness and a kind of dignified naïveté in the man, delightful.
Helen acknowledged his admirable address, and his style of liberal
Catholicism pleased her. A Catholic’s religion is an actual part of
his or her life; it is not placed outside of the daily transactions
of business or pleasure; in the devout it holds a lucid sway over
the motions of the heart and mind, and its admonitions neither
discourage happiness nor check speculation. Amato and Helen discussed
together very often the project of the Pope’s coming to America, and
for the instant this plan, looming larger and larger to her mind,
suggesting—it may be foolishly and vainly—immense moral consequences,
became a veritable comfort. She wrought it in her plans of social
betterment, into her designs for the religious awakening of the
state, into her dream of an established formulary for society, and
with true Catholic ambition—often incontinent and fatalistic—a
reorganized Catholic realm in the New World, free and triumphant,
bringing to earth new treasures of devotion and character, swam
temptingly before her mind.

And with all these blissful imaginings Amato was associated. Nor was
the association insincere. The same enthusiasm kindled in Amato’s
heart, and, with him, it mingled with his love. And both desires
added to one another their especial quality, which improved each.
Amato’s love became idealized, his religion gathered from the earth
the intensity and colorings of passion.

It was the last evening before the Lorimers, Helen and her
mother—Theodore Lorimer had already left the city—went away, that
Helen and Amato sat upon the tower’s roof, where the Mayor scarcely
three weeks past, had received her surrender. Helen Lorimer was
pale and silent. She remembered the very accents, and the voice and
words returned, as if syllabled by haunting spirits. How dismal this
desertion seemed. She stood up and moved about the little circle
along the edge of the parapet, and looked over upon the lights
beneath. The fire-flies sprang hither and thither in the trees and
gloom, shouts came up from the Kingsbridge road, and out over the
Hudson, somewhere beyond the dark wall of the Palisades, a shot was
heard. She swayed perceptibly, leaning on the stone railing and Amato
caught her tenderly.

In an instant the weakness passed, and she thanked him. His love
sprang to his lips. In the half darkness, for the young moon,
trembling in a vaporous sky, afforded only a transient light, she
appeared to him more beautiful than ever, and his own burning heart
beat impetuously. It seemed an enormity of carelessness not now
to say one little word! Hitherto he had been utterly impassive,
in speech at least, and his restraint had done him service in the
estimation of the proud girl. She had found him helpful, necessary,
and companionable. And he wondered—was this faintness in Helen
Lorimer, some feminine tell-tale yielding? He drew back almost giddy
with the sharp joy of the intoxicating thought. Ah! he knew so
little about women; that clouded past, its isolation, amid books and
students with a perverted intention! It thrilled him with wonder that
he had escaped it; he knew now, past peradventure, that if he had
surrendered to the Cardinal’s design, and this woman had crossed his
path, the war within him would have slain him. Did she wish him to
speak?

“Are you ill Miss Lorimer? Perhaps a little wine—?”

“Oh no,” she answered, “it is the heat of the day. It is high time we
were away!”

“Where is Stowe?” he queried, as they sat down upon the stone
bench, while over their heads whisking strips of light, from some
searchlight in the harbor, flitted right and left, and made her more
visible to him. Her hands were clasped to her throat and tears were
in her eyes.

“Oh I beg you Miss Lorimer, tell me if I can help you. To see you
unhappy hurts me so deeply,” he whispered.

“Ah Stowe,” she looked away, and her hands fell, and she turned to
him with a smile that made her tears more touching to the young
student; “Stowe is the loveliest spot! How much you will enjoy it; at
least I hope so for your own good reputation as an admirer of beauty.
From our porch a wide bowl of farmland and fields sweeps to the foot
of the western mountain—a singular rocky summit that seems a reclined
head staring immovably into the sky,—and when the sunset colors flame
and burn above it and the bowl of fields and farmhouses, begins to
grow purple and russet, in the fading day, and behind in the east,
almost as if above our house a reflected sunset repeats the west with
tenderer and chaster tints—then it is glorious. Not that exactly
either—then the place makes you strong and happy,” and the poor woman
bowed her head upon the stone and wept.

The worried embarrassed youth knelt at her side; he could have seized
her, held her face to his own with gentle and unyielding hands, and
kissed away the drops of her misery, that made his heart ache, and
yet brought to him—so his thought ran—assurance of her fondness for
him. The handsome man had no foolish vanity, no spoiled self-esteem,
and yet he was not unconscious of some merit in a woman’s eye, not
altogether unwise as to his power to entertain and help. His visits
to the mansion had been encouraged, he had talked with Miss Lorimer
upon so many things, which mutually interested them, had become so
familiar, even in the short three weeks, or slightly more, of their
acquaintance, with some of her fondest hopes, that he felt himself
somehow established as her admirer, and he had met no one else—as he
recalled—that might dispute that title.

Even as he knelt there, not touching her, indeed with no thought of
such a profanation, how odd it was that there suddenly ran in his
head Malvolio’s stupid conceit, and the words of the confessed fool:

“Why every thing adheres together, that no drachm of a scruple,
no scruple of a scruple, no obstacle, no incredulous or unsafe
circumstance—What can be said? Nothing that can be can come between
me and the full prospect of my hopes.”

Amato sprang to his feet. He felt disgraced with this fulsome self
flattery; could it be possible that he followed this new master Love
with so ignoble a confidence?

“Pardon me Miss Lorimer,” he said, “if you will let me bring you
something; perhaps—”

“No thank you—a little nerves, that’s all. You do not know American
women well Mr. Amato. Our cruel climate makes us less stalwart and
invariable than your European ladies.”

“It amuses me Miss Lorimer;” Amato cheerfully replied, “that you
assume I know anything about European ladies, and the disadvantageous
comparison you make, seems to me unfair. Are not ladies much the same
everywhere?—I mean as regards sensibility?”

“How very unflattering!” Miss Lorimer was smiling again, and she rose
too; “You should not make the mistake to regard us as all cast in the
same mould. Would you care to be confounded with all your fellow men
by an indistinguishable sameness?”

“Don’t be so hard with me. The question was whether American ladies
are more susceptible—more sensitive, I mean—than European ladies.
I have rather been led to think that while American women are more
sympathetic, they are quite as self-possessed.”

“Well we won’t quarrel now about ladies and that life-long difficulty
of telling just how much we are superior to our European sisters. You
know we intend to keep you at Stowe, and I shall put to the test your
strength and patience. I must be out and walk; and your assistance
will be much insisted on.

“I shall be surprised if you are not disappointed with our small
mountain. Don’t look for a Matterhorn and glaciers. Our pretty
country side is hardly dramatic, and the people are not costumed
guides, nor are there any titled tourists.”

“Why Miss Lorimer,” answered Amato laughing heartily himself, “one
would think that you take me for a foreigner. Have I not been all my
life in this country? Is it not mine? Please remember that I was born
here. Europe is more strange and unknown to me than it is to you.”

“Well there is the continental air about you somehow. After all it is
the blood rather than the atmosphere that makes race. When we say
blood what do you mean really?”

“Everything that goes to the making of this body and with it the
mind. It cannot be the red corpuscles alone. Blood means temperament
and temperament means after all just so much nerve and muscle and
flesh and bone, and because all flesh and bone is the same the
differences come in the putting of it all together, as you make
different patterns with the same thread.”

“Perhaps,” said Miss Lorimer, “it’s something like a difference in
air. The chemist tells us the air all over the globe, top and bottom
airs, yields to his analysis the same proportion of oxygen and
nitrogen, and yet how to our finer sense they vary, and how one air
kills, and another revives us,

 _like the sweet south
 That breathes upon a bank of violets
 Stealing and giving odour_.”

“Heredity stamps differences in us, and occupation or custom
strengthens them,” said Amato.

“Science, if this is science,” answered Helen, “explains very little.
You know Amato,” the young man started with this gentle familiarity,
“I scorn science. In a bad sense it is the great deodorizer; it
expels the perfume of every thing. In its grand role of making things
plain, it makes them common. It labels, and classifies, and sticking
names on all creation converts this mysterious and lovely world into
a huge encyclopædic catalogue. Oh, it is an old quarrel with me;” she
bent her head once more, for the thought of her serious arguments
with the Mayor returned and grieved her.

“It is pretty certain,” said Amato, “that science utterly fails to
explain anything fundamentally. Its Laws, so much boasted of, are
superficial rules which never go the smallest fraction’s depth into
the meaning and nature of things. We are less frightened than once,
but we are not the least bit less amazed.”

“That suits me, that suits me,” cried Miss Lorimer, “Ah Mr. Amato for
you and me, is it not permitted through our Faith to feel

 _that the spirit
 Clothed in its changeless purity, may know
 How soonest to accomplish the great end
 For which it has its being, and may taste
 That peace which in the end all life will share?_”

“Well! the vision grows in these later days, and I believe that
Science must be thanked for much. Lucretius said that as boys fear
many things in the dark so men in broad daylight have been afraid
of the future, and their lives, but that reason will dispel these
foolish fears as the sun or the lamp the darkness that scared the
boys. It is so.

“We must be duly thankful for the emancipation of this day from mere
stupidity.”

Miss Lorimer watched young Amato with an increased interest. She
certainly felt that she was admitting his friendship to a more valued
place in her esteem.

“Oh yes—stupidity is always hateful, when it blots and disfigures
thinking. But science in another way is stupid too. Its style of
knowing it all, its preposterous agnosticism, the patronizing air
of its professors, its denuded common senseness, the literal manner
of its assertions, and the blank stare it offers to all poetic
meanings—its absorption in names and guesses!

“And indeed what of it? If we think we know the way the world
was made are we any better off? We rejoice in some interesting
propositions and some men derive a few worldly honors for their
sagacity. It employs time and research and indefatigable brains, but
suppose it weakens our quality of aspiration, of religious hopes, of
contented living, of wondering joy in the mysteries of things, are we
the gainers?”

“I believe that science as a whole saturates our present life
quite favorably” said Amato, leaning with arms crossed against the
parapet and looking towards the southern city. “It advances along
so many lines of sane living, and marvellous conveniences! It is
not only that we have perhaps a new way of thinking—which you and I
may deplore—but with these new ways comes in a flood of inventions
which make living safer, longer, more delightful, and give us, for
instance, that miraculous light that to-night seems almost a message
from Heaven.

“The old latin and grecian philosophers guessed and wondered, and we
are told learning was much a littering of thought with systems of
whys and wherefores. But nothing certain. To-day if we are as far off
as ever from knowing the _Altogether_, perfectly, we are infinitely
nearer seeing the complexity of things, and the adjustments, the
checks, the rearrangements, the vast economy, the deliberate purpose,
the balance of creation, and so, without guessing, we have an
absolute gain in knowledge, and, like visitors to some beautiful
garden are IN it, and no longer OUTSIDE of it.”

“But all this increase of knowing does not change your Faith?”
queried Helen with animation.

“No,” said Amato “it does not. The habit of belief has been fixed in
me. But somehow I think that the area of faith grows less and less.
Lake a vanishing surface in a flood it is inundated on all sides. The
rock of resistance is our Church, and to that as to a very citadel of
safety, those, to whom religion is life, must flee.”

“_They must_,” rejoined Miss Lorimer. “And Amato” again the young man
resisted the impulse to seize the speaker’s hand, and confess his
soul to her, there in the quiet night; it almost seemed to him as if
she paused, waiting for him to take the offered chance—she continued;
“if this vanishing is a fact, is it not the part of wisdom, is it
not a supreme duty, to concentrate the army of believers here in
this country, where Catholicism wears the cleanest face, is the most
pure and radiant, lives up, I think, to the highest standards, and
nourishes a brighter flame of devotion?”

“I know what you refer to Miss—Lorimer,” said Amato “the bringing of
the Pope to this country. Well are we not both agreed about this?
The Cardinal told me but the other day that the plan broadens, and
grows, that the fathers of the Church are turning their eyes to
this country, and believe that in the slow subjection of the Church
in Europe, its losing hold upon influential thought, that the Pope
should come to America.

“The Cardinal says it would mean a new chapter in the Church’s
history—he calls it the Church’s DEMOCRATIZATION, for the Pope in
America would simply be a ruling Bishop, though yet the reigning
semblance of Christ on earth. Instead of a throned monarch he would
here become a citizen, and, while screened within the decent guards
of privacy, he would put on the expression of our common equality
before the Law. In this country, says the Cardinal, must come the
final apotheosis of the Church, from these shores it must rise into
the opening firmament of the WORLD TO COME.”

“As if” continued Helen, seizing his thought and finishing it, “the
Beatific Vision should culminate on these shores, before

 _The cloud-capped towers, the gorgeous palaces
 The solemn temples, the great globe itself,
 Yea, all which it inherits shall dissolve._”

Helen Lorimer’s face glowed with a strange beauty. Amato could see
in the half light—he was so near to her—that her eyes half filled
with tears, and the long dreaming gaze taught him the religious
depth of her nature, and, as she rose, and offered him her hand, and
she came close to him her loveliness shone in his eyes like some
transfiguration. She said; “Yes we all shall work for this. What a
triumvirate of plotters we shall make. Ah!—Well to-morrow even at
about this same hour, you shall see the HEAD at Mansfield watching
the skies, waiting like some bound and wearied Prometheus for the
day-star of perfect Truth to set him,—and _all_ free.”

“Miss Lorimer,” it was Amato speaking, and as he spoke he slowly
dropped her hand, “There are fetters besides Ignorance, and they are
bonds which I would not exchange for Liberty.”

Helen Lorimer had passed to the top of the stairs, leading from the
tower, and she turned to him, and on her face there rested he thought
a smile, as if she understood and wished him well. And now it came
to his mind, with an alacrity he wondered at, that through all the
time he had enjoyed her acquaintance she had been so grave, at times
a great vividness of speech, a rapid interest in things, almost as
if seeking a distraction—but always grave, perhaps sad—and now—this
keen smile as if joy came to her! It made his own heart leap, and the
blushes of delight flamed his cheeks. He felt his pulses quicken, and
something choking his throat stopped his voice. He almost ran to the
top of the stairway,—but she had vanished. He heard her calling from
below:

“Come! Come! We must start early to-morrow. Good-night, good-night!”

The next day Julieta, Helen, and Amato, took the White Mountain
Express for Vermont. The party attracted some attention at the
splendid Grand Central Depot, for Miss Lorimer was known, her mother
was yet a most beautiful lady, and the handsome beaming face of Amato
set tongues wagging, and did not fail to draw the eyes of ingenuous
maidens.

The trip was a new one for Amato, and he noted with studious interest
the changing rushing picture, which indeed Helen had commanded him to
observe, saying: “See! if we are not almost as good-looking as you in
Europe,” a reproach to which the young man vigorously protested. But
he obeyed devoutly, and let nothing escape him.

From New Haven the country grew increasingly interesting; the wide
fluviatile valley of the Connecticut River, with here and there
separated and towering meridional ridges of Triassic sandstone, was
very striking, while the testimony of enormous erosion and decay
was seen in the clay pits, all the way to Hartford, and beyond to
Springfield, and further. From Springfield they shot, on an arrow’s
wing, past Holyoke, and Mt. Tom, with its lonely surmounting hotel
(still maintained), past Northfield, with Moody’s ancient camp, and
then to Bellows Falls, which makes such a bright picture!

From Brattleboro, Windsor, to White River Junction, the charm of the
Connecticut was always with them; hundreds of the loveliest scenes
were encountered, and the varying phases of the river kept his
attention brimming over with pleasure. Charming homes appeared from
time to time, opposite pleasing or noble stretches of the river, and
Tobacco was a noticeable product, growing with great luxuriance in
the river farms.

From White River Junction they travelled to Waterbury. This ride
through a long turning and returning valley, quite closely hedged
with hills, lanes, and old beaches, was also delightful. The day
grew gray and rainy, and much of the beauty of this ride through the
lonely cold vales, with the compact little villages was lost, but the
wild and frugal life of the people, the keen set look of the country,
and the ever present strolling or rapid river were well seen. It
was the same as to-day, for that prevalent desertion of the country
for the city still prevailed at the beginning of the twenty-first
century, indeed the cities grew with startling ease, and the country
put on a new forestation, which renewed its ancient woods, and
brought back, with strange quickness, its wild animals. One thing
alone counteracted this tendency, and that was the increasing pride
of the rich in the country. They scoured it over for beautiful sites,
and revelled in giving to the broad acres they bought, a ravishing
pastoral charm. It was not unnaturally allied to that aristocratic
effort of establishment which we have referred to, and which Helen
Lorimer so much delighted in. She said herself; “if the rich, the
cultured, those who know the arts of living, will root themselves
in our country-sides, and spread beauty and elegance of rural grace
about, benefitting little communities with the application of
their wealth, ennobling natural views and places with expressive
architecture, weaving everywhere, like scarfs up hill and down dale,
good roads, that will throughout make living safer, easier, and more
delightful—if they will do this, they would justify their existence,
and they would surely become better Americans. They would truly love
their country.”

The movement had grown and distended itself, actuated by a new
sudden contagion of enthusiasm and the growth of emulation. It had
made whole sections of New England, and parts of the rural New York
counties marvels of loveliness. It ransomed from commonplaceness many
spots which needed only the touch of an artificial embellishment
to become pretty. It created the most fascinating surprises, where
ingeniously designed villas on hill top and spur, in valley and by
lake side, gave to the landscape a borrowed European air while at a
thousand points the nestling colonial homes, like the most delicate
visions, sprang to view with positive enchantment.

The most fortunate rivalry had arisen between states, and the rich
entered upon the most profitable sort—for the public’s benefit—of
competition. They strove to make their states attractive, to give
them an unusual beauty, and in this friendly and delightful contest
added new incentives to all the occupants of the state to support
them. Schools of Artifice—as they were called—came into being, each
having for its aim the development of a spirit of aesthetic ambition,
and the peculiar use of the features of the state, so as to produce
the best picturesque effects. So there had grown up a stronger demand
for beauty, and it began an almost despotic sway in the living of
men. It influenced the most humble and chastened the most vulgar. In
the cities it assumed such grandiose forms as the monumental splendor
of Carrara in New York.

It had touched the South, where it was indeed most needed, and the
old time slovenliness and dreary hopelessness of board and brick,
flatness and indifference, succumbed to a sprightlier fancy. The
South had become rich also, and this allowed its owners to resume the
old time princeliness of the Virginia colonial style, and so through
the devious changing beauty of the Appalachians, from the Potomac to
Tallulah, art swept its redeeming hand, and blent its best effects
with nature’s unharmed loveliness.

Mr. Theodore Lorimer had everything in readiness, and being something
of a manager, besides an almost acrimonious critic of conveniences,
the house and grounds of the Lorimers at Stowe were in perfect shape,
and the place wore its most fascinating guise.

Mr. Lorimer had suffered, though a selfish arrogant and narrow man,
some qualms of self-reproach over his treatment of the Mayor, and
his own obstinate concealment of their interview. He really feared
his niece, and any explanation to her of his conduct would he knew
precipitate a difficult situation. He avoided that. He realized
Amato’s helpful influence, and while his dull and sluggish spirit
lamented, in a torpid way, Miss Lorimer’s evident suffering, he
believed too much in the remedial and—as he thought—easy application
of the young man’s gifts.

But his gnawing sense of self-dissatisfaction—an unusual feeling
with Mr. Lorimer—sought relief in his own stricter efforts to please
and divert her. So he had taken great pains to have all things at
the Stowe villa in apple-pie order, and long before the family had
expected to leave New York, he had come up to the place, and made the
elaborate preparation in flower beds, trimmed outlooks, and cleaned
and painted rooms, with a few added pictures, a few new rugs, some
jardinieres, a set of new curtains, a dainty new setting of a little
veranda tea room, polished floors, some exquisite china for the bed
rooms, and a pergola with seats, where the sitters could see the
wide vale, dimpled with farm houses, and the peaked ridge of Mount
Mansfield. All these things betokened the man’s tenderness for his
niece, and the injurious sense of his own perfidy towards her.

He had exerted himself to be agreeable to Amato, and he showed his
personal pleasure in the young man’s visits. His encouragement had
been pleasantly assisted by Julieta’s address and winning welcome.
Amato had been careful not to overstep the bounds of an admiring
attention. He had been reticent and simply friendly, but in a hundred
ways his love expressed itself less obviously and, because Miss
Lorimer herself had shown the restraint of some pre-occupation,
he allowed himself only the formal privileges of a friend. His
conduct was adroit and delicate in its union of respect and the
warmer qualities of devotion. All this had its sensible effect upon
Miss Lorimer, yet she remembered the Mayor with an almost unshaken
attachment. The first harsh wound had healed, and her pride—perhaps
not so truculent and preservative as with less gentle and less
distinguished characters—had come to the rescue, as it always does
with women who possess it, as a gift from the armory of nature’s
prudent kindnesses. In this expectant and refined relation of hope
and preparation, of withdrawal and insistence, of sympathy and
regret, the matter stood when the Lorimers came to Stowe.

At length an auspicious day late in August dawned, after a throng
of thunder showers had swept over the country side, when the ascent
of Mount Mansfield could be made, and the clear lucent atmosphere
enjoyed, for a perfect view from its rugged summit.

The party which left the villa consisted of Julieta, Helen Lorimer,
Theodore Lorimer and Amato. Mr. Lorimer and Amato had at first
stipulated to go on horseback, but were overruled, and the party
left Stowe in a large carry-all in which Mr. Lorimer stowed away some
viands and liquids which he said “would do well enough to help out
the resources of the Summit House.”

The carriage bowled along the hard road, over the barricaded bridge
above the little stream that crosses the valley, and on across the
smiling vale, while Mr. Lorimer detailed the past history of the
place, and pointed out the expanding lineaments of the mountain.
Stowe, he told them, a hundred and more years ago had been a great
resort with a handsome hotel, burned down somewhere about 1895,
how it had been deserted and sidetracked by more ambitious places,
how a Mrs. Taber had come to the valley enchanted with its beauty,
and how her talented son, whose life had been actually prolonged
in these salubrious airs, had designed the house the Lorimers then
occupied,—so that the house was something more than a hundred years
old,—how that the returning sense of the delicious beauty of the spot
had brought back to it its old time prestige, and that now to the
left and right of them were farms owned by rich proprietors whose
houses he pointed out with much assiduity, as they could be discerned
rising among groves of trees, or were momentarily seen through
openings in the woods.

At last they began, under the very shadow of the range, the long
ascent and steadily rose, with many glimpses backwards over the
country, the village of Stowe—like a toy hamlet on the horizon—and
the far eastern range of Worcester. Finally after many turns they
came directly under the lee of the high rocky cliff, which is the
nose of the great face of rock, which fancy has formed from the
regular upraised pinnacles along this steep and elevated ridge. A
last long pull and they were at the doors of the Summit House.

Some of the day yet remained and after a slight refreshment Helen
and Amato—the work involved seemed too strenuous for Mr. Lorimer,
and Julieta absolutely refused—started up the Nose in a stiff gale
that made mutual assistance imperative. Helen likened it to climbing
the Pyramids, for the schistose rock was hewed out in terraced
steps. From the whistling peak of the Nose they could see the mimic
configuration that, in the distance, as seen from the valley looked
so much like a countenance. First to the south, the Forehead, and
then this beetling perpendicular mass of rock forming the Nose, then
the long Upper Lip northward, the Lower Lip—both made by projections
of rock somewhat superficially broken up into irregular heaps of
fragments—and finally the high upthrown Chin, and the receding throat
with an Adam’s Apple, made by a conical elevation.

The day was shutting down, a flying scud of mist speeding over the
Chin, and the outlook was discouraging for the morrow. The excited
visitors did indeed return to the level of the Summit House, and
then, in a rising gale, struggle on to the towering eminence of the
Chin, but the clouds closed down, and the dragging tails of mist
flung their moist wreaths around them, and effectually drew the
curtains of concealment all about them, and so they hurried back over
the tilting and pebble strewn upland path.

The next day opened ominously, and Helen, who was awake early to
catch the first glimpse of the sun rising over the Worcester hills,
was disappointed. She met Amato wandering over the mountain top. They
both stopped a little while to talk, commenting on the strangeness of
the silence.

“Just silence itself imparts a feeling of grandeur to places,” said
Amato, “The banishment of all sound affects one strangely, as if we
were no longer in the world.”

“I think it is not the silence alone that does so, but that lift from
the world that the mountain gives,” answered Helen.

“How unusual the voice sounds too here, as if its lightest tone was
megaphoned into a shout.”

“There is a great echo up here further north, where the sounds are
redoubled. It is weird enough when a cornet is played softly and the
slowly emitted notes chase each other, with others rising to the
call, as if a troop of invisible spirits sprang tiptoe from their
hidings, and flung themselves upon the man-made note to destroy it,
or else, one might think that they were freed voices, liberated by
the human call, to rush on forever and forever round the world.”

“You remember Wordsworth’s beautiful lines?” queried Amato.

 “_And they would shout
 Across the watery vale, and shout again,
 Responsive to his call—with quivering peals,
 And long halloos, and screams, and echoes loud
 Redoubled and redoubled; concourse wild
 Of mirth and jocund din._”

“Hardly so, though that is indeed most enchanting. Something more
idyllic and classic came to my mind about them. As if these echoes
were silenced voices waiting in the crannies and crevices of the
rocks for their resurrection, an aerial life to be given them by the
human call they were fitted to answer. How harshly science robs us of
all such fantasies!”

“And yet science delivers to us too a delightful image,” said Amato
“for it permits us to imagine the invisible waves of sound dashed
upon these hard rocks to be returned in reduplicated billows just as
we watch the refluent crest thrown back from the headland on the sea.”

Helen had risen from the stone boulder, from which she scanned the
circling fog wreaths around, and as she turned towards the Summit
House a splinter of light seemed to fall at her feet;

“The sun” she cried, “it will break away! We shall have our ramble!”
They returned to the House for the breakfast, which the consideration
of their host and the watchfulness of Mr. Lorimer had made inviting.
The table was set with early asters picked up lower on the mountain
side, and scrambling stems of the Bitter-Sweet with the orange cups
and red coral arils twined about the centre of the table. The temper
of the place was happy and exhilarating, and the company partook
of the infectious gayety which seemed to be a part of the pure
and sparkling air. For the morning, after the preliminary period
of distrust and uncertainty, had cleared, the mist rolled up into
clouds, the blue sky appeared, and the direct sunshine struck sharply
down, and from the windows the whole ridge, from the Nose to the
Chin, appeared in its unbroken extent.

Mr. Theodore Lorimer and Julieta were both in a happy vein, and
the isolation of the summit, inwrought with the singular power of
mountain tops to elicit a healthy hunger, made the morning meal
delightful.

“I almost feel as if we ought to hire this Summit House, indemnify
the landlord for losses through our monopoly, and make a stay here,”
said Mr. Lorimer, “it seems so interesting and unusual, and it is so
far away from worry.”

“It feels almost as if we were off the earth in a balloon,” said
Julieta.

“Perhaps loose on a whirling Meteor, would suit the case better,”
said Amato laughing.

“Yes,” responded Helen with animation. “We are. Just a bit of a world
all to ourselves, and near enough to the old one to still see it.”

“A satellite so close to the earth as that Helen will surely return,
we shall not be able to keep off long,” said Mr. Lorimer, looking
curiously at Amato and his niece, as they sat together on one side of
the table.

“But if we do,” continued Amato with a smile on his bright and
handsome face, “the thump we make when we come back will furnish
the newspapers with some terrible headlines, not to speak of the
astonishment Miss Lorimer’s admirers among the reporters will
experience to find her on the whisking asteroid.”

“If we should strike New York the Mayor would have no more problems
to consider,” said Julieta, and the speech was unfortunate. Even
Theodore Lorimer sent a glance of mute protest and rebuke to his
sister-in-law. Julieta meant it most innocently; she was one who
had realized Helen’s love for the Mayor, and she had noticed with
astonishment his absence and Helen’s pre-occupation. But she rather
favored Mr. Lorimer’s view, and she had thought the affair—as the
phrase goes—was a mistake. Then she was attracted by Amato who
had won all by his frankness, and ardor, and gentle reserve, and
then—well she had been forgetful.

Amato noticed the sudden shudder that ran over Miss Lorimer, her
pained eyes as she raised them to her mother, and in an instant he
too discovered the truth. It was an unwelcome discovery, and it may
have caused him some anxiety, but he was full of pity, and, with a
gay air, the melody of his voice penetrating her ears, and with his
eyes turned upon her with admiration, Helen heard him say:

“No, our little ball of rocks and stones and trees with its little
population will keep its own course,

 _Far into silent regions blue and pale_

and we shall all become very happy in each other’s
company, and finally by some pleasant accident land lightly on some
lovely sphere, inhabited by wondrous creatures who will give us their
services and show us the beauties of their planet, and make us forget
the cruel world, by their fairness and goodness and powers. Would you
like that Miss Lorimer?”

The image did please Helen, as if, in some such way, she might enter
Heaven, and she smiled, but the next moment she looked grave again,
and said:

“No—there can be no escape—just now; no running away from our duties.
There is so much to be done! Can you think Amato, of any greater
recreancy than leaving the Museum?”

“Or our incomes,” muttered Mr. Lorimer.

Julieta felt that she had been inadvertent; it pained her deeply, and
she turned their attention to the streaming sunlight: “Show Mr. Amato
the Smugglers’ Notch Helen. The day is really beautiful. We might
arrange for a luncheon near the Lips where those caves are, while you
explore the Chin. The views to-day will be glorious! The Lake and the
Adirondacks will be superb!”

“Yes, let us do that. All day in the air, and the splendid
pictures—!” exclaimed Helen.

So it was fixed that Helen and Amato should go on ahead to the Chin,
while Julieta and Mr. Lorimer would follow more leisurely and prepare
with the Host’s assistance a luncheon at the elevations called the
Lips, just above two natural caverns that pierced the mountain side
lower down. In a few moments Helen and Amato had started on their
way over the stony foot-path, broken by rough projections of rock,
and showing everywhere small patches of white pebbles dislodged or
broken from the thickly crowded veins of quartz, which seamed the
micaceous schist. The companions could not restrain their delight,
as on the right hand and on the left, their eyes traced the lowlands
to the distant mountains, the purpling Adirondacks in the west, the
hazy White Mountains in the far east, with the Hogback range just
beyond the vale of Stowe. And Lake Champlain!—how incomparable it
appeared, rifted with islands. The light flooding this vast domain
was broken by shifting shadows of clouds, and the effects of the
sheer deep escarpments of rock were bold and strong. The abruptness
of the verticality on either side of their tortuous path brought the
eyes of the climbers quickly to the lowlands, giving a delightful
visual sensation as if suspended above the caparisoned farmlands, the
woods, the sinuous waters; and ever and anon lifting their gaze to
the Adirondacks they saw the serrated peaks disappearing in a golden
rush of sun-haze.

Amato was busy in his mind with the suggestion of the breakfast
table, and he wondered as he helped the beautiful woman at his side
over the rougher passage of the path, whether it was wise of him to
think of speaking his heart to her. He felt the intangible obstacle,
and his confidence sank a little as he recalled the mental strength
of his rival—a sort of quality that would appeal well to Miss
Lorimer. But then many things had encouraged him, and the apparent
absence of the Mayor hinted at some disagreement which had come
about before he entered the scene, and which had perhaps severed all
intercourse between the Mayor and Helen Lorimer. That some rankling
hurt remained behind was natural, but his own devotion might justly
now, as at an appointed time, rise to allay that pain.

Miss Lorimer was debonair and happy, her spirits rose with the
broadening and divine day. She accepted Amato’s assistance with
laughing reproaches at his humiliating doubts over her own strength.
These had been especially offered, and thankfully used, when, for an
instant, they diverted their way down the rocks at the Lips to look
into the crevice that was called a cave, and Amato felt his own heart
beat faster as he pressed her hands or his face, by the accidents
of the scramble, came near her own. They hurried on over the lichen
crusted stones, slipping gaily, and feeling the intoxication of the
alpine air.

It was bliss to Amato, it certainly was pleasure to Helen Lorimer.
They had reached the extreme end of the Chin, and gazed down a
precipitous descent to a lower level, where rested a small lake,
while beyond rose again another eminence—the Adam’s Apple of this
titanic visage. Below them a forest covered amphitheatrical bowl
denoted the Smugglers’ Notch, where trees covered the whole lower
land, in unbroken expanses—spruce, birch, beech, and oak—and now
were tossing their arms in leafy confusion as some nether current of
air rushed through them. It almost seemed as if they raised their
multitudinous arms in salutation to the high perched humans who
wonderingly watched their violent agitation.

When a cloud swept across the sun, and the pinnacled cumuli were
beginning to float upward from the horizons, then the sylvan solitude
beneath them became dark, and with its foreground of the chased
mountain side, and the rough uninhabited rocks, put on an ominous
and romantic air. It suggested night prowling expeditions, and
hidden dens of the old smugglers. Amato asked about them, and why
the name was used for the place. And Helen told him about the early
days of the Embargo under Jefferson, when commerce with foreign
countries—France and England—was prohibited, in retaliation for the
depredations made by both countries upon American ships; and then how
business languished, and the desperate citizens crossed the borders
along the Canada line, fetching there their products, and how they
used the Great Lakes and the long waterway and the wooded shores of
Lake Champlain, with its many islands for retreat and harborage.

It was a new and pleasant story for her listener, to whom the woods,
they then looked down upon, became silvered in the moonlight, and
threaded by dark figures of the border smugglers in their nocturnal
trades.

Helen told him that the products of the Champlain region were lumber,
pork, brandy, and potash. These found a ready market at Albany and
the thriving towns of Fort Edward and Whitehall. But when the Embargo
came, the merchants turned to Canada; out of the lumber they made
rafts, on the rafts they crowded their liquor, potash, and pork,
hoisted the sails, and with the drifting wind floated down over the
whole lake, across the line to Canada. Windmill Bay was a favorite
place of shipment; sometimes twenty-five rafts went out, taking
advantage of the darkness and the south wind. Wagons traversed the
old roads to Canada, and these were sometimes closed by the militia;
the rafts were stopped or seized, but the trade kept on. A revenue
cutter came up the Onion river, southward toward Burlington, and
fired on the smugglers but in the encounters the militia were usually
unsuccessful. If a raft was captured, the captors were almost sure
to be surprised and the raft cut out a few nights later. One night
forty men, armed and painted as Indians, surrounded some troops near
the boundary, and frightened them into a profound sleep, while a raft
with thirty sails and measuring ten acres in surface, floated slowly
by into Canadian waters.

“In this notch below us,” said Helen, “the traders hid their stores,
and gathered in the nights to begin their smuggling march to Canada,
with wheels tied up in rags, and muskets on their shoulders, or else
they slipped away from here to the Champlain shores to embark upon
the tardy rafts.”

The thought of this early time, with its wildness and aboriginal
tinct, pleased Amato. He mused a little over it, and then, almost
unbidden by any preparatory thought, he uttered the deep motive that
ruled him.

“Perhaps Miss Lorimer,” he began “I can choose no better place than
this to tell you”—Helen Lorimer was looking at him with a startled
half puzzled stare—“that I love you from the bottom of my heart. I
loved you the first day I ever saw you. I need not conceal it, for I
am not going to be a nuisance. Let me be your friend at least—but—I
shall not long be content with that alone—it would be impossible if
my case is hopeless—I will fight my battle somewhere else. Does this
grieve you?”

Helen was looking strangely away from him and something drawn and sad
filled her face; the eyes were downcast, and she had pressed back her
hair from her cheeks, whither it had blown, and in this disarray with
a steamer cap in her other hand she became to Amato more bewitching
and irresistible. He could not stop.

“Oh please Miss Lorimer, let me stay—let me hope. Such speeches as
this you have heard before, but perhaps they have not been conjoined
with so much patience—for I will wait, and wait—wait to prove to you
how uttermost is my love—wait till you know yourself that my love can
be unselfish, strange as that sounds. Think well of me and keep me
here.”

“Ah—I do think well of you Rudolph”—Amato leaned towards her, he felt
like falling at her feet; most grotesquely he thought that if she had
asked him to jump down the ragged splintered cliff at their side he
would have, then and there, done it, _for her sake_. His name from
her lips sounded like music; he would prize that name henceforth,
because SHE had spoken it.

“—but” she was speaking, “you must not speak of this again—not
now—not till I say you may. I will try you. I have had my—pains:” she
caught her flushed cheeks in her two hands, as if she would crush
back to its hiding the tell-tale blood, “—but you are indeed my
friend—you can help me. Yes, Rudolph,” again that sweet familiarity
that seemed to close round the young man like a sensible embrace, “I
do think well of you—”

And then a queer thing happened. It was one of those sympathetic
responses in Nature that appear almost as if an intelligent
compositor—or better a composer—set upon the physical face of things
the seal of a spiritual and poetic beauty. A twittering cloud of
birds surrounded them; they were the chaste little black-poll
warblers, beating up from the forest, and, with an impulse of
friendliness fearlessly settling on the angular rocks, so that they
spread a confused surface of movement about them, and then—even as
the two friends forgot their surprise—they rose without sound into
the sky like a puff of smoke and went southward.

“The winter comes,” said Helen; she rose and began the slight descent
from the cairn-like pinnacle whose escarpment on the north ended in
the little tarn. In an instant Amato was at her side, helping her,
and when he released her hand upon the flat path homewards he raised
it and kissed it, and it was not withdrawn.

The lunch at the Lips was a great success. Amato was in ecstasies.
Mr. Lorimer and his sister observed that Helen had brightened, and
Helen herself launched out into brave railleries over the foolish
exuberance of the feast. It was a handsome and incongruous array.
A table cloth pulled over a flat rock made the table, and here
garnished with pickles and olives, curries and salted nuts, was
spread delicate cuts of cold beef and chicken, with interspersed
salads, and potted lobster, with fresh rolls and crisp biscuit, iced
tea and lemonade, and then, as a climax of the culinary bravado, the
waiters disappeared into the rocks and emerged with an ice-cream
freezer and a basket of champagne.

“Oh!” exclaimed Helen, “this is outrageous, monstrous, Uncle! It is
pure desecration. Convention against nature! The Oreads will become
our besetting Furies. Dew, roots, herbs, and wild honey should form
our repast—and all this omnivorous hunger at the very mouth of the
sleeping Giant!”

“Let us hope,” said Mr. Lorimer, “that the dear fellow will not wake.
He might prefer us to this gastronomic medley. As for honey, here
Helen, is some of the best—straight from Condits—and I think the dew,
roots, and herbs can be dispensed with; you will certainly forget all
about them when you have tasted this dry Mouquin.”

“And I,” spoke up Amato with mock gravity, “will take care of the
Oreads; if they venture to disturb us I will exorcise them with a
fine latin anathema that the Fathers recommended for Devils.”

It was a festival moment, and the savagery of the isolated ridge
blended with the comic contradiction of a Fifth Avenue banquet amused
and entertained them all. To Amato it was quite clear that good
food—the best—acquired a new charm when the farthest removed from its
origins. But their hopes for a continuance of the bright day were
doomed to disappointment; the sky became filmed with clouds, that
darkened into rain, and beating a retreat the merry company followed
by the little retinue of caterers, scampered back to the refuge at
the hotel.

Amato did wonders in entertainment. There was an old piano hammered
out of all semblance to a musical instrument by the ambitious banging
of innumerable strollers, lady performers and sledge fingered
bucolics, “who played by ear,” in the parlor of the hotel; and from
this Amato extorted enough becoming sound for the accompaniment
of his really excellent singing. So the day passed. To Amato it
had been one of victory, and when the night shut them in, and
after a few games, all had retired, Amato alone sprang out upon
the porch and walked up and down in the intervals of the showers,
smoking, and thinking, and planning. Life had never seemed so
desirable, so promising, and after midnight a stray fox, engaged in
a reconnaissance of the chicken fold, stopped a moment outside in
the wet boulders, to notice the perambulating spark that ceaselessly
moved to and fro against the dripping-clapboards of the Summit
House. To Amato his mission of love had become deepened with a new
meaning; he cherished it more than ever, since he knew it might mean
a _Mission of Mercy_ too.

CHAPTER XII

THE TRIAL


November had come and the day for the great Trial had come with it.
Events had been moving rapidly, and political and social forces
concurred to gather the citizens of the City-State into two opposing
parties. Within one of them resided the aristocratic principles
taught by Helen Lorimer, and within the other—though far more
obscurely—the disorganizing tendencies of The Beehive. Between these
formidable elements of political disturbance and political prestige
the Mayor directed his course with consummate skill. The man’s
enormous power of concentration, his impetuous activity, became
the marvel of the commonwealth. This ambitious scholar and orator,
touched with a utopian idealism, which Briggs had thought betokened
at the outset simply weakness, and mental inertia—a view which the
Mayor’s conduct at first quite appropriately justified—had amazed the
politicians, with his swift recoil from all their shifty opportunism.

He had introduced important reforms, he had established new standards
of proficiency in the city’s business, he had deliberately approached
his constituents of all shades of opinion, and he had not hesitated
to declare himself the exponent of a vast utilitarianism, which aimed
at the perfect government of the City, and the realization within
its borders of universal happiness. He had been referred to by some
wag as the City’s Augustus, who was engaged in the Sisyphean task
of reconciling irreconcilable elements, and building a permanent
commonwealth upon the treacherous sands of caprice, selfishness,
pride, extravagance, and envy.

They said the Mayor was trying to maintain the traditional State—a
well ordered union of all forces, conditions, and occupations—whereas
the State oscillated between two opposite inclinations, one making
for an oligarchical rule of wealth, and family, the other for a
proletariat of a levelling demagoguery. They pointed to the fact
that the Mayor encouraged the ceremonials of the Holidays, that he
officiated at the laying of corner stones, he affected a public
ostentation; his frequent allocutions to the people were in the
nature of lectures, and his endorsement of all plans of public
improvement, his familiar frequentation of the poorer section of
the City, his undisguised appeal to the rich for support, all these
things pointed to his realization of a conflict which only his
multifarious sympathies for the present concealed. And they were
right.

The Mayor was the visible bond of a provisional peace. His method, as
these ingenious and learned critics pointed out, was the Augustinian
_pax Romana_, by which he attempted as far as possible to reconcile
divergent interests, and, in a process of universal stimulation,
ministering to the municipal pride, to hide the increasing
mobilization of the forces of conservatism and radicalism in opposite
camps. But these forces daily grew more compact, and busily engaged
their respective resources—in papers, magazine writers, public
speakers—to illustrate and defend their theories. It was felt by
the more thoughtful that society stood upon the verge of conflict,
which might mean several things. Two prominently emerged. A civil
state on the one hand constituted upon a stricter enforcement of the
individual rights of property, and on the other some yet indefinable
new social organism which subjected individual rights to legislative
revision, and in all probability to legislative annulment. The
situation had become interesting, and below the mere academic surface
of affairs burned the fires of a temperamental hostility, that would
not be content with a peaceful solution.

The Mayor—smarting with an inner pain he confessed to no one,
as indeed he had no one to confess it to—became involved in a
propaganda of mutual toleration, and worked upon an Aristotelian
basis of a state utilizing all tendencies in the preservation of a
fabric of checks and balances, with mutual toleration. The sting
of his unmerited repulse by Mr. Lorimer, might have easily led him
into reprisals and retaliation. It might naturally have turned his
thoughts towards the creation of conditions bearing more heavily
upon the rich—the Carrara group of proud conservatives but it did
nothing of the sort. The man preserved a perfect mental serenity,
and weighed with even more scrupulous care the exact proportions of
his sympathies with either faction, hoping to develop a third and
balancing power, which would control the electorate.

His public acts were marked with a fine regard for justice, his
innovations in the financial government of the city were well
considered, and his appointments critically excellent. Not a trace
of selfishness could be detected in his work, not the slightest
yielding to corrupt influences, not the most tenuous showing of
political craft. And this in no wise jeopardized his reputation among
the very classes who suffered rebuff at his hands. For it was too
well understood, as Mr. Briggs told Tom Ratler, that the Mayor was a
compromise candidate at the last election, and had himself emphasized
the independent attitude he was entitled to bear towards all parties.
All this had won him praise from the moderates and sensible, but it
had also brought him into a less enviable estimate with the rancorous
and radical of both sides.

Had he been a weak man, intellectually, this even course might
have assumed the expression of timidity, or simply a puritanical
notionalism that, by some kind of exact partition of its smiles to
either quarter, hoped to conciliate all. Not so with the Mayor. His
strength was unmistakable, and his language tremendously forcible
and correct. No one knew that his ingenious and convincing language,
his incessant anxiety and explanatory emphasis, the vigorous novelty
of his addresses and Bulletins, had their source of power in the
smarting and bruised heart of the man, for, as will occur with such
temperaments, where a pride of despotic power, and at the same
time a pride of jealous apprehension, the pride of an unusual mind
associated with a personal history of struggle and self-denial, has
been deeply wounded, the energies of the mind storm some new citadel
of difficulty, and minister distraction. Such consolation is indeed
temporary, but it suffices to allay the worst aches, and if the
effort brings praise and distinction, smothers the fires of fever and
desire.

In an article of much length which had appeared in the numbers of
one of the best magazines, the Mayor had anonymously discussed the
situation. The article attracted attention, and was made the text of
editorials and sermons, of conversation and drawing room talk, and
every one knew that its author was the Mayor. In it the Mayor had
aired his studies of Roman History—a favorite topic with him—relying
as he did upon such pictures of the first days of the Empire as
Ferrero has given us. In a pregnant paragraph he depicted the whole
dilemma.

“The predicament of the ancient Roman State at the time of Augustus,
if we accept the delineation of Ferrero, was the supineness of the
governing classes, their reluctance to exercise the functions of
Rule, their intolerable surrender to indulgence, to the depravity
of a sensual and oriental luxuriance, and the dilettantism of
literary tastes. With Rome at that time engaged in a hopeless task
to revive an ancient decorum, and the stolidity of an antique moral
forcefulness, the problem of Augustus seems to have been how to
distribute the burdens of government, and save himself from the
arduous responsibility of “running the machine” alone.

“At the top was a nobility dissipated, selfish, and forgetful,
which had lost the manhood of its former state, and now revelled in
glorious depravities which an eastern civilization had introduced,
and made irresistible; next to it was a knighthood class interested
in attaining wealth, in successful business enterprises, in the
exploitation of the resources of the vast empire, and also too
willing to surrender the burden of government to any one—and Augustus
had grown in their eyes semi-divine—who would conduct it, so as
to least disturb their enterprises of thrift and emolument. Below
them came the masses of retainers, freemen, slaves, veterans, small
merchants, and artificers, professional loafers, and impecunious
litigants, who looked to the government and the richer guilds for
entertainment and support, for gladiatorial shows and bread.

“Our present condition is rather one of Social War, because all wish
to govern, and the political prowess of individuals was never more
imposing. There is here no disintegration of Ambition. Our danger is
not one from surrendered rights, but rather from the clamorous claim
for more rights, from the importunity of too many doctors.

“Society sensibly oscillates between two possible extremes, and the
old adage that government is Compromise, forms, with this City-State,
the _ideal programme_, under the control of sense, toleration, and
accommodation. Our vitality is enormous. Our talent unlimited, our
wealth boundless, the freshness and vigor of enterprise unimpaired,
our mental activity exaggeratedly quick and responsive. There is no
general lethargy here, though we enlist every element of pleasure.
The elective franchise was never more greedily enjoyed, never more
desperately believed in and used. We have become _doctrinaire_, but
the philosopher carries his shillalah and his gun. Theories have
become creeds, and there are martyrs to die for them. THE STATE
MIGHT BECOME DISRUPTED THROUGH WAR, IT WILL NEVER DISAPPEAR THROUGH
INANITION.”

The crystallizing process continued, and the two parties confronted
each other, the Conservatives—called The Plum-Puddings—and the
Radicals—called The Levellers—and the event of some determined
strikes had more sharply defined them. Force had been vigorously used
to suppress the disorders, that had arisen in their wake and The
Beehive, anxious to breed a deeper feeling of distrust, willing to
start into action the most wicked and dangerous characters upon some
crusade of retaliation and violence, had seized the chance to sow the
seed of discontent and Revolution.

The Mayor had been thoroughly straightforward and insistent He had
made it clear that street disorder must cease, he had denounced The
Beehive, and threatened it with suppression; he had summoned a mass
meeting of the temperate people and in a great speech had reiterated
his control over the best elements, and effected a complete
suspension of criticism. The Mayor loomed larger. His hand worked
with masterly precision, and he was found at every point able and
