building and though roaring within with a stentorian hum and pushing
upwards as floor after floor dissolved away in its awful heat the
roof was yet at a considerable height above the flames. It seemed
to him possible to maneuver so that he might get close in, on the
off side—the windward side—and he swept past the building once or
twice almost on the level of its upper stories, shouting through his
megaphone to the cluster of people—he counted twelve—who had also
seen him and who called back, some of them running to and fro, a few
motionless together, and others standing apart with bowed heads.

From the moment when they were incontinently hurried to the roof, at
the instant when the attacking rioters surrounded the Building, to
the welcome sight of Ned’s aerial arrival in the monstrous air-ship,
that, in the night, afire with its lights, glided to them like some
impossible globular exhalation, Helen Lorimer and Amato had passed
through some trying hours. The unexpected events developed with
such rapidity that no one had very much considered the situation or
understood at all well just what had happened.

Flight somewhere from an unexpected danger was about the only thing
at first clearly realized. The flight had been effectively quick, and
when the elevator started to the roof it held the ten who had obeyed
the hasty summons, they consisting of the Judge, Helen, Amato, Mr.
Moser, his opponent Meldexter, a court stenographer, two reporters,
and, strangely enough, two women who had been in the court room
during the trial.

There was a whimsical element in the escape thus effected to the roof
of a sixteen story building, for, by no apparent means, could their
isolation there be relieved, and, though pretty well startled, some
were not disinclined to see the humorous side of the situation.

“We shall be above the danger—whatever it is—but shall we be any
better off there? I should prefer to be nearer the ground myself,
where my legs might be more useful. I haven’t grown wings yet,” said
Mr. Meldexter.

“Well,” said Mr. Moser, “at least we may hope to be forgotten. Out of
sight out of mind, though, your Honor, it may be yourself that will
prevent any such fortunate oblivion. If these are really desperate
rioters they won’t forget the Judge.”

That distinguished jurist failed to see the amusing side of the
predicament and yet, driven to the wall by the real or assumed
coolness of the company, grudgingly admitted a forlorn attempt at
pleasantry, in his reply. It was hardly a subject he thought for
amusement, but if it was in his power to apply “summary proceeding”
for their extrication, he would not hesitate to stretch the limits of
his prerogative in the case.

Amato was unmistakably worried, and Helen Lorimer was silent. The
reporters had learned something about the facts, and their tale, when
understood, put a very serious complexion upon the disturbance. It
was an insurrection for the release of the prisoners, and had been
well planned and was unexpectedly vigorous. After the manner of their
class they enlarged picturesquely upon the few rumors they possessed,
and were not loth to impress upon the little company around them
their own unsupported convictions that the City was in the throes of
a monstrous and quite ungovernable upheaval.

“It can not be,” it was Helen Lorimer speaking, “There is no reason
for any thing so wrong, so frightful! A few wicked spirits have
done this. They mislead and destroy their helpless dupes. Oh! how
ruthless, shameless, unfair it is!”

“It’s probably no particular care of theirs how often they try this
same hopeless cause, as long as they can get a good living, a little
useful notoriety, and an uninterrupted holiday out of it. But,
Madame, your interest in miscreants of this class hardly permits you,
it seems to me, now to find fault with them.”

It was Mr. Meldexter speaking.

“I find fault with the real offenders, Sir; my sympathy for those
unjustly accused is all the more real, because I detest their selfish
leaders.”

Then the Judge interrupted; “It won’t do just now, my friends, for
us to argue this out, or even to run the risk of disagreement in
anything. Let us keep together, bodily and mentally, and give our
undivided attention to getting out of a bad scrape. Were we not told
that a marconigram had been sent for a Zeppelin?”

“Yes; Yes,” was the reassuring answer of the elevator-man: “a balloon
has been called. It’s pretty sure to be needed. You can’t go down
now. The building’s afire.”

This forlorn state of affairs might already have been detected, for
the acrid smell of smoke came up the shaft, carried by the hurrying
currents of warm air that was filling the place, though the eating
energy of the conflagration had not yet reached the shaft.

The faces of the majority blanched, and Amato felt a strange dread.
Perhaps to all the thoughtful there, the physical factors involved in
a building sixteen stories high, with a fire at its base, suggested
how the irresistible draught and pressure would add incalculable
force to the voracious flames, and how the enormous structure might
soon become a volcano-like funnel, consuming its own walls, and
guiding its seething storms of fire to the topmost pinnacle, until it
too was overtaken and sank within, amid all the crashing floors below
it. This sea of flames beneath them was a less negotiable quantity
than the insane mob.

There was no more conversation. Some whimpers of fear started with
the two women, but they were suppressed quickly. Mr. Moser undertook
the needful task, after a moment of silence, of supplying a little
cheerfulness.

“The fire won’t reach us. Long before it has made so tiresome a
climb—remember there are some fifteen solid floors hemming it in—its
strength will be exhausted. It will burn itself out in the room it
started in. And at any rate we are sure to be taken off.”

“How do you know that?” timidly murmured one of the softly sobbing
women, whose tears felt the relief of Mr. Moser’s agreeable
encouragement.

“Know it?” said Mr. Moser with a sort of querulous and yet jocular
interrogation, as he noticed the good effect of his confidence, “Know
it? How do we know anything? The call for a Zeppelin has been sent
out. It’s inconceivable that it will not be read, and if it is—By
the way” with a sudden emphasis, “what was the call? Do you know?”
turning to the elevator-man.

“Here’s the operator. Ask him.”

The elevator which had ascended slowly was now at the end of its
trip, at the lofty wind-encircled summit. The door slid back, and
the slender company stepped hastily out into a small chamber, where
the clicks and the soft hum of a “wireless” were heard. The operator,
with clasped ears and running fingers over the board of his receiver,
was almost insensible of their entrance, at least he paid no notice,
except an instant’s glance at the faces of the group. Instinctively
they gathered about him, feeling his absolute possession of the only
means now of escape. Amato had walked out through the doorway of the
apartment upon the rather wide gallery that encompassed the building,
and leaned far over to see the conditions below them. The spectacle
was not reassuring.

Amato had slowly made his way to the north-west side of the huge
structure, and could see down into Court Street. From the south side
upon which he emerged at first, when he left the room of the wireless
operator, he beheld Chatham Street blocked with people, a surge of
heads massed into individual obliteration far south into Nassau
Street and Park Row; he discerned the incessant shifting of squads
of men immediately at the foot of the building; he had already heard
the detonations of the bombs, which broke in the doors—and, at the
moment, a tempest of cries rose weirdly to him, and there was light
enough—that supplied by the street lamps—for him to make out the
massive inrush of the rioters, as they continued to shove through the
apertures of the entrance.

Before Amato had reached his dizzy aerie of observation the first
section of the rioters had begun the work of incendiarism, careless
of all consequences. It was done, as the guards defended their
prisoners in the vaults. Oil and combustibles soon displayed their
demoniacal powers, not averse, it might seem, to have escaped
incarceration in lamps and the innocent vessels of trade, laughing
with hiss and roar, in their new orgies of disaster. Spirts of flame,
wide surfaces of tortured tongues of fire, explosions scattering
fire-brands in all directions, to become new centres for the rising
fury of heat, quickly made the great court room a furnace of death,
and expelled headlong the panic-stricken invaders.

For many minutes Amato studied the exciting scene, leaning over the
balustrade in the cool night air, centered almost 200 feet above
the tumult—and then—_he saw the flames!_ They smote against the
glass windows, and leaped through them with sharp cracks of rupture.
He could see the reflected glare of their mounting waves upon the
faces of the receding multitude—now stampeded towards the City Hall,
following a large, somewhat straggling regiment of armed men. This
was just before the assault upon the City Hall.

There was no doubt the Municipal Building was on fire. The wind had
risen, the rain was suddenly abating, the fanned furnace beneath
him would increase into a volcanic violence, and the writhing iron
supports, the columns under the rising floors, would be twisted into
cork-screws, and the building would fall! Even as he speculated upon
this—a roar reached his ears, the crash of a fallen floor whose iron
work had crumpled in the fierce heat and pulled it down, offering
another mass of fuel to the ravenous element. It seemed indeed to
Amato that the building shook, though his excited fancy might just
have erred in this. He then went on the north side, and scanned the
light aureoled city, looking down the terrific cliff of masonry to
the street. He shuddered to think of the inevitable ascent of the
fire, as it marched from story to story, and the yielding strain
of the warped and racked supports, undermining the building, which
might sink or fall. It would take time, perhaps there was not enough
material to feed the fire—the floors might hold out, and caged within
its walls the fire might quench itself for want of reinforcement.

Amato shook his head mournfully, he half believed their doom was
very near indeed! There—again—a crash, and another vibration like
a thrill of agony, in the searing column, seemed to sway his feet.
He looked long over the sheer precipice—with rope enough they might
be lowered to the street, and some one—the strongest—himself—might
be the last to slip over the parapet, and slide down from window to
window! It was an appalling risk, and his heart halted in its rapid
pulse, as he thought of Helen Lorimer entrusted to this terrifying
hazard. A queer illusion seemed to master him, and his head turned
and he supported himself for one moment groaning aloud at the awful
extremity a chance had brought them in.

Then he heard shots, many of them—like a fusillade—he thought he
heard screams, but the wind carried them away. There was no doubts
about the shots. Again the cracking reports came thinly to his ears.
He looked down again—little flames were licking, far, far, below, the
window frames, and smoke in floating curls, like eddies in a stream,
rose past him. He supposed the firemen were busy elsewhere. He could
see to the east, quivering, rising and sinking, phantoms of reflected
light, on the sky—but yet very faintly. The firemen _were_ busy.
Putkof had them engaged at the river front. And thus he watched for a
half hour or so.

Suddenly his eyes fell upon a little galaxy of light moving like
a huge _ignis fatuus_. He wondered. It grew bright. Could it be a
balloon—was it theirs—the relief balloon they had called up? Perhaps!
Watch it. It waned and waxed, now eclipsed behind a high tower and
now emerging, and always brighter. It was certainly coming towards
him, Amato thought. He traced its sinuous, up and down, right and
left course, with fascinated interest. It came on, its bulk was
visible—high up in the air; it passed over his head, shutting out the
stars like a monstrous hand. Then it whirled about and rose higher.
He shouted:

“Here we are! Here we are! Come back!”

His cry was heard in the operator’s room, and the people there led by
Miss Lorimer came out in the gallery, and joined him, some shrinking
from the gaze over the parapet that seemed just leaning against space.

“Do you see anything?” said Miss Lorimer, “the operator has just
caught a message telling him that relief has been sent to us.”

“See! Yes, indeed! Can’t you see that dancing globe above your head?”
and Amato pointed to the balloon, where Ned Link sat, looking down
too on them, as the air draughts flung him away from his destination.
For now, by some reinforcement, the fire had gained an incredible
impetus; long forks of yellow lurid flames were crowding to the
windows on the south side. The windows went in by dozens, their
splintering tinklings of broken panes lost in the succeeding roar
of the flood of fire pouring out. The floors were falling too, and
actual shudders strained the building whose very top at one moment,
to Amato, seemed to describe an arc.

And now the balloon swept round and again vanished, and again came
into view, while all the watchers called and shouted, even the judge
bawling with the rest. Helen Lorimer laughed—Oh, just a little!—the
group, screaming and gesturing, shaking out handkerchiefs, even
coats, had there, over the furnace of white heat rising to them, a
ludicrousness that, for one brief spasm of forgetfulness, amused her.

But another shock came, and another, the building rocked, the two
women gasped and swooned, the men had white faces, the Judge reeled
against the parapet. It was unmistakable, Amato watched the finial on
the apex. It described a circle, and it was a larger one than before.
He looked for the balloon. Helen was at his side; he felt her hand on
his shoulder; he pressed his own over it and whispered:

“Oh! we shall get out of this. Are you afraid?”

“Yes—I am, but not terror stricken. There is a difference.”

“I understand you—there is—but some things can’t happen! _You_ cannot
be hurt!”

He turned to her; there was a light in her eyes that shone into
his own, and his heart beat fast, a tide of blood had entered her
face and made it more beautiful. He forgot everything, the danger,
the awful crisis, the place; for an instant, that carried it in an
eternity of bliss, he felt her submission, her peace was in his
hands, and he felt it somehow wrenched from her by fear, and yet not
by any stupefaction of dread; fear, perhaps the strange solemnity of
a portentous predicament, had only broken the chrysalis of silence,
wherein—_he knew it_—lay the formed powers of her love for himself.
The shouts of those around him awoke him; he looked up. The huge
air-ship was close at hand; it passed immediately over the apex of
the building, and from it he could see the dangling ends of ropes,
and affixed to them hooked irons, like anchors. He turned to her
again, she leaned against him, the hand upon his shoulder moved
softly around his neck. He freed himself gently, and took her arms
within his hands; their faces came closer; Amato’s lips moved,
Helen’s ears caught the whisper:

“The beginning of such joy CANNOT end in death! We shall be saved!”

“Look sharp there. Catch that kedge and fix it,” the voice came from
the skies, and on the top of it came another shock or explosion or
both; the building rocked again, to some it seemed that the roof on
which they stood, wavered and reeled, from somewhere a block of stone
slid from its pediment and dropped to the street; Amato heard its
thud on the pavements. But he was only thinking of the orders from
the skies. He seized the swinging ropes. He found their ends weighted
with grappling irons, like the flukes of an anchor; he called to the
men, and Moser and Meldexter came to him from the distant end of the
gallery, and behind them the rest. There were in all five anchors;
these the men fastened as best they could, holding them in place on
the arches of the gallery and calling to Ned to pull on them. Ned
pulled and with a long snapping rip the arches gave way, splitting
the railing of the gallery, exposing lengths of it, from whose naked
edges a few shrunk away, dreading the startling depth they confronted
without the saving intervention of the fence.

The balloon drifted away and the hearts of all sank, while the two
women sobbed and shrieked. It was disconcerting. But a voice came
back, shot from the sepulchral cone of a megaphone:

“Stand to! All right, I’ll be back! Set the anchors inside the
windows.”

The thudding action of the fans were heard, and the gigantic bolide
swam upward, preparing for another descent in one magnificent sweep.
Amato took occasion to look down and measure, if he could, the extent
of the building’s dislocation. The information vouchsafed by this
inspection was terrifying enough.

He could not see the street! There was a bulge in the side of the
building—and he detected a confusion of murmurs coming up to him, as
if from the lower floors, a complication of movements, of breakages,
strains, and slow surrenders of stanchions, girders and pillars, had
set in. The roar of the flames was heard, but the wind, now losing
strength, still kept the flames and smoke driven to the south, yet
the fire was mounting—it must be—sheets of redness blazed momentarily
in the air below him accompanied by low explosions. Amato dreaded the
greater ones, those that announced some falling floor, and almost
betokened the overtaking of stored combustibles or explosives.

There!—a tremendous racking reverberation sounded below, and, after
a pause, another—and distinctly the building moved! Amato caught
hastily at a support, and terror stricken looked up again for the
balloon, while others about him breathed in sighs and gasps. The
balloon was coming in again on a long sweep, and its daring navigator
intended to bring it very near. The other men saw it; there were
eleven all told, including Amato, and the wireless operator. The
windows opening on the gallery, and fortunately at its level had been
kicked in, and they were found to be the openings to a sort of attic.
There were no lights now; the operator, when they reached him after
their flight in the elevator, had kept at his task with only an oil
lamp and a candle to help him to see.

But these were brought round from the south side, relighted in the
garret room, and while five of the men entered this chamber, the
other six awaited the descent of the balloon to catch the ropes and
anchors, pass them to the men inside, who hoped—obediently to Ned
Link’s suggestion—to find some means within of making them fast. Once
moored, the men in the balloon could draw the air-ship down, and the
embarkation of the marooned company from the roof be effected.

All stood waiting. Helen Lorimer had joined the two women, and
her composure—outwardly perfect, though her heart beat fast with
doubt—soothed and helped them. Moser, Meldexter, Amato, the two
reporters, remained on the gallery, the three other men went into
the attic, the elevator boy stood near Amato, ready to assist. The
search of the men inside the attic for means of “tieing up” was
unrewarded. The garret-like space was almost destitute of furniture,
and presented no irons, links, staples, frogs, or even girders, for
holding down the balloon with the anchors and ropes. There were
however, some long timbers, joists, or beams lying on the floor,
as if remnants of unused lumber from the first construction. The
wireless man, coming through a window again to the gallery called out
despondently:

“Nothing here but sticks of wood.”

“How long are the beams?” asked Amato.

“Oh! twelve feet perhaps.”

“Well, tie the ropes to them, put them across the windows and let
them purchase on the walls, across the opening of the windows.”

“Well said—the very thing. Look out! here she comes! Catch hold. For
God’s sake don’t lose her,” and the wireless tumbles again back into
the attic.

The balloon was over them, Ned, backing her down with great success.
The ropes were eagerly seized—and held; Amato saw Link peering from
the “pilot,” and the crew’s heads over the sides of the compartment’s
deck. As quickly as he could Moser explained the scheme of fastening
inside the garret, while the ropes and anchors were flung inside and
the ropes paid out for length. They were taken in at three windows,
two at two windows, one at the third. The tieing up inside was a
matter simply of winding them round the beams, and driving the
anchors—so well as the men could—into the wood. Link kept shouting
orders—and said—he’d break off the ends of the ropes on the balloon
when he rose, and leave the anchors in the doomed edifice—just then
he was holding everything taut.

Doomed indeed the fabric, they were on, was. Its increasing danger
was frightful; the whole stupendous mass was leaning! Its interior
had not proven fireproof in any sense. The materials of its
construction were for the most part combustible, being wood encased
in stone walls, and held up by an iron framework. Few of its floors
were of brick—it had been a flagrant example either of misconception,
or carelessness, or of some shameless corruption. Many of the rooms
were sumptuously furnished, many sealed in splendid oaken walls,
and into this labyrinth of ignitable stuffs, leagued with hundreds
of square feet of thickly painted, or oil encrusted walls, the fire
made its way with exulting ease. Nothing could withstand its horrible
violence, the immensity of its ruthless power—only its possible
suffocation in the riotous profusion of things combustible—through
lack of air to feed its own ravenous vitality. And that could not be.

The men finished their work in the attic and came out through the
windows to the group, now all gathered together watching the slowly
lowering balloon. The ropes and anchors held, and Meldexter led a
feeble cheer as the hope of escape brightened. Ned was hauling on
the cables, and so far nothing yielded; only one thing kept Amato in
suspense, the recurrent jolts of the building, as it seemed to sink
or fall together—he hardly knew which it was. With each jolt the
balloon swayed, feeling the drag on the ropes. If they only held! The
crew was cautious, Link understood the danger; all waited, and ever
and anon as Amato’s ears strained to note the reactions at work in
the building, he heard the firing of the engaged forces around the
City Hall, though he thought its early vigor had waned.

“Hold fast. Lower the crib,” it was Ned’s voice. The creak of the
windlass on the balloon’s deck ceased, and over the railing of the
compartment descended a basket large enough for one person.

“Ladies first” called Moser, as all crowded towards the welcome
vehicle of safety.

One of the two women was lifted in the basket, and it was slowly
hoisted, disappearing above their heads or just seen as a swinging
spot until it came to the balloon. The balloon was kept floating at
about fifty feet above them; Ned did not dare to approach nearer.
How long the wait seemed to Amato before the little vessel again
swung into their hands, and the second woman was launched upward to
life and the world again! It was slow business, and the explosions
increased, and the heavy structure swayed. Was there time for all?
Amato glanced about him, the light from the balloon’s lamps made it
possible for him to finally see the pale faces of the men; he saw
too that Helen Lorimer had crossed to him, and was at his side. She
had refused the first proffer of the basket, and said she would go
last. It was now her turn; a second time the basket returned, and all
held tight. Moser and Meldexter spoke together:

“Miss Lorimer, we are ready.” The smoke was coming up to them, and
even spits of fire and a shower of sparks at intervals burst around
them; these Ned feared. His voice sounded from the pilot:

“Look alive. Let the men come double.” Miss Lorimer did not stir;
But no one was kept waiting, she spoke very clearly “I will go with
Amato!”

Amato felt his heart stand still—No—Madness—She must go. He too spoke
clearly:

“Helen, for God’s sake don’t wait! We will all come. But now—delay is
fatal—take the chair.”

“Come there” it was Ned Link’s voice, “This is no time for kidding.”

Amato seized Miss Lorimer’s hand; what had occurred flashed upon
him as her Acceptance. Strange to be introduced to one’s perpetual
happiness on the edge of the chance of Death! He helped her to the
chair, and it went upward as a fresh uprush of sparks and blinding
smoke issued even from their feet. That was an awful instant, but to
Amato its terror faded and—as though he trod along some earthly vale
of flowers in the very midst of applauding friends, with his soul’s
desires satisfied—he forgot its very existence; he dreamed:

“Rudolph, I would rather wait. But I will go. Be sure to follow.”

The whisper was in his ears, as the basket rose for the third time.

No one spoke; the men were by themselves; there was a possibility of
panic; Moser felt it, so did Meldexter: they drew together, there
had been a sort of scuffle between the reporters, and the court
stenographer had showed a suspicious excitement. The Judge had
intervened in the quarrel between the reporters, and pointed out
the danger of any folly; there was but one way out of the crisis; if
they lost their heads, they lost their lives. Stilled for a moment,
the group watched for the return of the basket, and it came back
quickly. As it approached the two scufflers rushed past Amato—they
were on his side—and seized the cradle; they were white with terror,
and at its side they struck out, the taller pushing the smaller man
off, as he fought with Moser and Meldexter to keep possession of the
basket. Amato had been almost hurled from the gallery through one of
the open spaces made by the previous tearing off of the gallery wall.
His recovery was just quick enough, and the next moment, discovering
the predicament this loss of self-control placed them in, he dashed
upon the tall combatant, seized him by the coat collar, and flung him
off the cradle. He stumbled upon the smaller man, and the three fell
together in a heap upon the gallery space. Amato was on his feet in a
trice, and as the other two rose, he knocked them both flat again on
their backs. The lesson was deserved; it was remembered as well.

During this incipient brawl Moser and Meldexter got the Judge into
the chair, which again swung off, and was pulled up. After that there
were no more interruptions. Discipline did its perfect work, but
there was good reason for speed. The smoke was increasing, and the
rain of sparks, not unmixed with heavier elements, fell about them.
In the next load the two reporters and operator were placed, as the
other men feared for their collapse. It was now actually getting hot,
and Ned, watching everything, was shouting for greater haste. The
huge inflated bag veered and tossed and strained on the ropes, and
the imminent danger of its freeing itself grew more alarming. Again
the basket descended, and this time Ned ordered that two men and the
boy get in:

“Get in sitting on each others’ lap, and hold fast the guide rope;”
thundered the impatient Ned. It was done and the three arrived in
safety upon the balloon deck. Once more the cradle shot down, and
there were now only the stenographer and Amato left. They would
ascend together. When the women were sent aloft some little pains had
been taken to insure against the possibility of their falling out, by
fastening them in with binding cords, and this had consumed a little
more time than was quite consistent with the urgency of the rescue.
The men had abandoned this precaution. Amato being the larger man
told the court attendant he would seat himself in the basket first,
and then the stenographer should place himself in his lap; both were
feeling the effects of the stifling smoke now shooting up from the
crevices on the roof in spiral volleys. Suddenly as both had seemed
ready for the lift, and had shouted to the men above to draw them
up, the building lurched, its great summit slipped away from their
feet, and thus, slowly sliding over, was dragging the balloon with
it, while the basket caught at an inclined angle against the gallery
railing, was tipped, and spilled its human contents out upon the
gallery space. Amato felt the heat of the copper surface, and he
heard as well, the command shouted from the balloon:

“Let go all! Rise!”

The basket was still there and it was attached to the air-ship. As
the anchoring ropes dropped, the basket rose following the upward
surge of the balloon. There was one chance for both. The stenographer
cowered at the feet of Amato screaming in a mania of terror. Amato
seized the basket, coiled the loose suspension rope about his arm
and wrist; there was some hesitancy in the motion of the balloon;
it settled back again, and this gave time; the rattle of the ropes
as they slammed around him, thrown off from the deck, whereon all
he loved was now deserting him, aroused him to an insane strength.
He could not leave the young man; he felt his arm and wrist would
stand the tug of that suspension and lifting; could it stand a double
weight?

“Throw your arms about my neck. Now clutch hard. I’ll hold you.”

Amato caught the hanging rope above his chanceried wrist, and thus
in mid-air the two swung above the crashing mass of iron, from
whose crumbling stories rose flame and smoke, as if, with one wild
onset of demoniacal fury, the penned legions of fire intended to
break up through the very apex of the proud edifice, crowning their
destructive work with a last towering splendor of flames, that would
assault the stars. So tremendous and amazing was the spectacle that
the men on the platform of the balloon, for one inappreciable but
blood-curdling moment, forgot their work. Amato with the stenographer
on his back hung unrelieved above the maelstrom of fire. Then came
the pull. Ned was raging with oaths at their slowness. But the
men were wiser than their captain. It required an even, steady,
unaccelerated pull, to hold the strange load; Amato felt the
wrench, as his shoulder, and ligament and muscle yielded; the pain
darted through him with excruciating force; if only he could keep
consciousness! they would yet be safe and—Helen!—Helen—!!

“Good God save him! save him!” thus Helen Lorimer cried and prayed.
All were watching the appalling sight—not of the fire but this
struggle there in the air-spaces, spaces made hot with the dust of
sparks—Helen alone did not watch, but smote her breast and plead
with suffocated sobs, upon her knees, her eyes dried in the agony of
suspense, her lips scorched and pallid with a heart-tortured fear,
her body bowed in the convulsion of her supreme petition for the life
of the man she _now_ loved, with an ecstacy of pride, and homage, and
wonder, and obedience.

AND THE HEAVENS HEARD. The head of the unconscious Rudolph Amato lay
in the lap of Helen Lorimer on the floor of the soaring balloon,
pitching and rolling in the billows of the ascending heat; her hands
soothed his temples, and pressed to his lips the restoratives that
slowly brought back color to his handsome face, and awoke the smiles
of recognition in his gracious and loving eyes.

Ned had not separated his inflammable gas-bag a moment too soon. As
Amato had been drawn “aboard” belching clouds of winged sparks, like
innumerable swarms of illuminated bugs, rose about the balloon; parts
of the riggings caught afire, and were extinguished by the crew,
and a stinging smoke followed. For the tremendous ranged pile of
floors that had made up the great Municipal Building—a landmark in
the wonderful city—had begun to double up, tumbling to the ground,
ensheathed in volumes of flames and blinding fire-motes; a roar like
the faraway beating of the shores by waves proceeded from it. Its
pyramidal top, with the gallery, fenced with stone, where the rescued
group had only just been standing, had bent curiously to the south—it
was this rising of the north edge that had kept the balloon’s basket
a little longer under the control of Amato—and the bowed head of the
giant, dropped stones and disrupted ornaments from its crest upon the
smashing pavements of the square south of it, some shooting outward
to the platforms of the elevated railroad and the causeway approach
to the East River Bridge. That great conduit of travel had been
deserted; Brooklyn at the beginning of the fight had retreated, or
the home swarming multitudes turned elsewhere for a safer transit.

Still the balloon drew off, mounting higher and higher. The blazing
furnace shone like a colossal wand of light, its thunderous sounds of
combustion died away; the last noises of the combat, yet unfinished
about the City Hall, were scarcely distinguishable as Link pushed the
aerial monster on, and, as it countered the upper air-currents above
the mammoth metropolis, beaming in its nocturnal dress of electric
aureoles, no note of the warfare there reached them. But lights
along the East River side of the city showed where Putkof was busy,
and, following Miss Lorimer’s request that they should all repair to
Carrara, Link directed his aerial car towards that famous section.

Congratulations were fervent enough among the adventurers in this
strange twenty-first century rescue, and the disorders that had
threatened the safety of all were forgotten. Amato had become the
hero, and the two men who had felt the force of his fists, seemed
willing to acknowledge his merit, soothing their discomfiture with
the thought that only his presence of mind and quick action prevented
a dangerous panic. The poor stenographer hung over Amato in the cabin
of the balloon, deck, where he lay nursed by Helen Lorimer. He had
very soon recovered consciousness but movement was painful, because
of the torn ligaments of his shoulders.

The cabin of the air-ship, of that remote day, to whose triumphs over
the powers of the air, the indications of to-day so certainly point,
was a small room on the deck of the balloon’s compartment, which was
suspended from the balloon, and formed the accommodation for the
passengers. In this space fitted with divans, its windows prettily
curtained, and holding a few chairs, a table, and some convenient
mirrors, serviceable, for their own vain inspection, to the merry
companies that made such frequent use of the Zeppelins of the city,
in the pleasant days. In this room Helen Lorimer sat by the side of
Amato, and watched him with a strange pride and pleasure.

Helen Lorimer’s nature was utterly lacking in coquetry, and she
could no more have concealed her feelings, her quick interior
acknowledgement of submission to this man, who had so modestly
insisted upon recognition, and at this minute had stirred all her
feminine reverence for courage, than she could have stultified
herself at any instant in her life, by self-deception. Her frank and
almost masculine candor prevented that, and her elevation of mind and
position made the customary deceits of courtship unnecessary. She
knew that this man loved her, and she frankly conceded her acceptance
of his love at the exact moment when her own met and embraced it.

“It was an experience—something of a night-mare—” whispered Amato
from his little couch as the balloon swam into the purer air; “and
how awful it seemed to see you there, and all the possibilities—Ah—!”
the pain made him groan a little, and he relapsed into an idolatrous
gaze at the lovely woman who bent over him, adjusting with such
gentleness the cushions and the wrappings about him.

“Night-mares are temporary expedients, fortunately, for the great
purpose of letting us awake into the joy of living, and—there!—is
that more comfortable?”

Amato almost laughed; “You did not finish that almost sublime bit of
wisdom—of course you meant to say—at least I DO—living and LOVING—now
did you not?” and he caught her hand and brought it to his lips.

“Under the circumstances,” and Helen Lorimer laughed almost as
delightedly, though the color shot into her cheeks, and the faintest
surprise stole from her eyes; “some indulgence is to be expected by
you, bold Man, but you must not be as indiscreet in your thoughts
as you are in your actions”—yet she did not withdraw her hand, just
apparently leaned a little closer to him, so that its retention was
more easily secured by the irresponsible party, who even tried to
lift the hand of his lame arm to affirm its complete possession—and
which brought on a sharp twinge of agony, and a few muttered
ejaculations of disgust.

“Be careful. You may be even more useful when we reach the earth,
than you have been in the skies. A little patience and your injury
will be quite well, and then, perhaps some great risks—I feel grave
over this revolt. What madness!—that shameless Longtinsky!”

“Risks,” he smiled delightedly. “The greatest risk I could run,
_you_, have turned to success—Isn’t it so?—Helen?”

“You mean a—risk—with me? Well I cannot say—but I will be kind to you
in your helplessness just now—I may retract later—you know women have
privileges—you probably ran no risk. It was your own modesty that
made it seem one——”

She disengaged her hand, and stood up beside him—his face shadowed a
little, but the cloud passed and he almost cried out:

“If you invite me to boldness Miss Lorimer, I fear I may excel in
that—it is an easier role to play!”

“Yes?—but you remember the old adage—the adventurer who passed from
room to room and met the last door, with the inscription ‘Be not too
Bold.’”

She retreated a little, and bent towards him with her hands pressed
upon her knees and a mock dissembling frown was allowed to add its
charm to her beauty—a fact so overwhelmingly appreciated by the young
man that he groaned at his impotence and then—without any attempted
exculpation for his ingratitude—complained that his nurse neglected
his comfort, that he needed more coverings—that his shoulder should
be lifted higher, that he must sit up, that he was hungry—thirsty—in
short that nursing, considered as an art of “absent treatment” was
insufferable torture, and that Miss Lorimer MUST come nearer to him,
and minister to his immediate necessities.

But that amiable lady remained at a safe distance, assuring him
that her care for him was directed by the most approved wisdom—that
excitement was absolutely fatal to convalescence—that he as a
patient was not cold, nor hungry, nor thirsty, but—if he indeed
did wish to sit up, and would ask for assistance to that end, less
offensively she, as his constituted nurse, might be willing to
offer him the requisite help. The patient did not remonstrate at
this view of his condition, nor did he palliate the resentment of
his nurse by assenting to her pacific proposal. He simply rolled and
emitted a qualified form of howling—it might have been so called
in the parlance of Scripture—and at any rate was sufficiently and
intentionally subdued to such a point that it could not have been
audible to the company on the outside, whose sympathy was, at the
moment, curiously ignored.

The effect of this uncontrolled suffering was immediate. It brought
the apparently subservient nurse to the side of the wailing victim of
circumstances—and, that her presence there was instantly consoling
might have been inferred from the cessation of that unfortunate
being’s distress—and, through some less conceivable reason than
trained service as a nurse, it brought about a train of results not
assumed necessary in ordinary hospital practice. As the young woman
leaned over her subject his healthy arm became engaged somewhere
in the vicinity of her neck—there was room for some confusion
of mind in this operation as she appeared to assist him in the
process—and then succeeded a slight cervical intermixture—_so to
speak_—it almost seemed as if the nurse lifted him into an upright
position, but there was a slight relaxation, and the patient
was finally left conveniently propped up on cushions, with a
singularly animated expression of complete relief, on his face,
while the nurse—recognizing the urgency of the situation—sat near
him with an appropriately pleased manner and some unmistakably—and
improving—flushes of color upon her cheeks.

The company leaning on the taffrail of the compartment deck of the
balloon, wondering at the marvellous prospect of the outspread city,
thrilled with a delight that kept them silent over their wonderful
escape, scarcely noticed the absence of Amato and Helen Lorimer,
having perhaps some correct intuitions as to their mutual happiness.
Onward in the illimitable lake of air the huge aerial leviathan made
its way; the city below it was indistinguishable now in its details
and became a punctured surface wherein dots of light ran into streams
of sinuous gleamings while ever and anon a spoke of light emitted
by some tremendous electric arc, shot up like a veritable pillar.
The city was delimited by the vaporous splendor of the overspreading
aurora of light, that strange wonder that had awakened Tom Ratler’s
first amazement, and far away over Long Island the reverberations of
light shimmered on the skies.

“Rather a narrow escape, your Honor,” said Meldexter, “I wonder what
became of the Jury—and the Case?”

The Judge laughed. “Yes, it is a trying predicament as a legal
precedent—a Jury liberated perhaps from mortal confinement by the
victims of its own deliberations could hardly be trusted if their
convictions were against the prisoners; they would be less or more
than human if they did not give them the benefit of a change of mind,
after they got out. There was probably no verdict reached and the
Case is—as if—it had not been.”

“Of course the Jury must have escaped,” said Mr. Moser “Perhaps—the
prisoners too.”

“The Case has undergone some enlargement since the morning,” it was
one of the reporters who was speaking, “any guilt on the part of
Longtinsky, Polinck & Co., has evolved into a tragic immensity that
means treason to the State.”

“LOOK, LOOK!” the cry came from the opposite side of the deck. The
men hurried round to the place whence the call had been heard, and
there were the two women, the stenographer, the wireless operator,
with the men of the balloon’s crew, pointing below them to a
conflagration which flickered and smoked, rose and fell. The balloon
had been rapidly falling. Ned Link intended to land somewhere in the
flat space near the intersection of the present Speed way, Dyckman
Street, and the Kingsbridge road. The anchors were no longer on
board, and his descent was made more precarious, and necessitated a
drop upon some even ground. This space was convenient, having been
made into a grass land, holding lawn tennis courts, and croquet
fields. But as he drove downward at 190th street, what was his
astonishment when he saw that the region was swarming with men, and
that the torch of the incendiary had fired the buildings of the
public park upon the summit of St. George.

It was this spectacle that startled the sightseers on the balloon.
But the balloon itself had not escaped detection from the earth,
and as it approached, a sizzling bomb shot up towards it to explode
harmlessly some hundred feet below it. The warning was however heeded
and again Ned raised his apparatus, and soared in circles like a
watching dragon, confusing and alarming his assailants beneath; they
evidently feared his maneuvers, for now a sudden volley of missiles
ascended, being for the most part pyrotechnical demonstrations, in
the shape of sky-rockets. The little party of aeronauts were visibly
impressed. It certainly resembled—this hostile gathering, there in
the northern section of the city—some prearranged movement, and might
as well as not have a direct connection with the outbreak at the City
Hall.

The balloon whirled upward again and Ned examined the conditions
around the opposite hill of Carrara. Here all saw lights moving
fitfully; confused murmurs reached them, and a sense of some sort
of trepidation in the inmates of the tiered palaces came to them.
Helen Lorimer had left for an instant her charge, after a confirmed
assurance from him that he was altogether comfortable—at least for a
few minutes, though his condition might, after the expiration of a
short interval, assume desperate extremes—and was gazing too, with
the rest, at the alarming symptoms of disorder in the city, with the
Carrara homes as the immediate object of its attack.

The suddenness of the assault upon the Municipal Building had been
astonishing, and its success unexpected; it was evident that the
City was unguarded. Moser and Meldexter both recalled the fact of
the absence of the Militia; the fighting around the City Hall showed
the perilous state of affairs, and now they realized that, more
significantly yet, a dangerous attempt to ruin the famous Carrara
Ridge was in progress.

The more it was thought over the clearer it became; the city was in
the throes of a convulsion. Helen Lorimer was agonized at the thought
of the danger to her Mother, and of her home. What was to be done?
She turned a questioning glance up to Ned Link in the pilot above
her, and he saw her wistful face, pale and anxious in the light of
the lamps, which had been again reillumined, after a short eclipse,
when in the neighborhood of the skyrocketing rioters at St. George’s.

“Well!” he answered exactly her mute interrogation, “we’ll sail
down the Kingsbridge road a way, and I’ll let you out near Depot
Lane—_perhaps_, if you care to chance it—you can get to the Carrara
by the back way along the Lafayette Boulevard. I don’t think it’s
very safe. I might get you somewhere else further away, but the ship
has got to light soon; there’s something wrong—she’s scorched a hole
or something—Hello—they’re off—” and he pointed to the east.

On the hillside where the broad street sweeps now from the brewery
at Fort Mendel, to the Inwood valley, a moving train of lights
was observed. There were not many and they came and went as if
extinguished and again relighted, but they allowed the watchers dimly
to discern descending a mass which could only be interpreted as the
invading army of the mob. It was indeed Bomber’s force, timing his
attack with the assault upon the City Hall. He was a little late; the
fight in the City Hall was then waning with the rioters discomfited.
Almost as the same instant red globes of fire ascended from the
ridge in Carrara, like a silent yet luminous challenge—but that
was not their meaning—they were the signals of distress, they were
meant to call assistance. The party on the balloon understood them
also; all there was again uncertainty and irresolution. Ned followed
his own head. He determined to alight near Depot Lane and let them
out; the balloon was becoming unmanageable. Suddenly it lurched and
slipped through the air sideways, pitching the groups and the crew
almost off their feet and righting again with a shock that knocked
Amato from his cushions. Helen was at his side—how it delighted him
to notice her alacrity—and as she helped him tenderly to regain his
position, she told him of the discovery that an attack was about to
take place at Carrara. It furnished him some food for thought.

Link kept driving the balloon, shouting to the crew to shift ballast
from time to time, and telling all to make ready to “GET OUT.” When
the critical state of the region was first noticed, the Judge and
the lawyers thought it would be best to go elsewhere, fearing the
danger of alighting in the midst of the social hornet’s-nest, which
was buzzing so obstreperously. But this council was intolerable to
Helen Lorimer, and to Amato as well—injured though he was. Argument
had progressed somewhat on these points when Ned Link finished all
indecision by assuring them he must descend; he thought he could
“fetch ’em a little east of the Arrowhead inn”. Thither their course
was now directed. But they never reached it. The balloon either
exhausted by its strenuous experiences, and perhaps, too, somewhat
overcome and distrait with the reckless and unusual proceedings in
the great city, even it may be surmised, suffering from a distracted
sense of responsibility with its official and distinguished
burden—or, it may be from some more prosaic cause, as a _hole_ in its
sides, began visibly to fall, still however sliding forward at lower
and lower levels. Ned saw its inevitable collapse, and fearing to
come down obliquely on the roof tops attempted to steer it into the
highway of the Kingsbridge Boulevard. With his fans alternately set
forward or reversed he managed to keep it, though rolling, (which
motion played havoc with the gravity of its occupants and forced
Helen to remain close to her charge assisted by Mr. Moser, the latter
to Amato’s personal weariness) in the roadway, when seized by some
last whim of desperation it rose, swerved, and—so to speak—dashed
its brains out against a chance stone structure, whose convenient
eminence caught its fancy, and went to pieces.

A violent surge upward, a tilt that sent men and women sprawling,
mingled with the irresolute and shifting furniture of the deck, a
terrific bump, magnified, past all conception, beyond ordinary bumps,
and then tearings, crashings, splinterings, and cries. That was the
end of it. The contributions of cries came more from the streets than
from the balloon. The streets were full of people: commotion and
excitement reigned everywhere, and this new portent dropping from the
air gave an additional and pleasurable impetus to the emotions of the
miscellaneous crowds; another consternation for their jumping nerves.

Ned had leaped from the pilot, getting down to the compartment by a
rope ladder, and ordered everyone to retire to the cabin. It was more
stoutly made than the deck, and the balloon pitching heavily would
probably split, and the extensions of the deck would act as guards.
He had turned off all the power, put out the lights, and shot a hole
in the gas-bag. Then he let things take their course, prepared for
the last bump, and hoped to escape injury in the enclosed cabin.

The bump came, and with it a long hoarse wail, beginning with a queer
murmurous hiss, and rising past the wail to a stertorous scream; it
was the escaping gas, and the ripping sides of the gas-vault. The
balloon shrank into a depleted cavernous bunch of waste, and this
abrupt disappearance of the last remnant of buoyancy saved them all.
They were all about flat on their backs, and the thud came just and
disarranged them a little more, and really no one the worse for it.
And after the thud came stagnation, and silence. It was again a
humorous situation, and both Helen and Amato thought so.

Not a sound had been uttered, even the two despairing women and
the two belligerent reporters had been shocked into voiceless
consternation. The distinguished Judge and the celebrated solicitors,
the famous Helen Lorimer, reduced by danger—as she herself felt—to
the ignominious level of clutching a more or less perpendicular
floor, and waiting for eventualities, had certainly a comic interest.
And when they at last settled down with the last jounce and no one
seemed hurt, though, by reason of the mechanics of an inclined plane,
they suffered from a little heterogeneous mixture, and Amato was in
greater pain, both these happy souls laughed. In the darkness that
laugh restored confidence and it was followed by Helen Lorimer’s
voice:

“Well we can be quite sure that we have arrived; that was an
unmistakable thump, but doesn’t it feel good to be here? Mother Earth
has an unforgettable reality about her. Mr. Link, we owe our lives to
you, and now if we can get out into the streets, and see about us,
all will be right with us I know. As a sky-pilot, Mr. Link, you are
a better success than many who boast of their prerogatives in that
respect.”

This alertness and cheerfulness put all in good humor, and relieved
the anxiety. There was really not much reason for alarm; the
underpinning of the deck had been crushed out of all recognition,
but its crumpling up had made the impact of the compartment less
severe, and with the exception of the few bruises caught in their
miscellaneous contact with the walls of the cabin, and with each
other, the party was altogether unhurt.

They found themselves at last on the earth again and proceeded with
all possible haste to extricate themselves from the wreck. Willing
and numerous hands helped them; they had fallen at about the junction
of the old Kingsbridge road and Amsterdam Avenue. They were not so
far away from Carrara. After they had “found themselves,” so to
speak, Ned Link telephoned for the wrecking forces of the Zeppelin
to which he belonged, to clear the wreckage; Mr. Moser offered his
assistance to Amato and Miss Lorimer; the Judge, the two reporters,
the two women, the stenographer, the wireless operator, and the
elevator boy scattered, with more or less haste, to their several
homes, after hurried congratulations. Moser, Helen, Amato repaired
to a druggist’s where Amato was made more comfortable, and then Mr.
Moser looked about for an auto to convey them to Miss Lorimer’s home.
Already that home and its bordering habitations were beleaguered by
the storming rioters, led by Bomber.

CHAPTER XVI

THE MAYOR’S DEATH


When the Mayor ordered out the 69th and 22d regiments, the sole
remnants of the large militia forces of the city, he enjoined their
commands to protect the properties at Carrara. At the same time,
even as his messages flew to their destinations, Bomber was awaiting
the influx of the men intended to effect its destruction. He had
made his headquarters in the Park on St. George’s Hill, and he was
not disappointed in the rapid concentration of an armed multitude.
They came from the east, approaching their improvised _rendez-vous_
over the Washington Bridge, and some moved southward from the north,
reaching their position, over the hills of Fordham by the Mosholu
Driveway, tramping in files or companies, all the way from the low
lands of West Farms. The riot at the Municipal Building and the City
Hall began at night-fall—about six o’clock,—and had been thwarted and
defeated somewhere between nine and ten. The refugees whose perils we
have recounted, had escaped to the roof of the Municipal Building at
six, had been taken off about eight-thirty, and had returned to the
north-central part of the city where this story has just left them,
seeking safety and refuge at—it may be stated—nine, for the dial
of the clock has some reference to the remaining incidents of this
narrative.

The Mayor, after the repulse of the mob, had sent in a call for the
ambulances, and essayed himself to help the wounded on both sides.
It was a sorrowful and bitter sight. This, he thought, had at last
been the outcome of the years of increasing class hatred. His heart
bled for it; the fame of the noble city vanished, grown so beautiful
and crowded too with manifold means of mercy! Was it just; was it
right; was he to blame! The accusation smote him with an insufferable
agony. He had worked hard to heal these social wounds, and the pride,
the joy, alleviating his forlorn sense of desertion, had been an
exulting realization that he had done so in these last months of
tireless effort. And now—this pitiful cheerless tragic sequel! Tears
started to his eyes, and sobs almost broke from his lips. He had
loved New York so well and all its people. Was everything he loved
to disappoint him?—and then the thought of the endangered Carrara
flashed through his mind. He stood up, rising from the task of
helping a poor wretch into an ambulance, with the young doctor that
accompanied it, and saw his secretary Mr. Briggs following him.

He turned towards him almost with a motion of anger; this man had
perhaps steeled his heart, blinded his sympathies, made him ruthless
in the use of power! He knew his worth but it was a worth caked into
a rigidity of cruel formalism by the prejudices of industrial needs,
the set phrases of an apprehensive Capitalism. Personally he—the
Mayor—had no faith, as faiths are counted, but he worshipped the
ideal of a social Brotherhood—whatever that might be called. Perhaps
he had allowed this man to obliterate this picture too easily with
his incessant lectures, his frigidism, his secularity. He let his
resentment flame out:

“Perhaps this Sir is your work—this slaughter—this fratricide. This
is the outcome of the discord you love to foment. Do you like it?”

He advanced towards him almost with a menace in his voice and action.

Mr. Briggs stopped suddenly and looked at the Mayor, just long enough
to let the shadow of something like contemptuous pity steal into his
eyes and then quietly answered:

“No Sir, it is not my work—nor YOURS; I have just put Tom Ratler in
an ambulance and sent him to the hospital. I found him unconscious
lying over there against the railing of the park.”

The Mayor was sincerely fond of Tom, and this information startled
him out of all his brooding misgivings; over the field the doctors
and nurses were working, lamps moved from place to place, groans
too issued from many quarters; the police from the Hall covered the
ground, and the outskirts of the park swarmed with crowds as the
police pushed backward the sullen or the indifferent or the curious.
The rioters had apparently vanished. Briggs told the Mayor he had
sent to the same hospital and in the same ambulance Tom’s bitter
enemy, Poniatowsky and with them had gone Jessie Longtinsky, though
he added:

“Her predicament is painful, they are both her lovers; she fears one
and loves the other; I need not say which is which,” and the speaker
looked oddly at the Mayor and smiled.

“How did it happen; was Tom in the fight?” asked the Mayor.

“Well, I think he _was_, and he was fighting his dearest foe—that
Poniatowsky—they are both near the end of things.”

Pain came into the Mayor’s face, and some reminiscent revery held
him for an instant. He looked over the workers gradually finishing
the gruesome duty of getting the dead together, and despatching the
wounded away to the hospitals. He turned again to Mr. Briggs:

“See that every one here—striker—” the word brought a sigh from his
lips—were these the men he had repulsed?—“Ah—rioter and policeman
alike are attended to. I shall take the machine and go north—to
Carrara—”

Mr. Briggs was inclined to resist this determination at first, but he
understood the Mayor’s feelings too well, and he really feared his
outbursts if he provoked them; they could be unpleasantly vindictive
and ugly; he would let nothing separate him from the Mayor. The man
had been his best friend and he almost adored certain qualities in
the Mayor, though he cherished a contempt for his dreams of social
rehabilitation.

“Everything shall be attended to as you wish. But Sir if I might be
permitted to offer one word of advice; it seems to me——”

“Not another word Briggs; the worst outbreak will be at Carrara—I
know it. The very sight of those white palaces will make the blood
boil in the veins of these unhappy, these misguided men. They will
feel there as if they were about to throttle their enemy, kill him in
his lair. If I can I will prevent it—whatever happens——”

He waved his hand and walked rapidly away; Briggs watched his
disappearing form sadly. He was not a man of psychic sensibility but
somehow he felt that a gulf of final separation was opening between
him and the Mayor, and in that gulf lay the prostrate body of his
master. A few moments later the Mayor shot from the City Hall park
moving through a lane made for him by the police. His vehicle passed
up Broadway which was streaming with crowds, restlessly surging
towards and from the City Hall. Except for the trolley cars that
occupied it, as they do to-day, there were no other carriages than
the Mayor’s and it swiftly sped on its way.

A few recognized the Mayor’s automobile, and a shout or some hurled
defiance, a cheer, a query, or simply the amazed gaze of the
spectators greeted him. The Mayor was buried in his own excited
thoughts. He felt the critical terror of this day, and he revolted
with his whole soul against the needlessness, the awful consequences
of it. It was not simply the pain of sympathy and bereavement, but
the more disturbing dread of a disordered state, with civil unrest
and embittered irreconcilable social elements, whose enmities would
corrode the foundations of government and infiltrate all the veins
and circulations of political life.

Sunk in the pillows of the Limousine he pondered over the action he
should now take. He was not unacquainted with previous threats made
against Carrara and he understood the hideous rage of the Longtinsky
people. That man’s hatred of Helen Lorimer possessed him like a
consuming fever. And now her home was to be destroyed, if it were
possible, and she—his heart almost stopped at the thought—herself
was in danger. And while the Mayor glided northward on the spinning
wheels of his noiseless carriage, at intervals bruising the silence
with its clamorous honk, and always sweeping the ground before it
with two broad brushes of light; while Helen Lorimer and Amato and
Mr. Moser were searching for a conveyance to the same destination as
the Mayor’s; while the militiamen were hurrying to their armories
expecting a sanguinary struggle at the same point, Bomber had
anticipated these contrasted forces of opposition, and—though much
later than he hoped—had moved down the Dyckman road and already was
filing in three columns through Nagle, Sherman and Post Avenues to
the marble terraces of Carrara, aiming especially to capture the home
and mistress of the Lorimer villa.

Bomber was a man after Longtinsky’s own heart, and he had moulded
him to his purposes, inflaming a bitter and criminal nature with
the promise of gain, and filling his ears and mind with the murmurs
of envy. An inordinate conceit, a feeling of injury, that he was
the victim of a false social system, and a heart grown ruthless
from brooding over imaginary wrongs, made him a fit instrument to
accomplish Longtinsky’s designs. Bomber was the child of an Irish
mother and an American father, and he inherited the verbal fluency
of the first, with the undaunted self-confidence of the latter, and
he had wandered far from the moral fortitude of both. His vanity,
and the smoothness of his tongue had hurried him into foolish
enterprises, and credulity and lack of discretion had invariably
brought about his ruin. These disappointments had vexed his
self-esteem and soured him. He had not been saved the consequences of
an admired physical charm, and indulgences had perverted still more
the easy rules of his life. Yet the strangest circumstance of this
man’s life remains untold. HE TOO LOVED HELEN LORIMER.

At the death of his parents, Bomber inherited enough money to be able
to live much as he wished. He had received a fairly good education,
an education which might have been improved, if he had himself helped
more the opportunities accompanying it. The city’s pleasures he
understood, and his skill in talking, his dalliance with the brighter
novelties of living, had given him a sort of vogue in a fast set of
young men, among whom, occasionally, he met some belonging to the
acquaintances of the beautiful Helen Lorimer. This was of course
some years before. Bomber had often seen the famous lady at public
places of entertainment, the theatres, the opera, at concerts—Bomber
had some skill himself in playing, and much musical taste—and he had
actually spoken with her at her Settlement House, when he endeavored
to put himself in as good a light as possible, and thought that he
had succeeded. Contact with women had bred in him an infatuation, a
very great sense of his conquering personal charm. He felt pretty
sure that Helen Lorimer must have noted it too. At the time he stood
well, and the sharp reverses that later left him penniless had not
yet developed. He had just then no use for socialism and rather
sported his aristocratic prejudices.

He undertook to give the “young Portia of the city,” as he called
Miss Lorimer, advice in some well written letters, upon her various
projects, and assiduously set himself about getting into her graces
by an apparent interest in the poor—an hypocritical affectation
then. The letters had been answered by Miss Lorimer’s secretary
and a little curtly. Then he made a mistake, a most ill-considered
blunder. He told her in a letter, full of ridiculous tropes and
foolish imagery—a letter such as self-deluded men often write when
fate drives them to their ruin—that he loved her. The letter reached
Helen Lorimer. She blushed, laughed and carefully burned it up.
Bomber waited for an answer. So intolerable was his conceit that he
really deceived himself into believing that Miss Lorimer’s silence
was to be interpreted as a favorable sign of her favor, and—making
“his will lord of his reason”—wrote her another fatuous epistle. This
letter Miss Lorimer did not see; it fell into the hands of her uncle,
who, opening it by a mistake and catching its contents fell into a
tremendous rage, and in an answer told the incorrigible Bomber that
he was a jackass, that his effrontery deserved public exposure, that
Miss Lorimer was insulted by any association with his name.

Bomber writhed under this repulse, and then his fortune failed, his
plans went all wrong, his money vanished, the better sort of his
companions deserted him, and he fell from round to round in the
scale of decency, and he turned with an almost impious hatred upon
all he had once admired. Bitten by jealousy, his heart poisoned by
failure, his stupid pride affronted, almost to others a spectacle of
indigence, the ulcer of discontent wore into his soul and he handed
himself over to the forces of annihilation. He made a living by
conceiving vitriolic attacks upon property, upon reputations, and he
avenged himself for his repulse by insinuations, false, cruel and
repulsive, on the fair name of Helen Lorimer.

The man became furiously bestial. His strength, his youth,
carried him through trials of dissipation, that less stalwart or
inured natures would have crumbled or decayed under. The fever
of denunciation grew on him; Longtinsky’s plan, mad, impossible,
delirious, suited him precisely. He rushed into it and helped its
author to more completely arrange and perfect its details. He shared
with Longtinsky the overmastering control of the whole impudent
and hopeless scheme. But it was the chance to him for revenge! His
impatience overcame every scruple, and he revelled in the dream of
destroying the homes of Carrara, the villa of the Lorimers—even, in
some more atrocious moments of desire of capturing, enslaving the
queen of that proud society!

Thus, when Bomber led his little army to destruction, he found
himself where at last he might work out his long cherished hopes. And
it was a strange fact that in the notable uprising of this distant
day, the leaders were entirely selfish—most of them—while the devoted
believers in some sort of social upheaval, the men who formed the
rank and file of the movement, were more moved by ideas of justice,
and of readjustment, in which their rights would be restored and
their ills righted.

The shuffling lines of men came down the long hill of Fort George,
they turned from Dyckman Street into the three avenues which lead
southwesterly to the Kingsbridge Road. There was no uniform among
them; here and there a red ribbon, and an occasional show of brass
buttons in some frayed coat, hardly helped the physical homogeneity
of about twelve hundred men of all sizes and service. Bomber had been
disappointed at the numbers of his army, and he had a discouraged
feeling about their enthusiasm. They were the enrolled members of
an anarchistic society, and probably would fight well if it came to
fighting. Longtinsky had once insisted that with any show of force,
the Mayor would compromise—and then, on some favorable terms perhaps,
they would gain power and there would be an UPRISING.

It all seemed simple enough, planned in a guarded room. Out in the
open, beneath the stars the conquest of the city was more a matter of
daring than words. So Bomber thought. Yet his hopes blinded him to
fear. He did not expect any difficulties at Carrara. The place should
be destroyed. He almost laughed to think of it. The police would be
kept busy at the City Hall. Perhaps Helen Lorimer would have a little
more respect for him after that!—suppose he took her a prisoner—an
hostage of that sort, might make the stubborn necks and the dull
heads of the Plum-Puddings bend, and think just a little. Indeed he
was not averse to dwell upon the favorable chance of a conversation
with Miss Lorimer and the diabolical fancy entered his mind of making
some matrimonial agreement with her. So banal and ludicrous is a
depraved vanity!

Bomber’s tall frame had been moving among the men, as they slowly
assembled, painting in glowing colors the ease of their work,
and just what it was. They would be almost unopposed. Scouts had
brought in the news that Carrara was being deserted; whether the
Lorimer villa was empty was uncertain; lights were burning there,
and figures had been seen moving in the chambers. But that was of
no consequence—only some frightened women! Bomber naturally thought
that Miss Lorimer had returned home from the trial, which, it was
universally known, she had most punctually attended each day. He did
not know of the conflagration and disaster at the Municipal Building.

He told his gloating hearers that the Revolution had been started
at the City Hall—Putkof had set the shipping afire throughout
the East Side—all the prisoners would be liberated, the city was
panic-stricken—nerveless; the militia away—no regular troop anywhere
around—the garrisons at the forts, at the Narrows sent away, and
those at Throg’s Neck as well. They would win on their own terms.
This was their chance to wipe out Carrara—the luxurious homes of
their masters, the tyrants of wealth, the sleek vainglorious idlers
who fattened on the sweat and earnings and skill and daily risks of
the workers.

So he walked and so he talked—yet he had misgivings. One thing was
annoying; his guns, his ammunition, had not arrived. They were
concealed in an empty house at High Bridge, and the wagons drawing
them with muffled wheels had not appeared. Only a few of those that
pushed their way into the entrance of the park carried arms. However,
he had no doubt that the guns would come—it made things late—though,
and time counted for something. But a hollowness in the whole effort
suddenly struck him; for one instant the great reserves of defence
in the mammoth city were indeed abroad, but how long would it take
for the four quarters of the country to pour in their support—how
long before these reserves themselves would be storming his little
host with all the implements of death? Unless—Ah, he pinned some hope
on that—unless as Longtinsky told him, the Time EVERYWHERE was ripe
for Revolution. He half believed it. The thought gave him nerve and
confidence.

The attacks of the rioters at the City Hall, and on the East Side,
had summoned all the police to the southern districts of the city,
and Bomber, acquainted with the fact of the presence of the members
of the 22d and 69th regiments in the city, was convinced that they
would not be able to interfere with him. The Mayor’s warning and his
own fears as well were destined to frustrate these hopes.

Still no supplies. Bomber chafed and raged—already more than an
hour had been lost. He was general enough to realize the importance
of promptness. Messenger after messenger had been despatched to
High Bridge, and none had returned. Bomber was in a group of
his lieutenants at the brow of the hill overlooking the Inwood
depression, and the viaduct, to Kingsbridge, of the present subway.
His first move had been to interrupt the trains of the Subway, and a
detachment of his men had pushed their way up to the Dyckman Street
station, and into the opening of the long tunnel leading south to
155th street—but no trains were running, the company deeming it
too dangerous for one thing, and because orders from the armories
requisitioned them for the service of the troops.

Still Bomber’s men tore up the free rails of the tracks, for a short
distance.

“What in thunder holds those men—Here—” to a dozen staring
auditors who happened to carry guns on their shoulders, “Get down
to High Bridge, a pack of you—Perhaps help is needed—Wait—” the
men turned—“Let more of you go. There is a kit of tools up on the
balcony—” pointing to the pleasure house raised in soaring terraces
at the highest point of the park—“a few crowbars may help you—and for
that matter a bomb or so—” he added savagely, for it was Bomber who
gave these orders.

“Perhaps they can’t get at the guns and ammunition. Hammer everything
to Hell, and bring them up;” then turning aside to a man who
stood apart, watching the fitful signs of agitation across the
valley, on the Carrara Hills—“We’re running our heads into a noose
perhaps—Eh—but if we stand here and wait for sunrise it will be cold
bullets!—”

The man did not stir; the whispered fear did not seem to interest
him, it certainly failed to withdraw his scrutiny from the pale
white palaces, now growing more and more indistinct, as the lights
went out, or again illumined in a spectral glare as some white flash
flared in the courtyards, or the gleam of a descending rocket shone
on them. At length the man dropped his glass, and showed he was
not unconscious of the speaker—but he only muttered—almost as to
himself—pointing with his disengaged hand to the opposite rim of land.

“Win that and if the worst comes—there are ways out—Money provides.”

Bomber seized his arm, drew him around to face himself, and then
dropped his hands on the man’s shoulders and brought his face close
to his:

“Yes, I know it does—but will a few empty houses furnish it? Do you
know anything?”

“Know,” said the other returning the gaze fearlessly, and just
perceptibly shrinking backward, as if finding this menacing scrutiny
repugnant;—“I know that those money-bags have been storing coins and
greenbacks in their safes for months. They expected this—some of
them. Sly foxes, they knew the cry of the hounds long before they saw
their teeth—they’re ready to give us the slip—Trap ’em and you are
made. They’ve sold stocks, bonds everything and were getting their
plunder together to slip over to Canada, make a dash for Europe,
Mexico, anywhere—They’re too late. Bomber, if our hands close on that
treasure we can take the same trip. Canada or any place might be a
happier home for us than this city.”

Bomber looked nervously around. No one stood very close to them—the
occasional rockets rising languidly from point to point in Carrara
intimating some telegraphy between the houses, as if preparations or
alarm were keeping their occupants busy, had attracted every eye.
Bomber pulled his friend farther away from any chance interruption;
his voice sank to a whisper, and his mouth sought his companion’s ear:

“I’ve been thinking of the same thing—But can we do it?”

“Do it—I can. You know me, a bank-cracksman for years. I’ve not
learned my trade for nothing. Get me in those houses yonder and I’ll
find the bottoms of their long purses. Trust me.”

A cry startled them. They looked behind them, and they saw the flames
shooting upward in the park—The pleasure house was burning—It was
this blaze, then culminating into a vast pyre, that the frightened
travellers in the balloon saw a half hour or so later.

It caused almost as much consternation to Bomber and his advisers.
Their force was revealed—the increasing light brought out with
terrible distinctness the formless straggling groups—their
position—and in its discovering brilliancy, illumined the long slant
hill, on which a few companies of men were moving. It conveyed a
further terror to Carrara—the rockets ascended in sudden flight from
new points. Evidently it was interpreted as a signal for the opening
of the anticipated attack.

Bomber was quick to act on his part. He left the man whose sordid
selfishness and cynical opportunism were already shared by himself,
and ordering his subalterns to rush through the crowds, commanded
them to hurry down to the hillside and screen themselves in the
oblique avenues below—Nagle, Sherman, and Post Avenues—somewhat
further away from this unfavorable exposure.

The conflagration had been started by the men whom Bomber had
despatched to High Bridge. In rummaging about for the crowbars and
picks, as weapons of assault on the closed doors that perhaps yet
foiled the rioters’ plans they had upset an oil lamp, whose trailing
fringes of flame had ignited the woodwork of the well dried and
combustible pleasure house, and, as if responsive to the growing
alarm of the great city, that frail fabric burst into a sheet of
swerving and dazzling light. The men in companies and scattered
arrangements of files and groups ran down the hill, and it was a few
last individuals that Helen Lorimer and Amato saw as they passed
overhead later. The floating habitation caused a little consternation
to the men yet unequipped for the attack upon Carrara, and Bomber
watched it with misgivings, while to the anxious sentinels—both men
and women—on Carrara, it was hailed as an omen of rescue.

In Carrara, to which place we must now direct our inspection, all
was in confusion, and in the dismay, increased every moment by
new disquieting news brought in by scouts, or received over the
telephone and by messengers, mingled the baleful prognostications of
some special upheaval, and the long dreaded and often forewarned
condition of affairs in the city was soon understood, though all the
happenings at the City Hall were not reported, and when it became
apparent that an attack was to be made upon Carrara itself, the
householders hurried together and council was taken as to what was to
be done.

Some argued for instant evacuation, others believed that an
armed resistance was possible, but that bold decision was coolly
shown to be impossible, unless the united men—proprietors and
servants—garrisoned some one of the stronger houses and deserted
the rest. Julieta Lorimer was in despair over the continued absence
of her daughter, and no information could be secured from the
offices down town while the few who in some way had heard of the
incarceration of the inmates in the Municipal Building, and among
them Helen Lorimer, and its consequent burning, none dared to tell
the awful tale.

Perhaps in all three hundred men could be mustered, and these poorly
supplied with arms, and resistance seemed hopeless with this number
against the overwhelming odds. There had not been much time for
deliberation, but such time as was allowed was well employed. Very
helpful to the householders was the path of communication between
the villas. It ran along the crest of the ridge irregularly set with
alcoves, stone benches, and ascending and descending steps that gave
it a most pleasing variety. Over this, that evening, the neighbors
passed and their hurried conference was made at the home of Helen
Lorimer. Hither came the family heads, young and less prudent younger
members, and gathering with them a squad of the male dependents who
were not all disposed to encourage a resistance to the approaching
invasion.

The impending danger hastened the deliberations. There seemed to the
wiser ones but one thing to do. It was a partial retreat, taking
with them such conveniently removable articles of great value as
