they could gather, and retire together to one of the strong Norman
keep-like erection on the Bolton Road, just over the Dyckman Street
pass to the Hudson River. If extremities, too serious, threatened
them there, escape could again be effected to the river itself
immediately behind, and finally embarkation in some steamboat to
be at once summoned and held in readiness. The season had so far
advanced that the many steam yachts that were familiar features on
the shore line of the river at Carrara were no longer to be seen.
They had retired to the winter station, as at present, near Gowanus
on the Long Island shores.

The last words of directions were spoken by a Duncan, the owner of
the picturesque mansion where they were to find refuge:

“Shut, lock and barricade all the entrances of your homes, the more
passive resistance of that sort we can devise the better. Remove all
valuables, the things you especially value of small size; concentrate
at my home and await relief. It must come! Should we be forced into
an extremity we can beat a retreat to the river and escape. This
seems the safest way. We are powerless against such odds.”

And so the hurried evacuation was made, while signal lights—the
rockets seen by Bomber—arose from point to point as a constant
warning for vigilance and for haste. There was much confusion. In
the short time possible to be employed before the invasion began,
it was out of the question to carry away the many things that each
householder dearly wished to save, and the bustle and struggle of
uncertainty was consequently frequent and prolonged. Doors banged,
improvised barricades were formed against the backs of the entrances,
windows were shut and fastened, the chasing of servants up and down
the stairs, the calls of the younger men and the impatient orders
of the older with many feminine sighs and exclamations were mingled
with the miscellaneous and confused murmurs and more violent noises,
proceeding from the opening and shutting of safe doors, the secreting
of objects of especial interest—and too large for removal—in closets
and cellars, while constantly mishaps, punctuated with cries and
recriminations, arose and added to the panic and disorder.

Amid it all Julieta Lorimer sat in her chamber bowed in prayer for
the safety of her daughter. She had quickly brought together the
jewelry of Helen, and her own, and packed the treasured trinkets
in a small agate-mosaic casket that now lay on the bed in front of
her. All sorts of dreadful possibilities crowded in her mind, racked
to the limit of endurance with fearful surmises, as happening to
her child. The thought of Amato kept up her courage. She knew his
readiness and courage, and she trusted his devotion.

How many wistful pictures of the past filled her mental vista of the
almost forgotten years—the years of Robert’s tempestuous courtship,
the birth of Helen, the increasing affection and chivalrous loyalty
of her husband, the periods of travel over the fair face of the
world, their mutual wonder of the beauty and promise of the girl
growing up in the revolving seasons, and gaining new charms of person
and mind, her splendid adoration of high ideals, and her rich and
embracing love of all human kind, her distinction, the patrician
courtesy, the austere ambition and her peculiar sweetness of thought
and word.

How Robert adored her; she saw them again and again walking together
in a very ecstacy of genial communion, and, as though breathed
upon her from the pregnant air, there came to her there, almost
audibly, the accents of his wonder and delight as he told her of
Helen’s unfolding excellence. She, whose respite from sorrow for
the death of her own loved one, had only come in the intervals of
her proud consciousness of the worth and buoyancy, of her daughter,
now with terrified questionings looked out in the darkening night,
and revolted against the sordid cruelty of circumstance if, in
this vulgar exigency of commotion, that daughter’s life became the
prey of accident. And as she gazed, half immersed in a praying
thoughtfulness, there suddenly came before her eyes in the night the
onrushing apparition of the lamp-lit balloon, in which that very
daughter glided above her to safety. She had tarried long. Carrara
was emptied and yet she remained. In the headlong flight her absence
had escaped attention, even among her own fleeing servants.

And yet she did not stir, for now she thought of Helen’s unhappiness,
the shocking discovery of her uncle’s perfidy, and the miserable
disappointment that had darkened her life. Julieta sighed—she could
not altogether regret it—Amato pleased her more; indeed, thus falling
into reveries, she married him to her, and then the strange fervency
of the desire of both to see the Pope placed in America and the new
missionizing plan rose before her, and she forgot her grief, almost
smiling over the Quixotic dream—for so it seemed to her. And yet no
movement; she saw the thickening clusters of rockets, the frightening
reminder of danger in the sheets of flame in the burning pleasure
house on the hill, and the black crowds of men gathered in slovenly
ranks for mischief. The lights yet burned in her room; she realized
that they prevented any certain view of the outside, and she rose
and extinguished them, and returned to the seat strangely oblivious
perhaps in the indulgence of the luxury of her reveries, wherein she
drew to her side the vanished dead, careless of remembering.

All had become still in Carrara, the shouts smothered in dull calls,
the movement, the sounds of running feet, the opening and shutting
of doors, the interrupted grating discords of shoved furniture and
reinforced bolts and locks—all was quiet, for the refugees had
crushed themselves into the Norman Duncan keep beyond the Dyckman
Road. Here some guns had been distributed, a sort of garrison
armament formed, scouts sent out, and a beleaguered attitude taken.
The precaution was looked after of getting a boat ready at the
water’s side behind them, and after some uncertainty and delay it was
decided, then and there, to remove the women. Under guard the latter
passed down the road to the Hudson in little companies, each with an
escort of armed men. In all this commotion and anxiety, the absence
of Julieta was somehow quite unnoticed.

Events were hurrying on to some fatal denouement among the rioters.
The obstacles, which had delayed the arrival of their stores of guns
and ammunition, and which were rightly explained by Bomber as due to
the desertion of his allies who had been watching them, thus leaving
them strongly locked up, inside barricades demanding destruction
before the agents of the rioters could reach them, had at length
arrived. The distribution of the arms to the men, the preparation
of an advance party with bombs, the slow infiltration of orders to
all the men and companies had consumed much time, and when at length
the host moved forward along the three avenues—Nagle, Post and
Sherman—the hour of nine had long passed.

Bomber had placed himself in the company of bomb-throwers, and the
man who had boasted of his skill in discovering the hiding place
of treasure was at his side. This little group headed the slow and
irregular entrance of the men on the Kingsbridge Road. Immediately
before them was the villa of the Lorimers, its marble terraces
shining spectrally, and the outlined palace above them on the
hillside, destitute of lights, inviting an easy assault. But Bomber
feared some ambush, and he ordered a company of younger men—who
had been acting as scouts for his army—to steal forward, and make
their way through the shrubbery to the top of the eminence, for an
observation of the measures of defence taken by the inmates. The
men returned and told their chief that apparently the house was
deserted. One man had broken in a window pane after forcing a shutter
open, and the sound, sharp and crackling, brought no response. Still
Bomber doubted, and he again ordered the men to push the examination
further, and see the other houses, spy out the gardens, and return.
The men dashed back, they were convinced that there was no danger in
the abandoned Carrara. After a longer absence they again came back,
and made their asseverations so strong that the place was deserted,
that Bomber gave orders for the whole body to ascend the terraces and
capture the ridge.

Julieta Lorimer raised her head; she had indeed fallen into a half
sleep wherein with half muttered prayers and clustering memories her
bewildered and exhausted body and mind had taken refuge. She rose
and moved to the windows again, almost as if in a dream, to see if
there was any sign of her returning daughter. It was the moment in
which Bomber’s spies were hurrying back with their reports about the
deserted Carrara, the moment when Bomber decided to rush forward and
take the houses.

The night was clear but there was no moon, the stars ruled the
skies with a soft and remote beauty, but their effects were less
apparent over the city of that coming day by reason of the spreading
pulsations of the upper light, artificially created and irregularly
distributed over the enormous commonwealth. This light, as Tom Ratler
noticed on the first evening of his arrival was more concentrated
in the lower parts of the city, but it now bathed the foreground
of the scene that Julieta looked out upon, though by it flushes
of brightness alternated, like shifting clouds, with intervals of
penumbral vagueness. Julieta pushed open the windowed door that led
to a balcony and stepped out into the ledge formed by its floor, just
at that instant when the light grew stronger.

She leaned far out, and her white dress formed a contrast with the
shadows on the wall behind her. She was seen by the watching men
in the roadway, and for an instant Bomber suspected treachery. And
then the thought arose with tempting plausibility that SHE—Helen
Lorimer—might have been left alone; this outstretched figure was
hers, and he might now be able, under less humiliating conditions,
than before, to ask for some consideration at her hands. The stupid
dream awoke in him a strange restlessness. He had not been altogether
thoughtless about his appearance either. He wore a khaki uniform
which became him; its contrast with the rude and often torn garments
around their commander had already awakened unfavorable comment.
It was regarded with some dissatisfaction. The broad unrolled soft
chapeau on his head added its theatrical touch, even gauntleted
hands and an unused sabre dangling picturesquely at his manly hip
contributed to an impression of dashing chivalry, which he had quite
serenely noticed himself. He now strode forward to the middle of
the highway, intercepting the movement that had begun to ascend the
terraces, and in a loud voice calling upon all to desist.

“He intended to reconnoitre the place himself. He would give a
signal for the charge as soon as he had convinced himself that
no rascally ambuscade awaited them. He prized their lives, and
rather than risk an unnecessary slaughter would first determine the
nature of the ground before them. One life,” he grandiloquently
concluded, “sacrificed, was better than the loss of many strong men
whose strength was now needed for the Renewal of Society, and the
establishment of the New Republic.”

The grotesque coxcombery of all this struck one man forcibly, and he
cursed the silly display, the bravado of an idiot.

“Why damn him,” he muttered hoarsely—and it was the man of the
lock-picking skill, the man who had plotted with Bomber for the
chance of interviewing the safes in the Carrara mansions—“Damn him;
what good will his maundering over there do. He’s losing time and
enough has been lost now to let those tin soldiers get here with
their pop guns and do us for ever. Bust the whole shee-bang, rip,
tear, and level it! That’s the way and then—GIT.”

Bomber was a mistake and for once the crafty Longtinsky had perhaps
miscalculated. But the element of mistake was in the unsuspected
fancy this apparently well chosen limb of mischief had for Miss
Lorimer, which entangled with his own preposterous personal conceit
made him the easy tool of circumstances and circumstances now most
ludicrously related to his stupid prepossessions. Nothing can be
more mischievous in unsettling a vain man’s sense of relations and
propriety, not to say his more important sense of his own position,
than the foolish imagination of his possible importance in the eyes
of a pretty woman.

Bomber turned his back to his astonished companions, and mounted the
steps with rapid strides, his own conviction growing that the white
figure above him was indeed Miss Lorimer the nearer he approached the
now excited and attending lady who began to awaken from the strange
coma of grief and recollection which had held her enchained in a half
unconsciousness.

Julieta Lorimer slowly recognized her situation, and something like
an intense keenness supervened to make it all plainer to her. She had
somehow failed—she now saw—to join the others when they had deserted
the Ridge, and she was entrapped in her villa, and stood in the face
of the storming rioters. She recalled the commotion of her fleeing
friends, and she remembered she had stolen to her room, and here she
had become isolated in her own sorrowful and oblivious thoughts. Her
faculties were now fully awake; the terror only served to sharpen
her appreciation of the predicament she was in. She did not become
useless in her fear, but her mind engaged quickly and sharply in
the preparation of some sort of defence. Who was this man—a figure
ascending in leaps the marble steps—and why did he come alone? Down
on the Kingsbridge Road she saw the massed men and women and seemed
to discern their faint prolongation back into the avenues. Was it
not strange—this immobile mob, voiceless, ominously still—while this
man—a leader perhaps advanced all alone?

What was to be done; should she escape and pursue her vanished
friends—the thought struck her suddenly that she would rush through
the mansion, and turn on all the lights—she perceived the darkness of
the place—it might create the appearance of relief or occupation. She
hesitated—and there below her under the window’s balcony, the figure
looked up to her, and she heard his voice: and noticed that his hat
was removed from his head, and something in his accents made her
stop, shocked with an incredible impression.

“Miss—Lorimer? I believe—I feel the awkwardness of
this—position—but—time presses—I must enter your home—it is
deserted—you are alone—you may trust yourself to me—it is an honor I
have asked for before, and which then you saw fit to repel—as leader
of a new Revolution—the revolution of the great heart of the people
you may condescend to treat me—less carelessly—” the words were
spoken with a mixture of impudence and menace.

Nothing had escaped Julieta’s ears and her mind worked with the
marvellous concentration of memory, acting under the impelling sense
of a real danger; she divined the situation; she recalled Bomber’s
declaration of love for her daughter—this man is doubtless he—his
name she had heard mentioned as the chief of the mob at her doors—she
knew that relief was under way—that too she had heard—could she delay
this aspirant for Helen’s hand?—keep him awhile—every moment might
help—was she equal to the dangerous ruse? At any rate the evident
delusion of the speaker must be encouraged.

Almost mechanically she asked—“Is this Mr. Bomber?” the question
answered would illuminate the situation, and as she asked it she half
turned away so that that gentleman might not discover his mistake.

“Yes I am Bomber, Miss Lorimer. Alaric Mitchess Bomber—I would be
happy to assist you in this extremity—there need be no alarm—but
the doors of this house must be opened at once—these men—” a
flourish of his scabbard indicated the waiting crowds at the foot
of the slope—“are in earnest—everywhere the forces of revolt are
in motion—the City must succumb—perhaps I could persuade you
to negotiate some sort of compromise with the Aristocracy of
Carrara—Excuse me, I dread violence but this parley cannot be kept up
too long—my men are impatient—”

There flew through Julieta’s mind several projects—and among them
was one of startling possibilities—almost too audacious too, full of
grave consequence to herself—and yet it tempted her by its amazing
daring and shrewdness. She would entrap the leader of the mob—put him
under lock and key—she, Julieta Lorimer!—he should be decoyed into
the house, and she would agree to meet him within, and then—a smile
crept into her face that was darkened the next instant with doubt;
the indecision had been noticed by Bomber, and had elicited his
threat. But the indecision was gone; Julieta committed herself to the
rash expedient—she would take Alaric Mitchess Bomber prisoner. Shades
of Longtinsky!!!

“Alaric”—in her sweetest tones—the figure started and stretched
itself upward—“I will see you in the house—it is possible we can
together do something to prevent mischief, and—if so—perhaps—we
may—” the last words were permitted intentionally to fade away
into nothingness—conveying thereby to the credulous hearer some
indiscreet concession, that made him bound with delight and desire.

“Yes—Yes, Miss Lorimer—may I say Helen?—” the stupidity and dull
conceit of the man made her project seem again very plausible to
Julieta—“but it must be done quickly—it may be necessary for me to
return to the men there”—pointing—“and make them understand—they
might suspect treachery—is there no one in the house? They expected
a fight and they are in the humour for destruction—you see—I will do
all you advise—but——”

“Alaric,” still insinuating and delicious to the ears of the
vanquished leader of revolution, “go tell them you are about to
confer with Helen Lorimer, who speaks for the whole colony of
Carrara—your own eloquence will convince them, or if they cannot be
controlled entirely, tell them to be patient a moment—surely you can
restrain them, and then come TO ME.” Bomber’s arms actually crossed
themselves upon his breast with the unction of an embrace. Julieta
saw it. “And we can at least devise some measure for my safety. That
is but just, is it not?”

“Helen”—the voice was deep with passion and obedience as well—“let it
be so. I will return and speak to them, and _then_ admit—me.”

Julieta hid her face in the silken scarf about her shoulders and
leaned down, whispering. “No one is here. I am alone. Come in at
the front, the iron gateway through the peristyle, through the
front doors. There is a great hall. Ascend the stairway to the
dining-room, and then pass into a side room and await me. The side
room is a butler’s pantry—you can tell it by the one light that will
be alight there. Adieu, Alaric.” The old star of Robert Lorimer’s
improvised show retained her skill, and her Italian hand still showed
a delightful fineness. But then she had such plastic stuff to treat!

Bomber waved his hat, his gauntleted hand touched his lips. He turned
and jumped down the terraces with really splendid youthfulness. His
youthfulness did not desert him in the emergency of getting to the
top of the gate post of the Lorimer estate; he clambered up somehow,
and there in full view, with his hat removed, a few assisting
gestures, he delivered his oration.

“Patriots of the New Order, we are about to accept the capitulation
of the Aristocrats”—a long rising hiss, culminating in a yell
of derision—“Miss Helen Lorimer, of whom we can all admit she
possesses the virtues of her class, with none of their faults”—a
mixed reception of cheers and hisses greeted this admission—“has
agreed to see me in her own palace of art and beauty, and there I
shall be permitted to dictate the terms of surrender. Miss Lorimer
has had the courage to remain, while all her BRAVE”—uttered with
impressive sarcasm—“defenders have fled. I only ask you, my noble
comrades——” Bomber was a traitor; while he spoke these words he was
secretly turning over in his mind the chances of freeing himself
from these now unnecessary associates, and turning body and soul
over to the abhorrent aristocrats. Here was the goal of his fondest
dreams—becoming with Helen Lorimer rehabilitated, and an ornament of
the class he affected to despise.

“I only ask you now to await the results of this interview; when
it is finished you shall enter Carrara and POSSESS it.” He paused,
and a cheer arose; he interpreted it as his permission—he was too
impatient himself to return—he jumped off his elevated rostrum, and
as he turned and ran again backward a voice—loud, soaring, he _knew_
it—shouted in the silence, “Al Bomber you’re a LIAR.”

But Bomber had no desire to refute the statement, his heart burned
with the strangest hopes, and his self inflation, his fatal aptitude
for gallantry approached, as he thought, a new and marvellous
satisfaction. He kept on his way unchallenged.

Julieta left the balcony; her heart beat tumultuously; the trick
was bold, and desperate—it almost seemed. Would she be successful?
There was danger—plenty of it—too; if he suspected; if he brought
men back with him; if his hearers were not affected with the reasons
for gullibility he succumbed to; if she herself could escape—and
WHERE? Certainly the butler’s pantry was a well chosen place for
the incarceration of the dashing leader of the New Order. It was
practically a strong box—literally a safe—with iron walls and
iron doors, where the silver of the family was also kept. Besides
entrance from the dining-room, it possessed but one other egress,
a back door, fastened on the inside. That she believed was already
secured; locked, with the key removed; it was the practice so to do
at night. It was to be her hands that would shove the bolts, upon the
imprisoned Bomber, from the outside, of the very entrance he would
use. Her limbs trembled with the agitation of thinking of the work
before her, but the instant she had groped her way down to the spot,
turned on the single lamp that would announce the rendez-vous and
then secreted herself in an alcove of the dining-room, with her dress
covered by a voluminous black overskirt she felt a singular firmness,
almost a jubilant expectation. She had concluded to rush from the
room at once when the deed was done, and make her way to the Duncan
keep.

Suddenly she bethought her that the doors below, the great gate of
the entrance also were yet shut. Gathering her dress about her, she
descended the stairs, and unlocked and unbolted all. It took time,
some strength was required, and her nervous excitement alone made it
possible to accomplish it. Of course she became visible to one near
at hand, when she pushed her way out into the peristyle, to open the
iron gate of the courtyard. She shivered as she crossed the yard;
her confidence almost deserted her as she saw through the trellis of
the gate the returning figure of the man she was about to capture.

It was indeed only by a superhuman effort she pulled down the huge
bolt for he was close upon her. Bomber had detected the woman form
moving behind the trellis, as he ascended; he knew it was SHE and he
hastened his steps. Julieta felt her heart fail her; she staggered—in
another second, before she could possibly return, he would be at
her side, and the revelation would be made—she turned irresolutely
to run, when an oath almost at her side, as she thought, came to
her ears, and the sound of a falling body. Instinctively she turned
again, and she saw Bomber sprawling on the outer steps—that unlucky
emblem of authority, his sword, had twisted between his legs and
tripped him. It was Julieta’s chance.

She raced up the inner steps—those leading to the dining-room—the
superb banquet-chamber where we have seen Helen Lorimer dispensing
the hospitality of the Lorimers—she crouched in the selected
alcove, not far from the open door of the butler’s pantry, where
the seductive little light gleamed secretively. She stiffened her
resolution, though the mounting beats of her heart warned her of the
terrific strain. She heard the rising footsteps—perhaps a little more
careful since their proud owner had learned the wisdom of caution—and
then something darkened the door of the dining-room. It was he. He
stopped; her shuddering heart shrank within her at the suspicion that
he saw her. No; he passed on, his eye had seen the interior light of
the pantry, and he stood at its entrance. Why did he not enter? The
strain was insupportable, the pale light shone on him, though dimly,
and he seemed to be examining the place as if fearing a surprise. If
he waited longer his accustomed eyes would find her out; there was a
little light diffusing from the lamp in the pantry; her hammering
heart, it felt so, was itself audible in the dead silence.

Julieta had run a serious risk in assigning the butler’s pantry for a
meeting with Bomber. She knew only its peculiar strength and adequacy
for service as a prison; she did not know how it had been left by her
fleeing servants. The great key which locked both the inner and outer
doors was always removed at night by the housekeeper and given to
Julieta. She had not received it that night; it was not in her room;
she concluded it still remained in the door of the pantry, but had
the INNER DOOR BEEN LOCKED? When she descended to the dining-room her
first anxiety was to assure herself of the position of the key. Her
heart leaped when she found it in the keyhole of the outer door, the
door leading from the dining-room. A moment later the lamp was turned
on in the pantry, and she tried the inner door. It had been locked.
Both locks were identical: one key fitted both. They were large
constructions, the key itself ponderous, and it threw three heavy
steel bolts when turned.

So far the trap was ready. One imprudence was committed. She left the
key in the outer door, but in order to execute her design she took
the risk willingly. To insert the key in the dark, to shut the door
and turn the lock would be an involved process, and in the delay,
and possible bungling, her quarry might have more than enough time
to break its net and escape. So the key was left; if Bomber took the
precaution to remove the key before entering the ambuscade and to
pocket it! Julieta would have been thwarted, and only headlong flight
would have saved her.

Bomber still stood at the door of the pantry, almost as if
suspicious; it was thus impossible to close it upon him. He even
stepped back again, possibly puzzled that the figure which, a minute
before had been in the courtyard, should now have so completely
vanished. If he struck a match! If he fumbled around and attempted
to turn on the lights! Julieta grew dizzy at the thought. She cowered
together, her knees trembling, and her hands abjectly pressed
upon her face. He looked again into the pantry and—walked in. His
unmeasured personal conceit had saved her.

Bomber’s eye had fallen upon the lustrous expanse of a superb silver
service-tray; in the half light this immense and beautiful work of
the silversmith, hanging on the wall of the pantry, shone softly,
and displayed invitingly a damasked surface, and its high decorative
borders. It tempted the eye irresistibly. Bomber had actually in his
delight with its richness and, in a feverish anticipation of a great
success, walked to it, and caught his own reflection in the polished
mirror of a central oval of smooth silver. He stooped and observed
that his hair had become artistically disarranged, that his cheeks
glowed, his eyes—yes, he was sure of it—emitted an unusual light, and
his mouth—ah! he smiled, he leaned over to the beguiling picture,
when—what was it?—a sluggish thud, and the click of a shot lock!

He turned like a shot animal, his face convulsed with terror;
the door was shut; he sprang to it with his strength—and it was
considerable—he wrenched at the knob, but though it flew to and fro,
nothing yielded. HE WAS A PRISONER. He looked around him; he leaped
at the inner door and attempted to open it. Locked! No window, no
crevice, no loophole of escape.

“My God,” he groaned aloud, “the she-devil, the tiger, witch, curse
her. Am I trapped here like a rat?”

His face grew almost livid; he raised his voice shouting, yelling,
shriek after shriek in the bootless effort to call help. He hurled
the silver tray at the walls, he searched them with knives but only a
steel wall met his fury. And then he sank back on the only chair in
the room, in a convulsion of sheer rage and agony.

And Julieta, like a flying cloud, her black skirt lost from her in
her flight, sped out of the house, and on the well-known pathway,
down and up steps, in front of and behind low shrubs, through
archways, quicker and quicker she ran on, stumbling now, even
falling, regaining her feet, and yet running on in a vertigo of
excitement, the first stages of a terrible reaction.

But while this half ludicrous _intre-acte_ was taking place, events
outside of them were rushing on to a tragic end. The waiting army
enormously enlarged by the running concourse of sightseers, the
various accessions of the idle and vicious, was waiting the return
of their fatuous leader. A growing discontent, with an increasing
fear that some one had blundered, that time was being lost, that
the police or the soldiers would soon confront them, made itself
apparent. A hubbub of voices was now heard where all, an hour
before, had been silent, and the presage of dangerous work before
them had kept the men grave and dumb. Disintegration was evident.
The disgusted safe-robber felt that, and sneered at the futile idiot
chosen for this work.

Suddenly a distant honk announced an approaching automobile, another
and another—it was coming on fast—the coarse grating alarm sounded
now almost among them. The foremost drew back from the roadway, a
few wandering in the Lorimer grounds, looking up the shining stairs,
slashing at the plants and grumbling aloud, turned back, so that
the vehicle passed between two solid files. It stopped; the door
of the limousine swung open—a tall, thin man alighted. He ordered
the chauffeur to assist him, as he mounted the puffed tire of the
wheel, and was pushed upon the roof of the car. He stood up, and by
the lucky incidence of light from a distant electric lamp he was
revealed. It was the MAYOR.

A long roll of subdued imprecations greeted him, rising with a
sudden access of noise into a clamor of shouts, oaths, and yells.
It increased, the suppressed impatience and their inaction, the
confusion of mind produced by the disappearance of Bomber, some
doubt as to their position, and the disquietude of a sense of
impending failure, all intensified by their hostility to the man
who had vanquished them in an industrial war, merged into an awful
expression of hatred. An abstract alarm sharpened into a real rage
at a concrete personality, and they stormed forward, filling the
highway, surging upon the automobile, while the cries of “Kill him,
kill him,” maddened them to violence. They crushed about the wagon,
and guns came to their shoulders. Not a missile had been thrown; the
passion of the mob vented itself in execrations.

“The Scab,” “He took our bread,” “The Carrara Stool-pigeon,” “Have
him down and tear him to bits,” “Rip his glib tongue out,” “To Hell
with him: He belongs there.”

The seething tide, in another instant would have overturned the
machine. It could not advance. It was wedged in a matrix of solid
humanity; that alone saved it from an ignominious upset.

The Mayor had been silent and held his place; his arms were folded,
and not a word of expostulation as yet. His attitude both vexed and
puzzled the insane mob. For one instant the voices were silenced—it
was the prelude to a murderous design—and then came the clear
startling words, strangely almost weirdly penetrating; “Yes! You
can kill me if you like. The thing is easily done—but you will lose
a friend—I beg to tell you you will lose your fight—” The silence
continued, the Mayor jumped at the chance and his voice rose higher
with that peculiar musical distinctness and charm that Helen Lorimer
had noticed when she first saw him in the City Hall.

“What have you got against me? One thing. I did my duty as the Law
commanded. I put down your Lawlessness. Could I do anything else?
Could you? But is that all I have done? Have I not also restrained
the men who would have destroyed you so far as they could? What is
your grievance? Are you starved? Are you naked? Are you unsheltered?
I know that you are _not_. Labor conditions are not bad here. You
are misled. You have been listening to foolish promises, you have
followed false leaders. You have let envy take the place of sense.
You dream of some sort of a new State. It is called The New Era; no
New Era can be well considered that begins with WAR. Unnecessary,
cruel, careless, brutal WAR! Any Economic idea that starts with WAR
will end with it! It cannot abide. Civil disorder, Civil strife,
means one thing—ANARCHY; and anarchy is not a thinkable civil state.
You might as well—you who are builders—think that a house can stand
which is built out of plumb, out of line—you who are plumbers that
you can have health with broken pipes, and empty reservoirs, you who
are cloth-makers or hat-makers or workers of any sort, that you can
do your labor without tools. You preach SOCIALISM but _society_ comes
first, and society is superior to any theory as to how society shall
be conducted. I think I understand as well as you do much that can be
said for the aims of Socialism, but I understand a great deal better
than you do, much that can be said against it. I too have learned
things. Are you above learning also? These people,” waving his arm
towards the houses of Carrara—“have done you no harm by living.
Would you care to live as they do? Hardly. Cannot we take a lesson
from Nature—a flat world would be an uninhabitable world—a flat
Society—all alike—would be impossible. I will talk this over with
you, but not here. IF YOU WILL ARGUE THIS MATTER WITH GUNS, YOU SHALL
BE ANSWERED WITH GUNS.”

His voice ceased and the silence was for an instant broken by cries
and questions. He had made an impression. The Mayor felt that, and
he was eager to take advantage of the favorable opening. He leaned
forward—when suddenly the Gun Argument, he had invoked, began
somewhere back in the streets toward the Saint George Hill. There was
firing—sharp unmistakable firing. All again was disorder, tumult,
panic. A terrible cry smote his ears.

“Clear out!—the SOLDIERS—make off!” It was true. The two
regiments—the 22d and the 69th—had been partially mustered, and
they were forcing their way down the three avenues towards the
Kingsbridge Road. And thus unexpectedly, shamelessly, this silver
voiced flatterer became to the men in front of him a deceiver—the
traitor had kept them there while the troops gathered; his very words
“_answered with guns_”, was the signal for this assault. The Mayor
realized the awful accusation of this coincidence. He recoiled from
it, his face darkened with suffering. Curses reached his ears, and
then the whistle of a bullet, and he fell backward on the roof of the
car.

An avalanche of men hurled out of the avenues. They were in flight.
The firing came closer. Only the rush of feet was heard. Still
firing, and the despairing race of men past the motionless Mayor
there on the top of the limousine, that, like some slight pebble in a
stream, sufficed to divide the tearing unguided current. And yet none
of those who passed, bore any evidence of wounds. There were none.
The Mayor, in ordering the soldiers had commanded that _dispersal_
not _slaughter_ should first be tried with the rioters. Fear did
its perfect work. It was as the Mayor wished, but he had fallen the
victim of his own imprudence; in a manner, of his own forbearance.

Soon it was all over; the troops emerged from the streets into
the roadway, and the vast throngs, the little army of Bomber, the
idle gazers, the haunting lines of free-booters, had EVAPORATED as
completely as the patch of water on the flagging succumbs to the dry
summer wind that sweeps it into space.

The Mayor was carried into the Lorimer mansion; the soldiers
patrolled the streets; the dispossessed returned, lights spread
over the deserted ridge, doctors hurried to the side of the City’s
executive, Briggs arrived, and, under orders from the Lieutenant
Governor, the surplus of the soldiers marched to the east side to
assist the police and the firemen, who still fought an uncertain
contest with the wily incendiary Putkoff. And in the midst of the
mingling confusion of renewed confidence, the bustle of return,
the flashing telegrams from all parts of the city, the news of the
speeding armaments from Chicago, the resumption of the whirling life
of the huge metropolis, stifled into inaction for a few short hours
by this flurry of insurrection, Helen Lorimer and Amato and Mr. Moser
arrived, and Helen learned all.

Julieta had made her way to the Duncan Keep after her successful
encounter with Bomber, and there became incoherent and dizzy with
fever. She managed to tell her story, and almost the first thing
done when the rehabilitation of the houses began and the colonel of
the regiments came on the scene was to open the now famous Butler’s
pantry. There indeed sat the raving Bomber. He was pale with frenzy.
He leaped at the first man who entered, and only a sharp struggle
overcame him. As he passed down the steps of the mansion, handcuffed,
he encountered Amato and Helen hurrying upward, but he averted
his eyes, and the bitterness of his defeat, the confession of his
shallowness and vanity made him writhe with anguish.

Helen had asked for her mother. Julieta had been very carefully
conducted back to her home, and lay in her bed yielding to the first
vagaries of a disordered mind. Helen stood at her side and leaning
over the changed flushed face of the beautiful woman whispered her
name, and pressed her lips upon her own. Julieta opened her eyes and
her hands feebly closed over the head of her child, holding her in a
delirium of joy. But the strained nerves gave way, and she relapsed
into a kind of inanition awaking at times to beckon from the air some
shape appearing there to her, as she called softly, “Robert, Robert.”
Then Helen herself surrendered. The ordeal had been a long one, and
this climax of anxiety, with the strange vicissitude of the Mayor’s
extremity in her own home, completed the breakdown. Still it was not
serious, and nourishment and rest would restore the strong nature to
its natural estate.

The Mayor’s condition was critical; life ebbed and returned; the
doctors camped at his bedside; skill and art and experience worked
hard together to keep him alive, yet sensibly the flagging heart
released its control, and the solemn end grew more and more certain.
Then it was—the next day in the afternoon—that the dying man asked
for Miss Lorimer. He knew where he was, from time to time in the
brief intervals, when pain left him, his eyes reviewed the decorated
and charming room, dwelling with such a depth of tender interest on
all these things that recalled—how pathetically who shall say—the ONE
love of his life.

Helen came. The Mayor lay in the room of her uncle—the man who had
separated them. It was a luxurious apartment, with every appointment
perfect, and its windows opening to the west, in the sinking
November day caught the last lights over the russet and gold of the
Palisades. The Mayor lay on the bed, with his face turned to the
flushing western sky, rent as it were, with seams of gold, as though
the purple tapestry of the clouds were torn, and the gateway of the
sun’s descent disclosed. Helen stood at the doorway; she had come
noiselessly, and waited for her own heart to beat less wildly. A
doctor held the Mayor’s wrist, timing the sullenly retreating tide of
blood from his young heart; a nurse bent over him. Both happened to
hide Miss Lorimer from him.

The doctor turned, the nurse moved away, the Mayor’s eyes fell on
the face of the woman who there awaited his summons. It came from
those blue eyes whose mystery had first enchanted her. And her own
answered them. Slowly she came forward, and sank into the chair the
doctor had just deserted. The Mayor looked past her for one moment
at his attendants, and a mute entreaty they read in his face, caused
them both to withdraw.

“Pardon me, Miss Lorimer,” said the doctor, “I must soon return.”
Helen bowed her head, a gesture of the doctor’s summoned the nurse
and the two went out together. Helen had taken the dying man’s hand;
she held back the struggling tears; his own eyes were strangely soft
and winning, the effort of this interview brought again some color
to his face—but so very little!—the pitiful remnant of his wasting
life—and his voice, that appealing musical voice just retained or
recovered its former note. She bent her face close to his as he spake.

“Miss Lorimer,” he began, but this noble girl whispered, “let it be
Helen please,” and he smiled, “Helen,—you recall that talk we had in
the museum,—your new museum—?”

“Yes,” she quietly responded.

“Well, here in my wandering dreams I have gone over it for hours. You
have always been near me, and your voice has sounded to me almost as
if it bore to me the music of another World”—his face darkened with
the overwhelming torrent of his sadness, but no sobs broke from his
lips. The spasm of grief passed, she pressed his hands, she wiped the
stricken eyes, she slid softly from her chair, she knelt at his side.

“Helen, I go too with the setting sun—my day is finished—I thank the
Everlasting that it passes thus. But let me be clear in your eyes.
Your uncle drove me from this house. My pride could not brook his
insult. Helen, my love has never ceased, never changed. Death only
takes away its earthly dwelling place, and IF—If, as you began to
teach me, there is still life THERE”—he pointed feebly to the blazing
west—“if there is, there too my love will stay unchanged. Ah! the
long day’s task is done, and I must sleep. But Helen you forgive
me”—then with an ardor that made the girl before him pale with
apprehension that the untoward passion would put out the fluttering
flame of life, he added—“Helen have I still your Love?”

The woman bent her face to his; their lips met—a golden light entered
the windows, it filled the room; it was so intense and brilliant
that objects seemed lost in it; it dazzled as though an etherialized
substance suffused the space, blotting out all definite shape, and
enthralling all the senses in the captivity of one. It was so for
the interval of half a minute, and then again the depleted room was
established in the commonplaces of its utilities.

The doctor and the nurse had returned, and behind them came the
bowed form of the Mayor’s mother, led by Mr. Briggs. Helen rose and
passed to the window, her eyes straining at the spectacle of glory in
the clouds. To her catholic heart and nurture it seemed appallingly
cold and ruthless, this passage of a soul into the awful futurity,
unattended by prayers, unaccompanied by the offices of devotion, with
no sensible presentation, still to hold the dissolving corporeal
powers fixed in adoration that the soul might more purely, more
wisely, pass on to its new appointments, rising on the wings of
aspiration.

The strength of a tremendous religious faith was hers, and in that
strength her lips repeated the Orison of Help for the dying.

A half-stifled cry, a movement behind her, caused her to turn. The
doctor was taking away the stunned mother of the man who had already
just made his confession to her ears. Helen passed quickly to her
side, took her in her arms, and brought her out of the room, passing
through a crowd of men, officers of the city,—state and citizens, and
gently made her way to her own chamber, with a tenderness that was
itself a ministration of healing to the crushed heart, the hopeless
spirit of a Mother.

THE MAYOR WAS DEAD.

CHAPTER XVII

THE POPE COMES TO AMERICA


The Mayor was buried with splendid pomp. His body lay in state;
the militia who flocked back as the alarm over the city’s dilemma
spread to the neighboring cities, with the garrisons of the regular
troops, who had also returned, formed his escort, and the music
at his funeral touched the limits of elegiac elaboration. A sense
of his excellence as an administrator, a sudden recognition of
his intellectual delicacy and skill, combined with the thrill of
admiration felt at his daring in confronting the rioters, the relief
that followed the dispersal of the insurrection together with the
repudiation of the whole Longtinsky anarchistic plot by the sane and
moderate socialistic organs contributed to elevate his memory, and
imbue his name with a peculiar—to some a mystifying—glory of renown
and nobility.

When all was over, the huge city-state resumed its myriad activities,
and for a while the political asperities abated, the differing
parties vanished, and the warring sections of society forgot or
dissembled their mutual hatred. The exhortations of the Mayor
were printed on cards, sold as mottoes, or decorated the pages of
the newspapers. He was regarded as a martyr, a sacrifice to the
turbulence and wickedness of agitators and plotters, and, with
something like a religious ecstacy the people idolized his memory,
and before the adoration, clamor, discord, petulance and envy,
for a time, hid themselves. A wonderful peace prevailed, and the
magnificent metropolis turned to its avocations or its pleasures with
unrestrained energy and delight.

Mr. Briggs, who now worshipped the memory of his master, became
attached to a newspaper, and furnished periodic outbursts of
Cassandra-like prophecy over the dark future which threatened society
at large, and while they, when read aloud, had a portentous sound,
and a certain rhetorical ornamentation with enough penetration to
make them significant, they were generally wisely put aside as
sophomoric and unnecessary. Mr. Briggs brought the Mayor’s mother
to his home, and this partnership proved wonderfully consoling
to the afflicted lady. Mr. Briggs lived in one of the charming
colonial restorations of the Greenwich village area of the city, and
maintained—though a bachelor—an establishment of considerable dignity.

Of course Tom Ratler survived his encounter with Poni, and seized the
fruits of his popularity with the gentle and pretty Jessie Longtinsky
and she discovered upon the death of her father, that a moderate
competence was hers. The conspirators—at least the formidable Bomber,
Madder, Putkof, and Poni himself—were put behind the bars for a
practically life period, and no ingenuities of law were expended in
proving the injustice of this arrangement. As to the rest, hundreds
of whom were under arrest, a general amnesty was proclaimed, and
the prisoners who had brought about the famous trial, all, except
Polinck, were discharged; Polinck being reserved for another trial.
In this general dispensation of urbanity and moderation none rejoiced
more than Helen Lorimer. She undertook the education and advancement
of the abnormal child Angelo Shimura, and especially she turned
to the project she had discussed so long ago with the Mayor—her
Museum—and which now might become a sort of memorial of the man
himself—with almost religious ardor.

The question of the classes still remained unsettled, and Helen
Lorimer as the apostle of the essential necessity of a _classified_
Society, with the avenues of promotion fully created through
education, and with emphasis laid upon the essential mutual
interaction of all parts of Society with particular reference
to the indispensable patriotism and benevolence—in a large and
economic sense—of the upper classes, began again her instructions,
and readings, and writings. For a time, Amato felt her intellect
put aside the claims of her emotions or else—WAS IT, that in this
outburst of energy and propagandist zeal she sought again in some
esoteric way, the companionship of the man she deeply mourned. The
splendid magnanimity of Rudolpho found no fault with this. There
was a little hurt, a little uneasiness and dismay perhaps, but the
gentleness of his nature, the fine heroism of his feelings, quickly
restored his equanimity and he followed at her side, encouraged all
her attempts, aided her designs, and so patiently and wisely obeyed
her wishes that he entered deeper and deeper into her affections, and
captivated her anew by the beauty of his unselfishness.

It was a few weeks after the death of the Mayor that a most
unexpected occurrence brought the man’s personality again vividly
forward and reawakened in Helen Lorimer the half forgotten plan
to strengthen the religious life of the country by establishing
the Pope within it. The latter project had momentarily lost its
interest, though with Cardinal Fenton, who now fully understood
the inevitable union of his nephew and Miss Lorimer, it had only
suffered a temporary suppression. Conditions indeed in Europe
became more and more insupportable for the Church. The exorbitant
so-called civilization with the still surviving colossal selfishness,
viciousness and hollowness of the representatives of inherited
privilege, had produced through two opposite influences an almost
total repudiation of religion, looked at in large way, and as
representing a national feeling.

First the strong socialistic tendencies—verging into radical phases
contemplating the extermination of Society as constituted—denied
the tenets of formal religion, and advised its abolition; second,
the sensual and sensational inclinations of the upper classes had
reached ruinous proportions. They amounted to a moral devastation.
Europe, advanced in the methods of enlivening life by exciting
appetite, had also become eclipsed in its intellectual aspects by
the hebetude that succeeds satiety, and religion was laughed at or
cultivated as a cloak to deceive the herd. The tabernacles were
empty, the priests were useless, the Pope was impoverished, the
prestige of the Church had vanished.

Cardinal Fenton realized sorrowfully that in the protestant
Teutonic nations religion yet held its sway more rigorously,
than in the former homes of his own church; that Russia, somehow
barricaded within a wall of barbarous autocracy and childish slavish
superstitions maintained its quondam religious expression almost
unchanged. To him and an advanced group of Catholic prelates in
Europe and America a surmise that American influences somehow
established the Church, renovated and revivified it had become a
conviction. To them for some years the dream of bringing the Pope
to America, of—the language was decorously veiled in its apparent
significance—liberating him from the somnolent antiquities, the
enthralling traditions, the half ludicrous isolation, or, to quote
language used less obsequiously, a hundred years before, by Father
McGlynn, of putting the Pope in the streets of New York with a
stove-pipe hat on his head, had become more and more alluring.

Here in America the Church had shuffled off the encumbering follies
which had grown up within her through a constant association with
a system of privilege, of monarchy, of the artifices of title and
place. In America, in a sweet exhilarating air of free institutions,
universal opportunity, and amid fresh pure aspirations for happiness,
the Church forgot cabals and intrigues and politics, and generated
itself in a region apart, in worship, good deeds, lofty teachings,
sacramental offices, and enveloped, at least by implication ALL,
under the aegis of its divine commission. Its personnel became
saturated with sense and sympathy, and no more useful men than its
priests could be found in practical charity, educational enthusiasm,
and broad philosophic erudition. Protestantism had almost protested
itself out of existence.

To this New World let the Pope come. It seemed to men like Fenton,
the last chance, the last entrenchment wherein the incessant fight
against Negation might be fought. And to pious Catholic patriots
it seemed that so good a possession as the Pope belonged to their
own dear country. On the other hand it meant the uprooting of an
established identification of Rome and the church of two thousand
years, something almost appalling to conceive of. Should the arc
of that covenant be ruthlessly touched—even though it shook and
tottered—by the modern Uzzahs of this movement?

Yet many believed the violent disruption desirable. As in a sedentary
organism parasitic things, mere unavailing accidents had become
encysted in the outward tissue of the Church, enfeebling it. These
would be dislodged, shaken off, and left behind. They had been
debilitating and even malign. Tone, a freshened vigor, a stronger,
readier, adaptive stage to the new outlooks in the New World would
succeed, or by another imagery, the barque of the Church launched
upon a broader, brighter sea would free itself of old wrecks,
floating debris, the relics of mistakes, collisions, abandoned
cargoes, the barnacle growths of centuries, and sail forward to new
ports with certainty and power. The encrustations of foolish practice
the mere hindrances of time-honored but stupid devotions—as that of
the Bambino—would be swept away, and the Church more intellectually
more healthfully, with a freer scope of thought, and with No Loss
of essential dogma would confront her enemies, her critics, the
whole antagonistic World, still championing the claim of her divine
authority.

Helen Lorimer and Cardinal Fenton had often talked over all these
things; for years the latter cherished the idea with a foolish
fervor. Gradually the number of adherents grew. It was recognized
as an inner illuminated circle, and without any formal pledges,
secrecy was self imposed and sternly guarded. Entrance to the College
of Cardinals at Rome had been effected, and with every renewed
outburst of hostility to the Pope, not only were the convictions of
the sympathizers strengthened but new converts were gained. At one
time, the moment alluded to in a former chapter when Helen Lorimer
and Amato met in the library of the Cardinal, the approaching crisis
seemed imminent and the little Society of the Americanists, as its
members called themselves, was transported with hopeful expectations.

But disappointment—a crushing reversal of all their dreams followed.
The Pope caught ear of the revolutionary scheme, denied and denounced
it. It became doubtful whether or not some sort of Bull might not
threaten the extermination of the cult, and put on it the stigma of
heresy. With this danger hanging over them everything became still,
and the frightened colleagues no longer ventilated their hopes, or
even indulged in the precarious pleasure of consoling each other.
Cardinal Fenton bided his time. His knowledge of the crumbling state
of the European religious institution was too large and keen to
permit him to doubt some eventual catastrophe.

Helen Lorimer plunged into her humanitarian and civil schemes. Amato
said nothing. He was her faithful hand servant, always ready, always
sympathetic, ever gracious, loving, willing and enchanting. So the
thought of the Pope’s Importation, as it was half ironically referred
to—had apparently been forgotten when the thing happened which again,
by some peculiar reasoning, brought it back compellingly into Helen
Lorimer’s mind; and almost immediately something else happened in
Europe that actually won for it toleration and final accomplishment
there.

In America the momentous influence with Miss Lorimer was a posthumous
publication of a little book by the Mayor—the MS was found among his
papers by Mr. Briggs—entitled MORE LIGHT. It created a sensation.
None read it more excitedly than Helen Lorimer, Cardinal Fenton,
and Amato. Helen detected in it some of her own ideas literally
reproduced, and saw plainly a deeper influence pervading it, which
she knew was hers also. The book made the Mayor live again before her
eyes and live even more strangely and despotically within her heart.
Amato knew this, and a sharp pain sprang through his being but he
persisted with the same admirable temper, and no reproach rose to his
lips, not even a trace of the almost insupportable knowledge could be
discerned in his words, or his behaviour. As ever he did her bidding
with incomparable zeal.

The book began with an eloquent and convincing picture of the World’s
distress, the aimlessness, fragility, hopelessness, pettiness of
life in millions upon millions of human beings, the astonishing
and apparently purposeless fecundity of the race, the silence of
the grave, the depravity and meanness of mere living—and then
in chapters, fervid with wonder and despair it pointed out the
extraordinary inadequacy of Revelation, even when supplemented by all
the authority and beauty of the Church—and it closed with a thrilling
imploring appeal to the shut and silent skies, urging the whole Earth
through its manifold organs of utterance to upbraid and beseech those
skies, to force their mocking canopy of blue to open and let down
new vessels of Consecration and of Knowledge. It was well conceived,
skillfully graded in a progression of intense feeling to its climax,
and it enlisted a lyric power of expression which caught the reader
in the broad and noble flow of its imagery and emotion.

Helen Lorimer had read the book aloud to her Mother—still unwell—and
to Amato in the great library of her home. It was a room the reader
has not entered, and it was the chief treasure of the marvelous
palace. It lay in a northern section of the mansion with its roof
upheld by columns of Numidian marble. The ceiling in groined vaults
sprang from capital to capital and each vault held the golden picture
of a constellation set in a blue background. The book-cases in square
rose-wood boxes encased the columns at their bases; only at two
points around the room did the book-cases rise higher than four feet,
and here on both the east and west walls latticed shelves carried
the books almost to the high ceiling. Everywhere upon the low cases
stood marble busts and Barye bronzes. The walls were fairly covered
by paintings, though they might easily have been increased in numbers
without exhausting the ample space. Windows pierced the walls above
the book-cases on the sides, nowhere reaching the floor, and only one
entrance permitted access to this generous study.

The north end of the library was surrendered to the installation
of a gigantic fireplace built of a soft sienna terra-cotta brick,
with a high broad marble mantle above it, on which was placed a
marble _replica_ of the Hudson-Fulton Water Gate, while over that
on the wall above hung an entrancing painting of the East River in
the Morning when the pearly exhalations of the dawn, the smoke of
a propeller and the projecting cliffs of the bridge-piers and high
buildings, with the tracery of the bridge cables and roads compose
one of the most wonderful metropolitan pictures on the entire earth.

Sheraton chairs and tables were pleasantly disposed throughout the
room, and on the tables were objects of interest and beauty. The
floor was covered from end to end and from side to side with a
deep cramoisy-red carpet that gave luxurious warmth to the whole
room; green tapestries framed the windows, and the illumination was
achieved by opaline globes from the apex of each groined vault while
on the tables burned oil lamps of captivating designs in glass and
brass. The room was almost too grandiose, too public and expositional
in character, but its beauty was really remarkable and the first
impression on entering it was memorable. The books were not numerous,
many book-shelves were empty; the lowness of the book-cases precluded
a very large collection.

The winter had grown cold and now, at the end of February a heavy
snow mantled the Inwood ridge, and Carrara amid its leafless trees
rose from the dazzling surface, an indifferent rival of its purity.
The wind drove through the ranged mansions, the bleak highway was
deserted, but at night, as ever, enhanced in its strange loveliness,
the electrical aureole crowned the huge metropolis, this monumental
city-state where hurrying feet sought work and pleasure, and vice and
devotion and labor followed their designed and inevitable tasks.

Julieta, Helen Lorimer, and Amato sat in front of the great hearth
and the eyes of two watched the scurrying wisps of blue and yellow
light moving waywardly over the blocks of coal. The third—Helen—was
somewhat behind the other two at a round table reading. The last
sentence of the Mayor’s book was heard; it ran, “the Revelation,
the churches, our spirits—to-day the sole witnesses to God—move
in regions of persuasion exorbitantly feeble for the purposes of
Conviction in this matter, and, through the avenues of prayer, by
which all our needs should receive consideration, we ask, or may ask
for a SIGN.

“If God answers, the world emerges into a welcome light of
reassurance, and, if there is no answer, then seizing, as it were
the reins of things ourselves, let society resolve into new units of
action, in conformity with the demands of an utilitarian UTOPIA.”

The voice was clear and penetrating with an indefinable sweetness in
it that thrilled Amato—as it had often thrilled the dead writer of
these pregnant words. There was a silence, and again the voice was
heard:

“Those are sad, they sound to me as dreadful words. It is the
expatriation of Hope, the convulsion of despair, and that means the
Death of the Soul. If only those who ask so much could FEEL; their
questions would then die on their lips.”

“No,” answered Amato, “perhaps not. The thought is plain enough and
as he—the author puts it—it does not really seem so unreasonable. It
has a noble ring after all.”

“But,” continued Helen, “the SIGN is here. Surely you, Rudolpho
Amato”—a start brought the listener’s face around towards the
speaker, and it was filled with an almost strained attention—“know
there can be for Us, you and me”—Amato’s eyes grew tender and
grateful—“but one sign. It is the CHURCH; THAT IS THE COVENANT. To
go beyond it, outside of it, away from it, is to stumble heedlessly,
and to stumble too, unforgiven and unhelped”; it almost seemed to
Amato that the voice grew a little hard. And yet at that very moment
his own heart leaped with a great joy—it meant Helen’s return to him
from the personified Memory that had so weirdly and just perceptibly
separated them.

Amato looked a minute longer at the wonderfully beautiful and earnest
woman and then turned to the dancing flickers before him, saying:

“Are honest doubts to be so harshly punished?”

“It is the LAW,” quickly answered the woman, and in her eagerness she
rose, the Mayor’s book closing behind her on the table; “we cannot
extricate ourselves from our own conditions: we are not permitted
to criticise them. It is not a question of whether this is the best
possible world. Rather is it a question, is this a world at all?—are
we here—is anything a reality? If one allows himself to drop into
the bottomless pit of Negation, HE IS LOST. Faith—faith only is the
solvent of doubt and—it must be either Faith or doubt. Yes, there can
be works, good works, this Man”—the voice paused an instant—“was a
worker, a hard, belligerent, noble worker—works it seems to me attest
to some sort of faith—but as far as the possibility of deliverance
from this Order of things appears, it is only through Union with
God—IN THE CHURCH”; something then happened—who can say what it
was—Amato turned—the woman had sunk back into her chair—her head lay
in her hands upon the table and she was sobbing. In an instant—so
rapid was he that his figure passing through the room might have
seemed almost moved by thought—Amato was kneeling at her side, his
hand pressed upon her shoulder. “Helen?” he said.

The face lifted—a smile broke through the tears—she stood up and drew
him to her—and then—she turned and left the room. Amato watched her
with his hands pressed to his breast; he looked towards Julieta—she
was fast asleep.

The next day the morning papers brought a new astonishment into
thousands of households throughout the New World. The Vatican had
been assaulted—an armed mob had invaded the gardens of the Pope’s
palace, and the Pope himself had retreated to St. Peter’s wherein
he was literally barricaded, while the troops of the state remained
on the defensive at its doors. The event in itself was astonishing
enough, but the most serious feature of the situation was the
temporizing, supine, almost approving policy of the government.
Catholics were terror stricken at the almost inconceivable
predicament. This news was received on Saturday.

The next morning at the Cathedral of St. Patrick’s, Cardinal Fenton
rose in the pulpit and to an enormous audience, among whom sat
Helen Lorimer and Amato, delivered a sermon of marvellous skill and
boldness. The note of defiance rose in it at times, to be succeeded
by invocations to the loyalty of believers, and to be followed
by denunciations of Europe as brutal, perverted sterilized, and
hopeless. Then came passages of extraordinary novelty. The speaker
dwelt upon the forlorn and debilitating position of the Church in
Europe—enveloped in a false civilization, of privilege, of title, of
social selfishness, turpitude, and callous heartlessness; he pointed
out the enfeebling influence of the association through centuries,
with the prejudices, misconceptions, and artificial structure of a
society that through interior decay presented only the glazed and
ornamented shell of civilization, and was decomposing into some final
product of sensuality and atheism.

Then he turned his contemplative gaze upon America, strong in its
immense material power, resolute in its support of its original
contentions in civic life, fortified beyond belief by the average
elevation of the masses who found in it peace, education, competence,
and opportunity. Yet here again the note of complete confidence
wavered and there was betrayed a deep misgiving. The recent
socialistic or anarchistic outbreak was analysed as the ebullition
of hatred and envy as also the symptom of a deep-seated disorder—the
false theory of the State as a collective organism with indisputably
no individualistic rights, a formless mass of industrial units,
animated by a vacuous sentiment of brotherhood, and destitute of the
power of real and vitalizing progress, because the Individual was
thwarted in his ambitions and denied his preeminence.

He paused a moment to dwell on popular vices, on indulgences,
on luxury, on the growth of pernicious habits, on the schism of
self-destruction, and then quoting an old author of the Nineteenth
Century—George Santayana—he proceeded “if a noble and civilized
democracy is to subsist, the common citizen must be something of a
saint and something of a hero. We see therefore how justly flattering
and profound and at the same time, how ominous was Montesquieu’s
saying that the Principle of Democracy is VIRTUE.”

From this point on to his peroration Cardinal Fenton pressed the
contention that the Church was indeed the safe-guard, and vindication
of Virtue; the terms he presented were inexorable, the consequences
of their rejection disastrous. But with again startling emphasis he
predicted the approach of the last conflict between Satan and God.
He urged upon grounds of sanity and strategy and experience that the
Church entrench herself, gathering in her forces, concentrating her
energy, establishing her prestige within the United States of America
where alone to-day, he concluded “her beauty is most ravishing and
irresistible, her efficacy most transcendental, her sanctuaries
most honored” and then in rushing winged sentences he plead for the
Translation of the Pope—of the Vicar of Christ—“to these western
shores where with the Faithful he shall yet defend this foot-stool of
the Most High against the swarming emissaries of Hell.”

The sermon was literally reproduced in the great dailies, indeed
printed copies of it were despatched over the entire country
throughout the larger cities, and in a moment, in the interval
between the setting of one sun and the rising of the next, the
reflective mind of the nation confronted an extraordinary proposition.

Censure, approval, comment and derision met it, but by some
psychological sequence, it became an absorbing and interesting
question that assumed greater and greater reality. Helen Lorimer’s
apathy vanished. She seized anew upon the discarded hope, and somehow
it worked harmoniously with her repudiation of the claim or plea
made in the Mayor’s book. The Pope established in America should be
the Sign—no other; in the displacement of his two thousand year’s
residence in Rome, in the abolition of an ancient glory, in the
new growth and sanctification of the Church in this free land—in
these things should be seen the Hand of God. Amato was no less
enthusiastic. He worked at the same problem and exerted all his art
and fluency to win converts.

The sensation caused by the Cardinal’s sermon was even greater
in Europe than in America where the custom of free speech was
recognized. Abroad its impact upon the formalized ears of the
dignitaries of the church was stunning. And yet there was no
resentment. Dismay and consternation spread, but not in remonstrance,
only in a heart breaking attitude of amazement that this extremity
should be so boldly risked. And deeply working, the leaven of this
suggestion—not unknown before to many, even well considered by many,
as we have seen in Amato’s report to the Cardinal—was converting the
heart of the POPE himself.

Suddenly there appeared a bull—_Ecclesia renovata et migratoria_—and
the edict was issued that the Pope would abandon Europe, walk out
of Rome, and transfer the seat of his See to the shores of America.
Amazing contradiction, when a few months before, this same procedure
had been anathematized. But Inconsistency is the secret of Progress.
The dilemma was sternly stated, the whole bull was minatory and yet,
in places it was tearful with lamentations, which in rolling Latin
had a most august solemnity. It was a moment of violent transition.
The bull, unannounced, unreviewed, calmly thrown into the faces of
the Propaganda, and the Congregation awoke no terrible opposition.
Cardinal Fenton by a stroke, had won. The position in Europe had
become unbearable. But it was an act of superb renouncement, of
stupendous humiliation; the emptying of its Glory by the Head of the
Church; the stripping bare to flesh and bone of the notorious and
priceless apparel which time, and devotion, and wealth, had dressed
him in; for it was ordained that He come to America as a Man among
men—a world-traveller seeking a new home, as of one who had nowhere
to lay his head. It was indeed a prostration, a vast penance, a
dislocation that at last severed the Church from its awful past, a
past superb in its spiritual conquests, but a past also black with
sins.

The house was made ready. It crowned the low hills of Staten Island.
Everything was provided by Helen Lorimer. She hastened every
preparation, with pride, with gayety, with love—for the pact had been
made between her and Amato that the Pope should marry them. At last
it seemed to Amato that the Mayor no longer ruled in her heart; the
conflict was quite passed, and he—he alone possessed her.

And the Pope came. He came in analogy with the whole wonderful and
incredible procedure. He came in conformity with just the requirement
which the jealous and suspicious temperament of the country he
visited, required. He came as a priest with priests, without pomp,
or retinue, or vestments. The affair had been anticipated with a
new delight by hundreds of thousands who rejoiced in sensations, in
spectacles, in violent surprises, in the climaxes of public events.
And it had been most curiously regarded by that protestant portion
of the society, which, a hundred years before, would have been
thrown into paroxysms of revolt, consternation, and rage, at the
mere thought of the transference of the Roman See to this country.
Now, at a time when spiritual power among their churches seemed so
near extinction, they hailed it with pleasure, with the most cordial
congratulations, and with actual endorsement. This very attitude had
been in a way expected by Cardinal Fenton, who saw how immensely it
ameliorated the abrupt Transition on both sides of the religious
fences.

The former of these groups—those who looked for some monumental
display of ceremony—was disappointed. It was all so quiet, so
informal, so commonplace, that except for its momentous moral
significance, it hardly stirred the surface of events with a
perceptible disturbance. Nothing could have been less phenomenal
or less memorable. Art and calculation had combined to make
its impression, as a physical event, almost imperceptible. But
the expatriation of the Pope was an extraordinary fact, and
no suppression of external show could minimize its stupendous
implications. It indeed marked a crisis in the religious life of the
whole world.

The Pope left Rome _incognito_, traversed France, embarked at
Havre, reached New York, was received by a committee, itself so
inconspicuously constituted, as scarcely to receive notice in the
public prints, and taken in a closed carriage to the modest mansion,
adjoining a small but beautiful chapel—the whole installation devised
by Helen Lorimer and Cardinal Fenton, and its expense wholly defrayed
by her—on the highest hill of Staten Island.

There was really something quite abnormal and disillusionizing
in it all. The Pope had even for the time divorced himself from
the appurtenances of his immense establishments. The Index, the
Inquisition, the Propaganda, the vast complicated machinery of
extension and distribution remained at Rome. The Pope alone appeared
at the threshold of the New World, coming without any artifice of
show and pageantry. It had something evangelical and primary about
it, and touched the imagination favorably. A wave of affection went
out to the prelate, and the absence of rebuke or injurious derision
marked a subtle influence propitious to the Cardinal’s project. The
personality of the Pope himself helped this delightful result. He
was charming, and tactful, and intellectually strong. He had already
said beautiful and true things about America. He had captured her
attention, and approval, long ago. Protestant bigotry restrained
itself, and the sightseers and the indifferent wondered, smiled
and applauded. It was all excellent throughout, and Pope Sextus
celebrated his first Mass in America without remonstrance, without
disturbance, almost without comment. He was indeed a Bernard of
Clairvaux.

Later the inevitable happened. That vast insatiable appetite for the
marvelous, and the recondite, and ritualistic, with its tremendous
possibilities of satisfaction, which the Catholic Church nurtured,
demanded its conditioning approval. It could only be given on one
assumption, that the resources gathered through ages of ceremonial
development, magnified by the most modern auxiliaries of light and
music should be employed to memorialize and establish the monstrous
revolution. Unless the seal of the Supernatural rested upon it, its
triumph would be temporary and crumbling. Thus, spontaneously and
together, the Catholic congregations throughout the country prepared
a glorious and stupifying Jubilee. In it the ardor of devotion,
the zeal of faith, and the pride of sect united with a discernible
prophetic instinct, to make it unparalleled. An incredible skill
controlled it. Cardinal Fenton and his agents were everywhere,
bringing the products of thought, of eloquence, of allusion, to be
inwrought and perpetuated through the myriad-tongued utterances of
its sermons and addresses. And in the midst of it with the most
thrilling nobility of style the POPE appeared at St. Patrick’s
Cathedral, as its cause and inspiration.

Pope Sextus was a peculiar man, shaped in a mould of mental, moral,
and physical perfection; perhaps the only Pope, that ever ruled the
Church, who could have taken this precarious step, a step threatening
such monstrous revulsions, and so inevitably endangered by collapse,
ridicule, the extinction of personal prestige as the Pope separated
himself from seclusion, and all the veils of splendid and historical
accidents. For once the MAN stood out before the world in the
habiliments of the street, as it were. He was not found at the end of
long corridors, mysteriously guarded by chamberlains and soldiers,
fixed beneath a canopy, and surrounded by the marvels of art. He
had abdicated all that, and his PRESENCE was in the open. It was an
ordeal, a shock that only the rarest combinations of qualities could
meet and survive. And the Pope met it and survived. This was the
magnificent fact that brought a profound ecstacy to Cardinal Fenton,
to Helen Lorimer, to Amato, and the leaders of this bold innovation.
But the Pope himself—he alone—made it possible.

A reporter of the New York Times, who attended the Pope’s Mass
at the Cathedral, thus described him: “The Pope seems young, but
that impression is dissipated when his bearing, action, voice, and
eloquence are considered. At first I could only think of the St.
Gabriel depicted by Guido where the young and stalwart archangel
descends with his sword upon the head of the falling Satan. His
type is certainly not blond, yet it is noticeably less dark than
the typical Italian. His hair is brown or chestnut, luxuriant, and
in light rolling waves surmounts a brow of fine proportions, in a
countenance of the most pronounced beauty. It is quite impossible to
describe the power of his eyes which appear always soft and tender,
and yet have a peculiar indefinable penetration. He is utterly apart
from the conventional pope, with a skull cap, and no hair, diaphanous
features, and the filminess of ascetic depletion, imparting to him a
ghostly sacredness. The Pope is probably fifty; the first impression
of youth is effaced as you watch him, and, above all, as you hear him.

“The service was a pontifical mass grandly rendered, the music
subdued, and full prominence was given to the celebrant, who was the
Pope himself. It was a lesson in the majesty and music of the human
voice. Never (and as a catholic I constantly attend mass), have I
heard singing so moving and ideal. But that again was forgotten, when
the superb figure—the Pope is tall—rose in the pulpit, and the same
entrancing voice preached from the Text; ‘Lo I am with you Always.’

“Here he was miraculous. His self assertion was most puzzling
but it was ineffaceable. He moved from appeals to historical
description, and from these to argument, and then decorating
everything with poetry, he threw all aside, and revealed himself as
The Vicar of Christ on Earth. It seemed appalling, the intensity
of his confidence, and yet more amazing it seemed convincing. As
he retreated to the altar, an anthem of Hayden’s broke forth in
delicate and joyous strains, its musical pulsations wavering over
the prostrate multitude, who were about to receive the Apostolic
Benediction while the subtler fragrance of the incense lingered
through the church, and the windows blazed their illuminated
suggestions of sacrifice and worship. As the dazed disciple said, it
‘seemed good to be there.’”

This hap-hazard reportorial encounter described correctly the feeling
created in the gigantic multitude that thronged the edifice. And all
this was followed by a most beautifully conceived Bull, issued from
Staten Island which, as with the finger of an act of transfiguration
rooted the Pope and the _Idea of the Pope forever_ in the New World.
