And then rising with thrills and transports of pleasure from one
surprise to another, the staring populace of the great City-State
were invited to consider the marriage of Helen Lorimer to Rudolpho
Amato, celebrated by this same democratic and transported Pope. It
was a climax. It came at a moment of precise effectiveness. Never had
Miss Lorimer risen to a higher power for good than since the Mayor’s
death, and the end of the unsuccessful socialistic revolution. We
have already seen how her own class struggled—and honestly—to create
a beneficent and democratic aristocracy in the state. This was
continued. It was one of the dearest dreams of Helen herself. She
arranged and animated its advances. Her action in the defense of the
suspected but innocent men, caught at the fire of her Settlement
House, had given to her a renewed and an almost impregnable
popularity and vogue. And this was supplemented and augmented by
the splendid gift to the City of her completed Museum—completed
one year after the death of the Mayor, and on the anniversary of
the insurrection. The New Museum was placed exactly where the old
Settlement House had been.

It was a square building with a flat roof, and possessed seven
stories. The roof was fitted out for recreation, and its height
supplied a healthful and cool resort in the hot summer months; the
two floors below it were kept for meetings, classes, libraries,
and a _creche_ for working mothers. The remaining floors held the
Museum, and it was developed in the line of Helen’s own explanations
to the Mayor, in that first night of their meeting at her home. It
was an objectified Gazetteer, with more history, and more allusion,
and a mild didacticism interwoven. Civilizations were depicted, and
contrasted, and much of their forlornness was not forgotten, the
lesson of progressive betterments being shown, and that gradual
emancipation of the race from a debased selfishness, but the growth
of all such altruism was regarded—a little narrowly—as the fruit of
Christianity.

On that score Miss Lorimer would abate nothing. It was an unique and
most original spot, and so well imagined to combine pleasure, and
usefulness, and instruction, and help—for in its lower basement it
had a dispensatory—that it was instantly thronged. But Miss Lorimer
intended to attach to it a proprietary interest. No one could enter
it and use it but the Members of the Order Club. Everyone could
belong to this club, but its membership came under the scrutiny of
its directors, and they were elected. Every head of a Household paid
five cents a month for his family, and all others one cent. The
scheme worked well, and the people took a pride in it, and in their
memberships. Miss Lorimer endowed it, and put well paid curators in
it as well. Its expansion was further provided for.

It was the night before their wedding that Amato and Helen were
together in the great library of her home. The winter cold had
hastened somewhat, and the fireplace was filled with flaring logs.
They had been sitting together in front of this cheerful light. Helen
sat forward on her chair her face resting in her hands while her
arms rested on her knees and she looked into the ranked and flaming
wood-pile. Amato leaned far back in his chair and his eyes were on
her alone, their rapt and happy gaze changing at intervals into
brief smiles that parted the curved lips and made him more handsome.
For never had that extreme physical charm seemed fairer to him than
to-night. Helen had only just turned her bewildered glance from his
compelling beauty, as if she fled from its overpowering tyranny.
Amato was perhaps half conscious of his control, and that brought
into his eyes the rising and vanishing smile. But his slavery to the
beautiful woman was abject. There may have been just then the sense
of a vanquishing power which maintained his freedom and his mastery;
but in his heart he knew the relentless passion that desired and yet,
through the benignity of his mind’s higher insight, feared.

“Helen, can you see the future in those blazing embers? Tea-leaves
are better accredited among the sybils.” The voice was half mocking.

Helen laughed a little, and without turning to her companion
answered: “Sometimes I wonder whether we can give inanimate
things—when they are not altogether stocks and stones—some power born
of ourselves to tell us what we wish to know. Surely those burning
motes filled with fire, all their parts vibrating with motion, down
to their last atoms, might now be ready to respond to the tyrannous
demands of our souls.”

“But how will they answer?” the voice now betrayed some interest.

“Why, by pictures. They have no other power. We ourselves are the
Seers and we catch in them thrown outwards from our own divinations
the images of the things to come.”

“Like the Pythagorean who by looking formed the clay after the image
of his thought, or those less recondite magicians who tell your
fortune by a crystal sphere.”

“Ah! Rudolph you must not trifle; the pictures are symbols and they
are allusions only—not photographs of what you and I will do—” she
rose from her stooping position and laid her hand upon his, still
regarding the crumbling and reforming mirages of the fire;—“but
types, as now a flame, and now a star, and now the—Yes I see it—the
Cross—”.

“Helen are you one who ‘looks into the seeds of time and tells which
grain will grow and which will not?’”

“Why Rudolph, it sometimes seems to me as if our souls left us, were
like sensitive glasses feeling the approaching years with what they
hold, as the barometer, long before the storm or the fair weather,
falls or rises, responding so minutely to the changing pressure
around it.”

 “O Love could you and I with fate conspire
 To grasp this sorry scheme of things entire,
 Would not we shatter it to bits—and then
 Remould it nearer to the heart’s desire?”

Amato leaned forward as he repeated the smooth lines, with his
voice tuned to the depth of his devotion for this woman, leaned
so far forward indeed that his cheek brushed against her own, and
then his hand was lifted and rested on her shoulder. She turned,
he slid forward to his knees on the floor before her. Their faces
were opposite each other, their eyes met, and then—almost with
violence—Amato leaped to his feet seized her hands, drew her up to
himself and crushed her lips with his own. She was plastic in his
arms, but there was no return of his frantic pressure.

He slowly relinquished his constraint, and a sudden pain wrote itself
in his face—it almost again seemed to him that that invisible
partition of the dead man’s memory yet divided them, very slightly
perhaps, but Love is so imperious, that the thought stabbed him like
a knife and Helen understood. She saw his pain—her words came to his
ears like music—such sweet blissful assurance—and yet—;

“No! No! Amato I love you—there is no reservation—” her voice
faltered, and tears sprang into her eyes: “We shall be happy—we shall
work—the greatness of human destiny will be for us a watchword for
endeavor—for all sorts of noble enterprises—”

It was her turn to rouse him—he stood looking at her wistfully,
thankfully—but somehow that deep egotism of the true lover craved
some greater personal surrender—Why now should designs of Work hide
HIM from her—was there not Love—Possession—at least for a little
while; let all else be forgotten, and the one personal act of Union
reign supreme.

He dropped her hands and laughed bitterly.

“Ah Helen—ever Work! Cannot I count for something?” the laugh
vanished—that tender boyish smile, so beguiling and winning came
into his eyes and his mouth parted. Helen Lorimer saw it all now—and
the revelation, pointing so deeply to the unsatisfied longing,
the quenchless thirst for surrender and temporal oblivion, lit up
within her a strange wonderful delight. She flung her arms about
Amato’s neck, she nestled her head upon his shoulder, she sought
his mouth with her lips, and in the flood—the tide running fast and
unrestrained—of joy, as if a touch had liberated imprisoned waters,
the two lost all reference to the Future—one abounding, mastering,
remorselessly ecstatic PRESENT held them wedded in mind, in heart, in
soul.

Six months later an official ceremony sealed this voiceless pact.
It was a glorious wedding. Such a nuptial splendor had never before
been seen in New York. Cardinal Fenton intended that it should be
made tributary to his own designs; it certainly accentuated again
the fact of the new Presence in America—the Pope’s. It was held at
dawn. The long procession of carriages from Carrara was resplendently
conceived. They were lit with oil torches, and, along the avenues
they passed, arches of lights and welcoming discharges of rockets
administered a civic public note that was unmistakable. Agreeable
to Helen Lorimer’s orders largess, in forms of banquets, gifts and
endowments had incorporated the whole great city in this noble
function. The Cathedral of St. Patrick’s was encrusted with light,
from arch and fret and angle the electric coronas sent their rays,
and at its entrance the Pope’s Arms deftly interwoven with the Eagle
and the Flag of the States surmounted a flashing arch of lights,
and, more ominously to some, above these shone the escutcheon of the
City-State.

Within the sacred structure the spectacle was a ravishing picture
of color and coruscation. Climbing coils of light terminating in
pendent colored globes, lined the central aisle, the altar dressed
to its very summit with roses sparkled also with lights; enormous
candles burned within the sanctuary, the thrones of the Pope and the
Cardinal were opposite each other and each was a hill of lilies.
The Pope celebrated the Nuptial Mass and awaited at the altar the
slow progress of the bridal procession which was itself a vision of
beauty. Priests and acolytes, in swaying groups surrounded the Pope
and the Cardinal.

Helen had wished to embody again her generous emotions in this
great act of her life. One of the lovely and touching features of
the bridal train was the presence of fifty little girls from the
East Side, and as many boys carrying chains of flowers, chosen
protegees from her many foundations of charity. In the centre of
this escort Helen, with her twelve bridesmaids, accompanied only by
her Mother Julieta, approached. Rudolph awaited her at the steps of
the Communion Rail supported by his company of groomsmen, while
through the huge temple of worship the music and the chorus of the
Lohengrün Wedding March breathed its festive melodies. The curious
archaic custom of enclosing the bride and bridegroom in the nuptial
cloth was observed, and when at last the rite was consummated the
blessing given by the transported Pope, the recessional begun to the
accompaniment of a full orchestra playing Mendelssohn’s Spring Song,
while three thousand guests watched and wondered—it truly seemed as
if the Great City was about to enter itself upon some new chapter of
development wherein should be seen again, in closer conflict, the
allied strength of Helen Lorimer’s beneficent Democracy of Wealth
and the Hierarchy of the Church, against the Lust of Life and the
disintegrating agitations of Socialism.

Was it auspicious that as Rudolph assisted Helen, and her Mother, to
the equipage, of which he was now an occupant, that the risen Sun of
the May morning shone brightly, and that throngs in the streets burst
into cheers?
